## P: P & T–perfing (out)

### P & T
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Post & Telegraph

>  <b>WILSON</b> Moving to the corner, with the P. & T. workshop on his left ... <i>(1957,Strip Jack Naked,147</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The Post and Telegraph of Christchurch were playing the College. ... two `All Blacks' were included in the P. & T. Team, Deans and Wood, the latter playing in the three-quarter line. ... [67] The game see-sawed to the tune of P. & T. leading. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,66</i>)

### P out
 
 <i>v.</i> to dose heavily on the drug P.

>  A friend.. said he told her he had been "P-ing out" - on pure speed - for most of the weekend. <i>(Apr 24, 2003,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

### P-class
 
 <i>n.</i> a class of small centreboard yacht (formerly aka Tauranga class)

>  [Harry Highet's] seven-foot P-class, produced in 1922, became the standard and is arguably the reason for New Zealand's leadership in international yachting. <i>(Dec 11, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> They came round raffling off a P-class yacht for a bob a ticket. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,61</i>)

>  "I was given a P-class by my uncle. ..." <i>(Dec 7, 2005,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

### P-head
 
 <i>n.</i> a P-addict

>  What if she had a hoody and some track pants on? Does that make her an evil drug smoking, alcoholic p-head as well? <i>(Feb 25, 2004,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  Gleefully anti-PC - the boys are described as "dumb-ass P-heads and no-hopers" by Carol Hirschfeld in a cameo - <i>bro'Town</i> revels in skewering Polynesian culture, Auckland, single parents, smug Pakehas, gays, and Indian dairy owners in equal measure. <i>(Oct 17, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),29</i>)

### P.B.
 
 <i>n.</i> Permanent Base

>  P.B. - Permanent Base. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)



 
 <i>abbr.</i> Pale Brandy

>  Quaffing fiery bumpers of P.B. [in a pub]. <i>(1869,Punch or the Auckland Charivari,169</i>)

>  <b>bath</b> Thatcher, a witty singer of topical songs, seized upon the incident which he recounted at his next entertainment partly in recitative interspersed with songs, and in describing the incident in the bar told how another leading citizen approached the victim, and after the manner of Madam Escott warbled: Take this glass of nice P.B./ Which I now hold in my hand,/ Tis of the brand of Hennissy/ Taste this most superior brand/ The amount of drinking that went on in those days was very much greater than has been the case for very many years. Pale brandy or P.B. was the favourite spirit [in Dunedin, 1860s], and a sparkling moselle, which was dubbed champagne, was popular under the name of No. 2. There was even a bar on board the harbour steamer ... and I have heard No. 2 called for there on the way to Port Chalmers. ... <i>(1925,Random Recollections,17</i>)

### P.H. Helmet
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  P.H. HELMET. - A kind of helmet worn as a protection against poison gas. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> Protestant Political Association

>  The new men are ... Stallworthy (son of old John of Kaipara & head of the P.P.A.) [ed fn: Protestant Political Association.] Minister of Health. <i>(Dec 17, 1928,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/155,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

### P.R.
 
 <i>abbr.</i> a piece of P.R. land

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> You have bought a good bit of the run now ... The P.R. up the White Rock river is very stoney. <i>(1981,Station Years,122</i>) [NOTES: 1878 Jan McCaw in 1981 Crawford Station Years 122]

### P.U.
 
 <i>abbr.</i>

>  <i>P.U.'s. - See </i>`Lead-Swinging.' <i>Lead-Swinging. - See</i> P.U.'s - they can tell you. The origin of this term is obscure - the destination, Blighty, or the clink. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,32</i>) [NOTES: (Digger's Dictionary)]

### P.W. Dears
 
 <i>n.</i> humorous elaboration of <i>P.W.D-ers</i>, Public Works employees.

>  <b>COOZE</b> Odds and ends. .... carted out to the footpath for hard-working P.W. Dears to clear. <i>(1946,My Little State Home in Suburbia,9 et pass.</i>)

### pa
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of fish hook, couta stick

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BARRACOUTA (MANGA) <i>Leionura atun dentatus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) INCREDIBLE as it may seem these fish, with their well-armed jaws of sharp fang-like teeth, were usually caught on a slightly curved stick about four feet in length. Fastened to this stick was a piece of stout fishing line about three to four feet in length, carrying a piece of red-coloured wood - usually cedar - about five to six inches in length. A bent nail was secured to this piece of wood in the form of a hook but no bait was used. When a school of Barracouta was found the Couta stick, or paw, was brought out ready for fishing. From the cockpit of the launch the piece of wood with the nail was violently swirled with a circular motion in and out of the water, causing a disturbance of the water in a good imitation of a number of Sprats, Pilchards or other small fish jumping in and out of the sea. The Barracouta would swim for such a place. Seeing the piece of red wood moving rapidly through the water they would take it for a fish, snap at it or even jump out of the water for the lure and were caught by the crude iron hook. As the fish was hooked during the time the paw was being drawn through the water the fisherman would swing the fish over his head above the boat or launch and when the caught fish was midway in the air, more or less above the cockpit, would expertly give the stick a dexterous smart twist. As there was no barb on the hook the fish was released and fell into the cockpit. <i>Ibid.</i> 311 Should there be any Barracouta about the... Couta sticks were then set to work to paw the water and attract Barracouta to the spot. <i>Ibid.</i> 314 The Maori also knew he could catch Barracouta without bait and he, too, used a short piece of stick and line with a baitless hook and leaned over his canoe and threshed the water to and fro, which is known as `Kaihau manga!' When the European fisherman called his fishing outfit [315] `paw', the Maori uses the term `pa'. This fish was a great favourite with them and was caught in large numbers. On returning to the shore, the women cleaned the fish and then hung them up on racks to dry in the sun. Old Maoris of Opotiki told me they used a chip of Tawhai (<i>Nothofagus fusca</i>), which is dark red, in the making of a pa (paw). <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,310</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (historical) a fortified village, a fort; a tribal settlement usually fortified. [ORIGIN: Maori, hold the pa P.S. Ardern: Pah Road, Auckland) (see also compounds PAFUL)] (See [hold the pa](hea-hom.md#hold-the-pa))

>  Cook describes a hippa or eppah as enclosed on three sides, and the place chosen could not have been better if the best engineer in England had been employed to choose it. <i>(Aug 25, 1892,Otago Witness,43</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera ed.</b> She had pored over pictures of Hokianga - a seascape of luminous dunes and tides, a landscape of pa sites and legends... <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,17</i>) [NOTES: (Lindsay Charman-Love)]

>  <b>156</b> Have since learnt that they have strongholds - or hippas, as they call them. [Hawkesworth spelt it, Heppahs] <i>Ibid.</i> (1955) 1/217 We pass'd round a point of land on which stood a Heppa or fortified village. [fn5] <i>pa</i>: Heppah from <i>he pa</i>, `it is a pa.' <i>(1769,Cook's Journals,(ed. Wharton 1893) ed.</i>)

>  Near the village was the ruins of an old Indian <i>Eppah</i> or Fort. <i>(Nov 10, 1769,Banks Journal,1/431,1962 ed.</i>)

>  ... they have strong holds or happa' s as they call them. <i>(Nov 15, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/203</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> Near the entrance to this river, which they named the Thames, there was a village, and a Hippa, or place of refuge, erected to defend it, which was surrounded by piquets that reached above the water when the tide was up; ... <i>(Nov 20, 1769,Journal,106</i>)

>  ... several large heppas were in sight ... <i>(Nov 28, 1769,Banks Journal,1/440,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> This hippah (Te Puke) is situated at the mouth of the fresh-water river on a beautiful eminence which commands the River Thames both above and below. <i>(Jul 18, 1820,Letters & Journals,260,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|a, <i>s</i>. An elevated village; ... a place cleared for residence. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,186</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> In the morning some of the gentlemen accompanied Krokro to his <i>pah</i> or fort, where he then resided, and which was about a mile from the ship. <i>(Mar 3, 1820,Journal,45,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1820 Mar 3 in 1823 Cruise Journal (1957) 45]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> At 3 pm came to an Anchor at Parkinneigh [Pakanae] a Village and had I suppose 150 Natives on board and a par [<i>pa</i>] or Fort about 12 miles from the Heads. <i>Ibid.</i> 33 Hattay [crossed out by Markham] or Ettay [<i>heti</i>] is a Tea tree, quite different to the Tea tree bush, a sort of myrtle; as Parr a Fort and Hipparr [<i>he pa</i>] a Fort in Cooke, / is put down The Hip or Hit, is only the Article. Tay, it is useless, it only grows eight to twelve feet and the bark rough, and does not change. It seems very often Solitary. Away from every other Tree. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),29,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> <i>Capt Cook </i>went to the <i>Hipa</i> [p-grave] & dug there up some more ground & sowed Strawberries, ... Sellery & Parsley. <i>(Jun 1, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/290</i>)

>  <b>DARWIN</b> These are the Pas, so frequently mentioned by Captain Cook under the name of `hippah;' the difference of sound being owing to the prefixed article. <i>(Dec 22, 1835,Voyage of the Beagle,Ch.XVIII (Everyman edn) 402</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> The Pa, or native fortification, is a place in which the natives of single tribes ... when living near each other ... assemble in times of war, and secure themselves, their wives, their children, and their slaves, from the attacks of the enemy. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),122</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Previous to our arrival at the <i>Pa</i> [a-acute], or fortified village, I halted for my companions [76] in a deep glen, from which the settlement was not visible. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),75</i>)

>  They put us into a fenced place which they called a `par', a sort of stockade; there they kept us three days, naked as we were; they gave us some potatoes. <i>(Aug 22, 1834,Examination of John Guard</i>) [NOTES: 1834 Aug 22 Examination of John Guard from Sir R. Bourke's Despatch 4 in Parliamentary Papers Relating to NZ (Canterbury Public Library)]

>  Lesson gives a footnote of which the following is a translation: `Navigators write this word in a variety of ways. The English have adopted <i>hippa</i>, and some of the French write <i>pa</i>. I prefer<i> i-pah, which means THE PAH, as being more in keeping with the spirit of our language.</i> <i>(1838,Examination of John Guard</i>) [NOTES: 1838-1839 R.P. Lesson footnote in Voyage autour du monde sur la corvette La Coqille transl. Diana Quarmby in 1971 Duperrey's Visit to New Zealand in 1824 54]

>  DECLARATION of Sovereignty over <i>Tavai Poenammoo</i>. This island, called Tavai Poenammoo or Middle Island of New Zealand ... was accordingly taken possession of ... and Her Majesty's colours hoisted at the paa of Hoiakaka, Cloudy Bay ... <i>(Jun 17, 1840,GBPP,(No.311) 112</i>) [NOTES: 1840 June 17 Thomas Bunbury's Report [paa] in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 112]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The natives who reside at Pah Taranaki [Te Aro, Wellington] complained to me ... <i>(Oct 9, 1840,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Oct 9 Shortland to Hobson in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 120]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Pa or E'Pa (the fort) and written by Cook as Hippah. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/25</i>)

>  <b>MATHEW</b> AAA. Reserves ... that on Thorndon Flat is occupied by an extensive native pah, which the natives have not alienated ... The second was intended for a basin, an idea suggested probably by the swampy character of the ground around it; but besides that, such a project is of very questionable advantage; ... <i>(Oct 20, 1841,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Oct 20 F.Mathew to Hobson in GBPP 1842 (No.569) 185]

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> ... confining the rest in their Pas, ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,31,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> . . .2200 natives [lived] in their Pas (or forts). <i>(Dec 25, 1842,NZ Journal,40,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... where the strong hold of the Natives is & which they call a <i>Hepa</i> [e-grave]. <i>(May 18, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/282</i>)

>  <i>pa</i>: Heppah from <i>he pa</i>, `it is a pa.' <i>(1785,Cook's Third Voyage</i>) [NOTES: (1785 Cook Third Voyage and 1798 [fn5]]

>  <b>KENDALL</b> I and Mr. Hall accompanied him to his principal hipwah (<i>pa</i>) or town, called Ranghee Hoo (Rangihoua). It consists of several warrees (<i>whares</i>) or small huts about five feet in height, seven in breadth, and eight or ten feet in length. <i>(Sep 6, 1814,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Sep 6 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 59]

>  <b>KENDALL</b> When he landed two or three of the principal natives surrounded him, and, pointing to the island where the hippah <i>(pa)</i> formerly stood, ... <i>(Jun 1, 1815,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1815 June 1 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 84]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> [hippah] [Korra-Korra] thought it most advisable for us to accept an invitation, which his uncle Bennee gave us, to visit his <i>hippah</i>, or fortified village. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/266</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> When we got upon the top of the island, which is very high, we had a full view of Korro Korro's hippah or castle which was situated on top of another island less than two miles distant; ... <i>(1819,Letters & Journals,153,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The New Zealander has a fixed and settled habitation: he resides in his pah, which is afortified stockade; or in <i>Kainga Maori</i>, or native settlement, which is not enclosed, where the houses are scattered about as in a village. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/332</i>)

>  Slept on the site of former pah and in the humu's. <i>(Dec 23, 1848,Torlesse Papers,44,1958 ed.</i>)

>  To enforce this <i>tapu</i> they have constructed a pa on Rawiri's section on the Bell Block ... <i>(Sep 20, 1854,Taranaki Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1854 Sep 20 Taranaki Herald in 1856 Fitton NZ 153]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> [Caption to illustration of huts in foreground and a palisaded fort on hill in background]. Native village with swing and pa in the distance. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/209</i>) [NOTES: [contrast pa/village]]

>  <b>NICHOLL</b> The Pah from the outside looks a most insignificant place. <i>(Apr 29, 1864,Journal,206</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLL</b> When we got there we could see some of the 68th in skirmishing order in front of the pa, the natives were firing at them ... <i>(Apr 27, 1864,Journal,199</i>)

>  <b>MEADE</b> The <i>runanga</i> ... was assembled at the great pah today. <i>(Dec 21, 1864,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Dec 21 Meade Ride through Disturbed Districts [pah] in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 433]

>  <b>LADY CAMPBELL</b> `No, no!' exclaimed the native vehemently. `Martinera is a raugaturah (chief), he will not betray Okinga; see, there is the pah.' <i>(1864,Martin Tobin,1/67</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> Curious customs in Maori warfare The Maoris also left an ancient matron in a very strong Pah at the `Wairoa' which they abandoned when the Left wing of our column was sent to storm it. <i>(1865,Journal</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> He'd shelled an empty pa there, To rush it then he tried, <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,16</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Thatcher Maori Captives in Local Songs 16]

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> There is nothing to ... worry the sheep in New Zealand unless it is a Maori dog or two; and a few carefully laid baits in the neighbourhood of a Maori Pah soon make the owners of the dogs more careful ... <i>(1881,Chequered Career,36</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> Another time I rode to a `pah,' or fortified village, surrounded on all sides by a high fence. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,34</i>)

>  A sally was made from the pah, but it was easily repulsed. Within the pah the enemy were secure. <i>(1889,Picturesque Australasia,V/46</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A Maori village of the old days was, if of importance, always a <i>pa</i> or fortress, although small temporary abodes might be made among the cultivations on lower lands. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,300</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Pa (pah)</i>: A fortified Maori village. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,186</i>)

>  <i> ~ n.</i> a native settlement or fort surrounded by a stockade; a fortified village. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> This pah, the clearing station for New Zealand's greenstone traffic, was soon afterwards captured by Te Rauparaha, ... <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,9</i>)

>  The Maori word for a Maori fort with its ditches and palisades. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),287</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The fortified hill-top village, the <i>pa</i>, was capable of offering resistance to weapons much more formidable than those of the Maori. <i>Ibid.</i> 302 [Maori glossary] <i>pa</i> Stockade;fortified village <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,24</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> PA Fortified village, or redoubt. <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> <i>pa</i> fortress, fortified village, field fortification <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  In this interval we saw several patches of Cultivation - a fortification or <i>Heppa</i> on the face of an [sic] hill and a few stragling flat Huts upon the ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/582 a village with an Heppa contiguous situated upon an eminence. <i>(Oct 12, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/575</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 12 W.B. Monkhouse in 1955 Cook Journal I-575]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>pa:</i> fortified village, usually situated on a mountain top. (See kainga <i>above</i>) <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  It was during this time that Waitaha had a pa on the mountain, before being defeated in the pre-European battle of Kokowai. <i>(May 30, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  So far as our information goes, we are led to the conclusion that the scene of the recent encounter, the Orakau pa, is a part of the new fortifications to which previous allusion has been made. <i>(Apr 4, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  PA AT ROTORUA TAKEN - EIGHT HAU HAUS KILLED - MILITARY SETTLER FIRED AT. <i>(Apr 8, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a Maori village or settlement [aka: kaik, kainga]

>  Mr. Luxford then hastened back to the pa to hurry the Maories [sic] for assitance. <i>(Aug 31, 1850,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,107</i>)

>  In this manner he was conveyed through the town back to the pa settlement, his attendants evidently impressed with the grave nature of the whole proceedings. <i>(May 28, 1885,Waikato Times,3</i>)

>  On Thursday evening, a motor car, which was to bring the Ministerial party from Papawai came towards the gate of the pa in a cloud of dust. <i>(Apr 12, 1911,Poverty Bay Herald,8</i>)

>  <i>pa n</i>. (occas. <i>pah.</i>) Maori settlement, village (<i>Hukanui Pa</i>), or marae (<i>Maketu Pa</i>), in 19 c. fortified (Gate Pa). [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,537</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 346]]

>  <b>Finlayson</b> pa: originally a terraced and fortified village on a hill-top; later, loosely, any Maori village. To get away from the pa, to make an effort to assimilate into pakeha society. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson's Glossary)]

>  <b>MONEY</b> A few Maories were in the habit of going over every year ... to see their friends at a pah or village on the Grey river, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,30</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... we saw the remains of an old pah or fortress ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,4</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Maoris who live in inland pahs usually make periodical trips to the coast for fishing purposes. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,62,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> I use the habitual expression Maori pah, but a description of this settlement would give a very poor idea of such a Maori village, consisting as it did of three miserable low huts, in which a very old couple ... were living. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,98</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `Moawhango, the postal name of the pah, as it is usually termed, lies in a lovely valley surrounded by hills. ... Besides the Post Office store and accommodation house referred to, there are a blacksmith's and a boot maker's shop, several well built houses occupied by Maoris and a large number of whares.' <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,46</i>) [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 46]

>  I went to the pa early and saw Wi lying in state, the greenstone mere at his head and many greenstone ornaments. His head had huia feathers on either side, flowers on his chest. <i>(Oct 30, 1897,Chudleigh Diary,396,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>The former [pa] is now considered the more correct spelling. A Maori word to signify a native settlement, surrounded by a stockade; a</i> fort; a fighting village. In Maori, the verb pameans, to touch, to block up. <i>Pa </i>= a collection of houses to which access is blocked by means of stockades and ditches. 1769. `Captain Cook's Journal' (edition Wharton, 1893), p. 147: [and other quotations, incl. a long are from Thompson 1859]. <i>(1898,Austral English,335</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Most Maoris still slept on the floor ... or on a mattress of sweet freshly-cut ti-tree branches, when they were in the guest-house of a big <i>pa</i>. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,133</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Everything was quiet in the pa by one o'clock in the morning. <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/101</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i>pa</i>, fortified or stockaded place; used as a place of retreat if danger threatened the ordinary dwelling-place, the kainga; by Cook and by others much later spelt pah to get the proper sound of Maori `a'; pah, pa, in O.E.D. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145</i>)

>  <i>pa</i> has not for many years been spelt pah in Newzealand: pah was Captain Cook's spelling, and was useful as indicating the long vowel, but is not correct Maori, and is not used in writing. [JCA no long v] <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/148</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> <i>pa</i> formerly a fortified Maori place, now commonly applied ot any Maori village <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,xxii</i>)

>  <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> A Pah, called in old books Trippah, is a fortified village, but as all the villages <i>are</i> fortified, <i>we</i> call them Pahs, and so do the Mauri. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,18/23</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> The inhabitants of the Pah or native village would not ferry us across the stream ... <i>(1843,Twelve Months in Wellington,12</i>)

>  <b>MONRO</b> In the bay in which we landed, we found two or three miserable primitive Maori cabins, inhabited by half-a-dozen helpless old creatures and a few diseased children - forming a pa named Rapaki. Leaving this pa, we passed through a very pretty bush, which runs up nearly to the top of the range, ... <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 235]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> On each side of the town [Wellington] is a native `pah', or village: ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/232</i>)

>  Two Maoris from the pa have also learned to make cheese. <i>(1928,Grace Pioneer Missionary,20</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 31 T.S.Grace Report of Turanga District in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 20]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Here we found a newly built pa named Antioch with about a doz. well built houses chiefly wharepuni, the old village seated amongst the boiling springs still remaining. <i>(Feb 5, 1853,Journal,8/74</i>)

>  ... the portion of the laid-out road which passes through the Maori pah is being drained by native labour. <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> We soon reached the Maori pah, where we were received ... with every sign of welcome. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,29</i>)

>  <b>MOSER</b> Two very natural questions arise from the heading of this article, the first being what is a pah? And the second why do I style it <i>pah? </i>... A pah is strictly a <i>fortified village,</i> but it has ceased to be applied to a fortified one only, and a collection of huts forming a native settlement, is generally termed a pah now a days, and the pah I refer to as `Our pah' has no gns of fortifications about it. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,14</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> ... she told me that when you go to a strange <i>pa</i>, and have your first meal on the <i>marae</i>, you must always drop a bit of the food on the ground ... <i>pa</i>: fortified native village. <i>Ibid.</i> 103 One hand came up in answer to my wave, and then she turned about and strode away on her two-mile walk to the little <i>pa</i>. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,60</i>)

>  <b>ROSIER-JONES</b> Emma picks it up. It's not heavy, and they walk across the road to the pa. <i>Ibid.</i> 89 `Everyone seems to be moving away from the pa. ... At least we're still within cooee of the pa. ...' <i>(1985,Cast Two Shadows,44</i>)

>  The bus disgorged at the pa and Danny trudged the dusty road to Nana's, gathering in his younger brothers and sisters to shepherd them home. <i>(Dec 1986,Landfall,160/491</i>) [NOTES: (Smither)]

>  <i>pah</i> var. of PA. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,539</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>ADAMS</b> To the darkness where they wept / From the <i>pa</i> an old chief stepped, / [fn] Village. <i>(1913,Collected Verses,25</i>)

>  ... the clustering huts of the old-time Maori Pah. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,60</i>)

>  `Alexa' contributes a note on the modern Maori Kuri. ... the Maori tykes that roam the wilds of Ohinemutu ... For six months, while `batching' near the pah I made a study of these ... animals. <i>(Apr 10, 1920,Quick March,27</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> <i>(1927,Journal,4</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> `Alexa' contributes a note on the modern Maori Kuri. ... the Maori tykes that roam the wilds of Ohinemutu ... For six months, while `batching' near the pah I made a study of these ... animals. <i>(1938,Brown Man's Burden,9, 52</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/224</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) pah: generally `pa', a Maori settlement or village, formerly fortified. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Absolutely free from care and full of childish frolic, the children of the pa ... played around, adding their shrill cries to the varied sounds of the prowling animals. <i>(1951,Confessions,34</i>)

>  Similar nucleated settlements, commonly if erroneously known to the paheka as <i>pa</i>, occurred in the vicinity of Whakatane, Opotoki, Rotorua and Gisborne. <i>(Oct 1952,NZ Geographer,109</i>) [NOTES: (Metge)]

>  Flowers, flowers at the <i>pa</i>, frothing about Hinewake. ... Flowers lapping further and further out upon the <i>marae </i>as the visitors arrive; adding and adding. <i>(Aug 1953,Here & Now,21</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> I mean it's common knowledge what happens at the pa when there's a big hui. <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,20</i>) [NOTES: (The Woman's Story)]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... is the cluster of tin shacks known locally as `the pa'. <i>Ibid.</i> 12 One night at the pah they saw the long poi done with lights. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,9</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> <i>pa</i>-Loosely, a Maori village. Properly speaking, a pa was a fortified village; a kaianga was an everyday settlement. In time of war, the populace moved from the kainga to the shelter of the pa. When Tamu spoke of the newcomers in Chapter 14 as `Real pa Maori, eh?' his meaning was, `These are not civilised Maoris; they're real bush Maoris.' <i>(1963,In the Sticks,181</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Unlike most of the Maoris at the pa she looked as if she came from the Arawa tribe, because she was olive-skinned and oval of face. <i>(1965,Outcasts,20</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> originally, a native settlement surrounded by a stockade a fort, now an unfortified one [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>pa, pah ... n</i>. orig. native settlement surrounded by a stockade, a fort, now an unfortified one. (<i>Pah</i> is now obs.) [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... the lights are still on/ In the houses in the pa,... <i>(1979,Collected Poems,455,1969 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (1969) 1979 Baxter Collected Poems 455]

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `Where are you heading for?' `Home, out at the pa.' That was four miles. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,151</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> [a Maori speaks] Not too many Maori live in the pa these days boy. You pakeha saw to that. Do you know the difference between pa and marae? <i>(1974,Maori Woman,167</i>)

>  ... to appoint from among the Maoris of any Maori kainga, village, or pa a Committee. . . who shall be called the Village Committee (Komiti Marae) ... <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents,170</i>) [NOTES: (1900) 1971 Speeches and Documents 170 [quoting N.Z. statues 1900, 64 Vict., No.48, pp. 252-60]]

>  Maori Social Welfare and Advancements Act 1945 repeats the above Act and repeats in Section 21 ... Maori Villages: A Tribal Committee may ... define the boundaries of any kainga, village, or pa. <i>(1971,Speeches & Documents,417</i>) [NOTES: (1900) 1971 Speeches and Documents 417 ibid. 417]

>  <b>IRVINE & ALPERS</b> A few expressive terms have been adopted into daily speech from Maori - <i>whare</i> (a hut), <i>pa</i> (a village) and <i>mana</i> (prestige) are examples; ... <i>(1902,Progress of NZ in the 20th Century,429</i>)

>  <b>IRVINE & ALPERS</b> A few expressive terms have been adopted into daily speech from Maori - <i>whare</i> (a hut), <i>pa</i> (a village) and <i>mana</i> (prestige) are examples; ... <i>(1902,Progress of NZ in the 20th Century,429</i>)

>  ... the remains laid in state in the runanga house at Tairooa Pah. <i>(Aug 19, 1905,NZ Truth,9/5/6</i>)

>  There aren't many Mabel Tainuis' knocking about the Maori pahs. <i>(Oct 21, 1905,NZ Truth,18/5/2</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> We drove on through sheep country - to Pohui that night - past Maori `pahs' and nothing else ... . <i>Ibid.</i> 40 - we lunch past the Maori pah and get right into the bush - <i>(Nov 20, 1907,Letters,1/30,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> pa <i>Maori village (originally fortified) </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  <b>EDMOND</b> Although it was after eleven o'clock, Tom insisted on calling at the pa on the way home. ... Louise had several times passed the scattering of houses and sheds that clustered round the meeting house and the open space in front of it. <i>(1984,High Country Weather,28</i>)

>  <b>CORBALLIS & GARRETT</b> <i>Further changes were made before the</i> first `Tangi' (in <i>Contemporary Maori Writing</i>) became the definitive version (in <i>Pounamu, Pounamu</i>). ... The characters `rub' noses in the earlier version; they `press' them in the later one. ... [24] Finally, in three passages (pp. 126, 130) - but not in a fourth (p. 128) - the `pa' becomes a `meeting house'. ... (`Pa' tends to have pejorative associations in Pakeha parlance, ...) <i>(1984,Witi Ihimaera,23</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> Ere yet the ruthless Pakeha/ Appeared with desolating axe,/ And tangled brake, where chance some pah,/ Was hidden there, 'mid scrub and flax - <i>(1912,Castle Gay,56</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> On the north side of the harbour ... there is a small `paa' or village. The `warries' ... are generally low, built of mud or reeds, and sometimes thatched with `t&diero.i-t&diero.i, a kind of grass or small flag which grows to the height of three or four feet ... <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,49</i>)

### PA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Probationary Assistant

### pa Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> one who has not been integrated into Pakeha society

>  <b>HYDE</b> `I'm not just a <i>pa</i> Maori, I've been about. Once I went to Sydney and they took me on in a <i>cafe[e-acute], ... But what's it like up at the pa</i>? I'll tell you: it's like being shut up with a lot of big kids. They say; `That Opal, she's not a Maori now, she's got a hat and thinks she's a <i>pakeha'</i>.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,169</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> Even young Tamu ... sidled up to me and whispered, `They [sc new Maori schoolchildren] real Maori, eh, sir? Real pa Maori, eh?' ... <i>(1963,In the Sticks,109</i>)

### Pa Wars
 
 <i>n.</i> an annual one-day inter-marae Maori sports festival organised by Te Runanga o Ngati Porou

>  In the early days of each New Year, one of the most delightful festivals of New Zealand life takes place in the East Coast of the North Island. Known as Pa Wars, it is a Ngati Porou celebration of whanau and summer and kids and sport. ... The Pa Wars, celebrating their 10th anniversary this year, do get media coverage... <i>(Jan 16, 2006,NZ Herald,A11</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Tongan monetary unit [ORIGIN: Tongan]

### Paaite
 
 <i>n.</i> persons living on the marae

The Paaites idiosyncacies are evoked with tolerance, humour and warmth. <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

### Pacific bench
 
 <i>n.</i> an apparatus for moving logs in a sawmill

>  PACIFIC BENCH A wheeled travelling saw bench on which logs are help while it moves past a circular or band saw. It is fitted with knees which permit accurate sawing to thickness or width, and thus can be used for recutting as well as breaking down. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### Pacific cricket
 
 <i>n.</i> an attempted commercial naming (See [kirikiti](kee-kiw.md#kirikiti))

>  Corned beef and a frenetic game involving up to 200 a side, both curious legacies of the colonisation of the South Seas, are about to make the big time together. The game is krikiti, kirikiti or kilikiti which arrived in the Pacific well over a century ago in the more sedate form of English cricket. Now a couple of Aucklanders have taken the game that's more a social event than a sport, given it some formal rules, and come up with [dollars]100,000 to remould it under the new name of `Pacific Cricket'. <i>(Jan 10, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,14</i>)

### Pacific decadal oscillation
 
 <i>phr.</i> a weather pattern

>  Weather variations would continue, but the new phase in the cycle, known as Pacific decadal oscillation, would result in a switch to a prominent easterly pattern and more severe weather, he said. <i>(Sep 22, 2005,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

### Pacific gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a particularly timid gecko, widespread in the North Island especially from Taupo north and west. [<i>Hoplodactylus pacificus</i>]

>  Among the 14 or so species of reptile on Little Barrier are Duvaucel's gecko (at up to 25 cm, including the tail, one of the largest geckos in the world) the very rare chevron skink (found only on Little and Great Barrier islands), and the Pacific gecko, seen here licking nectar from a pohutukawa blossom. <i>(2002,NZ Geographic,55/13</i>)

>  Among the 14 or so species of reptile on Little Barrier are Duvaucel's gecko (at up to 25 cm, including the tail, one of the largest geckos in the world) the very rare chevron skink (found only on Little and Great Barrier islands), and the Pacific gecko, seen here licking nectar from a pohutukawa blossom. <i>(2002,NZ Geographic,55/13</i>)

>  Regurgitated fish in a gannet colony on the Poor Knights Islands make a ready meal for a number of shore skinks and a local variant of the Pacific gecko. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 47/70</i>)

>  The Pacific gecko is nocturnal and feeds on vegetation or on insects, grubs and berries. By day they hide in cracks in clay banks, under stones or in cracks in tree trunks. Sometimes they are seen basking in the sun. Their colour and patterning are variable and they bear their young in the late summer. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.puketi.org.nz/kids/riverwalk.html]

### Pacific Islander
 
 <i>n.</i> a person from the Pacific Islands, especially those with strong New Zealand constitutional connections; early South Sea Islander now (in NZ) ISLANDER 2 q.v.

>  They tattoo the body, and are more addicted to dress than the Pacific islanders generally. <i>(Jan 31, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Prohibition of Arms and Spirits for Pacific Islanders. <i>(Oct 17, 1888,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  With his large canoes (and the Pacific islanders appear to have had little fleets of them in those days) he had traversed these seas far and wide, and in his last cruise previous to Turi's leaving Hawaiki, he had discovered New Zealand, and returned with the news of his discovery to his native home. <i>(Jun 21, 1851,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,72</i>)

>  It is shown by the Government Statistician's records that the total [European] population now is 1,174,139, compared with 1,095,994 in 1914. Including Maoris and Pacific Islanders it is 1,236,712. <i>(May 26, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

[The Pacific Island Education Foundation] says grants can only go to pupils of Samoan, Cook Island, Niuean, Tongan or Tokelauan descent. <i>(Sep 16, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Shore had claimed two Pacific Islanders had abused him. <i>(Nov 25, 1988,Dominion,10</i>)

>  A Pacific Islander's Protection Bill has been introduced into Parliament. <i>(Mar 22, 1872,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  .. the Australian aboriginal, the red Indian, the Pacific Islander - all are fading away before his [the Anglo Saxon's] onward march ... <i>(Oct 12, 1893,Otago Witness,49</i>)

>  Tera is a Pacific Islander, entrusted for the time being to the care of a quondam missionary, who gives convincing testimony to his belief in the equality of the races by falling in love with her. <i>(Sep 8, 1900,Evening Post,2</i>)

### Pacific mackerel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Blue mackerel Scomber autralasicus</i> ... An Indo-Pacific species ... Often simply called mackerel, from its close similarity to the common mackerel (<i>Scomber scomber</i>) of the Northern Hemisphere ... In New Zealand it has often been called English mackerel and Pacific mackerel, but it is becoming more widely and appropriately known now as blue mackerel. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,124</i>)

>  [species name] blue mackerel .. [scientific name] <i>Scomber australasicus</i> .. [other name(s)] English mackerel, common mackerel, frigate mackerel, pacific mackerel, southern mackerel <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### Pacific oyster
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Before the early seventies, the tiny native New Zealand rock oyster (<i>saccostrea glomerata</i>) clung to the rocks around the coastal shoreline and commercial farming of the species was not highly profitable... The Pacific oyster which has overtaken the rock oyster and is now widely known as 'rock oyster' tends to be much larger, milder and is often quite creamy in texture. <i>(Mar 1998,Cuisine,88</i>)

>  The Pacific oyster is not a native to New Zealand but was probably accidentally introduced. <i>(1985,Metro,47/28</i>)

>  [species name] oysters pacific .. [scientific name] <i>Crassostrea gigas</i> .. [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Pacific oyster Crassostrea gigas</i> ... A native of Japanese waters, this oyster is now widely distributed through the Pacific ... as a result of deliberate or accidental importations. ... Firstnoticed at mahurangi in the Hauraki Gulf in 1970 - though undoubtedly present for many years before that - ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,165</i>)

>  I'd go to the hotel and get myself a beer., stand there, and they'd all come over and buy the oysters off me. Pacific oysters, from a farm. Never the Bluff - the Bluff were too dear. <i>(Jun 17, 2012,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),36</i>)

>  The [Bluff] oysters has a silvery-grey sheen, were round, plump, meaty, full of flavour, and with less of an iodine tang than the Pacific oysters I am familiar with from the Freycinet Peninsula in Tasmania and from the Eyre Peninsula in South Australia. <i>(Nov 2001,Cuisine,127</i>)

### Pacific rat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Pacific rat or kiore <i>Rattus exulans</i> has become the subject of some controversy in New Zealand. Widespread across the Pacific, it followed human settlement from South East Asia, to Hawaii to Easter Island, to New Zealand. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 306/11</i>)

>  <i>Mus exulans</i>. (Pacific Rat.) Specimens of the Pacific rat examined on Sunday Island agree in every particular with those from Funafuti. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/536</i>)

>  THE KIORE, or Pacific rat, arrived in canoes with early Maori. It was regarded as a delicacy, skinned and roasted over a fire, or more elaborately baked while bound in a clay ball among the ashes, raked out and cracked open. <i>(Sep 2010,NZ Geographic,24</i>)

### Pacific Rim
 
 <i>n.</i> the countries bordering on the Pacific Ocean

>  Overlaying these classical mainstays are a medley of Asian and indigenous flavours and aromas which give Piko Piko its distinctive Pacific Rim style kawakawa tea, swamp cabbage, horopito spice; Pacific salsas and Asian pestos. <i>(Dec 1996,North & South,124</i>)

>  There was a symposium for indigenous Pacific Rim artists at Rotorua that year, for working, not talking. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,18</i>)

>  Think Pacific Rim, for goodness sake. Asia. Taiwan, Thailand. But oops, not the Sultan of Brunei. <i>(Feb 13, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  When I asked Stephen if there was much common ground among his chosen bunch of cooks, he didn't come up with a snap answer but gardually worked round to the idea that they are " ... losing a Pacific Rim identity in favour of becoming Aotearoa ... chilling out ... taking note of personal food memories ... becoming more comfortable ... settling down ..." <i>(Apr 20, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  But too right, he's influenced by the Pacific Rim, or fusion, movement as practised by New Zealand chef Peter Gordon, who lives around the corner in Hampstead. <i>(May 13, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  Pacific rim gardening, as you call it, can't be avoided... Most of these young designers are taking their references from the New Zealand environment and its Polynesian character. <i>(Mar 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  IBM will take over the bank's mainframe, mid-range computing and desktop services in New Zealand, Australia and the Pacific Rim. <i>(Oct 30, 2000,Dominion Infotech Weekly,No. 465 : 1</i>)

>  Polynesia, the whole Pacific Rim, is the way of the future for the major [fashion] collections in Europe. <i>(Apr 29, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

### Pacific Rose
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of dessert apple

>  No sooner had the Apple and Pear Marketing Board's marketing arm ENZA made its late April announcement that all sciros trees bearing Pacific Rose apples growing illegally in Chile had been destroyed.. than the battle for our wonder apple took a turn for the worse. <i>(Jan 2000,North & South,31</i>)

>  In recent years, we've seen the Pacific series of apples. Pacific Rose was the first, then came Pacific Beauty and then Pacific Queen... As its name suggests, Pacific Rose has a unique rosy pink skin colour, and was developed for its aesthetics as much as for its thin skin, sweet flavour and crunchy flesh. <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

>  "As long as New Zealand continues to develop apples with eating quality like Pacific Rose, apples will continue to attract fruit lovers," its SONZAF report said. <i>(Feb 14, 2003,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### Pacific Slope
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... Pacificans ... [B.Kernot] <i>(Mar 31, 1985,Report of the Human Rights Commissioner,41</i>)

### pack
 
 <i>n.</i> a backpack (and its contents); (tends to replace earlier swag, but usually refers to a shop-bought pack: cf <i>Bergan</i>) (See [flat pack](fin-fly.md#flat-pack))

>  Food and gear have to be carried. Everyone in New Zealand..puts it in a pack. <i>(1958,Tararua,12: 27</i>)

>  You women can't go carrying all that stuff. Here, Joyce, give us your pack, Joyce! <i>(Mar 15, 1968,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6/1</i>)

>  Hobnail boots and canvas pack..just the gear for pushing through scrub and supplejack. <i>(Feb 21, 1969,Listener (New Zealand Listener),4/1</i>)

>  Pack carrying is still the same old personal battle between man andgravity. <i>(Feb 22, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5/2</i>)

>  <b>ORBELL</b> The pack which will carry clothes comfortably will not be so good if you fill it up with tins nor will it pack out meat or skins and still be fit to carry clothes in. <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,16</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> There is controversy over packs too, more controversy, I believe, than over any other piece of equipment. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,27</i>)

>  Food and gear have to be carried. Everyone in New Zealand, almost everyone anyway, puts it in a <i>pack</i>, but probably no one stops to think that the word <i>pack</i> in this sense is not standard English. ... England since 1895 has borrowed <i>rucksack</i> from German dialect; New Zealand has preferred <i>pack</i>. Not that rucsac is not used here; it is noticeable that older Club members used it in several articles in early numbers of <i>Tararua</i>, and it still appears in advertisements. <i>Rucksack</i> seems a bit foreign, as is shown by the less correct but more foreign-looking spelling it is usually given. <i>Pack</i> as a synonym for <i>rucksack</i> is used in Australia to some extent at any rate. This sense of <i>pack</i> would most likely be in use long before it occurred in print. I remember having a home-made <i>pack</i> in Auckland in 1933. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,27</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  With the following dawn two tigers stood by for a quick trip back to Maruia Springs while each pack was [40] searched and emptied. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,39</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... special clothing, including a short oilskin or<i> parka</i> ... and especially a <i>pack</i>. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,161</i>)

>  <i>(1967,Landfall,21/144</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> It is 3,011 feet above sea level, and about a two and a-half [sic] hour journey with a light pack, in from where the track leading to it joins the Inland Patea road near Kuripapango. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,28</i>)

>  <b>SHADB</b> I hadn't had time to get my pack off my back, let alone search out Grandmother Flinders. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,90</i>)

>  Before long he was fair-dinkum bushed. ... So he got on a blaze and humped his pack up towards the cage. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  She moved the packs behind a rocky outcrop jutting into the cave's centre; ... and not at the back, where the ghostly cave wetas waved anaemic limbs. <i>(Dec 28, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B1</i>)

>  <i>pack ... 13. Austral. & N.Z</i>. another name for <i>rucksack. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,810</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 538; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 346]]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> ... the guards were very tolerant and only occasionally `went crook' when the van was cluttered with sleeping bags, packs and, sometimes, roaring primuses. <i>(1987,Rail,128</i>)

>  ... having cached our packs. <i>(Aug 1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,16</i>) [NOTES: (1933 Dec 30) 1934 Aug Canterbury Mountaineer]

>  ... leaving behind the ridge-rope to lighten our packs. <i>(1934,NZ Alpine Journal,6/22/8 [et. pass.]</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> With a pack it will probably take at least four hours ... <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,85</i>)

>  Heard that some white man who was travelling through the Taupo country, with a pack on his back, had, in crossing on a tree over a small stream, fallen and was killed. <i>(Jul 11, 1846,New Zealander, The,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to load a pack-horse

>  <b>HARPER</b> Meanwhile there I was with the responsible duty of packing those two horses with the baggage, ... <i>(Dec 25, 1856,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,9,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> After having packed the horse, he gave it in charge of a native boy. <i>(1889,Story of a Wonderful Dog, The,19</i>)

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> Parks and wife left with 6 old bullocks for the bush, and also packed `Dan' with piece of wire and camp over [sic, oven], etc. <i>(Jan 29, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Jan 29 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 125]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Two of us used to pack a horse with flour and meat ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,58</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to put (or press) wool into packs (? or bales)

>  `packing wool' [check the context]. <i>(1851,Canterbury Papers,170</i>)

>  I have been packing [wool in the press] all day as usual. <i>(Dec 1, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,68,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [wool]

>  Washing, Shearing, and Packing... These [sc. fleeces] should be pulled off and thrown aside, to be packed in separate bags... With regard to packing... it is necessary to have strong bags, which may be made of New Zealand flax. <i>(1850,Canterbury Papers,Nos. 5 & 6 : 170</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a light pack; a light bag, often with a stiffened frame, for carrying on the back; a rucksack.

>  <b>FRASER & GIBBONs</b> <i>PACK</i>: The infantry knapsack. <i>(1925,Soldier and Sailor Words,21</i>)

>  <b>COMO</b> His luggage [on his visit to Malvern] was contained in a simple, much-stained khaki rucksack that looked old enough to have been in his possession when he was a boy before World War 1. He always called it `the pack'; later in the visit it would be used to hold the food, raincoats, and everything else that we took with us on ... walks. <i>Ibid.</i> 204 Our routine was always the same. One of us would shoulder the pack and we would walk for half an hour. <i>(1979,C.S. Lewis at the Breakfast Table,203</i>) [NOTES: (G. Sayer: `Jack on Holiday': 1950s) The quot. possibly represents C.S.L.'s personal usage influenced by soldiers' `pack', WWI.]



 
 <i>n.</i> a wool bale

>  The committee came to the conclusion that wool linings for packs furnished a real rememdy, provided wool-growers could find an easy and convenient method of manipulating these linings in the process of packing. <i>(Jun 26, 1909,Tuapeka Times,4</i>)

>  Important tests are in progress of closely woven packs entirely paper lined. <i>(Dec 9, 1911,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The skirtings of the neck and shoulder can be called first pieces, and the britch ends made into second pieces. The `bellies' can be placed in a pack hung on the shearing-board, and when two of these are filled they can be pressed. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,121</i>)

>  Today the 42in. pack is the standard size for New Zealand. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,313</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> <i>Pack</i> see bale <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a pack-horse.

>  <b>CHEVALIER</b> The pack a strong heavy old chap, the third pretty good. The fourth a flea bitten Arab mare. <i>(1866,Reminiscences of NZ,7</i>) [NOTES: [check quot]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a musterer's or rabbiter's team of dogs

>  Early in the year I noticed that my rabbiter's pack of dogs were looking miserably-poor ... mangy skeletons. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/430</i>)

>  `Dick', Hepburn's dog nearly worried by our pack. planted spuds. <i>(Dec 2, 1858,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  `Dick', Hepburn's dog nearly worried by our pack. planted spuds. <i>(1888,TrNZI,21/430</i>)

>  <i>The rabbit dogs on a station are spoken of as the rabbit p</i>. Shepherds speak of their dogs as a team. <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> I often hear [musterers] say they're selling up the pack and turning in the mustering; and they often get as far as selling a few dogs.... <i>(1951,High Country,46</i>)

>  Never saw such a great pack in all the King Country, as old Charlie's. See him coming in from the back country at mustering-time with a mob ... and his dogs handling each woolly as though its fleece was worth its weight in gold ... <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,126</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton `Saving the Breed')]

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Each [musterer] has a `pack' of about five dogs ... <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,35</i>)

>  Immediately a broken down mongrel from a rabbiter's pack sneaked over the skyline. `Not a damn thing like that!' echoed the following scream far inland. `Send one like Old Toss!' <i>(Oct 28, 1984,NZ Times,13</i>) [NOTES: (J. Henderson)]

>  <b>CARR</b> In the North Island a pack of dogs and a gun are the rabbiter's stock-in-trade ... <i>(1913,Country Work,27</i>)

>  [Photo caption] A RABBITER AND HIS PACK OF DOGS, FAIRLIE, S.CANTERBURY. A RABBITER AT WORK: THE EXPECTANT PACK WAITING FOR THE RABBITS. <i>(Sep 2, 1908,Otago Witness,46</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> Then new gumfields were opened farther north ..., and a diggers' rush gave my brothers intermittent employment, they taking it in turns to pack the diggers to their new quarters. <i>(1956,Dim Horizons,58,1955 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>Tinne</b> ... supplies of oats - which are all `packed' from the coast, frequently run short ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,15</i>)

>  Left Goashore at 6 a.m. and commenced arranging our packs. ... Found that Jim had only packed up 96lbs biscuit ... Sugar 20lbs. <i>(Mar 1, 1849,Torlesse Papers,63,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WALKER</b> He talks about packing stores over there and wintering it [Dunstain diggings] <i>(Mar 4, 1863,Journal</i>)

>  the latter packed some beef on Harry [a fine pack-horse]. <i>(Jul 27, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>haa</b> was now obliged to leave my dog-cart ... and continued my journey next day, packing my provisions and camp utensils. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,110</i>)

>  I used to pack the tucker to the `Perseverance Push', ... <i>(1985,Penguin Book of NZ Verse,93</i>) [NOTES: c1890-1900 Edwin Edwards `Waitekauri Every Time' in 1985 Penguin Book of NZ Verse 93]

>  <b>STEWART</b> ... as the condition of the roads made wagon transport impossible, the unfortunate animals ... `packed' it [ammunition] up through the mud, making more than one trip a day. <i>(1921,NZ Division,122</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... it'd take me about three hours to pack it all the way up to the mine; I'd have to do that trip about two to three times a week. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [mustering or in early station years.] to load and conduct packhorses with mustering provisions or farm materials.

>  Humes and McDonald started with 'Harry', packed across the Maerewenhua pass. <i>(Jan 13, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  Packed Harry and started with McDonald to split rafters at Houndburn. <i>(Feb 11, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  C.H.S. and Kite packed a load of wool to Petane. <i>(Jan 7, 1878,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Jack packing posts on to Middle Ridge. <i>(Sep 3, 1913,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> gear rolled or packed for carrying on the back [cf<i> swag </i>?early transitional usage =the load, not the holder for which rucksack or swagbag has been used]

>  Left Goashore at 6 a.m. and commenced arranging our packs. <i>Ibid </i>63 Found that Jim had only packed up 96lbs biscuit ... Sugar 20lbs. <i>(Mar 1, 1849,Torlesse Papers,63,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> ... so we left them behind, shouldering our own packs, and pushed on at once. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,171</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> Disregarding the state of the roads [on 24 August 1852] we strapped on our `swags' (colonial word for pack). <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,49</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> We now commenced our journey in earnest, and shouldered, or rather `backed' our packs, which, with the exception of one, weighed over 40 lbs., and principally consisted of biscuits, bacon, sugar, chocolate, <i>(Jan 4, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: [?rucksack] 1858 Jan 4 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 351]

>  <b>JOSEPH MASTERS</b> ... my pack [tent, blankets, and a few tools in 1854] being covered with a painted cloth which was water proof I continued my journey ... <i>(1871,Autobiography</i>)

>  <b>GREEN</b> ... as Southerland offered to take a pack for us as far as his horse could go, we quickly bundled together a number of articles in the waterproof sheet and started him off. <i>Ibid.</i> 180 The amount to be carried on our shoulders when put together appeared somewhat alarming ... Kauffmann gave a significant glance now and then, as he contemplated the packs, but neither he nor Boss ever grumbled, though they knew full well that the heaviest share of the `swagging' must fall to their lot. <i>Ibid.</i> 182 Some years before, when in Norway, I remember having carried a pack of twenty-two pounds across the Justedal Glaciers, and found it quite a sufficient load. <i>Ibid.</i> 183 As soon as breakfast was over B. ... started off with his knapsack for the Hooker ... Then we helped the packs onto each other's shoulders ...<i>Ibid.</i> 185 ]I then dragged my pack through the thorns ... to ease my shoulders I took off my knapsack ... <i>(1883,High Alps of NZ,178</i>)

>  <b>LAUPER</b> ... he said he could easily swim across, then I could fasten the packs to the rope for him to drag over ... <i>(1863,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Lauper in 1960 Pascoe (ed) Over the Whitcombe Pass 44]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> After a reproduction of last night's nameless meal, we rolled our packs roughly together, crossed the river below us on horse-back, ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,187</i>)

>  A bundle of things done up ready for carrying as `The hiker had his pack slung over his shoulders'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),287</i>)

>  <b>CROZIER</b> The descent on the other side began down steep, grassy, and scrub-covered slopes. ... We put off our packs and scouted round for a good spot to spend the night. When we went to pick up the swags again, we couldn't locate them anywhere. <i>(1950,Beyond the Southern Lakes,64</i>) [NOTES: 1905-1906 Grave in 1950 Crozier Beyond the Southern Lakes 64]



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>ORBELL</b> In general a frame pack is the most comfortable to pack with provided it fits your body shape. <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,16</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  Although the market is still bare of good stock we have to note a considerable advance in the price of rough heavy hacks for packing to the new diggings. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,4</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> (supplies etc) earn one's living by packing

>  I knocked off packing up there. <i>(1864,Digger's Notes,54</i>)

>  <b>BROOKES</b> [The packers] had a rough country to travel in with nothing but a rough exploring line to pack upon. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,151</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> Another story about Ted Morrison concerned a time when he was packing for musterers on the Raglan. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,17</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>CRADDOCK</b> It is not too big to `pack' over rough ground. <i>(1904,Sport in NZ,41</i>)

### pack a sad
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be in a bad or depressed mood

>  Then Jen would pack a sad with her mother for growling her [sic] and she would stay away for a couple of weeks. <i>(Jun 1, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  Because of a row with his girlfriend, a young man who had been drinking and was disqualified `packed a sad' and drove, Mr Justice Roper was told in the High Court yesterday ... . <i>(May 22, 1985,Press, The,4</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pack a sad</i> to be depressed; from builders' term for a warp; eg `Having dropped the ball with the line and the winning of the game in sight, Mick proved the night's party pooper, packing a sad all evening.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,81</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> as he moved to the porch and found her packing a sad / <i>how blokes kept their distance - did he fancy a bit? </i> <i>(1982,Rose Ballroom, The,28</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>pack a sad:</i> to GET ostentatiously DEPRESSED <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,74</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> (of a vehicle or machine) to break down

>  I was interested to learn that Trans Rail's inter-island ferry Aratere had once again "packed a sad." <i>(Dec 24, 2000,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  Meg's computer's packed a sad and is away being fixed. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Angela (Secretary, English)]

### pack and cart horse
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a horse used for many purposes; an equine rouseabout.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Then there was old, fat Boss, the half-draught pack and cart horse who liked a drink of cocoa out of a pint mug. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,77</i>)

### pack box
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The `droppers' were first sling into place, swags, tents, or pack boxes, which were known as `side-loads', were hung above them, and the `top load' was then strapped in place. <i>(1949,High Country Days,39</i>)

### pack boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a person responsible for carrying provisions on a packhorse

>  The bridge over this stream, after standing in mid air for some months, is now being rapidly connected with <i>terra firma</i> and will no doubt effect a great saving in pack boys and other articles which have annually been washed away during the freshet season. <i>(Feb 23, 1887,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> When a gang goes out to muster the merinos ... the men work as a team. The `pack boy', whose age may be seventeen or seventy, carries all the provisions and other gear ... on his team of packhorses. He is the cook and odd job man ... <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,35</i>)

>  The road at present is, from a Taranaki packboy's point of view, fairly good... <i>(Feb 17, 1892,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### pack bullock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bullock used to pack supplies or farm produce [particularly in wet areas too difficult for horses.] [Also pack-bullock]

>  It was then discovered that Mackenzie had a pack bullock with him, which must have been "planted" during his visit to the Levels Station. <i>(Jan 3, 1871,Wellington Independent,5</i>)

>  The storekeeper who supplies the road party sent a pack bullock from Collingwood with a load of provisions, and at the end of two days' journey the animal fell over a cliff and broke his neck; then rain set in, and a flood prevented the two pack-horses from returning. <i>(Oct 13, 1893,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  <b>Hodder</b> Two pack-bullocks loaded with moa bones <i>(1862,184</i>)

>  Mackenzie had a pack bullock with him, and was prepared for a long journey. <i>(Apr 14, 1855,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Apr 14 Otago Witness in 1937 Ayson Pioneering in Otago 50]

>  <b>Collinson</b> The country is too difficult for pack-bullocks. <i>(1853,Remarks on Military Operations,31</i>)

>  <b>BARRER</b> At Mangaroa two pack bullocks were awaiting them. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,181</i>)

>  <b>BARRER</b> At Featherston a pack bullock was secured, and on it were placed two big hampers, in which the children were carried. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,197</i>)

>  A road for pack bullocks is about to be made, that will traverse the Takapu, Pawaitanginui Porirua, and Horaki districts, besides opening a communication with the coast road to Manawatu, &c. <i>(Jan 7, 1843,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,175</i>)

>  The Karori pack bullock road is completed, as well as that to Porirua. <i>(May 6, 1843,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

### pack cattle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  PACK CATTLE. - We are glad to hear that a Settler in the Hutt has been training a bull to carry himself and luggage. <i>(Jan 6, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

### pack down
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to pack provisions or belongings on to a pack-horse.

>  <b>STUART</b> James and Jack packing down their belongings. Jack going to Napier with letter to M.R & Co <i>(1889,Tutira Station Diary,24</i>)

### pack dray
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] a dray used to carry musterers' provisions to a hut, camp or outstation.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It was all walking country and no hacks were taken there, the musterers travelling either per 'Harry Pannel' (walking) or riding on the pack dray. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,35</i>)

### pack in
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to convey provisions, fenceposts etc by packhorse to isolated homesteads.

>  Packed in posts and firewood. <i>(Apr 2, 1878,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  The back country of the Waimakiriri is inaccessible by dray, so that all the stores and all the wool have to be packed in and packed out on horseback. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,59</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]



 
 <i>v.</i> to tramp into (back country, mountains) or make a journey in on foot, with a pack on the back.

>  Defendant packed in stores on March 17, and they were in the whare when plaintiffs arrived. <i>(Aug 28, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The back country .... is inaccessible by dray, so that all stores ... have to be packed in and packed out on horseback. Ibid. v/65 ... all the stores and all the wool have to be packed in and packed out on horseback. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,V/61</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... and they got me a job. Pretty hard yakker too, packing in stuff the mine from the sawmill ... <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,24</i>)

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> Bill had just seen off his party [of hunters], ... having sadly decided that his ageing heart wasn't up to packing in. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,123</i>)

### pack off
 
 <i>v.</i> to dismiss abruptly, to get rid of [ca1940 at least]

### pack one's snack
 
 <i>phr.</i> to lose one's temper [1960 18C M B11 Nelson Boys C. 30B SNACK FROM `TO PACK ONE'S SNACK', WHICH MEANS `TO LOSE ONE'S TEMPER']

### pack one's swag
 
 <i>phr.</i> be sacked (See [swag](spa-sys.md#swag))

### pack out
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The back country .... is inaccessible by dray, so that all stores ... have to be packed in and packed out on horseback. <i>Ibid.</i> v/65 ... all the stores and all the wool have to be packed in and packed out on horseback. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,V/61</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs</i>) [NOTES: [?or Press]]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to take supplies or fencing materials to an isolated part of a farm or station by pack-horse or to transport goods by pack-horse off a station for sale or disposal.

>  The back country of the Waimakiriri is inaccessible by dray, so that all the stores and all the wool have to be packed in and packed out on horseback. <i>(1860,BLANK ENTRY,59</i>) [NOTES: in BUTLER First Year (1863)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A certain amount of chaff is packed out to the camps, but even so it is necessary for the gang to come into the homestead to get fresh hacks every now and then. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,149</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Jack was going over also to pack out grass seed. <i>(Sep 1913,Alexander Mowat Diaries,1</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the 'pockets', which weight from 70 to 90 lb each, are packed out to the homestead by horses. <i>(1952,High Country Days,78</i>)

>  <b>PORTER</b> Once he had packed out to a musterers' hut and after unsaddling his pack team, he lay down on the bunk to rest. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,78</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> A shed was built to stockpile the manure until it was packed out and spread on the hills. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,112</i>)

### pack rape
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>L BAUER</b> Have you noticed the recent use of `pack rape' (e.g. this morning's Morning Report) to replace `gang rape'? <i>(Jun 30, 1987,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (nb: date provided to database was 31/6/1987)]

### pack road
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DOBSON</b> A pack road already existed as far as the Cass, which could be improved into a coach road at no great expense, ... <i>(1930,Reminiscences,97</i>)

### pack shit
 
 <i>phr.</i> to be afraid

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pack shit</i> to be afraid; eg `Man, when those bikies arrived all at once, skull jackets and shades and all, we packed shit, no two ways about it.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,81</i>)

### pack team
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of packhorses, used to transport stores, wood, musterers' provisions.

>  <b>PORTER</b> Once he had packed out to a musterer's hut and after unsaddling his pack team, he lay down on the bunk to rest. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,78</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> We turned sharp inland and down over the hill-tops, the trail faintly marked by the station pack-tem. <i>(1921,Tutira,150</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> All over the High-Country the pack teams and musterers are winding out to the back ... <i>(1951,NZ High Country,32</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Her main mate in the pack team was a big grey, Snowy, who had no mouth at all ... [pack-team] <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,17</i>)

### pack up
 
 <i>v.</i> v.t. to pack sheep close together

>  <b>BOWEN</b> On a cold day the shearer requires the sheep packed up to warm them. This makes a big difference to the shearing, especially on sticky sheep. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,13</i>)

### pack yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area outside farm buildings, store or stable where provisions are packed on or off pack-horses.

>  <b>Tait</b> Unloading stores in the packyard. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,57</i>)

### pack-bridge
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Engineer was instructed to take means to protect the pack bridges from bush fires. <i>(Feb 3, 1892,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  The stream is now crossed by a light temporary pack bridge. <i>(Apr 12, 1906,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>BARRY</b> The connection between Cromwell and the country lower down the river Clutha, was a pack-bridge erected over that river. Wagons with stores and goods had to unload, and everything was packed across on horses. <i>(1879,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,203</i>)

>  Anyhow, out-door work has been almost impossible, the pack-bridge across the Waingongoro on Opunake road has been swept away, as well as a couple of embankments on Cheal Road, and things generally have been very uncomfortable. <i>(May 15, 1882,Hawera & Normanby Star,1</i>)

### pack-liner
 
 <i>n.</i> the lining of a back-pack
