>  <b>xxx</b> <i>(Jan 21, 2000,North & South</i>)

>  Whap, whap, whap - an up valley wind was beating at the pack-liner. <i>(Nov 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

### pack-saddle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I have at last bought a pack saddle quite new and a very good sorte ... <i>(Aug 25, 1963,Chudleigh Diary,100,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Packsaddle, n.</i> On stations where <i>cradle-ss.</i> are used the ordinary<i>kind of</i> ps. <i>are called</i> swing-ss<i>. (C.) </i> <i>(1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  The pack-saddle over the hill and the tax-cart from the foot of it to Christchurch are wretched makeshifts ... <i>(Aug 22, 1857,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

### pack-team
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> We led our horses over the `shoot', the almost perpendicular drop, down which the pack-team used in muddy weather to slide with stiff legs and unlifted hoofs. <i>(1926,Tutira,148</i>)

### pack-track
 
 <i>n.</i> a track made for packing supplies (? by horse)

>  In the meanwhile prospectors on the new quarty [sic] and alluvial goldfields and the employees of the Consolidated Company have to depend upon the pack track and the field is being held back; developments without a road being impossible for any but a very wealthy company. <i>(Jul 26, 1907,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  The journey was over a pack track from Rutherford's homestead, at The Peaks, to Lake's Station, and back, an hour being allowed before the return. <i>(Mar 24, 1910,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  To request the Government to make a road passable for drays, is a mere waste of time, for when they grudge a track to the golden Shotover, the residents of Moke and Moonlight may rest assured they will not grant them such a road - even a pack track being one of the things of the future for that rich and populous district. <i>(Oct 2, 1863,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  Is it a pack-track? <i>(1896,AJHR,D.-4/146</i>)

>  ... a metalled pack-track. <i>(1870,AJ,3/40/6</i>) [NOTES: (Reports Goldfields West Coast and Auckland)]

>  A pack-track is now being made to this reef. <i>(1887,Handbook NZ Mines,220</i>)

>  <b>MOFFAT</b> [We] came back down the Buller from the Grip, the pack track being then under construction. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,67,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> [In February 1864] he suceeded in getting commissariat through to the troops ... by rapidly cutting a pack-track from Raglan Harbour over the ranges to the Waipa ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/303</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> ... in the open country there were pack tracks everywhere. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,172</i>)

>  <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,219</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Molesworth is forty-four miles from Hanmer by pack-track, about seventy-five from Blenheim by road. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,1</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> A similar signal later was used by packers coming in from Takaka. The packtrack, even though infrequently [25] used, is best visualized as their main road. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,24</i>)

>  There's roads all round here now, but they were just pack tracks in those days. They used to pack stuff out to the miners. <i>(Dec 25, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,7</i>)

>  `Last summer we constructed ten miles of swag-track down the Karamea towards the Crow [a tributary of the Karamea], this track diverging from the main pack-track in the vicinity of the Falls. ... The distance from the end of the swag-track to the mouth of the Crow I cannot positively say.' <i>(1962,Newport Footprints,242</i>) [NOTES: 1898 Nov 15 <i>Colonist</i> Newspaper, Brough `A Note from the Wilderness' in 1962 Newport Footprints 242]

>  At last we reached the pack-track.. thence to the chilly ranges and the silly "jambucks" that were to be our spoil. <i>(Sep 30, 1924,Otago Witness,67</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a narrow track cut through bush or rock wide enough only to accommodate a pack-horse or pack-bullock. (See [horse-track](hom-hyp.md#horse-track))

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> From there the pack-track went up the Rakaia Valley to Lake Coleridge then over the Craigieburn Saddle just west of Redhill ... <i>(1951,Chatham Islands,54</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> The five mile steep and rocky pack track from Cape Runaway was impractical for all but light stores and requirements. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,163</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> When Turnbull bought it, there were no buildings on it, and only a pack-track to it. It is miles by pack-track to the nearest neighbour. <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,244</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Molesworth is forty-five miles by pack-track from Hanmer ... <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,1</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The only access, of course, was by pack-track and the wool-clip - 450 to 500 'pockets' was packed out to a shed on the Mohaka River about six miles from the Taupo road .. For the first three days the sheep had to pick their way along the pack-track almost single file ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,194</i>)

>  (FROM THE DAILY TIMES CORRESPONDENT).. A few observations are, in the first place, necessary upon the very dangerous state of the pack-track between Queenstown and Maori Point... <i>(Apr 29, 1865,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  The tracks betwee Mcnally's and Jones's Creek are in a very bad condition, and the pack-track is in all places almost impassable for either man or beast. <i>(Nov 14, 1865,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  (FROM THE DAILY TIMES CORRESPONDENT).. Two or three thousand pounds must be spent at once in bridging the Shotover, and opening a passable pack-track between Queenstown and the reefs ... <i>(Feb 24, 1866,Otago Witness,14</i>)

### pack-track hut
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rural hut used by mustering pack-horse teams and horseback travellers.

>  As the area has been farmed since the 1850s, the park boasts an important collection of historic farm building, including the 1860s Quail Flat cob homestead and woolshed, as well as mustering and pack-track huts dating from the 1920s. <i>(Aug 2008,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,30</i>)

### pack-trail
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The valley apparently escaped any further geological attention until 1946 when Brian Mason examined the area by "following the pack trail from Kekerangu [sic] to Coverham, thence up the reiver the Quail Flat. . . ." (Mason, B. 1958: The intrusive rocks of the Kaikoura mountains, Marlborough, New Zealand. Transactions of the Royal Society of New Zealand 85: 247 - 262.) <i>(Nov 1985,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,70/32</i>)

>  <b>DON</b> Former pack-trails and bridle-tracks are now dray-roads. <i>(1905,Chinese Mission Work,5</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The pack trail led up the bed of the Tutaekuri river from Puketapu to Konini, 30 miles from Napier, ... <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,39</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> ... where the bulldozed track (bulldozed as discreetly as possible) and its now-healing scars stopped, and Flora's footpath and packtrail resumed. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,48</i>)

### packbag
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a saddlebag on a packhorse.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> When the packbags were full, Dad moved off with the horses to dump the rabbits where he intended to skin them. <i>(1974,Open Country Muster,259</i>)

### packer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BROOKES</b> I have often watched packers, who would carry a load of seventy five pounds on their backs, through a rough survey line <i>(1892,Frontier Life,117</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> one who earns his living by packing or is employed (by a station) to pack supplies

>  An old man named Jas. Bryson, better known as "Jimmy the Packer," aged about 75 years, was found dead in bed at Waikaia this morning. <i>(Oct 6, 1909,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> Two packers, with some of the mules which have been used for many years on Orari Gorge, had gone ahead of us to pitch camps for the night - ... <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,18</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

>  <b>ROGERS</b> `Good healthy work, mustering. Up in the ranges. ... We send a packer up each week with supplies.' <i>(1960,Long White Cloud, The</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> Like an army [the musterers] moved with their baggage train, consisting of pack horses or mules in charge of the `packer', who also fulfilled the duties of cook, [106] butcher, and numerous other roles. <i>(1938,High Country,105</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> A similar signal later was used by packers coming in from Takaka. The packtrack, even though infrequently [25] used, is best visualized as their main road. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,24</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... stores ... before the packer came up from the beach with more provisions. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,224</i>)

>  Open to all horses that have been packing regularly in the district for three months prior to the day of the race, and the <i>bona fide</i> property of packers living in the Grey Valley District. <i>(Feb 18, 1871,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  It appears that some packers were leaving town ..., when one of the horses, in kicking up the sand, unearthed a number of glittering specks ... <i>(Jan 5, 1866,West Coast Times</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Jan 5 West Coast Times in 1914 Pfaff The Diggers' Story 127]

>  <b>115</b> The packer, who had been hourly expected did not get in before the storm came on. <i>Ibid.</i>135 The rear [of the string of pack-horses] was brought up by the packer on horseback, his broad-brimmed wide-a-wake hat pulled well over his weather-beaten face. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> An endless train of gold-diggers with pack-horses, packers driving horses before them, ... [68] all going to the new Eldorado. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,67</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> One of his ould [sic] mates told me that Jack, the packer, who goes past that way, had told him that Mr. Leslie had got on the spree ... , and had been at it pretty well straight on end since then. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,299</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> ... a packer offered me higher wages to drive pack-horses down ... towards Hokitika. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,76</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> A good packer never swore, or threw stones at his horse. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,13</i>) [NOTES: 1890's (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 13 [MS. I.T.G.]]

>  MUSTERERS, SHEPHERDS & PACKERS' DISPUTE [title] ... relating to musterers, shepherds and packmen ... <i>(1912,Sheepowner's Handbook, The,6</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> There were nine of us who fell in ... at Harry Tanner's little bush store and accommodation-house, and each ... picked out a horse from the little mob, of Maori-bred animals that the packer brought along ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,2</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Packers, that is men exceptionally strong who acted as beasts of burden for the storekeepers, were arriving every now and then with great packs on their backs. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,30</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Grey River Argus in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 30]

>  Packer, Packman.- One who loads the packhorses and lead or drives them from camp to camp. He also cooks for the musterers. (C.) <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  John Mulligan, a packer, .. said that in April last he was residing in Queenstown, and had a man in his employ named James O'Brien. <i>(Jan 17, 1866,West Coast Times,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a wool packer

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Sawyers, and stockmen, carpenters, packers, and shinglers and, loafers,/ Smoke as they work to assist them, and then knock off for a <i>`smoke oh!'/ </i> <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,124</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Hexameters in 1874 Kennaway Crusts 124]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Ticket sellers, ticket takers, ushers, barrier men, packers, and stage doorkeepers employed in connection with casual performances or engagements which do not exceed three weeks shall be paid 3s 4d per performance. <i>(May 18, 1914,Evening Post,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) 1. a shearing shed worker who packs fleeces into bales 2. a person in charge of station pack-horse team 3. a person in charge of a mustering pack-train, setting up camp and often cooking. (See [packman](p-per.md#packman))

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The packer went to Fulton & Co, camp for stores. <i>(Jul 14, 1914,Diary</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> The packer took 12 cwt wire onto Schultz's boundary where Harwell is working. 3. <i>(Jul 15, 1914,Diary</i>)

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> .. eight shearers, one packer, one fleece roller, one picker-up. 2. <i>(Dec 23, 1868,Chudleigh Diary</i>)

>  The packer, who is in charge of the packhorses, acts as camp cook. In the evening there is a huge meal, with a 'duff' if the packer has had time to build one. <i>(Aug 1, 1936,NZ Dairy Exporter,69</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The team of pack-horses was kept in control by dogs. The packer had them well-trained. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,159</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Jack Cooper has been the regular packer here for about 15 years now. <i>(2001,Riding Country,99</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>packer n.</i> a pack-horse; a person who transports goods on pack-horses. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <i>Pack&#769;.er </i>(p&abreve.k&#769;.&etilde.r), <i>n. [Add.] 2</i>. A pack horse. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

### packer-cook
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) packman for a mustering team, who doubles as cook.

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> An extra three shepherds and a packer/cook are on the payroll. <i>(2001,Riding Country,99</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> The men worked in teams of seven or eight musterers and a packer-cook. <i>(1993,Station Country I,182</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Following that there was a season with the hard-bitten mustering team, not as a musterer, but as packer-cook. <i>(1993,Station Country I,185</i>)

### packet from Paris
 
 <i>phr.</i> a baby [also parcel from Paris]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Packet or parcel from Paris</i>. A baby: Australian and New Zealand: C. 20. [f. French letter] <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,598</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>packet from Paris</i> a baby; eg `Hello, dear. As you can see, I'm your Plunket nurse. And what have we here, a dear little packet from Paris. Isn't he gorgeous?' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,81</i>)

### packet of birdseed
 
 <i>phr.</i> in phrase: I wouldn't have him on a packet of birdseed

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> He's worthless, or, without value in this connection. .in 4;`So I said, `Don't tell me you're taking any notice of him! Why, that drongo, I wouldn't have him on a packet of birdseed.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],1106</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63)1974 Syder and Hodgetts Aust. and NZ English [TS] 1106]

### packhorse
 
 <i>n.</i> elliptical for packhorse crayfish q.v.

>  The introduction of air travel by flying boat in the 1950s made the commercial exploitation of packhorses possible, and led to a crayfishing boom on the island. <i>(Jan 1991,NZ Geographic,106</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Whether, in the event of a coach service being established between Christchurch and the Hanmer Plains, the Government will establish a packhorse mail service from the said plains to Ahaua via the Amuri Pass, if obtainable at a moderate cost?" <i>(Oct 21, 1876,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> From Glen-iti, pack-horses or bullocks can travel all the way to the big wood ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,245</i>)

>  ... a pack-horse ought not to carry more than half-a-load (100 lbs). <i>(1862,AJ,E-9-24</i>) [NOTES: (C Hunter Brown)]

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> Potatoes were not on the ration until the end of 1909, and then much to the annoyance of the packie ... as it meant an extra packhorse in the team. <i>(1969,Molesworth,103</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> ... small loads only can be carried over by pack-horse ... <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,27</i>)

>  In reply to Mr Kenny, the Government said they would call for tenders for a pack-horse mail service from the Hamner [sic] Plains to Ahaura, via Amuri. <i>(Oct 25, 1876,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

### packhorse crayfish
 
 <i>n.</i> Jasus verreauxi, a large dark-green marine lobster; aka green or smoothtail crayfish [also packhorse elliptical]

>  One of three marine reserves that were announced last week, Te Pepae Aotea, lies in the path of the relatively warm east Auckland current that sweeps down from subtropical regions north of New Zealand, allowing some rare warm water species to flourish, including Spanish lobster and packhorse crayfish. <i>(Dec 14, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,1</i>)

>  Picked up a whacking great packhorse (big brother to a crayfish) in Whitianga late one afternoon this week and was eating him in Auckland as an early tea about an hour later. <i>(Dec 20, 1963,Auckland Star</i>) [NOTES: [? p. Noel Holmes's Col.]]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> <i>Packhorse or Green Crayfish</i> - koura papatia, <i>Jasus verreauxi </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> CRAYFISH ... The biggest saltwater variety is the Packhorse Crayfish, so named because he grows to a prodigious size compared with his offsider, the spiny, or red crayfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,39</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SMOOTHTAIL CRAYFISH ... <i>Other names: Jasus verreauxii</i>; green crayfish, pack-horse. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,299</i>)

>  The green or packhorse crayfish occurs in New Zealand only from the Bay of Plenty north, but is also found in New South Wales and adjacent coasts. Its flesh is denser and less delicate than that of the smaller red species. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,66</i>)

>  [Caption] Brian Hoult fished up a packhorse crayfish weighing 6.3 kilograms and 1.34 metres long... Mr Campbell said the cray was a female packhorse crayfish and was possibly 100 years old. <i>(Sep 27, 2003,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> There clawing its way up at him was the biggest packhorse crayfish I had ever seen. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,188</i>)

>  Another [local], Bill Owen, began lifting packhorse crayfish in large numbers in the 1950s. <i>(Jan 1991,NZ Geographic,106</i>)

### packhorse rock lobster
 
 <i>n.</i> Jasus verreauxi, aka packhorse crayfish

>  [species name] packhorse rock lobster .. [scientific name] <i> Jasus verreauxi </i> .. [other name(s)] crayfish green, rock lobster, packhorse <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i> Packhorse rock lobster Jasus verreauxi </i>Alternatively, green rock lobster or smoothtail lobster. Also occurs in eastern Australia. ...Found mainly from Castlepoint northwards ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,151</i>)

>  Similar work was now being done with packhorse lobsters, though it was the smaller red rock lobster that held the greatest hope of commercial benefit. <i>(Mar 14, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

### packhouse
 
 <i>n.</i> a fruit packing facility

>  Investors can either have a packhouse fully manage the orchard or enter a lease arrangement with it. Typically, the packhouse will pay $1 a tray up front then distribute a profit share following harvest. <i>(Sep 21, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D16</i>)

### packie
 
 <i>n.</i> short for "packman"

>  Horses of every description have increased in price, and any `old screw,' that can do a `packman's ' labour, is seized upon as a prize. <i>(Aug 30, 1862,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The `packie moves on again' <i>(1947,Wayleggo,14</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> [Packhorses] set off in single file with the packie bringing up the rear on his hack. <i>(1951,High Country,32</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Bundling the bird up in his tucker bag he finished his beat and on arriving in at camp ... gave it to the packie to cart round to the homestead. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,180</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Next ....is the `packie' - half cook, half handyman, always good with horses or mules - who takes provisions into `camp', as hut or tent life is called. <i>(1952,Land Uplifted High,162,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> With the long, dry spell we soon had all our deer hides dried and bundled up ready for removal whenever the packy called. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,106</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> IT'S AN OLD MUSTERER'S SAYING that `the packman earns his dough'. I always supposed it was true; but of course I hadn't the least hesitation in calling the packie a lazy so and so, if the spuds weren't cooked and the billy on the boil when I got into camp. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,10</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Although they [mules] are renowned for their dirt ... most of the old packies swear by them. They can live on the smell of an oily rag ... and areas tough as old boots. <i>(1973,Big Country,87,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Fergie yelled to the packman to get the swags before they got wet, and he and Dave rushed down to the packie's assistance. <i>Ibid.</i> 45 The packie hurried back to his horses, ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,26</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> Potatoes were not on the ration until the end of 1909, and then much to the annoyance of the packie ... as it meant an extra packhorse in the team. <i>(1969,Molesworth,103</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> That autumn I was given the packie's job. I was shown how to make an `oil drum' stew and, apart from a few slabs of toast and black tea, that was about the musterers' sole diet. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,22</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Joe Ferris was head shepherd and Chris Mccauley and Jimmy Muir paddock shepherds, and Bukkie Cordie, packie. ... Murdock McKay was still boss of the shearing board. <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,42</i>)

>  Next on the list is the 'packie' - half-cook, half-handyman, always good with horses or mules - who takes blankets and provisions into 'camp', as hut or tent life is called. <i>(Apr 1945,NZ Geographer,I/21</i>)

>  The packie's very chatty this morning. <i>(Jul 7, 1965,Auckland Weekly News,39</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Packmen are as scarce as hen's teeth and they have a job that tries a man... According to the packie, when he cooled down a bit, it was a good one [ sc. the new job] on which to cut my teeth. <i>Ibid</i> 100 The packie seemed as cheerful as ever. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,13</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] a packman/cook in a mustering team.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. most of the old packies swear by them [donkeys] <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,87</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The process is repeated, the 'packie' moving on again. <i>(1949,Wayleggo,14</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> .. the packie gets to work on a full-scale dinner of mutton, spuds, vegetables and duff ... <i>(1951,NZ High Country,32</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> A skilled packey could produce from one [camp oven] an appetising stew, or a roast, or something deep fried, or sometimes even new bread rolls. [packy; packey] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,15</i>)

### packing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the loading of provisions on to packsaddles for mustering gangs or for station supplies.

>  <b>JONES</b> The packing of the wire and standards was an undertaking up the high hills - about 6,000 feet high. <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,96</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The bulk of the Bluff packing was done from the Clarence Reserve homestead ... <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,87</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When the narrator left Muttontown he lost his mate who went packing (carrying goods with packhorses) ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,159</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) acting as packman for musterers.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Packing is a thankless job of long hours and primitive conditions, but I was on the threshold of the mountains and life was good. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,23</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They employed two packmen at that time - one to do the packing for the musterers - and his mate was Billie Thompson. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,88</i>)

### packing shed
 
 <i>n.</i> a shed for sorting, grading and packing fruit [also known as 'packhouse']

>  "Packing-shed" - or "pack-houses", to use the common .. really shorthand for what is an operations base, responsible for everything from establishing and .. orchards to loading fruit .. in Tauranga. <i>(Jan 2005,NZ Geographic,No. 71/</i>)

>  The Packing Shed was located well down the orchard, about a quarter of a mile from the front gate. <i>(Aug 2003,NZ Memories,No. 43/38</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large shed for pressing scoured wool. (See [pressing shed](pop-pyo.md#pressing-shed))

>  <b>BREMNER</b> Wool press is in the packing shed on right. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> In the hand-carts, dried wool was taken to the packing shed to be pressed. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,72</i>)

### packing-in
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> Duffer rushes collapsed so quickly that storekeepers could find themselves in the back-of-beyond with a fully-stocked shanty and no sales to pay for the cost of packing-in and packing-out. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,281,1962 ed.</i>)

### packing-out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> Duffer rushes collapsed so quickly that storekeepers could find themselves in the back-of-beyond with a fully-stocked shanty and no sales to pay for the cost of packing-in and packing-out. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,281</i>)

### packman
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who moves equipment, makes camp, and cooks for sheep musterers.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Packmen are as scarce as hen's teeth and they have a job that tries a man... According to the packie, when he cooled down a bit, it was a good one [ sc. the new job] on which to cut my teeth. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the rugby football sense `forward', general English : first recorded NZ 1905 Aug 19 Truth 9/2/7 (See [packer](p-per.md#packer))



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering team cook, packhorse keeper and packer.

>  Witness sent her off to Maketu in charge of one of the packmen. <i>(Jan 5, 1872,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Luke and Co. - Want packman. <i>(Mar 23, 1889,Bush Advocate,2</i>)

>  He took the door and slammed it, leaving the packman and his pack outside at the step. <i>(Jun 30, 1898,Otago Witness,61</i>)

>  Packer, packman, one who loads the packhorses and leads or drives them from camp to camp. He also cooks for the musterers. <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> On the Hill 3 .. they went out to camp in tents and huts on the hills with a string of packhorses and a packman to cook for them. <i>(1954,On the Hill,3</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> The pack-man seemed completely unconcerned and after all the others had filed past, got off his horse, walked off the track and pulled up a handful of tussock. [pack-man] <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,57</i>)

>  The Inspector of Police received word from Culverden this afternoon that Sams, the missing packman who was lost over Jollie's Pass, Hanmer, is returning with the search party safe and well. <i>(Aug 1, 1912,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  A fortnight ago the packman refused to take his horses over a part of the road, so the settlers had to swag their stores. <i>(Sep 22, 1908,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  Witnesses heard from a packman that prisoner had sold a mare. <i>(Jan 17, 1866,West Coast Times,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOWAT</b> So the packman, W, Sansom, dealt out a pound to each man to carry in his tucker bag. <i>(1907,Diary</i>) [NOTES: c1907-1914 Mowat diary in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 103]

>  MUSTERERS, SHEPHERDS & PACKERS' DISPUTE [title] ... relating to musterers, shepherds and packmen ... <i>(1912,Sheepowner's Handbook, The,6</i>)

>  In the midst of them [the musterers in the whare] the packman was filling his bread baskets. <i>(Apr 10, 1917,Triad, The,66</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> After the bread ran out [in the musterers' camp] we would have flap-jacks. Firewood was ... picked up by the packman as he went along. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,121,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A man who cooks for the musterers is called a packman. As the musterers move over the country he has to pack up, shift camp, and follow them. <i>(1949,High Country Days,196,1950; 1966 ed. 188 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A man who cooks for the musterers is called a packman. As the musterers move over the country he has to pack up, shift camp, and follow them. <i>(1949,High Country Days,196,1950; 1966 ed. 188 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> On one place I knew, the packman, who cooks for the musterers and carries their equipment on packhorses, had to make a day's journey of 28 miles.... <i>(1951,High Country,9</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Later I was to discover that he had spent years as a packman and, in general, hated musterers like poison. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,3</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> During the latter part of the packhorse trail days, Jack Arnott had the Napier - Wairoa mail contract, and [8] Charlie Nicolls was his packman. Charlie had to be up at 4 a.m. each Monday, feed, groom and harness his team of from eight to twelve packhorses and his riding hack, have breakfast, and be round at the Petane Post Office with his team ready to load up by 9 a.m. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,7</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Packman</i>: <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The rare perspiring trackmaker and the flitting packman were Chaffey's passing neighbours, who'd drop in and leave stores and mail from time to time. The camp cook who was in charge of packhorses for mustering. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,122</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> One of the sons ... later spent many years as a packman on Molesworth and was well known in that locality. <i>(1973,Big Country,26,1977 ed.</i>)

### packman-cook
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Weeks out mustering without a packman-cook, crowded ... in the little smoky huts out-back. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering team cook, packhorse keeper and packer.

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Weeks out mustering without a packman-cook, crowded on wet days in the little smoky huts out-back. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,2</i>)

### packout
 
 <i>n.</i> proportion of harvest which can be marketed

>  Planted in high demand varieties including royal gala, braeburn and brookfield, the orchard boasts a 90% packout of the royal gala - no small achievement but indicative of the quality of the block. <i>(Aug 16, 2003,Hawke's Bay Today,C8</i>)

### packstrap
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The swags [were] lashed together [on the packorse] with long packstraps. <i>(1949,High Country Days,38</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> Pack straps were tied to the ends of the ridge rope and led down to a ... standard driven in. <i>(1969,Molesworth,100</i>)

### packy
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [packie](p-per.md#packie))

### Pacrim
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) Pacific Rim

>  Colin Douch, Jenny Arden and Phil Tailor attended the Pacrim Conference in Brisbane, and Colin then travelled the length and breadth of Queensland visiting mines. <i>(Mar 1988,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,79/29</i>)

### pad
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>pad 3 n. NZ & Austral.</i> path or track (<i>cattle-pad</i>). [Eng. dial.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,53</i>)

### pad (the) hoof
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> He'd scaled the heights of Nevis, `padded hoof' to Albertown,/ Tramped and coached it through Kawarau Gorge and spelled at Arrowtown./ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,63</i>)

>  Therefore, Woollcombe, get thee hence; go home thou wowser on the wallaby; hump bluey, carry Matilda, pad the hoof, thou parsonical howler, thou Wowseristic whaler Woollcombe. <i>(Nov 6, 1909,NZ Truth,228/1/3</i>)

### pad run
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a track in bush left by possums.

>  <b>PRACY & KEAN</b> The trap is paced on a pad run (opossum track) on the ground or on a fallen log or tree .. cyanide paste is best applied at ground level on opossum pad runs ... <i>(1969,Opossum in NZ, The,18</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Droppings, pad runs, and bark biting as well as the virtual stripping of favoured food trees is easily observed. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,13</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Moving from one location to another causes 'pad runs' to be established by constant use. <i>(1984,Possum Hunting in New Zealand,39</i>)

### paddle
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I sent Fitch with 400 flour to the paddle. [fn] The paddle was a ferry by canoe over the Waimakariri in the region of Tainui's property, and operated by Tainui. 1849 ibid 110 ... fetched Boys, Tom Bryant and Alfred Weston from the paddle. <i>(Oct 19, 1849,Torlesse Papers,109,1958 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also dolly](dig-dou.md#dolly))

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Generally there has been little change in fellmongering practice since 1892. Skins are still cold-water washed in `dollies' or paddles, deburring machines are no longer used, but the de-wooling paint is applied on a moving conveyor instead of on the piled skins. <i>(1957,Canterbury Frozen Meat Company,69</i>)

### paddle crab
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [crab](con-cyp.md#crab))

### paddle pop
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i> paddle pop</i> ice-block; 1960s teen rhyming slang. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,81</i>)

### paddle-leaf
 
 <i>n.</i> puka

>  an ornamental tree (Meryta Sinclairii), paddle-leaf. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

### paddle-weed
 
 <i>n.</i> ora-weed

>  <b>MARTIN</b> (3) <i>Oar-weed, Paddle-weed, or Occtopus-weed, (Ecklonia radiata)</i> ... It ranges to Otago, but is uncommon save on North Island coasts ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,11</i>)

### paddock
 
 <i>n.</i> (in flaxmilling) the drying green

>  GENERAL VIEW OF THE BLEACHING PADDOCKS. <i>(Jun 17, 1908,Otago Witness,50</i>)

>  [The flax fibre] is carted ... to the drying green, generally called the `paddock', where it is spread out to dry and bleach. ... It is [then] taken in hand by the `scutcher', who feeds it into a revolving drum ... which beats the refuse out. <i>(1913,Country Work,44</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an area of land or water used in gold-sluicing

>  He has watched the progress of the placefrom the day when the first paddock wastaken out in search of gold. <i>(Dec 30, 1909,Otago Daily Times,8</i>)

>  I hear that Rawlings and party in Mr Edward Tubman's paddock on the mining reserve have struck a good payable run of ground. <i>(Aug 13, 1881,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  <b>EASTWOOD</b> Miners stored their dirt in a "paddock" made from logs and sheets of iron at their tunnel mouth. <i>(1982,Brief History of Gold Mining, A,132</i>)

>  Mr Lord moved that if the Government engineer considers that a grave danger exists we hold that it is the duty of the Government to construct the proposed embankment as a Colonial work for the protection of the Midland Railway, the settlers around the Inchbonnie paddock, and the mining interests on the Teremakau. <i>(Sep 4, 1908,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  The Bendigo Goldlight Dredge has recently set to work to open out a paddock. <i>(Aug 7, 1935,NZ Free Lance,19</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> When all the first area of wash had been put through the sluice box, they began on the second 'paddock'. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,15</i>)

>  He shovels the dirt and stones from his "paddock-'" on to the iron plate. <i>(Nov 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,39</i>)

>  The starboard bow line slackened during the heavy flood, causing the dredge to get athwart the current, when she filled and sank on her own paddock, which contained about 12 ft of water <i>(Dec 4, 1897,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to form a paddock by fencing land.

>  <b>BAKER</b> We have also made a verb out of it: to paddock land, to put up fences ... [to paddock out] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> =field

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> In the very early morning/ ... I lay down in the paddock/ And listened to the cold song of the grass. <i>(1923,Poems (various authors),25</i>)

>  We're all over the paddock this morning. <i>(Jun 3, 2005,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Nine to Noon]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> In the early sixties..expense and the scarcity of water prevented much sub-division into paddocks on most of the plains stations. <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,7</i>)

>  'Creek' and 'paddock' are New Zealandisms, because they mean something quite different in the English of England. It is of some significance that Katherine Mansfield uses both words only in their New Zealand sense. <i>(Nov 22, 1957,Listener (New Zealand Listener),4</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> In the lush paddocks on the river flat, a few prosperous farmers ran sheep. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,310</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> With the gate into the field (they call it paddock) shut. <i>(1962,Edge of the Alphabet,81</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> He then turned his attention to the garden, and to the astonishment of his neighbours engaged a landscape gardener to transform the slopes of his bare paddocks into a garden worthy of the Old Country. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,117</i>)

>  [South Mauku] is fast becoming open country, with comfortable homesteads and paddocks of good grass <i>(Mar 22, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  The paddocks even in the lowlands are parched. <i>(Jan 8, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  We paddock train him now and have changed his feed quite a bit. <i>(Nov 19, 2004,Dominion Post RaceForm,1</i>)

>  Would far rather hop over the parapet andgallop across the paddock than crouch inthe bottom of a trench waiting for a tripwest. <i>(Aug 30, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/1 : 9</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> Even I use words that are out of date. And where have all the creeks gone, and the paddocks? <i>(1988,Carpathians, The,30</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> goldmining: to excavate a paddock [goldmining] (See [paddock goldmining 2: to store (excavated washdirt)in a paddock or other area or receptacle](../P#paddock-goldmining-2:-to-store-(excavated-washdirt)in-a-paddock-or-other-area-or-receptacle))

>  Many .. who held river claims worked .. so as to cut off a portion of its bed, which, being drained by pumping, was paddocked out and passed through the cradle. <i>(1863,AJHR,D-6 /18</i>)

>  The washdirt was 'paddocked' or 'driven out', and carried or carted to where there was water. <i>(1906,NZ Mining Handbook,204</i>)

>  <b>Chudleigh</b> Went down several more feet with our pit. One of the digers [sic] .. came to the conclusion we should have to paddock it which is to work it like a quarry, large at the top instead of driveing levells [sic]. <i>(Jun 3, 1950,Diary,41</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced area of land on a farm or station. [see accommodation p-, airstrip p-, back p-, bottom p-, bull p-, bush p-, caving p-, capture p-, cell p-, clean p-, cocksfoot p-, County p-, cow p-, cowshed p-, cultivation p-, dip p-, dirty p-, dog-tucker p-, dog-training p-, drying p-, ewe p-, fattening p-, feeding p-, finishing p-, flat p-, flattening p-, foaling p-, front p-, grass p-, grazing p-, handy p-, hay p-, hemp p-, hill p-, hillside p-, holding p-, home p-, homestead p-, horse p-, hospital p-, hotel p-, house p-, isolation p-, killer p-, lambing p-, lane p-, long p-, maternity p-, mothering p-, night p-, paspalum p-, pig p-, pony p-, ram p-, reserved p-, river p-, run-off p-, sacrifice p-, saddling p-, safe p-, school p-, shearing p-, side p-, silage p-, spelled p-, spelling p-, station p-, stock p-, stubble p-, stump-, swamp p-, tailing p-, trial p-, tupping p-, tussock p, twinning p-, weaning p-, wether p-, wahere p-, wintering p-, woolshed p-. ]

>  The Undersigned is prepared to offer excellent Grazing and Paddock accomodation in a securely-fenced and well-watered paddock of 120 acres, at the following very moderate rates to suit the times ... <i>(May 30, 1868,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> Outside of these [paddocks] were larger paddocks (everything that is enclosed by a fence is called a paddock in New Zealand) averaging from 4000 to 5000 acres. <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,19</i>)

>  <b>GRANT & FOSTER</b> On large properties a 'paddock' as it is called, of 1,000 to 1,500 acres is a very common thing. <i>(1880,New Zealand: Report on its Agric. Conditions,14</i>)

>  There are a lot of very patchy paddocks of turnips, and rape in many instances has been a failure. <i>(Feb 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,154</i>)

>  The rape crop at Ruakura, on an undrained paddock, has been very successful and proves the advantage of sowing in drill on the ridge especially when sowing early, and on wet and foul land. <i>(Mar 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,204</i>)

>  Late weaning means patchy paddocks and bare sheep-camps. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,221</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Flockmasters shudder to watch the uneducated efforts of a young dog endeavouring to work sheep in the 200 acre paddock of today. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,12</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> Leaving the precincts of the house, we came to the paddocks - 'ram', 'killing', 'holding', and 'horse', they each served a particular purpose. <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,80</i>)

>  <b>HARRISON</b> He put the bull in the yard paddock one night after milking and ran some heifers in with him. <i>(1986,She's a Fair Cow,8</i>)

>  A paddock of 1000 acres is provided for mares, where they will be kept free of expense. <i>(Sep 1, 1860,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Mr. Morgan's station is on an extensive scale; he was building a large and excellent weather-boarded house; has a garden stocked with young fruit-trees of every description; and a capital paddock laid down in English grasses. <i>(Aug 19, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,4</i>)

>  .. the paddock where the plaintiff's cattle where [sic] put was no worse than the land outside... <i>(Apr 28, 1843,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to place stock in a paddock, usually sheep.

>  His principal Stand will be at Seadown where Mares can be paddocked one month free of charge. <i>(Oct 13, 1880,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> We .. reached the station .. in time to see our horses paddocked safely before the train started for Christchurch. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,86</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> On arrival I paddocked the horses out and set about killing the eight sheep. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,29</i>)

>  The sheep should be paddocked conveniently to the dipping yards. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Farmer,1481</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> While he paddocked the cattle, I went to look for food and a bunk. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,66</i>)

>  Loaded in the afternoon, they were paddocked overnight at Feilding. <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,37</i>)

>  Breeding cows can be draughted [sic] off the main mob and paddocked on to longer feed. <i>(May 1982,NZ Journal of Agriculture,43</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> We paddocked the sheep by the yards at the Hanmer River ... <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,84</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> The goodwill of the farmers along the way was also essential as the cattle had to be paddocked and grazed for the night. <i>(1998,Droving Days, The,5</i>)

>  Witness afterwards went to Normanby, and saw the sheep paddocked at the Recreation Ground there... <i>(Aug 28, 1902,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  The Auctioneer would beg to remind parties wishing to dispose of stock, that they must have them early paddocked for the sale, as his hammer will be in prompt attendance at 12 o'clock. <i>(Apr 16, 1859,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Mares from a distance will be paddocked for six weeks free of charge at any of the above-named places, and all care taken, but without responsibility. <i>(Nov 21, 1863,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  The evidence disclosed that 250 sheep were paddocked in eight acres without food. <i>(Sep 2, 1907,Hawera & Normanby Star,7</i>)

>  The evidence disclosed that 250 sheep were paddocked in eight acres without food. <i>(Sep 2, 1907,Hawera & Normanby Star,7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  No. 14 on this lead commenced washing up on Saturday, and the wash-dirt averaged 14 ozs. to the load; their first washing produced 30 lbs. weight of the precious metal; and in case their paddock of washdirt turns out as well all through, the fortunate owners may retire with a handsome fortune each. <i>(Jun 6, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  From one small paddock of washdirt they obtained 18dwt of fine gold, but it will take a few weeks to prove its real value. <i>(Oct 5, 1895,Marlborough Express,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to store excavated washdirt in gold-washing in a paddock or other area or receptacle. [goldmining] (See [paddock: goldmining: to excavate a paddock](../P#paddock:-goldmining:-to-excavate-a-paddock))

>  There are now eighteen parties paddocking dirt or preparing to paddock, and twenty-three on gold. <i>(Jan 15, 1891,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>Reid</b> I accosted a party of four who were paddocking a goodly looking wash. <i>(1884,Rambles on the Golden Coast,66</i>)

>  The aerial tramway is being kept at work nearly every day, transporting quartz from the mines to the flat, where it is being paddocked for the present. <i>(Nov 23, 1989,Te Aroha News,2</i>)

>  Paddock: to work an area of a certain size: To paddock washdirt, that is hold it in a bin, until water is available for washing. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,200</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a playing field

>  <b>KNIGHT</b> [He] described the incident as `the most frightening sight I have ever seen on a rugby paddock.' <i>(1986,Geriatrics, The,146</i>)

>  .. whose independent spirit, openness and rugged attitude on the paddock are everything southern men and women expect of one of their own. <i>(May 8, 2005,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  "I came off The Paddock absolutely stuffed"; "he was the best player on " The Paddock"; "he was the dishiest player on The Paddock"; "she's a beaut Paddock to play on"; "that bloody paddock - mud up to your landing gear" ... Paddock, paddock, paddock. Transport Twickenham, to the middle of Te Kuiti and we'd call it a paddock <i>(Sep 7, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  It also appears that cricket-playing is not a sin on a Sunday if you rent your own paddock. <i>(Dec 5, 1885,Evening Post,4</i>)

### paddock  wether
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. hill wether](../H#hill-wether),[paddock sheep](p-per.md#paddock-sheep))

>  100 FAT PADDOCK WETHERS. <i>(Sep 7, 1877,Nelson Evening Mail,3</i>)

>  200 - Paddock Wether LAMBS. <i>(Mar 19, 1929,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Shearing usually begins about November 20 with the rams, which are followed by the paddock wethers, hoggets, and hill wethers, and finally the ewes. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349</i>)

### paddock accommodation
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a holding paddock for stock in transit.

>  <b>BISLEY</b> First-class Stabling and Loose Boxes. Good paddock accommodation. <i>(1897,Practical Treatise on Wool and Sheep Breeding,60</i>)

### paddock cart
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large horse-drawn dray used to take scoured wool or flax to drying paddocks.

>  <b>BREMNER</b> The rakes delivered it [wool] to the mangle which then dropped it onto an elevator to be carried up onto a grating to await the paddock cart. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,58</i>)

### paddock cow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a beef cow grazed on low or down land [as opposed to a station or run cow.]

>  Jersey oddments have met keen competition, good-framed young paddock cows being keenly competed for. <i>(Nov 8, 1951,NZ Farmer,41</i>)

>  Meaty works cows, paddock cows and fat cattle met spirited competition and prices realised showed a slight advancement on recent rates. Paddock cows, young and in good condition GBP4. <i>(Nov 25, 1939,Hawera Star,12</i>)

>  Sale realisations were paddock cows GBP9 to GBP11. <i>(Dec 20, 1951,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

>  Paddock cows earned $1.20-$1.25/kg LW and lighter boners made $0.95-$1.10/kg LW. <i>(May 30, 2005,NZ Farmers' Weekly,18</i>)

### paddock deal
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sale conducted on a farm.

>  Fewer sheep had been sold at that time for delivery to the Waikato as a result of paddock deals. <i>(Mar 11, 1943,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

### paddock dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheepdog used for mustering and hill work, rather than yard work.

>  A litter of handy dogs make good paddock and yard dogs easy to work. <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,111</i>)

>  Sheep dogs are of three distinct classes - the show dog, or fancy-worker, the mustering or paddock dog, and the yard or forcing dog. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,209</i>)

### paddock ewe
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stock sales] a store ewe to be conditioned before slaughter.

>  Store sheep: light in lamb and paddock ewes $25-$40. <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Dominion,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high-altitude areas] a breeding ewe grazed on flats and downs, not on high country.

>  <b>J D ORMOND</b> Docked the lambs of paddock ewes. <i>(Oct 12, 1864,JD Ormond Stockbook 1864-1866</i>)

>  <b>J D ORMOND</b> Washed the rams a second time. Washed the paddock ewes twice. <i>(Jan 20, 1865,JD Ormond Stockbook 1864-1866</i>)

>  Amongst paddock ewes one dog is quite enough for a shepherd to have when going his rounds at lambing time. <i>(Oct 1918,NZ Farmer,1251</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> I soon learned that shearing fat paddock ewes was vastly different from prising the wool off skinny Merinos. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,57</i>)

### paddock farmer
 
 <i>n.</i> a downland farmer as distinct from a high-country farmer with large runs and ? natural (unfenced) boundaries

>  Of course we know that until the Manchester Road Board rally with the Kiwitea Road Board to clear the roads of stock not much can be done as the long-paddock-farmers [sic] have only to drive their stock a yard or two past the Cheltenham Hotel or an equal distance down Mackay's road to be at rest and safe. <i>(Nov 8, 1893,Feilding Star,2</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> How many down-country paddock farmers would be happy with an equal result? <i>(1969,Molesworth,192</i>)

### paddock farming
 
 <i>n.</i> farming in fenced areas of land (See [cf. forty-acre farmer](fly-fuz.md#forty-acre-farmer))

>  This increase in number of these sheep year by year at previous shows used to be looked upon with no small amount of satisfaction, giving as it did undoubted evidence of the extent to which paddock farming was being adopted, and the consequent cultivation of the waste lands by the plough. <i>(Oct 30, 1874,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  Since the advent of crossbred sheep and paddock farming for the production by [sic] lambs and mutton for freezing the demand has become more frequent for sheep. <i>(Sep 28, 1891,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  Other papers treat of "Sugar production," "Paddock farming with sheep," "The stud-book of the Clydesdale Horse Society," &c. <i>(Nov 4, 1884,Star, The,3</i>)

>  <b>MARSHMAN</b> The writer .... would ... prefer paddock farming to a run. ... It is a more pleasant kind of life. There is no living in `the bush', as the phrase is, with the nearest neighbour ten miles off. <i>(1864,Canterbury, NZ, in 1862,44</i>)

### paddock fence
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence enclosing a land area, usually pastoral.

>  When the thunderstorm was at its height, a horse, mad with terror, broke through its paddock fence, ultimately charging Mr Trains' cash store, smashing the door posts and glass door to pieces. <i>(Sep 12, 1887,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>Newton</b> Most of the paddock fences were renewed and attention was then given to the hill ... <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,199</i>)

>  The defendant admitted that the horse had escaped from his keeping, but pleaded that it was not through any fault of his own, as a ferocious white bull, for which no owner could be found, was in the habit of breaking down the paddock fence and had done so in that instance. <i>(May 17, 1873,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  One corner of a paddock fence runs up to this cliff leaving a space of about two feet from the post to the edge of the terrace, and at about a yard from this post were marks as of a person haven [sic] fallen down ... <i>(Mar 26, 1898,Feilding Star,2</i>)

>  C. Young, poundkeeper, wrote drawing the attention of the Council to the unsatisfactory condition of the pound and paddock fences. <i>(Apr 8, 1909,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

>  A fencer will be starting next week on the stock paddock fence at Waikohu, and the fence round the cottage at Wright's. <i>(Sep 13, 1918,Poverty Bay Herald,7</i>)

>  <b>Mansfield</b> A lovely kingfisher perched on the paddock fence preening his rich beauty ... <i>(1920,Stories,233,1984 (Alpers) ed.</i>)

>  They turned to go down to the paddock fence where I was working with the groom... <i>(Jun 29, 1865,North Otago Times,2</i>)

### paddock flock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [ high country farming] breeding ewes that graze on down and winter country close to the station, [as opposed to wethers that graze large areas of steep unfenced land.]

>  The lambing this season (says the Alexandra Herald) is a very prolific one and in some of the Central Otago paddock flocks, constitutes almost a record. <i>(Oct 16, 1914,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  Generally speaking, lambing has been good throughout Southland this season and the paddock flocks will return unusually good percentages. <i>(Oct 24, 1914,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  Shearing will be general among the paddock flocks at this time... <i>(Nov 25, 1882,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### paddock forker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a threshing-team worker who forks sheaves to the drayman.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> paddock forker: usually a permanent member of a threshing-mill team who forks the sheaves up to the drayman. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,59</i>)

### paddock gate
 
 <i>n.</i> a gate in a fenced pastoral area of land

>  When the train was approaching, the animals had evidently been at the paddock gate, where the line is crossed, and the railway fence giving way, the mob ran on to the line and galloped in front of the engine till they reached the cattle-stops... <i>(May 15, 1880,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  I hereby give notice that the person or persons who wilfully moved my paddock gate off its hinges will be prosecuted if detected. <i>(Sep 17, 1881,Hawera & Normanby Star,3</i>)

>  Frederick Bills, for a like offence, also pleaded that the case was purely accidental, a woman having left the paddock gate open, not knowing that a horse was there. <i>(Nov 15, 1872,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

>  William Forsyth said, acting upon instructions, he placed the mare in a paddock at Onehunga, and the following day he met Gumm at the paddock gate. <i>(Apr 24, 1915,NZ Truth,12</i>)

>  When the milking is done in the evenings, for an hour Harry will wait / To help her over the footbridge, and open the paddock gate. <i>(Mar 1, 1890,Oxford Observer,3</i>)

>  A petition was received from Mr. Wakeford and others, asking that the Washington-valley road should be formed and gravelled from Russell-street to Stafford's paddock gate. <i>(Apr 18, 1863,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  The larrikins who were so mean as to take the school paddock gate off and put it in the creek on sports night deserve to be severely reprimanded. <i>(Feb 26, 1908,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

### paddock graze
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to use an entire paddock for grazing for a short time [rather than break or block grazing].

>  Topics include .. Taranaki research into the effects on pasture and cows when stock are either block grazed or paddock grazed through winter ... <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,37</i>)

>  The first compromise would be to split the difference and paddock graze, but for 2-3 days only. <i>(Jun 1, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming</i>)

### paddock grazing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) allocating an entire paddock for grazing for up to five days, with hay or silage added in the last 2-3 days.

>  More recently, howver, the heavy labour requirement of this practice [block grazing] has been superseded by a modified system called paddock grazing. This is where up to 5 days pasture feed will be allocated in one hit (often meaning one complete paddock) with any hay or silage allocated usually being fed out in progressively larger amounts in the last 2-3 days. In minimising stress the paddock grazing was clearly superior. <i>(Jun 1, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming</i>)

### paddock hand
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BREMNER</b> <i>Paddock hand</i> hand who spread and turned wool on drying screen. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person employed to spread and turn wool in a drying paddock.

>  <b>BREMNER</b> Paddock hand - hand who spread and turned wool on drying green. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  Paddock hands were vigilant; wool could bleach to prefection or become yellow with too much sun, and had to be turned two-hourly. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,21</i>)

### paddock lambing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country] the lambing of ewes on lower fenced land.

>  The paddock lambing was luckily pretty well finished before the rough weather set in. <i>(Nov 27, 1890,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  The paddock lambing is over and the results on the whole are fairly good. Prospects for hill lambing are good. <i>(Oct 3, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,4</i>)

>  The storm came between the paddock lambing, which was over, and the hill lambing, which had scarcely begun, and disaster was thus escaped. <i>(Oct 1, 1902,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  .. to the north the easterly rains are always mild, and there the paddock lambing was finished in favourable circumstances... <i>(Oct 14, 1903,Otago Witness,8</i>)

### paddock lunch
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm-worker's meal eaten at the worksite.

>  Cakes are not my strong point. If I just knock one up for paddock lunches, it turns out a real success. <i>(Apr 15, 1943,Straight Furrow,45</i>)

>  Meal eaten on the job or in the paddock: a paddock lunch. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### paddock man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd or musterer working on downland hills, flats and paddocks [as opposed to large blocks of hill country.]

>  <b>KERR</b> The fencers fenced, the paddock men mustered paddocks, the hill men like me mustered hill country.. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,73</i>)

### paddock manure
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They have constructed four large concrete save-all pits, by means of which the heavy material is trapped and removed for paddock manure purposes, and does not now pass into the river. <i>(Jan 26, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,7</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The ground first received a [5] good dressing of paddock manure - this means free from straw, ... <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,4</i>)

### paddock mate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Like her paddock mate, she shows evidence of her visit to Gold Reef. <i>(Jul 25, 1899,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  A paddock mate of the Maniapoto - Surf colt is the fine son of Cordon Rouge, from a member of the Vivandiere family. <i>(Mar 24, 1909,Hawera & Normanby Star,3</i>)

>  It seems that she was kicked by one of her paddock mates, with the result that one of her legs was broken, and it became necessary to dispatch her. <i>(Jul 18, 1911,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  `In February, my sister answered an advert for a paddock mate for a weaning foal on a Glen Murray farm,' she said. <i>(Jul 10, 1988,Sunday News,7</i>)

### paddock offal
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) coarse material and weeds in pasture.

>  <b>WHITE ET AL</b> If a seed crop is to be taken in the second year, the paddock offal (straw and leaf) should be baled and removed, burnt or hard-grazed. <i>(1999,NZ Pasture and Crop Science,261</i>)

### paddock out
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> [gum digging]

>  But as this fine gum became scarcer, and the article had to be searched for at a greater depth the long spears of later times came into use, and now the scarcity of the gum has become such that, except in swampy ground, the use of the spear seems to be almost abandoned, and the diggers are beginning to find it necessary to dig in a more systematic manner, paddocking out the ground as they go, so as once and for all to exhaust its contents. <i>(Aug 26, 1893,Northern Advocate,7</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> [gold mining]

>  Adjoining this track we noticed some heavy workings, 40 to 70 feet deep, all paddocked out, and a little further on something more pleasing to the eye, a nice vegetable garden containing well-grown onions, cabbages, parsnips, celery, and even that well-known luxury carrots. <i>(Mar 27, 1878,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

### paddock sale
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stock sale held on a farm property, where animals are grazing.

>  Due to our vendors selling their property, their total capital dairy herd and replacements, plus machinery, are offered by auction. Never offered for paddock sale. <i>(Apr 14, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,28</i>)

>  Following a heavy period of paddock sales in Poverty Bay, a large array of buying talent ... <i>(May 21, 1942,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  Paddock sales accounted for most of the butchers' lines sold ... <i>(Sep 1, 1949,NZ Farmer,24</i>)

>  Butchers have been obtaining their mutton requirements from paddock sales. <i>(Oct 25, 1951,NZ Farmer,34</i>)

>  With the first major cattle sales coming up on June 1 in the wake of low prices at the last Matawhero cattle sale a month ago, few paddock sales are taking place. <i>(May 26, 1993,NZ Farmer,47</i>)

### paddock sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep grazed on fenced flat and down country [as opposed to hill sheep that are grazed on hills and high country.]

>  By all means feed Leicester and other paddock sheep up to the highest mark, feed Merinoes in the same way if you mean to treat them as you do Leicesters and others. <i>(Jul 13, 1865,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  In store sheep we have sold 4000 station wethers at 7s per head, and several small lines of paddock sheep at current rates. <i>(Jul 30, 1875,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  As to the third, I believe it would be a great gain to the West Coast, but as our Bishop, according to his speeches, is better acquainted with the larger towns and cities than with the country, I may point out that the long woolled paddock sheep require much more care and supervision to prevent them getting "cast" and breaking fences, than the hardy short woolled sheep of the ranges, and therefore we want a young energetic man more than others under different conditions. <i>(Dec 10, 1898,Ellesmere Guardian,3</i>)

>  It is admitted, however, that the wool was in better condition for the November sale and that the special clips that brought the highest prices were paddock sheep, whereas the most of the best halfbred and the other fine crossbred clips offered to-day were from front hill stations. <i>(Dec 10, 1909,Feilding Star,3</i>)

>  I think sheep and wool will be up to last year; run sheep much better. Ewe and paddock sheep not so good. Lice in wool and [dust from] ploughed ground all through Ewe mob. All the paddock sheeps wool must be scoured. <i>(Nov 15, 1889,Chudleigh Diary,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This block, at the time, was carrying two-tooth wethers and they were like paddock sheep. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,170</i>)

>  <b>J D ORMOND</b> Had in the flock of paddock sheep that lambed to the Leicester rams. <i>(1865,JD Ormond Stockbook 1864-1866,18</i>)

>  For some time before shearing the paddock sheep require constant watching. <i>(Oct 1902,NZ Farmer,509</i>)

>  Shearing is getting on well on the farms and in the paddock sheep on the stations. <i>(Nov 7, 1903,Farmers' Advocate,4</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> He looked after more paddock sheep than any man I can remember. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,84</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The hill sheep's performance is better than our paddock sheep. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,109</i>)

>  <b>BROSNAHAN</b> With the development of the downland country came the need for better utilisation of the improved pastures. But hill sheep could not be turned into paddock sheep. <i>(1998,Glenaray 1898-1998,71</i>)

>  The Merinos were not as successful as paddock sheep. <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,140</i>)

>  Paddock-sheep should be yarded and examined previous to shearing, and all dirty and clotted locks removed from the breech... <i>(Nov 25, 1882,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### paddock shepherd
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd who looks after sheep on flats and down country [as opposed to a hill shepherd or hill man who works on larger upland blocks.]

>  WANTED, by a practical Man from the Borders, of long experience in Leicester and other breeds of Sheep, both at Home and in this Colony, Situation as PADDOCK SHEPHERD. <i>(May 29, 1875,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  WANTED. A PADDOCK SHEPHERD. One accustomed to and with a thorough knowledge of Crossbred and Longwooled [sic] Sheep. <i>(Apr 23, 1895,Timaru Herald,1</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Two lucky men were selected as paddock shepherds to stay behind and help at shearing time. <i>Ibid.</i> 29 ...; the paddock shepherds were fattening up and loafing about the shed; ... <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,25</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Joe Ferris was head shepherd and Chris Mccauley and Jimmy Muir paddock shepherds, and Bukkie Cordie, packie. ... Murdock McKay was still boss of the shearing board. <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,42</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Two lucky men were selected as paddock shepherds to stay behind and help at shearing time. 29 The paddock shepherds were fattening up and loafing about the shed. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,25</i>)

>  <b>Stacey</b> Alan Ede was permanent paddock shepherd. [flats man] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,104</i>)

>  WANTED, for station, Head Ploughman 27s 6d to 30s weekly; Married Couples; Paddock Shepherd; Men do milking, gardening, etc.; Ploughmen, 22s 6d; Farm Youths 12s. <i>(Jun 25, 1904,Star, The,6</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He finished his career with the dogs as paddock shepherd with Donald Manson at St Helen's Station. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,55</i>)

>  <b>Newton</b> .. he finally gave up the hill work, but he put in another six years there as paddock shepherd ... <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,214</i>)

### paddock stock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) stock on large stations that are kept on fenced land at lower altitudes rather than on large blocks.

>  <b>MCGILL</b> holding paddock: pensioners' flats or old-age housing settlement; from paddock stock held in on way to slaughterhouse ... <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,57</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> .. until the snow showers stopped it was not safe to even feed out to the paddock stock. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,173</i>)

### paddock system
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [pig-breeding] the process of allowing pigs free run in a paddock for part of the day.

>  The system of pig-keeping known as the 'open air' or 'paddock system' is becoming increasingly popular in many districts of New Zealand. <i>(Aug 19, 1936,Weekly News,83</i>)

>  Those farmers who have adopted the open-air or 'paddock' system of rearing the litters will need to be constantly on the watch for a number of things which can cause losses or severe setbacks in the litters. <i>(Jul 7, 1937,Weekly News,76</i>)

### paddock value
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the value of stock as judged on the farm.

>  At this stage I have generally set the paddock values between 30 and 40 per cent above last year's values on those same bulls. <i>(May 25, 2001,Dominion,25</i>)

### paddock work
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) sheep work carried out by shepherds and dogs in fenced areas [as distinct from hill work in blocks.]

>  <b>HALL</b> The young dog had never been run on the hill before. In paddock work he was excessively enthusiastic. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,83</i>)

### paddock-cred
 
 <i>n.</i> rural credibility

>  These poems are at their weakest when they are either self-consciously striving to establish Turner's black singlet paddock-cred ("cobbers" talk about the "footie" and revile the smooth talkers from "the Big Smoke", etc), or openly lament the metrosexualisation of the New Zealand bloke: "but oh, real Kiwi jokers, Whaddarya / even Valentine's Day has caught on like the pox". <i>(Jun 4, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

### paddock-dry
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to sun-dry flax fibre or wool in paddocks.

>  <b>BREMNER</b> From the scouring tank, wool was pushed down a slide and swished around with pot sticks in perforated zinc washboxes on a floating stage in the Heathcote River, and then paddock-dried. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,49</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> Wool was still paddock-dried. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,74</i>)

### paddock-drying
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the spreading of wool or flax over grass for sun drying.

>  <b>Bremner</b> A variety of drying rooms or sheds supplemented paddock-drying. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,22</i>)

>  <b>Bremner</b> There was no paddock-drying at Begg's - the land was too steep. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,43</i>)

### paddock-grazed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock] allocated an entire paddock's grazing for a short time.

>  Using a heavy silage wagon in the winter to supplement the paddock-grazed ewes had also taken its toll on pastures, some of which were up to 40 years old and of poorer performing species, he said. <i>(Jul 4, 2000,Southland Times,4</i>)

>  The paddock grazed herd got the whole paddock for the whole 5 days, but received their supplementary silage only on days 3,4 and 5. It was suggested that the main reason for this lowered pasture production on the paddock grazed area was cows actually grazing regrowth pasture - they were in fact often observed to do this in days 4 and 5. <i>(Jun 1, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert farming</i>)

### paddock-lot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an allocated number of farm stock in one paddock.

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> To still further eliminate this risk [of blood poisoning] it may be a good thing to dock each paddock-lot of lambs in a separate pen. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,25</i>)

>  <b>MCKENNY</b> On hill country where there are paddock-lots of two or three hundred or more lambs, docking is often delayed till the sheep have finished lambing. <i>(1927,Sheep-Farming, Fencing etc.,27</i>)

### paddocked
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock] enclosed in a paddock.

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> The night is clear and down below the paddocked cattle and horses dwindle into insignificance. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,40</i>)

### paddocker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARR</b> Wages. ... - Stripper keeper and feeder ... Shaker 30s a week ... Paddocker 30s a week to 1s and hour. <i>(1913,Country Work,44</i>)

>  Wanted, for Flaxmill, Nelson district, Fly-boy, Cook, Scutcher, and Paddocker. <i>(May 21, 1907,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Wages and rates of pay - Feeders, 1s per hour . . paddockers, 1s; assistant paddockers 11 1/2d ; .... <i>(Mar 17, 1910,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The Department, in its wisdom, will send a clerk, a butcher, a baker, or a bootmaker away up in the backblocks road-making. Send him anywhere - get rid of him. .. He becomes a jack of all trades, a meandering nuisance, a paddocker, a navvy, a watersider, or a builders' labourer. <i>(Oct 17, 1914,Evening Post,11</i>)

### paddocking
 
 <i>n.</i> working a gold bearing area by excavating a paddock [goldmining] (See [paddock (goldmining)](p-per.md#paddock)))

>  <b>SINCLAIR & HARREX</b> The method used to extract the gold from the gully was 'paddocking'. The top soil was removed off a section of the 'paddock' to expose the auriferous pay dirt. <i>(1978,Looking Back,75</i>)

>  <b>BRACKEN</b> The operations of `paddocking' and `sluicing' <i>(1879,NZ Tourist, The,24</i>)

>  Workings [at Mount Ida, Otago, in 1871] were for the most part alluvial, the distinctive process used being `paddocking'. [fn] Paddocking was the systematic sluicing of large areas of flat ground from which the surface layers were first removed to reveal the `wash-dirt' beneath. <i>(Apr 1953,NZ Geographer,29</i>) [NOTES: (Kibblewhite)]

>  <b>EVANS</b> Then they began `paddocking'. They marked off a strip of land and stripped off the soil down to the wash. ... When all this first area of wash had been put through the sluice box, they began on the second `paddock'. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,15</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> During May, Haast saw miners on the outskirts of Hokitika, `actively at work paddocking at high-water mark, and cradling the wash-dirt of a thickness of 18 inches to two feet on the sea-beach' <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,169,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> More elaborate methods of winning gold include cradling, crevicing, setting up a sluicebox or `Long Tom' ..., sluicing, paddocking, and underwater dredging. <i>(1970,Gold Trails of Otago,81</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> <i>Paddocking:</i> working alluvial gold in a dry region by storing the paydirt in a gully or bin and washing it when water is available; also, digging or sluicing a claim one section at a time methodically. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,113</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Paddocking: A square of ground was dug down to the paydirt layer. The paydirt was either extracted and worked in a pan, cradle or sluice box, or the paddock had one open end and was sluiced. Paddocks were often waterlogged and had to be continually bailed or pumped dry. <i>(1976,Historic Gold Trails of Nelson & Marlborough,88</i>)

>  There are several parties of miners sluicing in the main gully of this basin, who are averaging about 12s (twelve shillings) per day each in shallow sinking or paddocking, the greatest depth not exceeding 3 (three) feet, with plenty of water. <i>(May 10, 1862,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  The ground is of a loose shingly nature, and in paddocking, the sides are continually falling in... <i>(Oct 24, 1862,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  The ground is being worked in the most desultory manner, - here a party paddocking - there a couple of men sinking a hole which they hope, if not swamped out, to be able to bottom... <i>(Apr 12, 1862,Otago Witness,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the drying of flax in paddocks. [flax]

>  Paddocking [of flax] is entirely superceded ... <i>(1906,NZ Farmer,128</i>)

>  .. colour and strength being much weaker as a result of the inability to properly breach the fibre, a defect emphasized by ther holidays interfering with the work of paddocking. <i>(Feb 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,135</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the placing of stock on to fenced paddock areas of a property, usually after being on hill blocks. [farming]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Furthermore, the paddocking and turniping of sheep inclined to make them more difficult to muster. <i>(1949,High Country Days,182</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the systematic digging over of a gumfield in gumdigging. [gumdigging]

>  <b>KEENE</b> In later years, in some areas the more systematic 'paddocking' method of digging was introduced. A team of men worked together, digging over every square yard of gumfield thoroughly, and this method often proved fruitful in an already worked-over field. <i>(1977,To the Northward,123</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) making available a paddock for grazing or for stud purposes. [farming]

>  The reserves of 3 acres are necessary for the use of the linesmen whose duty is to keep the fence in repair, and who require a paddock for their pack-horses, the land throughout the line being chiefly bush with no convenience for paddocking. <i>(Nov 26, 1891,Letter</i>) [NOTES: from Hawke's Bay Rabbit District Board]

>  Paddocking free for four weeks, after which 2s. per week will be charged. <i>(Dec 1, 1880,Hawera & Normanby Star,1</i>)

>  Paddocking presented no worries for the first four days, for he had made his arrangements coming in. <i>(May 1933,NZ Farmer,367</i>)

>  Good paddocking provided at Kokonga at 1s per week. All care, but no responsibility. <i>(Nov 20, 1901,Otago Witness,44</i>)

>  Understood Mr. Cuthbertson to say to Mr. Exley that he was not responsible for the paddocking. <i>(Aug 28, 1902,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the holding and feeding of stock in paddocks [farming]

>  Paddocking free for one month. <i>(Oct 1, 1879,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On arrival, after arranging for a feed and paddocking for his horse George wandered into the hotel bar to enquire about accommodation for himself. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,50</i>)

>  From experience of companies now working (in other countries), it is found that the operations can best be carried on by having the freezing works away from the port of shipment but adjacent to a line of railway, ... and where paddocking can be got for holding the stock until slaughtered. <i>(Dec 21, 1881,Report of Committee on Frozen Meat Trade</i>) [NOTES: 1881 Dec 21 Report of Committee on Frozen Meat Trade in 1957 Macdonald Canterbury Frozen Meat Company 77]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> working a gold bearing area by excavating a paddock [goldmining]

>  <b>MILLER</b> But even at that [sluicing] was going to pay me much better than paddocking. <i>(1946,There Was Gold,74</i>)

>  <b>STALLWORTHY</b> On the higher and drier lands, the here-and-there-pot-holing practice gave place to continuous digging, known as paddocking. <i>(1916,Early Northern Wairoa,54</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the process of storing excavated washdirt [goldmining]

>  There are now eighteen parties paddocking dirt or preparing to paddock, and twenty-three on gold. <i>(Jan 15, 1891,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>EASTWOOD</b> They used a method called dry paddocking, i.e., storing dry material in a heap until they could get water for an hour, and then sluicing it through a box. <i>(1982,Brief History of Gold Mining, A,125</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [wheat]

>  <i>Paddocking.</i> Sacks of headed wheat can stand in the field through a rain if they are stood on the seam end with the ears of the sacks tucked underneath, ... <i>(Jan 25, 1934,Press, The,11</i>) [NOTES: (Acland)]

### paddocking expenses
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) fees for temporary grazing [ usually in a holding or accommodation paddock.] [farming]

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> .. the trip takes over a month, and with wages to be paid and paddocking expenses, plus the food and truck expenses.. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,34</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) pertaining to land and farming activities.

 <i>n.</i> a train

### paea
 
 <i>n.</i> (Marine) a broadbill swordfish

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Paea, ... Xiphius gladius, broadbill, spearfish. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,245</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Paea,</i> (ii) n.<i> Xiphius gladius,swordfish.]</i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,286</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BROADBILL SWORDFISH [<i>Xiphias gladius</i>] ... Swordfish and the Maori paea are alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,54</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BROADBILL SWORDFISH ... <i>Other names: Xiphias gladius;</i> swordfish; paea (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,276</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a turnip
