### paenga
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a boundary. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>ATKINSON</b> Harry and I and Stark went out in the morning and began to fell the first rimu (at the corner of our paenga.) <i>(Jan 20, 1854,Journal,141</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

### paepae
 
 <i>n.</i> (also pae) the threshold beam of a meeting house; a bench to seat orators at the front of a meeting house. [ORIGIN: Maori, The beam forming a bench at the threshold of a meeting house.]

>  The final resting place for Ross is overlooking the paepae, and beneath the olive tree planted by two elders he respected and who have since died (Tui McPadden and Paddy Maguire). <i>(Sep 2005,Wel-com (Wellington),20</i>)

>  "In the old days the paepae had to be exposed and they were the speakers and they were men. ..." <i>(Nov 18, 2005,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  He recounts his early mortification as his elders, including Sir Tipene O'Regan, cast him unceremoniously on to the paepae (orator's bench) in the hectic days after the tribe's historic $170 million settlement with the Crown. <i>(Jul 29, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  It had probably been used when fishing for the koura with a paepae, or dredge-net. <i>(Mar 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  A greeting was exchanged with a senior member of the Parewahawaha tribe who stood at the entrance, then the Queen and her elders stepped over the paepae and led the way into the house. <i>(Jun 1967,Te Ao Hou,59: 34</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> `This looks like the <i>paepae</i> of a large house,' said Mr. Teviotdale, and the luxuriant growth of the tussocks seemed to justify the suggestion that the soil had been fed by human refuse. <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,203</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> ... she nodded in the direction of the paepae where the speakers were sitting . <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,74</i>)

>  TOKO-JONES Stuart Temai 1990-2006 ... A great big thank you to out kai karanga, our koroua on the Pae Pae, na manu Tioriori, and our kaimahi who worked tirelessly at the back. <i>(Jun 16, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,22</i>)

>  The door and window have been put in their usual position, and the carving on the doorposts and paepae are especially good specimens of old-time native craftsmanship. <i>(Nov 28, 1908,Evening Post,9</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

The name, pagoda moss, is suggested by plants which have sent up a stem above the original umbrella to bear aloft a second umbrella. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,1929,67 ed.</i>)

### pah
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pa](p-per.md#pa))

>  <i>Pah</i> (pa.), <i>n.; pl.</i> PAHS (pa.z). [Maori <i>pa</i>.] {Written also<i>a]. 1.</i> A Maori stronghold, fortified with stakes, fences, and ditches. <i>2</i>. A Maori village. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

>  Why is the Maori like a dutiful son?Because he is fond of his <i>pa</i>. <i>(Jan 2, 1861,Taranaki Punch,3</i>)

>  A shoal of upokororo fish was seen in the Waipawa river on Thursday afternoon... and about fifty natives at once came down from the pah to endeavour to catch them. <i>(Jun 25, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> The maori ladies of a small pah here, it seemed, wanted to see me, and I had to wait a little until they had donned their best. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,69</i>)

### pahau-kakapo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> In Pahau-Kakapo (<i>Dawsonia superba</i>), which is confined entirely to New Zealand, it has the tallest moss in the world, attaining ... a height of two feet or more. <i>(1951,Confessions,136</i>)

### pahautea
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [kaikawaka](k-kea.md#kaikawaka))

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pahautea, <i>Col[enso]. Libocedrus Bidwillii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> CEDAR - PAHAUTEA.- (<i>Libocedrus bidwillii</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,13</i>)

>  Pahautea (Otago cedar) .. Librocedrus Bidwillii .. Wood of a reddish colour, straight, and of great durability; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Libocedrus bidwillii</i>, Hook. Pahautea, Cedar.--A handsome conical tree 60-80 feet high, 2-3 feet in diameter. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,123</i>)

>  Cedar, or pahautea, pokaka, and hinau are not unfrequent, while most of the beeches are plentiful, especially in the southern parts of the district. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,115</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The tree [<i>Libocedrus Bidwillii</i>] is often known to bushmen as the <i>Kaikawaka</i>, [68] though Colenso states that its correct name is Pahautea. ... It and the previous species [<i>L. plumosa, Kawaka</i>] are sometimes also called<i> the New Zealand Cedar,</i> but must not be confused with <i>Dysoxylum spectabile,</i> to which this name is often applied. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,66,1940, p. 68 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... the pahautea (<i>Libocedrus Bidwillii</i>), a very light wood, of a red colour, out of which canoes have been made; ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,41</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> [Kawaka] is the commoner species [of <i>Libocedrus</i>] in the North Island as the PAHAUTEA or MOUNTAIN CEDAR is in the South Island, ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,171</i>)

>  [Ruahines] [We] take the track into splendid kaikawaka (pahautea) and mountain beech bush <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,99</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Special mention could be made here of the New Zealand cedar pahautea (<i>Libocedrus bidwillii</i>- sometimes called kaikawaka, although that name should probably be confined to <i>L. plumosa which does not extend this far south.) <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,162</i>)

>  The most valuable trees are the enumerated: .. L Bidwillii, the Cedar or Pahautea... <i>(Mar 22, 1878,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  Already a large English firm is using New Zealand pahautea, kohekohe, and kawakawa, and the top branches of the totara for the manufacture of lead pencils... <i>(Feb 6, 1907,Otago Witness,14</i>)

### Pahia weed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Pahia weed [=] cotula pulchella</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,55</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SEA PERCH ... <i>Other names: Helicolenus papillosus</i>; Jock Stewart, scarpee, highlander, fivefinger, Maori chief, scrodie gurnard (Australia); pahuiakaroa (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,287</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>SCORPION FISH Scorpaena cardinalis </i>(Grandfather hapuku, red rock cod, pahuiakaroa) ... [196] They are basically red in colour ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,195,1984 ed.</i>)

### pai
 
 <i>adj.</i> good; pleasant; willing (See [cf. pie on](per-pop.md#pie-on),[kapai](k-kea.md#kapai))

>  Pai ... <i>a</i>. Good; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,187</i>)

>  <b>BEGG</b> good, pa|i [pai], or ma|i ta|i <i>(1979,Boultbee Journal,284</i>) [NOTES: 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 284]

>  ... but all on the understanding that they were to go each on to their own land when the Waikato was `pai' [fn] willing. <i>(Apr 21, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/37</i>) [NOTES: (H.A. Atkinson)]

>  good - pa|i or ma|i ta|i [pai <i>or</i> maitai] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 112]

### pai kare
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [py korry](../P#py-korry))

### Pai Marire
 
 <i>n.</i> a 19th century semi-religious movement begun by Te Ua. [Maori PAI + marire[a-macron] gentle, appeased] [also Hauhau(ism), Ringatu also used as a cry when attacking] (See [also Hauhau(ism)](h-hea.md#hauhau)),[Ringatu](red-roa.md#ringatu))

>  [Te Ua] sought consolation by praying tohis God, and Atua Pai Marire... Te Uasent out two prophets to the tribes ofthe Eats Coast to establish the PaiMarire doctrine, Kereopa and Patara. <i>(Jan 25, 1933,NZ Tablet,15</i>)

>  Before he was hit, Matene cried, "<i>pai marire, hou, hou!"</i> <i>(May 21, 1864,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

>  This man, Rua, is a Hau Hau, which is a religion, so a Maori told me, based on the prophesies in the Bible, and he also told me that it was also called "Pai Mariri." <i>(May 14, 1906,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  Te Ua made a long speech, chanting waiatas and "Pai marire" songs, the purport being that he was anxious to be at peace and to do no evil. <i>(Jul 2, 1864,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  The contest eventuated about the middle of 1866, near Pungarehu, on the banks of the Waingongoro River, in Southern Taranaki, while the gallant soldier was serving as one of colonel M'Donnell's Bushrangers, who so nobly and strenuously followed the Hau Haus into the fastnesses of the New Zealand bush, and so stamped out the frightful creed of Pai Marire. <i>(Oct 21, 1911,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  Saw the wife of Epiha (one of the surrendered Taranakis) & got some further information about their new religion, the Pai Marire. <i>(Jan 27, 1865,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/148</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>BABBAGE</b> The great moving principle of Pai Marire is that it is a scheme which promises a successful termination of the war with which we are afflicted. <i>(1937,Hauhauism,21</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Mar 25 Annual Report of the Bishop of Waiapu, Dr. William Williams to the Secretary of the CMS in 1937 Babbage Hauhauism 21]

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> It has lately adopted a new feature as far as the natives are concerned namely fanatisism [sic] which is a bad characteristic in an enemy. ... They invented a new religion which they call `Pai Marire' or the good message. They have given up Christianity ... <i>(Mar 1865,Journal</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The new faith was called <i>`Pai marire',</i> but afterwards, from the noise the votaries made in their devotions, resembling the barking of dogs, they acquired the name of <i>Hauhaus. </i> <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,147</i>)

>  <b>MEADE</b> ... We met a small party of [440] mounted natives, who brought news that an apostle of the new <i>Pai Marire,</i> or <i>Te Hau,</i> fanaticism had arrived at Taupo ... They are called <i>Pai Marire</i>, from the words which they subscribe to their signatures and use in greeting or parting from their co-religionists. The words literally mean `good! be appeased'; but the real tendency of the belief is to unite the Kingites of all sects in one common bond of fanatical hostility to the pakehas. <i>Te Hau </i>means only the <i>hau </i>(faith). <i>Ibid</i> 466 ... a host of perverts were chanting <i>Pai Marire </i>hymns round a <i>Hau-hau</i> post of worship, erected close to the late Christian church. <i>(Dec 26, 1870,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Dec 26 Meade Ride Through Disturbed Dists in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 439-440]

>  Down came the Hauhaus, a whole mob of them, armed, yelling "Pai-mariri!" and such-like war-cries. <i>(Jul 20, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  The character in question is a Hauhau or Pai Marire adherent and although many of the faithful from this Wanganui prophetic movement did, in fact, eventually join the Parihaka resistance in Taranaki, 300 or so were captured and exiled to the Chatham Islands where they came under the leadership of Te Kooti, founder of the Ringatu religion. <i>(Dec 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The new religion preached by Te Ua was called Pai Marire. <i>(1983,Illustrated History of Taranaki,47</i>)

>  Many upper river Maori converted in the early 1860s to Hauhau or Pai Marire, a syncretic religion founded by Te Ua Haumene of Taranaki. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  This was.. while the Pai Marire superstition was rife. <i>(Apr 26, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,12</i>)

>  "Pai Marire" was the cry, which may be interpreted "Peace in our days". <i>(May 24, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 2</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> ... Paimarire, the Hau Hau religion, was being fanned into flames ... <i>(1881,Chequered Career,41</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> Several tribes joined in this movement, and the new religion spread. It was called the Pai Marire by its adherents, who are known as Hau-Haus, or How-Hows, for the reason that they pronounce that sound in loud tones during the ceremonial worship or when engaged in battle. <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,239</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The description of the Pai-marire, or Hauhau religion ... contains much that has not previously been recorded. For this ... my thanks go forth to my old warrior friends, both Kawanatanga and Hauhau. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/V</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Pai Marire,</i> or Hauhauism as it was called from the short barking cries of Hau! Hau! that besprinkled the chants, was a mxture of Old Testament borrowings with ancient native paganism and blood-rowsing ritual. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,508</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> But there was a new and intractable fact to be taken into account in Taranaki: <i>Pai marire</i>- the Good and Peaceful Religion. In 1862 the Angel Gabriel had appeared in a vision to Te Ua, a Maori who had fought the Europeans in Taranaki. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,137</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> PAI-MARIRE The Taranaki form of the Hauhau faith. (There was a modified form, even more barbaric, if anything, founded by Te Kooti and rife in East Coast and Urewera Country, known as. <i>Ringa-tu Pai-marire</i>, which revived ritual cannibalism and other revolting practices, strangely enough means `Good and Peaceful.' <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> <i>Pai Marire </i>- `The Good and Peaceful', also known as `Hauhauism' - was a syncretic religion originated by the prophet Te Ua Haumene, of the Taranaki tribe, in 1862. It had messianic and millenarian aspects, and theologically combined traditional Maori religion and the Maori versions of Christianity with Te Ua's own innovations. ... The original form of Pai Marire may have died out as early as 1866, with the death of its founder, but related or similar cults developed around leaders such as Hakaraia, Te Whiti O Rongomai, Titokowaru, Te Kooti, and Tawhiao himself. <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,204</i>)

>  .. and the words "pai marire", which at first were mere adjectives, by constant repetition became a sort of invocation, in which sense they are now used. <i>(May 27, 1865,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  They say Wiremu Tamihana does not approve of the new religion (the Pai Marire); that Henare Ngatata came to Hauraki to bring the new religion, and request the Thames natives to join it. <i>(Dec 17, 1864,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  .. they have also been influenced by this "angel Gabriel" business - a sort of religion they have started, what they call 'pai marire.' <i>(May 14, 1864,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [attrib]

>  He had a lot of influence with the natives, and his "Pai Marire" doctrine spread all over New Zealand, or, at least, the North Island. <i>(Jul 15, 1882,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Te Ua made a long speech, chanting waiatas and "Pai marire" songs, the purport being that he was anxious to be at peace and to do no evil. <i>(Jul 2, 1864,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  The attack on the mission station had nothing whatever to do with Te Kooti; it was the work of the "Hauhaus," or adherents of the then new "Pai Marire" religion, which had already caused the death of the Rev. Mr Volkner, of Opotiki, and other murders. <i>(Dec 22, 1894,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> [Captain Steele] gave me an account of a visit he had paid to a native village to witness the Pai Mairire religion. <i>(Mar 19, 1865,Waikato Journals,48,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>T.S. GRACE</b> As we approached the landing place it was clear to me that we were amongst the Pai Marire fanatics. <i>(Mar 1, 1865,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Mar 1 T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 135]

>  <b>S.A.LEVY</b> Previous to the arrival the natives had erected a very high flag-post ..., and ... the Paimarire flags were hoisted on it. <i>(Mar 25, 1965,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1865 Mar 25 S.A.Levy in Williams East Coast Historical Records 91]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... the other commanded him to go lie down and die, making pai marire signs at him, on which he immediately raised his gun and shot him. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,167</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> In the meantime, the early part of 1866, there were indications of the spread of Pai-marire propaganda in Hawke's Bay. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,85</i>)

>  In the flat open space behind the pa there was a pole (or niu) standing used in their new Pai Marire religion. It was about 10 feet high, square for 2 or 3 feet at top & bottom & rounded in the middle. <i>(Oct 11, 1864,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/125</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson]

>  The survey party came upon a good specimen of the <i>pai mariri</i> superstition. <i>(Mar 2, 1865,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  [The document] is in Te Ua's handwriting, and is interesting as being the earliest known edition of the Pai Marire ritual, probably the earliest extant, for, besides the date (which is 26th October, 1862), there is internal evidence of its being ancient by the absence of the long since universal "<i>hau</i>." <i>(May 27, 1865,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a follower of Pai Marire, Hauhau

>  The fanaticism which believes in the utterance of the word "hau" in a yelping tone, as a powerful means of procuring the favor of Heaven and bringing death on the settlers, is not likely to meet with much that will strengthen it, and after awhile [sic] these "Pai Marire" will be as little heard of as the Lizard Hunters of a year or two ago. <i>(May 17, 1864,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

>  Politically speaking, they are Pai Marires themselves. <i>(Apr 10, 1865,Evening Post,1/53/2</i>) [NOTES: 1870 see 1 above]

>  The Pai Marire, or "followers of the good Mary" as they impiously call themselves, were full to the bung with fanaticism... <i>(May 28, 1864,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  The Bush Rangers started off on a reconnoitering trip .. at the end of which they found a stick with a letter written on flax, warning the party to proceed no farther, as the country beyond was in the possession of the Pai Marire... <i>(Jul 1, 1865,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  The natives at this settlement, most of whom are of the Ngatiwhanaunga tribe, although they are friendly, or at least peaceful, are Pai mariries of the strictest sect. <i>(Jul 16, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### Pai Marire pole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Presently two men on horseback appeared on the Kaipikari road, coming towards one of their Pai Marire poles erected there, to complete the morning's devotions. <i>(Mar 15, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  There is a flagstaff here [Waimate Plains], fenced in and surmounted by the figure of a bird, probably a kawekawea as it was a great <i>niu </i>or Pai Marire pole at one time. <i>(Feb 20, 1867,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/228,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>KELLY</b> There remains a huge paimariri pole, round which renegade Christians danced, and held their superstitious ceremonies. <i>(1892,Journey Through Waitara,11</i>) [NOTES: Narrative Journey Upper Waitara Valley]

>  They rung the Pai Marire bell with tremendous fury, and one or two ascended the niu, or Pai Marire pole, with a success that elicited the rapturous cheers of their shipmates. <i>(Jul 16, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The native villages could be seen through a glass scattered throughout the district, most of them distinguished by the well known <i>pai marire</i> pole, which is now without use, although not long since the centres of frantic dances and demoniacal absurdity. <i>(Nov 15, 1867,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Horomona gave the prisoners the vessel, and the mast was to be taken for a Pai Marire pole. <i>(Mar 24, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

### Pai Marireism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Such being the case, it was all the more surprising to find the Maoris being led astray into the fanaticism which was perpetrated under the name of Pai Marireism. <i>(May 4, 1887,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>P.C.B.</b> That fierce outbreak of fanaticism known as pai marireism. <i>(1866,Two Years Experience of the Maoris,700</i>)

>  We have ascertained, by reliable information, that the Bishop of Waiapu and Archdeacon Leonard Williams still believe that they can see a glimpse of hope for the Tauranga district, notwithstanding the great defection to Pai Maririeism... <i>(May 6, 1865,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  [sub heading] PAI MARIREISM IN THE ASCENDANT. <i>(Apr 7, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  So successful have these attempts [to convert the natives] been elsewhere, that it is not too much to say that in no single district where Pai Marireism has been preached has there been any lack of support fonnd [sic] for it, even amongst those whom we should have imagined least open to its influences." [sic] <i>(Apr 13, 1865,Evening Post,2</i>)

### paikaka
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a potent home brew [common in rural Northland.] [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Paikaka, or homebrew, was very prevalent and very potent. It was made from a plant with sugar and anything they liked added, often pumpkin or maize. It was clear like lager and had a kick like a mule. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,108</i>)

>  <b>HUTT</b> Maori "home brew" was called "paikaka" and was made from maize, potatoes and kumara. <i>(1999,Maori & Alcohol,14</i>) [NOTES: see also Riley <i>Maori Healing & Herbals</i> 1994, p. 18; also Gluckman <i>Tangiwai</i> 1976, p. 226]

### Paikatore
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Nonetheless, there is considerable evidence of the special status of Paikatore, which later became Moutoa Gardens. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### paikea
 
 <i>n.</i> the blackfish, a small whale. [Maori]

>  blackfish - pai|ka [paikea] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,110,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283]

### paikere
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STEWART</b> We fished for paikere, a pretty but tasteless kind of parrotfish. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,104</i>)

### pain in the puku
 
 <i>phr.</i> a pain in the neck

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pain in the puku</i> a bellyache or irritating person; Maori `puku', stomach; eg `That Pom's a pain in the puku.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,81</i>)

### painkiller
 
 <i>n.</i> the proprietary Perry-Davis Painkiller [ca1949 Still sold in Havelock] (See [see also Maori painkiller (horopito)](m-mar.md#maori-painkiller)))

>  Bathe the painful parts with clear "Painkiller" 3 or 4 times a day - and keep warmly wrapped with flannel moistened with "Painkiller." <i>(Jul 8, 1926,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  The evidence showed that the deceased took a dose of poisonous fluid instead of painkiller. <i>(Apr 30, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Another great drink those days was Perry Davis Pain Killer. It was ninety-eight per cent proof spirit.. supposed to be medicine. The miners drank it as booze, mixed up with water. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,24</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> I always purchase myself a box of Holloway's Pills ... and also a bottle of pain-killer; but last shearing they was out o' pain-killer, so they put me up a bottle o' Cain Pepper. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,135</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [Goldigger speaks] `Here's the only liquor I've got [Percy Davis's painkiller] will you have a nip.' ... So we had a pannikin of hot water each with a nobbler of pain-killer in it. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,144</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> For medicinal purposes, Douglas carried `pain-killer' and pills, and I `Eucalyptus', depending on natural medicines for other things. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,182</i>)

>  <b>PRINCE</b> They bought a good supply of provisions, not omitting ... a bottle of Perry Davis' Painkiller, the latter a valuable bush medicine, for it has a healing, stimulating and satisfying effect when taken internally ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,17</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> [a drink made of] logwood, painkiller, fusil oil and treacle. <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,20,1938 ed.</i>)

>  The men held the beasts' heads up in turn, and the skipper poured the painkiller down their throats. <i>(Dec 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,252</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> The men held the beasts' heads up in turn, and the skipper poured the painkiller down their throats. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,14</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> We got 'em and kept 'em in a painkiller bottle for a long time. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,58,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> In the field of medicine, he put his trust in Castor Oil, Epsom Salts, Painkiller ... <i>(1970,Way Up North,6</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Kolynos and Calox, toothbrushes and tea and painkiller and ruby Baxters Lung Preserver. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,200</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> `But not before kindly Mrs Muir, a tattooed Maori lady ... handed me a `painkiller' bottle full of yeast to start mine, which enabled me to make bread more quickly,' Mrs Bird recalled. <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,92</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> These old-timers used to wean themselves on "Painkiller", a patent medicine of the time having a high alcoholic content, and possibly a little morphia. <i>(1965,Open Country,250</i>)

>  PERRY DAVIES [sic] VEGETABLE PAINKILLER an INTERNAL and EXTERNAL remedy for the cure of Weak Stomachs, General Debility, Canker, Bowel Complaints, and Dysentry Painters Colic, Broken Breasts, Liver Complaints, Dyspepsia, Teethache, Cholera. <i>(Apr 5, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>) [NOTES: All punctuation, and lack of, sic.]

>  He ate green fruit and at night had Cholera Morbus. His Mother brought a bottle of PERRY DAVIS' PAINKILLER and by morning he was well. <i>(Jun 3, 1887,Otago Witness,27</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

Mining at Boatman's has declined very much, and work is now limited to intermittent tributers and occasional prospectors. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/92</i>)

### paint-table
 
 <i>n.</i>

### painted apple moth
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) [Australian; but spec. use in NZ.]

>  MAF and the local council can stomp about in search of painted apple moth. <i>(May 3, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  We're infintely more at risk from the painted apple moth than from any gun-toting biped. <i>(Oct 26, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Some of it is to do with the painted apple moth spraying, although that only affects the caterpillars they feed on... <i>(Jan 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  Biosecurity's allocation has fallen by $21 million to $151 million, due to spending in the year 2005-06 year being lifted by "one-off' funding for all responses to disease - namely fall webworm, painted apple moth, didymo, sea squuirt and the southern saltmarsh mosquito. <i>(May 31, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

### painted duck
 
 <i>n.</i> a paradise duck or putangitangi [(obs.)]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We found ... a new tern, one kind of Duck, namely the large <i>painted Duck. </i> <i>(Jun 8, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/298</i>)

>  Ducks of a variety of sorts, ... one, which on account of its varigated plumage we called <i>The Painted Duck</i> was the most beautiful bird I ever saw. [fn] the Paradise Duck Casarca variegata <i>(1773,Cook's Journals,2/786,1961 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Wales Journal in Cook Journal (1961) 2/786 ..]

>  <b>McNAB</b> We shot about 6 [ducks] - the last of which was a Painted one. [2/523 at Dusky Bay] Mr Arms ... shot about a dozen ... among which was four painted ducks. <i>(1908,Historical Records,2/516</i>) [NOTES: 1793 Oct 12 Murry's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/516]

>  Among the [wild fowl] was four painted ducks <i>(Oct 14, 1795,Log</i>) [NOTES: 1795 Oct 14 Log of `Endeavour' (Capt. Brampton) 14th Oct. in 1907 McNab Murihiku]

>  <b>McNAB</b> Cook saw the paradise duck for the first time here [at Dusky Sound], called by him the painted duck. <i>(1907,Murihiku,21</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a butterfly

### painted moki
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DOAK</b> Painted moki <i>Cheilodactylus ephippium </i>(Aust: painted morwong) ... Close relative of the red moki but more strikingly patterned, the painted moki was discovered in northern waters only in recent years. <i>(1972,Fishes,69</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>PAINTED MOKI Cheilodactylus ephippium</i> ...The painted moki is similar in shape and size to the red moki ... It may be distinguished from the red moki ... by its elaborate colour pattern. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,245,1984 ed.</i>)

### painter
 
 <i>n.</i>

### Paintergate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I'm pleased to see that Helen Clark and Labour are campaigning on trust. So let's see - paintergate, corngate, speedgate, Winstongate, foreshore-and-seabedgate ... <i>(Sep 27, 2008,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Paintergate showed that Clark can be cavalier with the truth: she signed a picture that she did not paint. <i>(Aug 31, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  Clark has been denied by paintergate, but has largely been forgiven by the public for her first offence. <i>(Apr 3, 2002,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  [Hdg] Lawyer joins in Paintergate saga. The Auckland lawyer who paid $5000 for Prime Minister Helen Clark's infamous "Paintergate" piece says he never discussed the painting or its purchase with her. <i>(Jul 8, 2002,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  It was one of those loyal-to-a-fault staff.. who burned the painted icon of government sleaze on her barbecue; the Paintergate artwork that Clark did not paint but was happy to sign for political points. <i>(Feb 22, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

>  The Alliance has grown a Siamese head, the Nats are engaged in internecine warfare, Labour is still coping with the embarrassment of 'paintergate", and Act has Rodney Hyde making a dick of himself over a shonky investment conference. <i>(May 19, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  Like Paintergate, it catches her cutting the corners of basic etiquette, and being greatly irked to be pulled up for it. <i>(Apr 26, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### pakaha
 
 <i>n.</i> fluttering shearwater [sp. pakaka (1925 below) an error?]

>  <i>63. Puffinis gavia gavia</i> (Forster, 1844) <i>Fluttering Shearwater (Pakaha)</i> Breeding on many offshore islands from Three Kings to Cook Strait ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,28,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> FLUTTERING SHEARWATER <i>Puffinus gavia Maori name:</i> Pakaha <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,43</i>)

>  <i>Hutton's Shearwater Puffinus huttoni </i>MATHEWS, 1912 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Titi, pakaha, muttonbird. </i>... Hutton's shearwater breeds only on the South Island. <i>Ibid.</i> 100 <i>Fluttering shearwater Puffinus gavia </i>(FORSTER, 1844) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Pakaha, Forster's shearwater</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,99</i>)

(FORSTER'S SHEARWATER.) ... <i>Native name.</i>- Pakahaa: `Rainbird' of the colonists. ... {237} They fly low but swiftly, and utter a note resembling the native name by which the bird is called, but somewhat prolonged, as <i>paka-ha-a - paka-ha-a.</i>} <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,318,1888 2nd edn 2/236-237 ed.</i>)

 <i>adj.</i>

### pakake
 
 <i>n.</i> a seal

>  hair seal - pa'hka|k [pakakae] [check ?kae] <i>(1935,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: also Begg 1979/285]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Pakake (in one place erroneously spelled Pakaka) the hair seal. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]
