### pakapoo
 
 <i>n.</i> a gambling game [various spellings; associated with early Chinese settlers.]

>  Pakapoo, a Chinese game similar to Lotto in which numbers are marked off on a ticket, drew huge number of people to the area. <i>(Jun 5, 2002,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  At the City Court Fee Fook and Tu Tune were fined GBP20 or three months for keeping a gambling house where the game of pakapu was played. <i>(May 17, 1881,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>DON</b> He won 120 [pounds] at a pakapu lottery. <i>(1891,Chinese Mission Work,2</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> They went ... sometimes down to the Chinaman's Place, where fantan, pakapoo and two-up were the sports ... <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,69</i>)

>  Six chinamen.. were charged.. with having been concerned.. in the play or conduct of what the police style "pakapoo" in a house in Haining Street. <i>(Jun 22, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  <b>BODDY & MATTHEWS</b> Usually pakapoo.. is absolutely fair. <i>(1991,Disputed Ground,283</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> "Pakapoo's the game for you to make a few quid quick." <i>(1942,Brown Man's Burden,93</i>) [NOTES: 1973 edition]

>  Pakapoo, which is a game played with 80 Chinese characters. is a permanent favourite. <i>(Nov 11, 1953,NZ Observer,9</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> They went back to the pakapoo shop at seven <i>(1942,Sweet Beulah Land,21</i>)

>  <b>McGILL & TILLY</b> You paid sixpence for a pakapoo ticket, each ticket bearing 80 Chinese characters. Ten had to be mar ked. You won a prize if five or more corresponded with the master ticket. <i>(1980,In Praise of Older Buildings,116</i>)

>  He got pak-a-pu struck out of the Gaming and Lotteries Bill as a lottery. <i>(Sep 3, 1904,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  For most non-Chinese, however, one of the main attractions of the street was pakapoo - which was similar to modern-day Lotto or Keno, but with better odds. <i>(Mar 26, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  The present furore over race broadcasting only accentuates the gulf between real lovers of the turf, and packapoo players. <i>(Sep 24, 1900,Listener (New Zealand Listener),115</i>)

>  Some weeks ago Yah Chang was convicted at Lawrence of being unlawfully a partner in a lottery called "pak-a-pu," by which prizes of money were competed for by lot. <i>(Mar 24, 1877,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  In the case of gambling,the popular game "pakapoo" drew people from all over Wellington and from all walks of life. <i>(Sep 22, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,3</i>)

### pakapoo den
 
 <i>n.</i> a venue for playing the illegal game pakapoo

>  Nothing was said on the subject, beyond a fearful confession by Mr. Bedford that he had been in a pak-a-poo den. <i>(Jul 8, 1905,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  They had been conducting pakapoo dens. <i>(Feb 17, 1938,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  The Wellington police believe that there are from 40 to 60 houses about the Chinese quarter of the Capital being used as pakapoo dens. <i>(Mar 10, 1934,Feilding Star,6</i>)

>  We enter a pakapoo den, as the newspapers call one of these places. <i>(Jan 20, 1937,Tomorrow,3(6) : 184</i>)

>  I had been warned that mysterious Chinese men lived there in pakapoo dens. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Memories,No. 30/26</i>)

### pakapoo ticket
 
 <i>n.</i> a ticket used for the illegal game of pakapoo

>  <b>3</b> A police raid in 1904 uncovered a ton of pakapoo tickets. <i>(Jun 5, 2002,Capital Times</i>)

>  Two bits of "documents printed in a foreign language" were stated to be pakapoo tickets. <i>(Jun 22, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> The ... bawdy-houses of Upper Queen Street ... mingled happily with Chinese grocery-shops, masonic clubs, and pakapo [check ?oo] saloons, all known to the city's then very few moral uplifters as `Chinese Dens'. <i>Ibid.</i> 11 Grey Street remained the sort of place ... where it is possible ... to win [pounds]60 by marking your ten characters correctly on a green sixpenny pakapoo ticket. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,10</i>)

>  In his hand he held a small brush which he used with amazing dexterity, marking pakapoo tickets. <i>(Aug 18, 1938,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  There's the Chinamen who sell pakapoo tickets. <i>(Mar 17, 1937,Tomorrow,3(10) : 311</i>)

>  A serious middle-aged man in short sleeves.. sat at one table with brush and ink for the marking of pakapoo tickets. <i>(1942,Brown Man's Burden,93</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pakapoo ticket, look/marked like a </i>something confusing or incomprehensible; from Chinese lottery ticket with its (to Europeans) incomprehensible markings. Housie-type game played in illegal gambling and opium dens such as those in Haining Street, Wellington; eg `This ruddy exam's marked like a pakapoo ticket!' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,81</i>)

>  His Honor [sic] said pakapoo was an illegal lottery, and the sale of pakapoo tickets was illegal. <i>(May 4, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,7</i>)

>  The evidence of detectives was that the accused was a seller of pak-a-poo tickets and that they had purchased them from him. <i>(Nov 5, 1904,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  Three Chinese were to-day fined GBP25 each for selling pak-a-poo tickets. <i>(May 11, 1908,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### pakaru
 
 <i>adj.</i> ruined; broken [puckeroo. 1960 17C M B8 Wanganui Girls C.I 19 PAKARU,MAKE IT GO PAKARU (NOT SURE OF SPELLING)] (See [puckeroo](pop-pyo.md#puckeroo))

>  This man, when ejected, shook his fist at Lieut. Snow, and threatened, in the jargon that passes current between the two races, to make a "pakaru" of him "tihoa." <i>(Oct 27, 1847,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  Had the bullocks four months; got it from Haera; could not say whether wounds were deep or not; spoke in English to defendant; don't remember accused saying, "bullock was not hurt"; did not not [sic] reply, "you make a pukaru"; accused did not say "its [sic] all gammon." <i>(Aug 1, 1885,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Pukkaroo</i>, adjective and noun, (to make) worthless, useless - it could be used, for instance, of an engine that had broken down - is of dubious origin; ... perhaps from the Maori <i>pakaru, </i>... or conceivably ... an adaptation of `buggered'. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>R. GILBERD</b> So common I thought it was a soldierism for <i>buggered </i>.... English writing <i>`puckeroo'</i> and it is used in Australia as<i> buggeroo. </i> <i>(Feb 15, 1952,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (Northland]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Pak|adu, <i>v. n</i>. Bursting; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,187</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Pakaru - breaking, bursting <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/377</i>)

>  PAKARU. - Broken, smashed. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pakaru</i>. Broken, crushed, smashed: New Zealand military: G.W. Ex Maori <i>pakaru</i> (to destroy), common as N.Z. coll. in late C. 19-20. (F. & Gibbons.) [see addenda and corrigenda] <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,601</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include; ... <i>pakaru</i>: broken, ruined <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> ... Bob related how a row of Maoris would push him off the paths of Wellington's main streets, and with many insulting gestures, tell him to go to Taranaki, where they said he would be pukaru or finished. <i>(1952,Faring South,76</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> WERIHE. Then they come, to visit. But no hui! The hui is pakaru, pakaru. <i>(1969,Awatea,89,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> But there is no doubt that more enjoyment arose from attempting to `puckeroo' a top owned by another player, than from smashing any other object. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,165</i>)

>  .. he said, "Rangi says pukaru;" I said, "The bullock is not dead;" ... <i>(Aug 1, 1885,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to damage; ruin

>  The roar of the musket broke the silence, and immediately the cry arose: "Pakaru! pakaru te pakeha!" ("Smite the stranger!")` <i>(Jul 13, 1904,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  `We will pukeru you!' ` ... pukeru me?' <i>(1885,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/19,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS-check]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> We had begun to pukaroo things, when we broke something, confused an issue, or ruined some plan of action .... derived ... from the Maori <i>pakaru </i>... though many New Zealanders [have thought] that it comes from the verb b----r. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,42</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> [It] is the surest way to buckeroo an axe. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,179</i>) [NOTES: -->1948 Finlayson Tidal Creek 179. (Northland)]

>  As they retreated from our troops into the bush ... they sung out in Maori, `Soldier, aramay; Pukaru the soldier,' accompanied by shouting and yells. <i>(Sep 16, 1863,Journal of William Morgan,85,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>pakaru</i>, ... v.t.<i> break in pieces. </i> the usual anglicisation [k 'ru:] <i>puckeroo</i>, etc, the adjectival use of<i> puckeroo</i> (she's puckeroo, mate) and the meaning, suggests a borrowing from the Maori adjectival form; and the common meaning `ruin', and a common form as pa ptc aj <i>puckerood</i>, has suggested to some a co-derivation from <i>bugger, buggered.</i> Maori<i> `pakaru </i>could be heard as [bg'ru:] `buggeroo', but only <i>buckeroo </i>is recorded: cf, however, Australian <i>bugaroo. </i>spg (early)<i> puikero, pukeru; puckaroo, puckeroo, buckeroo, pukaroo, pukkaroo. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,105</i>)

>  The Mauries swore that we should all be Puckarooed as well as the gun and away they scuttled. <i>(Dec 13, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,267,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARNICOAT</b> Gideon [a native,] forseeing the collision .... shouted out Puikero! puikero! puikero! (broken). <i>(1845,Journal,160</i>)

>  [The Maoris said] `We will <i>pukeru</i> you' `Very well,' I said `Pukeru me.' <i>(1885,Life of Mr Thomas Hancock,19</i>)

>  We remember that when our horses were allowed to stray off the narrow path among the grass how the Wahines seizing an axe, and calling the Pakeha sanguinary names, threatened to "pukaru" the horses. <i>(Mar 12, 1879,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### pakata
 
 <i>n.</i> a flax cloak

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The outside mat that they wear over the shoulder Pagata <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/338</i>)

### Pakatoa
 
 <i>adj.</i> with reference to Pakatoa Island (`the Island') where alcoholics were sent to dry out under the aegis of the Salvation Army.

>  Peripatetic attempts have been made by various sanguine parties to make Dunedin take an interest in itself, but they have had as much effect as a half pint of shandygaff would have on a Pakatoa `reformed' drunk. <i>(Mar 16, 1912,NZ Truth,351/4/6</i>)

### pakaura
 
 <i>n.</i> stingray

>  <i>Raja nasuta</i> Muller and Henle. SKATE, WAEWAE, OR PAKAURA. <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/103</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SHORTTAIL STINGRAY ... <i>Other names: Dasyatis brevicaudatus</i>; stingray, stingaree; whai, pakaurua, oru, roha (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,192</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> STINGRAY, SHORTTAIL <i>Dasyatis brevicaudatus:</i> ... Also known in New Zealand by the Maori name whai, pakaurua, oru and rohu. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,201</i>)

### pake
 
 <i>n.</i> a flax or kiekie cape [1952 Mitchell]

>  <b>BUCK</b> The pake, as we know it, is the roughest type of rain cape with an outer thatch of overlapping free ends to shed the rain. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,160</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> To be in sympathy with this hapu.. Readers would be well-advised .. to imagine themselves bare-limbed, bare-headed, brown, the pake of everyday wear thrown over their shoulders. <i>(1926,Tutira,52</i>)

>  Their Cloathing (both Men [and] Women) is a kind of matt or cloth made of silk grass or fine flax, curiously wove and tied about the shoulder that reaches to their knees ... [they] call it an Ahow, [fn ahu] and over it they have one of a coarser sort made of Grass well thrumbed with the same to keep out the wet which they call a Bugy Bugy. [fn] pakepake often made of undressed flax. <i>(1773,Cook's Journals,2.740</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Furneaux's Narrative in 1961 Cook's Journals 2/740]

>  That night they lay ... covered with their Cloaks or Bugge bugge's. <i>(Feb 24, 1777,Cook's Journals,3/1002</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb 24 Samwell Acct in Cook Journals (1967) 3/1002]

>  ... they add what they call the Bugabuga or Toga. <i>(1783,Cook's Journals,14,1963 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1783 Ledyard's Journal (? Cook Journals) (1963) 14]

>  From [flax] the Chief's elegant and ornamented silky Paipairoa, and the shaggy bee-butt looking Pake and Ngeri, - with their many intermediate kinds of clothing mats, - were alone manufactured. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/33</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,166</i>)

>  <i>pake ... n</i>. coarse mat, used by Maoris as a cloak [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a sacking rain-cape, usually made by punching in one corner to make a peaked hood with the sack covering shoulders and back

>  <i>pake n</i>. a mat or piece of sacking used as a protection from rain. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pake<i>[e-diereis]</i>, n.</i> Maori name for a coarse mat used against the rain. A sack thrown over the shoulders is called by the settlers a <i>Pake. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,337</i>)

### pake-has(n't)
 
 (the <i>has </i>is stressed) <i>n.</i> a Pakeha with (or without) money

>  <b>BAKER</b> The colloquial uses of <i>pake-has</i> (the <i>has</i> being accented), for a wealthy white man, and pake-hasn't, for a penniless white, may be noted. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,16</i>)

>  Among composite forms in which the adjective appears are <i>pre-pakeha times, Journal of the Polynesian Society,</i> 27 (1918), <i>pre-pakeha days,</i> V. Roberts <i>Kohikohinga</i> (1929), etc. and the jocular uses <i>pakehas</i> (the `has' being accented) and <i>pakehasn't, </i>for a rich and penniless white man respectively. (These uses may have occurred in print, but I have not seen them; only heard them.) <i>(1945,Journal of the Polynesian Society,54/228</i>) [NOTES: (Baker)]

### Pakeha
 
 <i>n.</i> a New Zealander of European descent (contrastive with Maori) [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Why are the Pakehas so foolish as to bathe in Whanganui-a-Tara (Wellington Harbour). Is it not known that Whanganui-a-Tara abounds with sharks. <i>(Jan 20, 1876,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  When we were defeated by Rauparaha we looked about for a protector: we invited the pakaha to settle here: the French came, after them the English. <i>(Jan 2, 1869,Timaru Herald,6</i>)

>  You Pakehas burned up the witches with fire until there was not one left alive. <i>(Nov 24, 1890,Star, The,2</i>)

>  Mr Carroll is reported to have advised the natives not to sell their land to the pakehas, as the natives would then become the pakeha's [sic] slaves, but to keep the land and make the pakehas slaves of the natives. <i>(May 10, 1911,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  The pakeha this time had out-taihoa'd the Maori. <i>(Jun 14, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> ... half the board (i.e., half the total shearers) are Maori, and the other half Pakeha (white). <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)

>  I observed that the term `New Zealander' is already employed in this district; only, in lieu of `Noui-Tireni", as the natives of the Bay of Islands pronounce it, they say `Noui-Tirangui' [Nui Tirangi] [sic], which gives the word more of an indigenous sound. The word <i>pakeha</i> serves them to signify all whites, whom they also call `Iouropi' (Europeans). I did not observe that they had a special name for the English. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/139</i>) [NOTES: (Smith transl. D'Urville's Visit to Tolaga Bay in 1827)]

>  This is why the debate of the lowestcommon denominator, couched in terms suchas "social disaster", "utu" and "Pakehabacklash" is irresponsible to say theleast. <i>(Sep 7, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),77</i>)

>  When my lot arrived they crowded the rail of the ship and cried, "What-ho, natives" (or, among the lower orders, like mine, "Wotcher, natives"). The people on the beach, somewhat nonplussed, I expect, needed a name to identify the new arrivals. They chose "pakeha".... These days it is used in government documents, even though there is confusion over just who is Pakeha: are Pacific Islanders Pakeha? Or Malaysian Chinese immigrants? Or Sudanese refugees?... I do not want to be identified as Pakeha, a colonial term that should have died out when the colonial century ended. <i>(Dec 28, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>) [NOTES: letter from Denis Wederell]

>  These white-skinned beings of the imagination [patupaiarehe] were also known as pakepakeha and pakehakeha. These words are the direct antecedents of the word Pakeha. <i>(1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  Baxter was seeking to establish an alternative Pakeha community that could 'learn from the Maori side of the fence'. <i>(2009,Mana</i>)

>  <b>Allen Curnow</b> I am a Pakeha and proud of it, Although I seldom brag aloud of it. ... No less than Kumara or Kauri, Pakeha's perfectly good Maori; <i>(2005,Whim Wham's New Zealand</i>) [NOTES: poem originally published (Press) October 1960]

>  What does it mean to be a Pakeha in Branch Office New Zealand experiencing that same sense of dispossession that our ancestors inflicted upon Maori? <i>(Feb 3, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  The Maories, seeing the greediness with which the "pakehas" hunted after old Moa bones, have long since carefully found, and deposited them in some safe hidingplace. <i>(Jul 8, 1859,Auckland Provincial Gazette,VIII(14) : 94</i>)

>  <b>PAYTON</b> The birds naturally prefer districts where they are not likely to be molested by the <i>pakeha</i> and his <i>tupara</i>. <i>(1888,Round about NZ,315</i>)

>  I am a Pakeha New Zealander, which I can explain and define with certainty. I was born and raised in New Zealand; my ancestors, going back at least five generations, were born and raised in New Zealand. My first ancestors, as far as I can tell, came from England. This is what it means to me to be a Pakeha. <i>(Mar 3, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  Pakeha, the Maori term for the white inhabitants of New Zealand, was in use before 1815, most probably derived from imaginary beings resembling men, or from a sea god. <i>(Nov 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  It was their duty to keep bad pakehas away from the camp. <i>(Aug 6, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,23</i>)

>  When dressed on Sundays, with a Manilla hat and trailing ribbons a la pakeha, he would stand in the yard strutting about and admiring himself. <i>(Oct 1, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  I am Pakeha and aware of my Pakeha-ness. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,114</i>)

>  A tale of Te Pakeha, a race with a rich cultural heritage. <i>(Jul 30, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  My experience of the natives is that they are generally very decent sportsmen, and nearly always insist on giving way to any <i>pakeha</i> (i.e. white man) who comes prepared to fish the pool in the ordinary way. <i>(Mar 20, 1918,Blackwood's Magazine,370</i>)

>  <b>RENWICK</b> &#65279;Na ko te Kuini e hiahia ana kia wakariteate Kawanatanga kia kaua ai nga kino eputa mai ki te tangata Maori ki te Pakehae noho ture kore ana. <i>(1990,Treaty Today, The,23</i>) [NOTES: &#65279;1840 Treaty of Waitangi in 1990 RENWICKThe Treaty Today 23&#65279;Note: The word Pakeha does not occur inthe English text given here.]

>  Sir George [Grey].. addressing himself tome, said "As long as I have been in thecolony, I have never been able todiscover the origin of the term pakeha. <i>(Jan 7, 1905,NZ Herald,Suppl. 1</i>)

>  I asked [an old chief] why his peoplegave the name pakeha to the strangehuman beings they had seen for the firsttime in their lives... He said "He meapakewhakewha." I took the phrase.. tosignify "A thing wafted from the ocean tothe shore." <i>(Jan 7, 1905,NZ Herald,Suppl. 1</i>)

>  In the evening we had been surrounded by many of the natives (as was the usual custom when we rested at a pah,) they had been giving us information respecting different places on our route, singing their wyates or native songs, and indulging in their wonted easy familiarity of intercourse with the pakeha. <i>(Nov 7, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  And after dinner (it will scarcely be believed by those who look upon the New Zealanders as savages) the young and hospitable Chief treated his Pakia guest with a good bottle of Port wine. <i>(May 27, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Te Whiti and Tohu were taken over the Kaiapoi Factory to-day, where their eyes were opened to a still wider extent as to the power of the Pakeha. <i>(May 18, 1882,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Has the time arrived for the removing of legislative distinctions between the Pakeha and the Maori? Mr. Fraser urged that the Maoris should be placed on the same electoral rolls as the Pakeha and allowed to vote for the candidate he thought best. <i>(Jul 17, 1908,Taranaki Herald,8</i>)

>  .. but although it is known to most of the Pakea that the Maories consume large quantities of the juice of the tutu in its unfermented state as a summer beverage, I am not aware of its having been manufactured into Wine. <i>(Oct 22, 1859,Otago Witness,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the English language

>  "The third thing I see is Pakeha being able to speak in pakeha on the marae. ..." <i>(Nov 18, 2005,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

### Pakeha marae
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAXTER</b> `This is a kind of pakeha marae;/ I'm only a lodger - ...'/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,471,1969 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GEBBIE & McGREGOR</b> The bar is the Pakeha marae. <i>(1979,Incredible 8-Ounce Dream,11</i>)

### Pakeha New Zealander
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We're all New Zealanders of course, but we happen to be Pakeha New Zealanders and Maori New Zealanders, not forgetting all the other nationalities here of course. <i>(Jan 1992,North & South,82</i>)

>  I am a Pakeha New Zealander, which I can explain and define with certainty. I was born and raised in New Zealand; my ancestors, going back at least five generations, were born and raised in New Zealand. My first ancestors, as far as I can tell, came from England. This is what it means to me to be a Pakeha. <i>(Mar 3, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### Pakeha time
 
 <i>n.</i> time concerned with punctuality (See [contrast Maori time](m-mar.md#maori-time))

>  All people who live in urban industrial societies have their lives requlated by measured time. This concept of time is referred to by Maoris as `Pakeha time' and they conform to its dictates inasmuch as they are on time for work, to catch buses or to fulfil engagements. However, because they are bicultural, Maoris function in their own time-frame as well, which they refer to as `Maori time'. <i>(Sep 24, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>) [NOTES: [Ranginui Walker]]

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> The hardest part was getting started. No sooner had I cast Don Selwyn from Wellington as the old man, and Thomas Ratima from Napier as Matt, then, as if intent on proving right the stereotype about `Maori unreliability', they both disappeared without trace. They turned up in the end, of course, and, just to prove me prejudiced and wrong, subscribed more rigorously than anyone to `pakeha time'; but meanwhile I was driven to desperation trying to find the other twenty or so Maoris needed to complete the cast. <i>(1980,All My Lives,115</i>)

### Pakeha track
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a trail used by European pioneers. [cf. Maori track]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> Today we know this trail as the Napier-Taupo road and it is an interesting commentary on New Zealand history that such an old Maori trail became a pioneer missionary route, a pakeha track and ultimately a graded road. <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,39</i>)

### Pakeha Wars
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Tiri has been at war with the "Pakeha-goblins" all her life; from the signing of the Treaty of Waitangi, through the 20 years it takes to fall apart - "we called them the Pakeha Wars". <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

### Pakeha-bashing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Iwi/hapu expectations seem driven by opportunism and greed, and another chance to indulge in Pakeha bashing. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Dominion Post,A12</i>)

### Pakeha-Maori
 
 <i>n.</i> (formerly) a European living as a Maori. [in recent years used in a pejorative sense for 'bone people' q.v.]

>  In the Press, and will shortly be published, Price 5s., bound, a new work, entitled, OLD NEW ZEALAND; A TALE. BY A PAKEHA MAORI. <i>(Dec 15, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <i>Death of a Well-known Pakeha-Maori.</i> Mr. C.O. Davis, the well-known Maori scholar died to-day. He has made valuable bequests to the Library and Museum. <i>(Jun 28, 1887,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Adventurers, whalers and sealers, Pakeha Maori were European men and women who came ashore in New Zealand in the early 19th century and took their chances among the local Maori. Much more than the Europeans who arrived after 1840, the Pakeha Maori were integrated into tribes and depended on Maori for their livelihood and survival. <i>(Feb 3, 2004,Dominion Post TV Week,3</i>)

>  Peta Watere.. once dismissed him [sc. a Maori] as a "pakeha Maori". <i>(Jun 1998,Cuisine,No. 22: 8</i>)

>  Pakeha Maori, like the Musket Wars, have not had the dignity of a book before... Of Jacky Marmon, one such escaped convict turned Pakeha Maori, Bentley writes: "He admitted to shooting one of his Maori wives for adultery, feasting on human flesh, and shooting a fellow Pakeha Maori in a dispute over a pig." <i>(Apr 2002,AA Directions,No. 51 (Autumn) : 61</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> The Pakeha Maori must not be confounded with the idlers and beach-combers who loitered about Kororareka. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,I/297</i>)

>  As a Pakeha Maori of no very nice principles has been known to be visiting the district lately, it looks as if his visit had something to do with the changes in the conduct of the natives. <i>(Mar 29, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  It is all very well for a few pakeha Maoris to roll round such words as Waipukurau and descant upon the beauty of the Maori lingo. <i>(Aug 27, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,30</i>)

>  I might have been a Pakeha-Maori to this day. <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas Number : 17</i>)

>  He was a descendant of a famous pakeha-Maori. <i>(Jan 2, 1904,NZ Observer,10</i>)

>  The Pakeha-Maori was the one white inhabitant; he had lived there since the middle Seventies, with his Maori wife. <i>(Mar 1938,NZ Railways Magazine,9</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> There was the odd Pakeha-Maori, and I don't know what their position was exactly, but they seemed to be tolerated by the Maoris to give it a go, dig gum and sort of live off the land. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,132</i>)

>  The cumbersome machinery of the Native Land Court had to be brought to bear on the several blocks... the Natives being fleeced by the lawyers, interpreters, Pakeha-Maoris, et hoc genus omne. on the one hand, and by the publicans on the other. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  [Photo caption] JOHN BULL'S HEAD, TAIERI RIVER. So called after a well-known Pakeha Maori who lived near this spot for many years. <i>(Feb 19, 1902,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  <b>SANDYS</b> [Wiri] had lived too much of his life as a "pakeha-Maori". <i>(1982,Love and War,104</i>) [NOTES: 1989 edition]



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The man who takes up the Native's case is a low-class practitioner - a pakeha-Maori lawyer - because he exposes the honorable gentleman's transactions to view. <i>(1877,NZ Parliamentary Debates,XXV/316</i>)

>  A Pakeha-Maori agitator, He will soon be a cooked pertater. <i>(Nov 8, 1890,NZ Observer,7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a way of speaking that accommodates characteristics of both Maori and English speakers. [ORIGIN: Maori, Used primarily before revitalisation of te reo]

>  <b>Stella Morice</b> The meaning of words is as correct as possible, but the pronunciation is just the pakeha-Maori which most of us use. <i>(1944,Book of Wiremu, The,50</i>)

>  Maori is a very beautiful language; and English is not a bad one as languages go; but that delightful 'patois' called Pakeha-Maori, which is the vulgar tongue among half-castes, loafers, missionaries, Maori doctors, and other kinds of vagabonds in the North Island, goes beyond either of its component roots for breadth of expressions, and euphony of sound. In Maori again no two consonants occur together, so that in using English words in Pakeha-Maori, a vowel must always be stuck in between consonants where they happen to meet. <i>(May 5, 1875,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

### Pakeha-Maori-ship
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... so, leaving the insignia of my <i>Pakeha-Maori</i>-ship in the care of Bolt's wife, and borrowing a jacket and pair of trowsers, I prepared for a final start in the morning. <i>(1866,Chatham Islands,21</i>)

### Pakeha-Maoridom
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> We had got news from that late seat of kingly power, Herekino; it had lapsed into its primitive state of Pakeha-Maoridom, and Waipeha was no longer a king, but only Taniwha's white man, married to his daughter. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk4/6/289</i>)

>  The quarrel is between Mr. M'Lean, the Native Minister, and Mr. James Mackay, jun., who at one time was a Native Commissioner and versed in all the secrets of Pakeha-Maoridom, but retired from the service of the Government a considerable time ago, finding more profitable occupation as a Native Land Agent. <i>(Apr 13, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

### Pakeha-ness
 
 <i>n.</i> the nature of being European in New Zealand.

>  I am Pakeha and aware of my Pakeha-ness. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,114</i>)

>  My wife and I haven't stopped laughing since it was revealed on TV last week that Hone Harawira's orginial surname was Hadfield and was changed by deed poll to hide the family's Pakeha-ness. <i>(Aug 25, 2010,Wairarapa News,11</i>)

### Pakeha-style
 
 <i>adv.</i>

>  I see someone has been trying to hoax the Auckland and other intending visitors to Tawhio's meeting through the <i>Herald</i>, which states that the Europeans will be put up and feasted in pakeha style. <i>(Feb 28, 1882,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  IT was generally conceded that the pakeha style of swimming is superior to the Rarotongan; but the young ladies were loud in their praises of the physical proportions of the copper-coloured strangers. <i>(Feb 8, 1890,Observer (Auckland),8</i>)

>  The bride was dressed and adorned in the orthodox pakeha style, in a dress of white silk, with the usual veil and bouquet, and truth to say looked exceedingy well. <i>(Jan 12, 1907,Observer (Auckland),8</i>)

>  The license was taken out in the correct pakeha style, and the youthful couple made one at the Putiki church, the ceremony being performed by the Rev B.K. Taylor. <i>(Jul 11, 1872,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

>  ... mussels and eel which you cooked pakeha-style with garlic and butter ... <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,9</i>) [NOTES: (Keogh, Chiaoscuro)]

>  In most of the pas visited new houses were being built after the pakeha style. <i>(Nov 16, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

### Pakeha-trained
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... he was one of the few collectors of Maori lore ... who got their knowledge direct from the Maori, before it ... was subtly changed by the younger men with pakeha-trained minds. <i>(1959,Early Travellers,350</i>)

### Pakehadom
 
 <i>n.</i> (contrastive with Maoridom)

>  But if the Maori will pursue the trades of the pakeha his end will be that he will be trodden under foot, his mana will depart, he will no longer be heard of, he will disappear to rise no more in the whirlpool of pakehadom. <i>(Aug 5, 1908,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  After complaints to the UN and hikoi have failed, nothing would put the wind up Pakehadom faster than a whiff of Islamic-inspired terror. <i>(Dec 4, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  How a project ... with the blessing ... of all the tribes, including some but not all the tangata whenua Ngai Tahu, plus Pakeha-dom ... could have become so unstuck ... is a complex tale of human foibles. <i>(Mar 5, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Pakehadom, as controllers of media, choose and create the so-called spokespersons for Maoridom. <i>(Oct 23, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A12</i>)

>  "I've never heard them say that anything a white man does affects pakehadom. It's just f******bullshit. I don't buy into it." <i>(Jul 5, 2000,Evening Post,23</i>)

>  The attack from Mr Prebble may indeed be political but has nothing to do with slurring Maoridom, any more than those who blew the whistle on Morgan Fahey were racially motivated and attacking "Pakehadom" (t coin a word). <i>(Jul 6, 2000,Dominion,9</i>)

### Pakehafied
 
 <i>adj.</i> (contrastive with Maorified) (See [also Pakehafy verb](p-per.md#pakehafy))

>  <b>Liu , James et al</b> .. I was really Pakehafied if you get it .. What does it mean Pakehafied? ..like I didn't go to Kohanga, I went to a kindy. <i>(2005,NZ Identities: Departures & Destinations,196</i>)

>  Nowadays, they [sc. Atiu Islanders] are all quite "pakehafied" as to religion. <i>(Feb 10, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  <b>MEIKLE in GIFKIN</b> All Pakeha Taurangians were superior to all Maori; even an educated 'Pakeha-fied' Maori would end by going 'back to the mat'. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,128</i>)

>  On the other hand, he is interested in a return to the spontaneous actions of the classical period in favour of the Pakehafied synchronisation that has overtaken action songs since the last century. <i>(Dec 3, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  But even though I've pretty much been brought up Pakeha-fied, when I get together with a group of Maori I feel a special bond. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 14 : 28</i>)

>  If his blackness has been whitened, his Maoriness pakehafied, then his story is another tragedy, ... <i>(1985,Landfall,156/491</i>)

### Pakehafy
 
 <i>v.</i> [woman or women in citation? And Maori not maori presumably] (See [also Pakehafied adjective](p-per.md#pakehafied))

>  Instead an [sic] attempt to liberate woman, any outside comment can be seen as pressure to "Pakeha-fy" maori further. <i>(Feb 4, 2006,NZ Herald,Cancas p22</i>)

### Pakehaland
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Where's this place called Pakehaland?... There is no Pakehaland that pakeha come from. It encompasses all the rest of the world! <i>(Nov 11, 2000,Dominion,4</i>)

### Pakehaness
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... but you can't bring your pakehaness into my home unless I say so and I won't! This is a Maori home ... bringing up his children to be Maoris and not half-pie imitation pakehas. <i>Ibid.</i> 73 What, after all, was wrong? It was his Pakehaness. ... What is wrong with the Negroes is their Negroness. What is wrong with the Chinese is their Chineseness. What is wrong with me is my Maoriness. <i>(1969,Green River,56</i>)

### Pakehatanga
 
 <i>n.</i> (contrastive with Maoritanga)

>  `Format of First Year Courses' Introduction to Pakehatanga (in this I include such things as classical mythologies, Bible stories, cultural institutions, etc., ...) <i>(May 14, 1986,Ephemera</i>) [NOTES: 1986 May 14 Victoria University Faculties of Arts & Language and Literature]

>  In fact, exploring our Pakehatanga can be a very rewarding experience. <i>(Jun 12, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,10</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pakehatanga</i> emerging word for cultural attitudes and origins of European and/or Pakeha New Zealanders. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,82</i>)

>  <b>Bell</b> A new word was coined: Pakehatanga. <i>(1996,Inventing New Zealand,26</i>)

### pakeke
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) New Zealand sea lion [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Surat Bay on the Catlins coast is now the most accessible site for viewing the New Zealand sea lion/pakeke, making it a very special place indeed. During Conservation Week 2007 interpretation panels will be unveiled that trace the return of pakeke and explain how visitors should behave around them. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,13</i>)

### pakepake
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pake n](p-per.md#pake))

### pakerikeri
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WEDDE</b> Heberley dropping his line forlornly down to where the little pakerikeri can polish the hooks for him. <i>(1986,Symme's Hole,95</i>)

### paketi
 
 <i>n.</i> a spotty

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SPOTTY [<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>] ... Other names include paketi, kelpie, guffy, butterfish and pakirikiri. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,22</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SPOTTY ... <i>Other names: Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>; butterfish, kelpie, guffy. None is widely used. Paketi, pakirikiri (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,257</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> PAKETI <i>see Spotty </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 207 SPOTTY The common little Spotty is known to every boy or girl who has even gone fishing along the tide line. ... Other names are kelpie, guffy, butterfish, pakirikiri and paketi (Maori). Gets its most common name from a large, black spot on its upper side. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,174</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>SPOTTY </i>(Paketi) <i>Pseudolabrus celidotus </i>... The spotty is a moderate sized wrasse that reaches a maximum length as an adult male of about 26cm ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,254,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HALEY</b> `You're still talking about paketi?' [Pseudolabrus celidotus] <i>(1986,Settlement, The,121</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Spotty Pseudolabrus celidotus </i>Alternative names include paketi, kelpie, and butterfish (the last two being misleading). Restricted to New Zealand ... The girdled wrasse, P. cinctus, is rather similar to the spotty but grows larger ... It occurs around the South Island but is not common. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,107</i>)

>  Well out into the [Auckland] harbour was the `paketi pond', where children could easily catch `paketi', as we called the fish usually called `spotties' or, by the Maori, pa[macron]kirikiri. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>) [NOTES: Sinclair `At the Point']

### paki
 
 <i>n.</i> a garment

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Pakii [a-acute],<i>s</i>. A garment for a man; breeches: name of a person. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,188</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> pakihi q.v. [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  Pakihi has in the South Island been borrowed from the Maori for a patch of country naturally clear of bush and is applied particularly to the poor wet land, unsuitable for development, on the West Coast. Haast wrote of 'pakis or open tracts of land.' in Nelson. As pakihis are frequently surrounded by bush, a bald spot on one's head may be called a pakihi, but that is slang. <i>(Sep 1959,Tararua,13/46</i>)

### paki/pakihi
 
 <i>n.</i> a bald patch [after pakihi, a clearing in the bush]. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>MASON</b> Paki [pa:ki:] Heard in Christchurch for a bald spot on the head from a man who was often on the West coast in the course of his work. <i>(1949,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> An indigenous one is <i>pakihi</i>, a natural clearing in the bush ... on the West Coast. It has been facetiously applied to a man's bald patch, but the word in its basic meaning is not very common and this transferred sense even less so. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,161</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> paki/pakihi bald patch on a man's head, from Maori word 'pakihi' for a natural clearing in the bush. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,82</i>)

### pakihi
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large, low-lying area of leached soils with scanty natural growth of scrub, fern and rushes.

>  On these plateaux are numerous 'pakihis' or natural clearings which are mostly extensive tracts of swampy lands .. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,21</i>)

>  This type of country [sour and swampy] is usually known locally as pakihi land or 'the pakihis.' <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,22</i>)

>  Of the many good works which will be remembered to his credit, not the least will be his recognition of the possibilities lying dormant in the Collingwood pakihi lands. 'Pakihi' is a Maori word signifying an opening or clearing free from forest. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,295</i>)

>  The Collingwood pakihis are of limited extent, probably within 10,000 acres. 300 Thus the three dominant plants of the pakihi are the manuka, the Gleichenia fern and the sedge. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,297</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> pakihi: as it also a Maori word for barren land, we have adopted the word to refer to a certain Westland-type soil that has been leached by constant heavy rain and is difficult to bring in to good pasture. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,59</i>)

>  The pakihi seems to need further capital fertiliser after the fourth year, otherwise it starts reverting to yorkshire fog, lotus, native grasses and fine-stemmed pakihi rushes. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,26</i>)

>  The home farm has 50 ha of alluvial ground and 80 ha of pakihi ... This farm is all pakihi but carried 217 cows to produce 25,748 kg last season. <i>(Feb 1990,NZ Dairy Exporter,4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a barren area of land.

>  <b>STEVENSON</b> .. pakihi was used to describe any tussock covered flat ground. <i>(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,15</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> pakihi: a desert or barren land. <i>(1995,Reed Dictionary of Modern Maori,198</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in English usage chiefly South Island, with two main applications: (a) West Coast, usually connoting heavy, swampy (originally bush) land; -->(b) open grass or scrub tract, often in bush country, or above the bush line.

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Pakii kura[u-acute]; Red land where fern root has been collected: name of a person. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,188</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> We now advanced along the plain [towards Moeraki], which was covered with `patiti' and ... stunted fern and `tutu'. These plains are called `pakihi,' a word used in the North Island to signify dried up. So that `tupakihi,' the name by which the `tutu' is there known, appears to be a contraction of `tutupakihi,' or `tutu' of the [196] plains, which are well called `pakihi,' being for the most part arid. <i>Ibid.</i> 311 PAKIHI, open country, bare of trees, covered with coarse wiry grass, stunted fern, and low shrubs. It does not necessarily signify `a plain'. The `a' is sounded long and broad in this word, by which it is distinguished from `pakihi,' to be low water, or to drain off as a river after a flood, in which the `a' has a sound both short and soft. `Kua pakihi te wai o Waitaki,' the water of Waitahi has become low. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,195</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 9 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 195-196]

>  <b>MONEY</b> In the afternoon we suddenly came out of the bush upon an open pakihi some miles in length, covered with short fern and rushes. <i>Ibid.</i> 70 ... we took a contract ... to clear a road twelve feet wide across a `Parkihi' or open piece of country, chiefly covered with fern and tea-tree (manuka) scrub, for a distance of three miles. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,63</i>)

>  The Wakamarama Range [Nelson] is for the most part densely forested, though there are patches of open <i>pakihi</i> on its summit. [fn] <i>Pakihi </i>(bare) is a Maori term applied to flat, open spaces. <i>Pakihis</i> are generally swampy. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 3. (N.S.) 24</i>) [NOTES: (Bell)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... little pakihis ran up into the bush every here and there <i>(1947,Wayleggo,110</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The pakihi was broken by a series of old watercourses. ... [97]. `Top Pakihis', a narrow bush-bound stretch of flats far up the river. <i>(1949,High Country Days,91, 97</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Then there are two well-known and often disputed words, Parkee and Wareleggo. Parkee is very expressive. It is used of an open space partly surrounded by the dark green birch bush whose compact and shapely foliage suggested to the early settlers the ordered parklands to which they were accustomed. A clearing completely surrounded by bush will be called a `blind Parkee'. <i>(1951,High Country,43</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Pakihi</i>: Open clearing partly surrounded by bush. Blind Pakihi - totally surrounded by bush. Maori word anglicized to `Parkee'. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,124</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Pakihi</i>: A clearing in the bush, perhaps only a bay. If totally enclosed, a `blind' pakihi. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,255</i>) [NOTES: [Cby high contry]]

>  <b>WILLIAM MARTIN</b> crossed Parke plain by 4-35 the only open country I've seen these 4 months <i>(Oct 7, 1863,Diary</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Pakihi</i>: Maori word for open space in bush country. `Blind' pakihi is completely surrounded by bush. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <i>pakihi n. NZ </i>area of open swampy land (<i>the West Coast pakihi plantings</i>). [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,539</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> [Also (misspelt?) pakahi, see Geological Society citation 1981.]

>  Travelling at over 100 knots, the machine flies parallel to the Tasman Sea beaches and substantial wetlands dotted with pakihi, lagoons and lakes. <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  [Gleichenia] is soon overpowered by the [bracken], and the entire area is quickly covered with a luxuriant growth of `aruhe,' thus affording a suggestion as to the way in which the wide fern-clad `pakihis' were originally formed ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/15</i>)

>  On the lowland fringes of the range ... there are considerable tracts of boggy treeless land called pakihis (locally `parkees'). <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,161</i>)

>  The <i>pakihi</i> was accessible only by a very precipitous route up the face of the cliff. <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/338</i>)

>  The pakihis ... are open swampy plains formed mostly by the action of the sea, and having an impervious substratum of cemented gravel where the rain-water accumulates and is held as in a sponge ... <i>(1907,TrNZI,3/385</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> We suddenly came out of the bush onto an open pakihi some miles in length. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,63</i>)

>  A patch of rata bush on the flat, the rest being partly open `pakihi' and partly covered with low scrub and timber. <i>(1896,NZ Alpine Journal,2/9/148</i>)

>  Easterfield, Rigg and Bruce (Cawthron Institute) were uncertain of the relationship of the Pakahi soil pan, referring to the pan below the pakahi soil. <i>(Oct 1981,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,54/47</i>) [NOTES: Assuming that pakahi should be pakihi - after notes with TD. Jan]

>  <b>HAAST</b> I had been assured repeatedly that Ishould be able to obtain new provisionsat the so-called Pakihi, a large forestmeadow near Lake Brunner... <i>Ibid.</i> 82After a mile or two we came out of thehigh forest and the pakihi or paddock laybefore us. This is the name given by thediggers to a grass-covered plain betweenone and two miles broad, bordered on bothsides by thickly wooded mountains. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,81</i>)

>  <b>FARRELL</b> Mary Agnes's older brother Leo played thejovial Irishman, yarning and crackingwith the men in the bar, while in privatedespising them for the bogtrotters theywere, the feckless idle Paddies of thejokes and the skits, content to root abare living from the sour coastal pakihi,exactly as they might have done at home. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,54</i>)

>  They soon struck pakihi (the iron pan) a few feet below the topsoil and, disgruntled, moved up the valley south of the Karamea River where they cleared the forest and brought into being "the promised land." <i>(2000,Directions,Summer 2000 No. 46 : 31</i>)

>  <i>Pakihi</i> has in the South Island been borrowed from the Maori for a patch of country naturally clear of bush and is applied particularly to the poor wet land, unsuitable for development, on the West Coast. Haast wrote of 'pakis or open tracts of land.' in Nelson. As pakihis are frequently surrounded by bush, a bald spot on one's head may be called a pakihi, but that is slang. <i>(Sep 1959,65 Years of Guides,13/46</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> <i>Pakihi</i>, open country, bare of trees, covered with coarse wiry grass, stunted fern, and low shrubs. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,311</i>)

>  These 'pakihis" are valueless for agriculture. Absolutely so. <i>(1896,AJHR,D.-4/210</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Pakihis : Clearings, or bays in the bush. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,188</i>)

>  North Islanders know of "pakihi" landonly by name. <i>(Dec 3, 1947,NZ Observer,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a scrubby growth

>  This area was previously logged and has regenerated as pakihi and tussock, due to the infertile nature of the soil... <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,108</i>)

>  <b>Wright</b> .. some extremely infertile or poorly drained sites have reverted to pakihi (stunted scrub such as manuka, bracken and gorse). <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,124</i>)

>  <b>Slatter</b> Bush dripping in the rain and green swamp-water, a tangle of blackberry or pakihi beside the twisting railway line. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,76</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> Geol. definition [should this be pakihi? no such word as pakahi in dicts TD Thanks; checked that this is as in source but surely meant to pakihi. JB. 2012 Oct now moving to pakihi wiht note. JB]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) (attrib.) of pakihi land or swamp areas. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Waterlogged counterparts of these soils, the <i>grey podzols</i> or `pakihi soils', cover 3/4 million acres ... in Westland ... <i>(1959,Descriptive Atlas,30</i>)

>  In March 1906, the first pakihi soil was analysed by Mr Aston. <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,22</i>)

>  The pakihi lands of the West Coast of the South Island were described by me in the first number. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,293</i>)

>  Pot experiments with Collingwood pakihi soils. Mr Macpherson states "that inspection of other pakihi lands around Westport shows that they do not present the same diffcult proposition as that met within the Buller Domain ..." <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,297</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> .. very poor soils are formed called strongly leached podzols, such as are present in the gumland soils of Northland and in the pakihi soils of Westland. <i>(1961,Agriculture,133</i>)

>  .. big responses to inocculation of clover seed have been obtained in field plots and large-scale sowings on the Northland gumland, on pumice soils of the central North Island plateau, and on the pakihi problem soils of Golden Bay and the West Coast. <i>(Feb 1966,NZ Journal of Agriculture,42</i>)

>  <b>MCCONOCHIE</b> The fact of their coming from poor pakihi land lent an added interest to my observing their future development ... <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,193</i>)

>  The pakihi seems to need further capital fertiliser after the fourth year, otherwise it starts reverting to yorkshire fog, lotus, native grasses and fine-stemmed pakihi rushes. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,26</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> as distinct from bushed land

>  <b>HAAST</b> After travelling a mile through dense bush, ... we arrived at an open tract of swampy land, a <i>paki,</i> as the natives call it, covered with rushes, swamp grasses, and ... with manuka and ferns ... <i>Ibid.</i> 131I shall now enumerate the different pakis, or open tracts of land, and give a short description of them. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,53</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Their wartime job was to drain ... the stunted manuka swamps Bill calls parkee (pakihis). It was once, he said, a great area for red pine, rimu, and the black <i>(1985,G'day Country,156</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> The supplies .... had been packed by horse along a track cut out of the bush, and further, up towards the Ridge, the pakahi. <i>(1930,Bushman Burke,14</i>)

>  Large areas of marshy lands on the west coast of the South Island of New Zealand are known locally as `pakihi' lands, or the `pakihis'. <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/231</i>) [NOTES: (Easterfield)]

>  [w. Nelson/Westland] Much of the terrace land consists of `pakihi' - barren treeless moorland with an undrained, sour, peaty soil, deficient in lime and phosphates, and resting on hard ferruginous gravel. <i>Ibid.</i> 807 23. Aston, B.C.: The Pakihi Soils of Westland. New Zealand Journal of Agriculture (1910), Vol. i., pp. 22-27. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,61</i>)

>  Gold was found in Whiskey Creek, which drains the pakihi, and flows in an east-westerly direction before it enters a steep-walled gorge. <i>(1938,NZ Geol. Memoir,4/45</i>) [NOTES: Modriniak and Marsden]

>  In the Grey and Inangaluae valleys, on the Westport flats and in the Collingwood district, .... there are large areas of swampy pakihi, underlain by clay or iron pans, and for these no profitable method of farming has yet been devised. <i>(1937,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,19(2)/74</i>) [NOTES: (Henderson)]

>  Instead the pakihi (a very general term used for most srcub or waste land on the West Coast, from the Maori for `open space') has been selectively logged for its large native trees.... <i>(May 12, 1983,Press, The,17</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 387 Pakihi = a term for a swampy acidic barren type of land <i>(1983,Bone People, The,469</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> The progression from bog to bog forest has been studied in South Westland where island-like patches of bog, known locally as pakihi, are scattered in forest on the lowland gravel plain. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,120</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a clearing in bush [either open or blind.]

>  A little above, on the edge of a bleak pakihi stood dilapidated houses for the mine staff. <i>(Oct 1980,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,50/75</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... we suddenly came out of the bush upon an open pakihi some miles in length, covered with short fern and rushes. 1910 J of Ag June 15. 22 'Pakihi' is a Maori word signifying an opening or clearing free from forest. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,63</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> These miles of hidden pakihis somehow seemed to have some special attraction. <i>(1952,High Country Days,138</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> .. keeping as he judged parallel with the edge of the bush and aiming to hit the blind pakihi about the middle. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,53</i>)

>  <b>MCCONOCHIE</b> .. there, standing in an open pakihi a hundred yards distant, was a magnificent beast having the appearance of a cross between a wapiti and red deer. <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,127</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> This is an open pakihi on the watershed of Andrews Stream. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,59</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> a blind pakihi - a clearing in the bush which is totally enclosed. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,255</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [?esp. pakihi land (see also fern, rush)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... extensive flats of open `pakihi' land, in the birch forest, ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,220</i>)

>  Though much of the <i>pakihi</i> country near Rockville is wet, there are almost no true swamps actually within the subdivision. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.3. (N.S.) 31</i>) [NOTES: (Bell)]

>  The vegntation consists of the usual stunted `pakihi' growth of fern and rushes, ... <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,12/241</i>) [NOTES: (Penseler)]

>  EXPERIMENTS ON THE PAKIHI SOILS OF WESTLAND. ... `Pakihi' is a Maori word signifying an opening or clearing free from forest. There are in Westland large areas of sour, swampy, terrace land which supports a flora such as rushes, fern, moss, and plants one usually associates with sour, water-sodden soils. ... This type of country is usually known locally as pakihi land, or `the pakihis'. <i>(Aug 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,126</i>)

>  <b>WASHBOURNE</b> With the exception of the pakihi terraces all the country [Nelson-Takaka] was covered with bush. <i>(1933,Reminiscences of Early Days,4</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> The Otira Tunnel through the mountains now gives [the West Coaster] a new way of escape from his rain and his parkihi, waterlogged land. <i>(1944,Understanding NZ,243</i>)

>  Westport is surrounded by worthless, sour, marshy pakihi lands rather like the Russian podsol. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,103</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Drainage is often impeded by an ironstone pan, and what are known as <i>pakihi</i> areas are formed, these being wet, boggy areas dominated by sphagnum moss. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,38,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>pakihi</i> The Maori word for open grassland, now meaning glades of open strips of grass in bush country. (Pronounce <i>pah-kee|</i>). <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),288</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Japan again. And hippies. They first harvested it [sphagnum moss], because it was there, growing naturally in the wet paddocks. Doug calls it paki country, pakihis or dryish river terraces. <i>(1985,G'day Country,149</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> [caption] Aerial view of the transition from pakihi bog through silver pine (<i>Lagarostrobos colensoi</i>)- dominated forest to rimu ...-dominated forest ... South Westland. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,118</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> "He carted a ladder across a pakihi swamp?" snorted Doddy. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,141</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HEINZ</b> One of the favourite amusements of the diggers at Pakihi diggings [Charleston] on the day when the store steamer arrives at Constance Bay from Nelson, ... <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,35</i>) [NOTES: 1869 Dilke Greater Britain in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 35]

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> We travelled to ... the Grey, and back to the Buller, calling at Charleston and Waite's Pakihi's; all familiar diggings to any west-coast digger, and very rowdy diggings too. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,76</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAAST</b> Most of [the gold-seekers] ... begged me to leave them flour enough for at least one day, which I did, ... as I had been assured ... that I should be able to obtain new provisions at the so-called Pakihi, a large forest meadow near Lake Brunner. <i>Ibid.</i> 82 After a mile and a half we came out of the high forest and the pakihi or paddock lay before us. This is the name given by the diggers to a grass-covered plain between one and two miles broad, bordered on both sides by thickly wooded mountains. This place, generally so desolate, was now highly animated. Cattle and horses were grazing peacefully in all directions. Numbers of tents were put up near the forest for the accommodation of gold-diggers, cattle-drivers, and storekeepers. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,81</i>)

### pakihi bog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a swampy area, of marginal value.

>  Much of this potential [86,000 acres] lies in the 46,000 acres of marginal land, most of which is pakihi bog .. If the pakihi bogs are to be developed, it would seem inevitable that the Government should take the lead. <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Journal of Agriculture,41</i>)

### pakihi country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that is low-lying and swampy, with scant vegetation.

>  The major portion of the pakihi country is still Crown land. <i>(Aug 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,126</i>)

>  The major portion of the pakihi country is still Crown land. <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,22</i>)

>  These terraces are often referred to as semi-pakihi though they're not nearly as difficult as the real pakihi country further north... <i>(Feb 22, 1968,NZ Farmer,5</i>)

### pakihi fern
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) fern that grows on boggy and otherwise barren swamp lands.

>  <i>pakihi fern [=] swamp umbrella fern [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,55</i>)

>  The natural cover of pakihi fern and wiwi rush has sufficient regrowth some nine months after burning to enable establishment of an oversown pasture. <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Journal of Agriculture,40</i>)

>  In its native state, pakihi is usually bleak and desolate with a cover of pakihi fern and rushes. <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Journal of Agriculture,41</i>)

### pakihi land
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The annual rental to be 9d per acre for the river flat and 6d per acre for pakihi land. <i>(Mar 22, 1909,Colonist (Nelson),1</i>)

### pakihi rush
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rush](roa-ruv.md#rush))

>  <i> Cladium teretifolium</i> <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/236</i>) [NOTES: (Easterfield <i>et al.</i>)]

>  <i>pakihi rush [s] Cladium teretifolium</i> R.Br. <i>Ibid.</i> 65 <i> rush, pakihi [s] Cladium teretifolium </i> R.Br. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,55</i>)

### pakihi starter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fertiliser mix used to renovate swampy, sour soils.

>  At sowing down they applied five tonnes of lime a hectare and 625 kg of Westland pakihi starter per hectare in two dressings. <i>(Sep 4, 1997,NZ Farmer,24</i>)

### pakihi terrace
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a terrace composed of pakihi soils.

>  From Mr A. Creber, who has just returned from the rush to the north of Cape Terrace, we learn that the ground is worked by a tunnel driven into the pakihi terrace about six miles from Kumara and a short distance from the Marsden road. <i>(Sep 6, 1890,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  Their hard work and drive are transforming rough pakihi terraces into promising pasture at the back of their farm at Anatoki in Golden Bay. Theirs is hard, demanding country; mostly terraces of scrubclad pakihi, with an underlying pan.. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,25</i>)

### pakihis, the
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) lands that are low-lying and swampy, containing sour soil.

>  This type of country is usually known locally as pakihi land or 'the pakihis.' <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,22</i>)

>  The successful working of the pakihis is dependent to a very large extent on cheap lime being available. <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,26</i>)

>  The Collingwood pakihis are of limited extent, probably. <i>(Aug 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,298</i>)

### pakikau
 
 <i>n.</i> a gannet

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The old chief ... pulled off his pakikau, a rough flax mat, which he took and cast down a precipice that nobody might again find it. <i>(May 26, 1849,Journal,6/102</i>)

### pakipaki
 
 <i>n.</i> a cloak

>  <b>FORSTER</b> I got a small [289] Dart, two small Fishhooks & a small bit of green Stone, together with a <i>Bogee-Bogee </i>[os-grave] or rough-shaggy Mat, for a couple of nails & beads. [ed. fn] Possibly <i>pakipaki</i> - cloak or <i>pake</i> - a rough cape. <i>(May 29, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/288</i>)

### pakiri
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DRURY</b> FISH [MET WITH IN PELORUS]. Rock Cod - Pakiri of the natives, red and brown, or black, very numerous. Snapper - Tamuri, not abundant. ... <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,67</i>)

### pakirikiri
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Pa k|idi k|idi; A fish so called: <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,188</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] Pakirakiri [sic] a sea fish resembling a perch <i>(Aug 1843,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,51</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] Pakirakiri [sic] a sea fish resembling a perch <i>(Aug 1843,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,51</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species I will name a part, viz. ... Pakerekere [Paekirikiri]; [<i>Glossary</i> 119 paekirikiri butterfish (<i>pseudolabrus celidotus</i>)] <i>(1843,NZ Journal,40,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Pseudoslabrus celidotus</i> (Forster). SPOTTY; PAEKIRIKIRI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,41,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE SPOTTY <i>Pseudolabrus celidotus (Richardson) </i>... Maori name: PAEKIRIKIRI ... This was one of the first species of fish collected in New Zealand by Europeans, and was described and named in 1801. The Spotty belongs to the Parrotfish family (Labridae) and is also related to the Butterfish and Kelpfish. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,131</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SPOTTY (PAEKIRIKIRI) <i>Pseudolabrus (Lunolabrus) celidotus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... [<i>Ibid.</i> 275] The name Spotty is derived from the presence of spots on each side of the fish. The name Butterfish used in the North Island for them is from the yellow or buttery colouring on the undersurface. In Canterbury they are called Guffy, but for what reason I have yet to learn. It is a very pretty fish, more especially when seen in the water. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,272</i>)

>  Well out into the [Auckland] harbour was the `paketi pond', where children could easily catch `paketi', as we called the fish usually called `spotties' or, by the Maori, pa[macron]kirikiri. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>) [NOTES: Sinclair `At the Point']

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Kirikiri,</i> and <i>pakirikiri</i>, a rough-skinned fish, having two spines on the back, which it can elevate at pleasure; it is a short but broad fish (<i>labrus paecilopleura</i>). <i>Ibid.</i> 624 Fam. <i>Plectognathi</i>. - The <i>Pakirikiri, (balistes,) </i>file fish, is distinguished by two strong spines at the commencement of the dorsal fin, in shape like the letter V, both fronting the head, and standing out from the fin, these are elevated at pleasure, some fish have only one. The Pakirikiri is abundant in the Bay of Islands. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,411</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> [paek] <i>Pseudosabrus celidotus </i>(Forster). SPOTTY; PAEKIRIKIRI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,41,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> [paek] <i>Pseudosabrus celidotus </i>(Forster). SPOTTY; PAEKIRIKIRI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,41,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> [paek] THE SPOTTY <i>Pseudolabrus celidotus (Richardson)</i> ... Maori name: PAEKIRIKIRI ... This was one of the first species of fish collected in New Zealand by Europeans, and was described and named in 1801. The Spotty belongs to the Parrotfish family (Labridae) and is also related to the Butterfish and Kelpfish. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,131</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> [paek] SPOTTY (PAEKIRIKIRI) <i>Pseudolabrus (Lunolabrus) celidotus</i> (Bloch and Schneider) ... [ibid. 275] The name Spotty is derived from the presence of spots on each side of the fish. The name Butterfish used in the North Island for them is from the yellow or buttery colouring on the undersurface. In Canterbury they are called Guffy, but for what reason I have yet to learn. It is a very pretty fish, more especially when seen in the water. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,272</i>)

>  <b>wils</b> SPOTTY The common little Spotty is known to every boy or girl who has even gone fishing along the tide line. ... Other names are kelpie, guffy, butterfish, pakirikiri and paketi (Maori). Gets its most common name from a large, black spot on its upper side. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,207</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SPOTTY [<i>Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>] ... Other names include paketi, kelpie, guffy, butterfish and pakirikiri. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,22</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SPOTTY ... <i>Other names: Pseudolabrus celidotus</i>; butterfish, kelpie, guffy. None is widely used. Paketi, pakirikiri (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,257</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> He and Ted would spend hours.. fishing for 'spotties', other names are butterfish or kelpie or.. Pakirikiri. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,105</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 38. ROCK COD. ... This (<i>Percis colias </i>) is the Coal Fish of Captain Cook and Blue Cod of the settlers in the South, and the Pakirikiri of the Maoris ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,113</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Pakirikiri, Blue Cod .. [Scientific Name] <i>Percis colias </i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> ROCK COD (<i>Percis Colias </i>). ... This is the coal-fish of Captain Cook, and blue-cod of the settlers in the South, and the pakirikiri of the Maoris, and is the most commonly-caught fish among rocks on the coast. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,15</i>)

>  Common Name of Fish ... Blue cod (pakirikiri) ... Scientific Name ... <i>Percis colias </i> <i>(1922,TrNZI,54/473</i>) [NOTES: (Johnson)]

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

>  42. <i>Parapercis colias </i>Forster. Blue Cod. Maori: Pakirikiri <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/147</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> This [Blue Cod], is the fish known to Captain Cook as the `Coal-fish', and it has frequently been referred to by older authors as the `Rock Cod'. It was known to the Maoris as `Rawaru' or `Pakirikiri'. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,141</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> ... around the wharf piles where barnacles and mussels clung ... flitted the little pakirikiri and maomao; and down in the ... shadows swam the trevalli. <i>(1959,Stone, The,13</i>) [NOTES: (First Adventure)]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLUE COD ... <i>Other names: Parapercis colias</i>; coal fish; rawaru, pakirikiri, patutuki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,264</i>)

>  Pa-ki&#769;.ri-ki&#769;.ri (pa.-k&emacr.&#769;.re.-k&emacr.&#769;.re.; -->colloq. p&adot.-k&ibreve.r&#769;.&ibreve.-k&ibreve.r&#769;.~i), <i>n. </i>[Maori.] <i>(Zoo.l.)</i> The blue cod of New Zealand. <i>[Local, N. Z.] See under COD. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

>  We have some of the finest eating fishes in our waters. For instance, our blue cod or pakirikiri is one of the best in the Southern Hemisphere. <i>(Mar 7, 1906,Evening Post,7</i>)

### pakohe
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol: baked argillite

>  Starting with a brief account of the geology of this complex area and its significance as a source of pakohe (baked argillite) to Maori adze and tool makers Johnston goes on to give an account of the activities of various prospectors, promoters, business men, investors, and companies during the period that commenced with the discovery of copper in 1852 and ended with the auction of the Maoriland Copper Company's assets in October 1911. <i>(Nov 1988,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,82/45</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

[186] The Swamp-hen is widely distributed over Tasmania, the greater part of the continent of Australia, New Zealand, and the Chatham Islands. *[fn: 1888: Pakura] So called by the Ngatipukeko tribe of Whakatane; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,185,1888 2nd edn 2/79 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Pukeko Porphyrio porphyrio melanotus </i>(TEMMINCK, 1820) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Swamp hen, pakura, puke, pukaki. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,171</i>)

>  They [Moriori] also had the pakura (<i>Porphyrio melanotus</i>). <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/166</i>)

### pakurakura
 
 <i>n.</i> a pigfish

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Verreo oxycephalus </i>(Bleecker). RED PIGFISH; PAKURAKURA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,41,1971 ed.</i>)

>  VERREO OXYCEPHALUS (Bleeker). <i>Spotted Pigfish; Pakurakura</i>. A common food fish, Bay of Islands. Secured up to 10lb. near rocks and kelp. ... Distribution: Rare in Hauraki Gulf and Bay of Plenty; unknown farther south. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/117</i>) [NOTES: 1921 June Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> RED PIGFISH ... <i>Other names: Verreo oxycephalus;</i> spotted pigfish, pigfish (Australia); pakurakura, tanahanaha (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,261</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>RED PIG FISH</i> (Pig wrasse, pakurakura) <i>Bodianus vulpinus (Bodianus oxycephalus, Verreo oxycephalus) </i>[253] The long pointed snout ... and forward projecting, fleshy lips suggested the name pig fish for this species <i>(1982,Collins Guide,252,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>Moreland</b> RED PIGFISH [<i> Verreo oxycephalus </i> ] .. Other names are pigfish, spotted pigfish, and the Maori pakurakura. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,22</i>)

### Palace
 
 <i>n.</i> Proposed new building for Parliamentarians [Building was never built due to public protest; became a park instead at the back of Parliamnent Buildings]

>  Robertson decided to act in May 1997 when MPs agreed to demolish the Radio New Zealand building and construct a $94 million "Parliamentary Palace" in its place. <i>(Nov 28, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  [Hdg] Palace politics... As long as the suspicions remain, Mr Prebble will find ready followers in his attacks on the palace. <i>(Apr 20, 1997,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  A deferral would be a victory for ACT leader Richard Prebble, who has whipped up a storm of protest around what he calls the new Parliamentary "palace". <i>(Apr 20, 1997,Sunday Star Times,A1</i>)

>  The Cabinet may postpone till next Monday a decision on whether to proceed with the planned $94 million ministerial building, dubbed a "palace" by ACT leader Richard Prebble. <i>(Apr 21, 1997,Dominion,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Anti-'palace' petition 'going like wildfire'. ACT New Zealand's petition against the proposed $94 million cabinet office block near Parliament is "going like wildfire," says ACT leader Richard Prebble... We had old ladies saying, "Oh, the palace! The 'palace' phrase has got through to people." <i>(Apr 24, 1997,City Voice,1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Cabinet 'palace" suffers setback... I am confident that, armed with the facts and a proper regard to the views of the taxpaying public, the palace will be stopped in its tracks. <i>(May 23, 1997,Dominion,1</i>)

>  The Te Papa white elephant and the proposed new ministerial palace are prime examples of their [sc. the government's] largesse. <i>(May 30, 1997,Independent (Auckland),22</i>)

>  The new Parliamentary palace will cost far more than the quoted $94 million. <i>(Jun 7, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

### palace
 
 <i>n.</i> an obs type of tram with a raised centre compartment. -->1951 F.O. Brien [check date of use]

### Palagi
 
 (g as Maori ng) <i>n.</i> a European (equivalent to Pakeha) [ORIGIN: Samoan, (g as Maori ng) in cits below also Palangi, Pa'alagi; see also Papalagi]

>  There's a label in Samoa for him and his peers which means potato. "I'm brown on the outside but white on the inside. It's a huge insult for New Zealand-born Samoans, it denotes that you are just a brown pakeha or palagi." <i>(Oct 9, 2002,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

>  Join us for a lecture by Anthony Haas titled "<i>An Aspect of Being Pa'alagi: Reconciling Cultures</i>". <i>(Oct 16, 2003,VicNews,[16]</i>)

>  ..when Stevenson died in 1894 he was wrapped in ceremonial fine mats and carried up a steep track to the summit of Mount Vaea and there buried, a singular honour for a palagi. <i>(Apr 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E10</i>)

>  The palagi and his world have turned us and people like your rich but unhappy father, and all the modern Samoans, into cartoons of themselves - funny, crying, ridiculous shadows of themselves. <i>(Apr 15, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  <i>Ranterstantrum</i> is trying to explore the dynamic that I often found muyself in being the one Polynesian surrounded by a whole lot of palagis and being expected to defend the whole of the Samoan race for all of the ills that they have done. <i>(Oct 21, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  We saw that our bananas and produce were bought by New Zealand Palangi, shipped to New Zealand by Palangi and, in return, Palangi sent us Palangi-made butter and corned beef. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 38</i>)

>  It is told from the viewpoint of Joe, a Samoan man.., who finds himself terrorised by two Palagi strangers. <i>(Mar 6, 2002,Dominion,17</i>)

>  In the present climate, Palagi will be inclined to do something first for Maori as tangata whenua and then point to Asians and say: "Look how well they're doing without any help." <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 27</i>)

>  To my own, I'm not a Samoan, I'm a Palagi, and to my parents I am a child. <i>(Mar 6, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  [She] is mostly believable, except for some of her scenes with Michael Hodgson, who, as Mervyn, the "skinny palagi", at times sounds a little too skinny and Palagi to be true. <i>(Oct 26, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),89</i>)

>  "Palagis are scared of us... Even though most of us were born in New Zealand, they still think we eat dogs in the backyard and they're not sure if we'll say 'hello' back... This play is to tell them just how scared we are of palagis" <i>(Jun 12, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  <b>Bernard Gadd</b> 'You say my name very well for a palagi - that is a Pakeha,' I told her. <i>(1981,Where to Go?,35</i>)

>  I suppose Danny and I expected them to say, 'Oh, we laugh at palagi and stuff.' But they actually said, 'We laugh at our ministers and our parents.' <i>(Jun 18, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)



 
 (g as Maori ng) <i>adj.</i> European (equivalent to Pakeha) [ORIGIN: Samoan, see also Papalagi]

>  And this is palagi history as well. <i>(Jan 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  There is rumoured to be a third - a Palagi kid sporting an enormous orange growth - but he remains as elusive as the white whale or the Siberian tiger. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  In contrast, Palagi culture was viewed as less emotional, less respectful and with fewer financial obligations to the family. Religious life was less pervasive and worked on an individual rather than community level. <i>(Mar 6, 2003,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  We start in New Zealand in the 70s, when this five-year-old Palagi kid meets the Island family that moves next door and forms this strong friendship with an Island kid the same age. <i>(Apr 17, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  The majority [of the Niueans] could speak no English, had never worn footwear or eaten palagi food, none had been out of the tropics before, and within three months of departure for Egypt and then France, 82 per cent were hospitalised, suffering from complications due to lack of immunity to European diseases. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

### palm

<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Areca sapida</i>, nikau

>  The young inner blanched leaves and heart of the Ti, or `Cabbage-tree' (<i>Cordyline australis</i>), and of the Nikau, or New Zealand Palm, (<i>Areca sapida</i>), were eaten both raw and cooked. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Nikau</i>, areca sapida, New Zealand palm. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,106</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> The New Zealand Palm [<i>Areca</i>] is a plant of great interest as being the most southern representative of the fine Natural Order to which it belongs ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 261</i>)

>  You have to make yourself a small hut of poles and thatch it with nikau (the N.Z. palm) ... <i>(Nov 10, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/157,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (H.A. Atkinson)]
