>  The nikau, or southern palm, is plentiful all through the district, and is everywhere accompanied by noble tree-ferns and palm-lilies. <i>(May 1, 1907,Progress,255</i>)

### palm-tree
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> cabbage tree [<i>Cordyline australis</i>]

>  In the afternoon we strolled for some distance, though with difficulty, up the river, passing on our way through a grassy spot of about an acre in extent, occupied by clumps of large <i>ti</i>, so as almost to form a wood - their tall stems and terminal bunches of long leaves resembled a grove of palm trees, and gave quite an oriental aspect to the place. <i>(Dec 22, 1847,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The Scenery of the country was bold & worthy of the Pencil of a <i>Salvator Rosa</i>, all the Sides of hills were steep, rocky, & on all the little slopes & small terrasses or Settlements covered with fine shrubs, of various kinds; we saw here a great many <i>Palmtrees</i>, [ed. fn <i>Cordyline</i> sp.] of the same kind as had been found on April Ye 24th. <i>(Apr 27, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/267,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> <i>Cordyline australis</i> palm-tree <i>(1856,Letters from Early NZ,1914 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

Asch. & Graeb. <i>Ibid.</i> 62<i> purple pampas grass</i> [s]<i> Cortaderia atacamensis</i> (Phil.) Pilger <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,56</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> NZ nikau palm [<i>Rhopalostylis sapida</i>, aka savoury palm tree]

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> Honeysuckle, and passion-flowers, and cluster-roses hung in masses over the verandah, and here and there a tall aloe or native palm-tree rose towering up, ... <i>(1884,Our Maoris,41</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> [New Zealanders' houses] are built of bulrushes and lined with the leaves of the palm-tree, neatly platted [sic] together. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),153</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> [?NIKAU: Andersen Cordyline] The savoury palm-tree's pithy heart,/ By Ranolph just cut down .../ <i>Ibid.</i> 2/336 Savoury Palm-tree (Nikau) .....<i> O.</i> Palmeae; <i>G</i>. Areca; <i>S</i>. A. sapida. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/40</i>)

>  At the sides of this are representations of a Maori canoe and a New Zealand palm tree. <i>(Apr 22, 1897,Otago Witness,20</i>)

### Palmie/Palmy
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) Palmerston North [ORIGIN: abbreviation, c1960s p.c. G. Gabites]

>  <b>Geary</b> And Gary regales us with how he chased this Hoon through backyards all over Palmie, till he finally cornered the whimpering wimp behind a row of beans. <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,103</i>)

>  I went to Marton and Wanganui and Palmie and Hamilton, finding small jobs to do. <i>(Jan 21, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  [Hdg] Barmy in Palmy. <i>(Jun 2004,North & South,34</i>)

>  The "scarfie" returned to Palmy with sound university grounding in philosophy, logic, psychology.. and some useful law papers. <i>(Jan 11, 1997,Dominion,14</i>)

>  No wonder he feels grossly impugned by the impertinent parvenu from Palmy. <i>(Aug 16, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  The weather at the time Ansett Flight 703 crashed in the hills near Palmerston North in June 1995 was unremarkable, "just a bad day in Palmy", the High Court trial.. was told yesterday... "Just a bad day in Palmy, strong westerlies, reasonably low cloud. <i>(May 18, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

### pampas
 
 <i>n.</i>

### pampas grass
 
 <i>n.</i> toetoe

>  At the great Lucknow paper-mills the paper is made from pampas grass, which is practically identical with toi, which has hitherto been cleared out from the flax swamps as useless. <i>(Nov 20, 1911,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  We have not seen the staging in Wesley Hall so nicely draped before, dark curtains with light hangings formed the background, while handsome screens and pampas grass filled up the corners, the interstices being made use for pot plants. <i>(May 31, 1893,Marlborough Express,3</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ... we struck up ... towards the [Canterbury] mountains, the country being all level ... with a strong growth of coarse tussocks, pampas-grass, snow-grass (similar to the pampas, but having a deep red tinge in the blade) and spaniard or spear-grass ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,40</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> The whole [of the temporary Maori shelter] was then covered by layers of <i>toi-toi</i>, a tall fibrous rush-plant resembling pampas grass, tied in position with the ubiquitous <i>harakiki</i> (New Zealand flax) <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,89,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>OLLIVANT</b> Tohi-tohi ... which we improperly - I write liable to correction - call Pampas grass. <i>(1879,Hinemoa,120</i>)

>  <b>GREEN</b> ... bitterns rose frequently, and flew lazily over the stagnant pools to islets covered with flax and pampas grass. [fn] <i>Arundo conspicua</i>. <i>(1883,High Alps of NZ,298</i>)

>  <b>BOY</b> <i>Arundo conspicua</i> pampas grass <i>(1900,Our Own Country</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>COWAN</b> And along the river bank the <i>kakaho,</i> the <i>toetoe</i> or pampas grass, once waved its plumes abundantly; ... <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,146</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> For several days they travelled over miles of silver tussock country with, here and there, clumps of pampas grass or boxwood growing in patches of peaty swamp. <i>(1970,Tat,59</i>)

>  ... the pampas grass was ghostlier than ever for the stars had lost their twinkle somewhat with the high dust cloud that now shone menacingly above Aotearoa. <i>(Dec 28, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B1</i>)

>  The banks of the Waikato and some of its islands are ornamented with the waving flowers of the Toi-toi (<i>Arunda conspicua</i>) which resembles the pampas grass, but is yet more graceful. - <i>Sydney Morning Herald</i> 5 January <i>(Feb 8, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,6</i>)

>  The ordinary male and female forms of Pampas grass, Gynerium argentums, have, from their first becoming known, proved to be amongst the very best decorative plants ever introduced into this country. <i>(Mar 8, 1879,Otago Witness,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> OED : The popular name of a gigantic grass, Cortaderia selloana, having ample silky panicles of silvery hue borne on stalks rising to the height of twelve or fourteen feet; a native of South America, whence it was introduced into Europe in 1843 as an ornament of lawns and shrubberies. 1850-1 PAXTON Flower Garden I. 175. (See [also purple pampas grass](../A#also-purple-pampas-grass))

>  Pampas grass, planted for shelter when the Agriculture and Fisheries Ministry ran the island as quarantine station in the 1970s, has been sprayed and the department is removing boxthorn and other introduced species. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Dominion,9</i>)

>  Toetoe (Cortaderia) is excellent for the holiday home, but avoid pampas grass, which is a pest plant... <i>(Jan 24, 2004,Dominion Post,E14</i>)

### pan
 
 <i>n.</i> a gold pan; a pan-ful of washdirt [goldmining]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> `I went there [to Coromandel] on yesterday,' says the reporter, `prospecting, and found gold in the first pan washed. Out of about six pans washed four gave gold specks and grains. ...' <i>(1853,Auckland,103</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Went out to Dome Gully and had only washed three pans of dirt ... Tried to borrow a cradle. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,95</i>) [NOTES: 1863 May 22 Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger in 1931 Aug 27 Star (Christchurch) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 95]

>  <b>PYKE</b> Borrowing a pan (<i>Anglice</i> : tin-dish) from some neighbourly miners, he progressed up the Gorge trying `prospects' in many places, and at length he was satisfied to `peg out'. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/xi/40 When the Senior Partner first sighted them, one ... was washing off a pan of earth; whilst the others ... eagerly awaited the result. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iv/17,1889, 2974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOGG</b> 'Twas when the Rocky River rush began/ ... `and stories told/ Of ounces gotten in a single pan,/ Of nuggety, and round, and shotty gold./ The news like a bush fire through the diggings ran,/ <i>(1875,Lays and Rhymes,176</i>)

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Pan</i>: To wash metalliferous dirt; also the dish used to wash sand, clay, etc from gold. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Could I peg out a claim of my own and in a dish swill dirt to find if there were any specks of gold in the pan? <i>(1968,Seddons, The,44</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to wash gold-bearing sand, gravel, etc. in a pan to separate the gold. distinguish from <i>dish</i> n

>  <b>WOOD</b> During prospecting the gold was separated from the dirt by hand panning in a gold-dish, but it was slow, tiring work. <i>(1970,Gold Trails of Otago,7</i>)

>  <b>BALE</b> We now began to pan the stream and a couple of times obtained a few small specks of gold. <i>(1971,Maratoto Gold,91</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Luring, encouraging, enticing, odd nuggets up to the size of a small fingernail bare their beauty in black sand in pan or sluicebox. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,113</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> teamsters'

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> PAN: Flat iron with turned up nose placed under forward end of log. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,187</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 187]



 
 <i>n.</i> (logging term) [logging]

>  Constable Gray immediately proceeded to the scene of the accident and found that the victim was Duncan Cameron, aged 36 years, a native of Otago, who was employed by the firm as assisting "panman" of the log-hauling in the bush. <i>(Feb 1, 1902,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> PAN: Flat iron with turned up nose placed under forward end of log. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,187</i>)

### pan dashing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Dashing</i> seems to be a shortened form of<i> pan dashing,</i> extracting gold with a pan about thirteen or fourteen inches in diameter. It is tempting to wonder whether the expression `he's done his dash' ... is connected with the gold-miner's <i>dashing. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,152</i>)

### pan iron
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SALMOND</b> <i>Pan iron</i>. flat iron roofing sheets with edges turned up against adjacent sheets and covered over to exclude water. <i>(1986,Glossary of Building Terminology,232</i>)

### pan off
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  In some places the gold is so fine that it readily floats in water, and as a matter of course, large quantities of the precious metal are lost in the process of panning off. <i>(Sep 13, 1862,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  The Westport paper reports that a few days ago it was noticed that the small quantity of sand at the bottom of the bucket used in scrubbing out the bar at the Exchange Hotel contained gold. Since then the proprietor has panned off the sweepings of the bar, and can always get a nice prospect of fine gold. <i>(Jul 7, 1896,Bruce Herald,3</i>)

>  The indications of gold were most satisfactory, and the result of panning off from many of the reef-washes was exceedingly good. <i>(Mar 6, 1875,Nelson Evening Mail,4</i>)

>  COLOR [sic].- A minute quantity of gold left behind in panning off. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,53</i>)

>  [Scheelite] generally occurs as a coarse white heavy sand, difficult to pan off when washing gold, and is called `White Maori' by the miners. <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,39</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> I panned it off carefully. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,147</i>)

>  <b>'H'</b> He began to pan it off. <i>(1898,Grain of Gold, The,13</i>)

>  <b>KITTO</b> Save all the cleanings [gained by washing the gold, etc] until there is a dishful, which you may silverise. Work the quicksilver in in an iron dish and when all of the gold is taken up pan off the sand. <i>(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,46</i>)

>  <b>MURRAY</b> If you watch a skilful ... miner panning-off a dish of gravel you might say `There's nothing much to it. <i>(1977,Costly Gold,8</i>)

>  This will include a procession, headed by the Band and old pioneers, galloping and trotting events, panning off competitions, treasure hunt, military events, quoit match, putting the shot, obstacle race, sack race, throwing the hammer, etc. <i>(Nov 18, 1915,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

>  Some fine stuff was taken from near the stamper-box, and carried in buckets to another part of the shed, where it was panned off... <i>(Dec 25, 1872,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,4</i>)

>  Mr Bould, being an old veteran digger, volunteered to take them up the gully to the claim, after procuring a proper dish to show them how to pan off the gold. After panning off two or three pans of dirt we could not rise the color [sic], it being too late in the evening and getting dark we had to return. <i>(Dec 24, 1888,West Coast Times,4</i>)

### pan out
 
 <i>v.</i> [eventuate]

>  Matters may have panned out even better for Mandane had she forced the pace more out in front. <i>(Apr 12, 1928,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  As the prospect panned out badly, however, the face disfigurement might have been inflicted from rage and disappointment, the patriarch's possessions comprising an unattractive watch worth half a sovereign and 2s in coin. <i>(Jul 2, 1910,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  <b>EYTON</b> This did not pan-out as he expected <i>(1896,Rugby Football,7</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> And there's your [cut] foot. You don't know how that will pan out, you see <i>(1907,Return of Joe,7</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> That [sort of arrangement] never pans out well <i>(1924,April's Sowing,130</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> If things pan out as I hope <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,104</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> If the investment I had in mind didn't pan out, or didn't show immediate returns, our principal means of livelihood might have to pay forfeit ... <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,86,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> [pun or lit and fig senses] It somehow didn't pan out as he thought <i>(1897,St B's Prospector,19</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> the general English mining `yield gold' [gold mining]

>  The new piece produced last night by the company, under the title of "The Golden Giant," is built upon "those days of old, those days of gold," which has "panned out" dramatic material already this season in "Forty-nine." <i>(Mar 15, 1899,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Between the solid stone there is a sort of loose rubble, about the same as that in the latter claim, and containing loose gold, as shown by panning out a prospect. <i>(Nov 17, 1874,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  <b>WASHBOURNE</b> ... the gold ... [was] so much of a gold-leaf character ... I have frequently [c1860s] seen large specks floating in and out of the dish when panning out there, and these in digging terms are `floaters.' <i>(1933,Reminiscences of Early Days,4</i>)

>  A `new chum' on the digging was busily working in a claim, an washing a dish of stuff, while the banker and an old diger were quietly observing him. After painfully panning out, he threw the dish down with disgust, and exclaimed ... `Not even the color!' <i>(May 6, 1864,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: 1864 May 6 Press (Christchurch) in 1966 Turner English Language 17]

>  <b>HAY</b> Here, the mining is all quartz ... There is no alluvial washing to enable one to pan out one's dust, ... <i>(1882,Bright Fine Gold,2/274</i>)

>  Having arrived at Cann's Point, we came upon Daly and his party, and saw them "pan out" their morning's work (three hours), and got merely from the surface, such as loose sand and pebbles, 9 1/2 dwts... <i>(Oct 24, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  And after panning out several prospects all over the claim, we get on the dredge to get "took." <i>(Feb 27, 1901,Otago Witness,34</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> Alistair Murray Isdale offers the evidence, and he has spent a lifetime panning out the information on the Hauraki goldfields. <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,147</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> Measure it as carefully as we could, it wouldn't pan out more than 1 and one quarter cords. <i>(1938,Me and Gus,21,1925 ed.</i>)

>  A yearly subscription pans out just on 9.5d a fortnight. <i>(1936,Tomorrow,2/32</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  A vote of thanks and confidence in the candidate was carried amidst a scene of wild enthusiasm, but a collection to defray expenses panned out most of the buttons in the settlement. <i>(May 9, 1908,NZ Truth,151/5/3</i>)

### pan-breaker
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a plough-like implement for breaking up hard pans in sub-soil.

>  Two swing ploughs were used, one turning the furrow, and a second following with mouldboard removed, to act as a subsoiler and pan-breaker. <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,198</i>)

### pan-iwi
 
 <i>adj.</i>

### pan-Maori
 
 <i>adj.</i> of or for Maori as a whole

>  The fisheries settlement is pan-Maori,and the Treaty of Waitangi is to beapplied "as a living instrument in thelight of developing nationalcircumstances". <i>(May 2, 1996,Dominion,2</i>)

>  In 1992 the pan-Maori fisheries deal - granting Maori a share of fishing quota, the Government was readying itself to sell-off - was signed. <i>(Nov 30, 1999,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  He promises to be Parliament's foremost advocate of tribal rather than pan-Maori asset restoration. <i>(Oct 20, 1996,Sunday Star Times,c3</i>)

>  Moore's fear is that, unless the iwi system delivers on a pan-Maori basis, there will not just be a white backlash, but a brown backlash as well. <i>(May 3, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  Then there was the fact that Maoris had been given this thing, whatever it was, through a pan-Maori trust. No one has ever been able to define a pan-Maori anything, and only the brave are still trying. <i>(May 18, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Moore's fear is that, unless the iwi system delivers on a pan-Maori basis, there will not just be a white backlash but a brown-lash as well. <i>(May 3, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### pan-Pakeha
 
 <i>adj.</i> of Pakeha as a whole (contrastive with pan-Maori)

>  There is no more a pan-Maori view on any issue than there is a pan-Pakeha one. <i>(May 1, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### pan-tribal
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Professor Winiata attended the Hopuhopu hui, which endorsed the establishment of a national pan-tribal paepae (place to speak). <i>(Dec 22, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  The decision was also welcomed by JohnTamihere of Auckland's major urban Maoriauthority, Te Whanau O Waipareira, a pan-tribal trust which provides business andsocial services in West Auckland. <i>(May 1, 1996,Evening Post,9</i>) [NOTES: See also Pan-Maori, Pan-Iwi]

>  If urban Maori do unite.. the pan-tribalmovement is set to become a majorcommercial and social force... Tribal leaders.. would have to keep theiryoung happy or risk them joining a pan-tribal association. <i>(May 12, 1996,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>) [NOTES: Judge Durie suggests that pan-tribal wasimported from South Africa]

>  Urban marae - often pan-tribal such as Wellington's Ngati Poneke - were built and, together with church, sports and cultural groups, became lynchpins for the new communities. <i>(Nov 30, 1999,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  They will need a pan-tribal movement, but that is very difficult to achieve. <i>(May 15, 2004,Dominion Post,E3</i>)

>  In recent times the argument has been further complicated by four pan-tribal urban Maori authorities. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 64</i>)

### panacan
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pannikin](p-per.md#pannikin))

### panahe
 
 <i>n.</i> prob. a confusion of panahi and panake

### panahi
 
 <i>n.</i> an indigenous scrambling perennial herb that grows on coasts and lake edges and which has a stabilising effect on sand. (See [cf panake](p-per.md#panake))

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Panahi, <i>Col[enso]. Convolvulus sepium.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Besides which the following roots and plants were often eaten, viz., the roots (cooked) of the Panahi (<i>Calystegia sepium</i>); of the Maikaika (<i>Arthropodium cirrhatum</i>); ... the roots of the little sugary Ti-koraha (<i>Cordyline stricta</i>), of the large Ti, or `Cabbage Tree' (<i>C. australis</i>), and of the large Fern, Para, (<i>Marattia salicina</i>). <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Panahi is the well-behaved cousin in the convolvulus family .. Calystegia soldanella, or panahi .. knows its rightful place and like all plants in their rightful place, doesn't become weedy. Panahi is a perennial herb that occupies the edges of coasts and lakes and helps to stabilise sand. <i>(Jul 1, 2006,Dominion Post Indulgence,19</i>)

### panake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Calystegia<i> sepium</i>, Br. ... Nat. name, `Panake and Pohue', <i>Col</i>. ... This beautiful plant, the English `Bind-weed', is as common in the Southern Hemisphere as it is in the Northern, being found in Chili, Australia, Tasmania, and Java ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 183</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Panake, <i>Col[enso]. Convolvulus sepium. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Calystegia sepium, R.Br.] Pohue; Panahe[sic: check]</i>; Bindweed. ... Widely dispersed in most temperate countries and everywhere highly variable. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,476</i>)

### panakeke
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori `pancake' bread

>  <b>CARKEEK</b> The Maori word for bread is paraoa which is a trans-literation of the word flour. Various kinds of bread were made by the whalers, traders, and missionaries and some of them survive today and are regarded by present-day Maoris as non-Pakeha items of diet. One of these is called rewena (leaven) bread, another is the panakeke, or pancake-type bread. <i>(1966,Kapiti Coast,52</i>)

### panakenake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Pratia angulata Paanakenake</i> ...Creeping, formimg green patches ... Berry pink-purple, hollow, almost tasteless. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,252</i>)

### panakin
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pannikin](p-per.md#pannikin))

### panako
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Panako - The collector [sc Beattie] asked a Maori about this fish, as it was a new name to him, and received the reply: `The panako is the rock trout, and is nearly extinct. It looks like the kokopara in shape, but it is black in colour.' <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 63]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Asplenium obtusatum</i> SHORE SPLEENWORT ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,438</i>)

### panapana
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Cardamine <i>hirsuta</i>, Linn. ... Nat. name, `Panapana', <i>Colenso. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 13</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Panapana, <i>Col[enso]. Cardamine hirsuta. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Panapana. Cardamine hirsuta, var. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/39</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Carcamine hirsuta Panapana <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/124</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>HAY</b> There are the native spinach or Renga-renga (<i>Tetragonia expansa</i>), the Pana-pana, or cress (<i>Cardamine hirsuta</i>), and the Reti-reti, or sorrel (<i>Oxalis [211] magellanica</i>), which do for salad and green vegetables. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/210</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> Panapana [Maori name] .. Cress [Settlers' name] .. Cardamine hirsuta .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,178</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>CARDAMINE ... heterophylla </i>... Land Cress. PANA-PANA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,29</i>)

### pancake
 
 <i>n.</i> this is more like a girdle scone (See [also panakeke](p-per.md#panakeke))

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... their looks fell on the yellow pancakes ... After slowly sipping a quantity of hot tea ... and eating some more scone, they revived ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,38</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FRAME</b> [The Bull Calf] I have trodden in cow muck. Cow muck, pancakes, cow-pad ...[sic] She mused on the words. The Welsh children up the road said <i>cowpad.</i> <i>(1966,Reservoir, The,139,1966 ed.</i>)

### pancake rocks
 
 <i>n.</i> at Punakaiki on the West Coast

>  The "pancake rocks" and blowholes at Punakaiki are in Paparoa National Park. <i>(Aug 2000,AA Directions,35</i>)

### panel
 
 <i>n.</i> orig. slip-panel, then the distance between posts of first a post-and-rail fence, then any fence

>  Put up two panels of stockyard. <i>(Mar 10, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  ... a splendid piece of work, yards wire fencing and all. ... His posts look like one line of wood. From end to end his work is a study; bog, rock or sand is all the same. 236 panels; all the gates soaked in tar boiling hot 1/2 stockholm 1/2 coal. <i>(Oct 25, 1882,Chudleigh Diary,313,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> A small paddock, or a stockyard, opens out of the larger one ... The slip-panels between are guarded by four men. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/209</i>)

>  <i>panel n</i>. a section between two posts in a post and rail fence; a movable fence. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> from fencing panel:?made of panels

>  <b>GREY</b> `You came down easy,' he said. `But this panel over the river will be hard.' `Huh! What's a panel!' I asked. `Hoka, I've begun to have suspicions about you.' He soon showed us the panel. It was no less than a rickety pole bridge, swung on wires attached to branches of trees, and spanning a dark rushing little river that must have been beautiful at some other time. <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,189</i>)

### panel van
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>panel van n. Austral. & N.Z. </i>a small van, esp. one having windows and seats in the rear. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,816</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 348]]

### panfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> PANFISH A collective name given to any fish that's good eating fits into a frying pan and isn't a game fish or any other category. i.e. snapper is a panfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,31</i>)

### panful
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On washing out another panful of pulverised quartz 16 specks of gold were found in the bottom of the dish. <i>(May 7, 1881,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Three more dishfuls of earth were washed out, each showing gold; in one instance thirty-two very small specks were obtained from one panful of wash-dirt. <i>(May 3, 1881,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Every panful of the stuff lifted showed the "color." <i>(Jun 30, 1870,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The gravel, however, yielded gold - several specs out of every panful tried, but nothing like payable pospects were obtained. <i>(Aug 29, 1866,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  The progress made at the Coromandel diggings has, until within the last eight and forty hours been suggestive rather of the possibility of hidden wealth, than illustrative of its speedy attainment; for although minute samples of gold had been obtained from every panful of washed soil, still it was so insignificant in quantity as to lead the impatient to imagine that little short of failure would be the result. <i>(Nov 5, 1852,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Moonlight went over the hill, and found what is known as Moonlight Creek, and your humble servant went up the river on this side until I got above the canyon, where I found what was known as Sutherland's Beach, getting 13oz out of the first two pansful I washed out. <i>(Nov 10, 1898,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  ... they reached a bend of the Creek, where they found Ring's party at work, sinking into the bed of it, the water having been turned off a short distance above. Your Committee had a quantity of the stuff taken out of this hole, and washed ..., and every panful of it produced a few specks of gold. <i>(Nov 9, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/26/165</i>) [NOTES: (Report of Sub-Committee on Recent Gold Discovery in the Coromandel District) V]

>  Your committee had a quantity of stuff taken out of this hole and washed under their inspection, and every panful produced a few specks of gold. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Earp NZ in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 108]

### Pania of the Reef
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On June 9, 1954, the prime minister, Sid Holland, unveiled a bronze statue of Pania of the Reef in Napier... Whatairi was one of 120 girls from the area on the "short-list" as the model for Pania, then it was whittled down to four... "Pania will be there long after I have gone, but my mokopuna will see it and say 'there's granny'." <i>(Jun 8, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  <b>DUDER</b> We.. read the legend of Pania under thebronze statue. Sitting, smiling Pania ofthe Reef, sea-woman, part-taniwha,married to human Karetaki but drawn backto the sea to live with her family. <i>(1987,Alex,159</i>)

### panican
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pannikin](p-per.md#pannikin))

### panikeke
 
 <i>n.</i> a Samoan pancake [ORIGIN: Samoan, transliteration]

>  <b>Maepu</b> We had cups of tea and panikeke, and when we were finished, Kesa's mum said, 'Please forgive Moala for what he did to Fia today ...' <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,35</i>)

>  Yummy, all that talk about panikeke Samoa is making me wanting to make some tonight ... <i>(2008,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.samoan-sensation.com/q-and-a/1608]

### panikin
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pannikin](p-per.md#pannikin))

### Paniora
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori descendants of Spanish whaler Manuel Jose who arrived in NZ around 1830.

>  Manuel Jose's descendants now number in excess of 10,000. They are widely scattered around New Zealand and abroad, but they are all immensely proud of their Spanish blood. They are known on the East Coast as the Paniora - the Spaniards. <i>(Feb 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

### panman
 
 <i>n.</i> [logging]

>  Constable Gray immediately proceeded to the scene of the accident and found that the victim was Duncan Cameron, aged 36 years, a native of Otago, who was employed by the firm as assisting "panman" of the log-hauling in the bush. <i>(Feb 1, 1902,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  WANTED, at once, a first-class Panman for log-hauling engine; none but a good man need apply. <i>(Oct 11, 1900,Evening Post,1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> member of gold-mining team who uses pan to sift wash-dirt for particles of gold.

>  .. the gentleman in black with an aristocratic beard acting as panman for the occasion, whilst the other two supplied him with the precious wash-dirt. <i>(Dec 25, 1872,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,4</i>)

### Pannell country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] walking country as opposed to pigskin country. (See [Pannells](p-per.md#pannells))

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Pannell country: see marrowbone country. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,59</i>)

### Pannells
 
 <i>n.</i> mustering boots (See [also  Harry Pannells](h-hea.md#harry-pannells))

>  <b>McLEOD</b> At one time practically all the musterers in Canterbury got their boots from Pannells in Christchurch and even now I think the majority do; so much so that a Canterbury man will quite understand if you refer to boots simply as Pannells. <i>(1951,High Country,43</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> Some of us [early mountaineers] wore Pannell's famous mustering boots ... <i>(1975,Thousand Mountains Shining,219</i>) [NOTES: 1975 Andy Anderson in The Canterbury Mountaineer Jubilee Edition in Knox Thousand Mountains Shining 219]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) distinctive nailed mustering boots, custom made.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. when I was on that place in my mustering days we had to travel per 'Harry Pannell.' <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,112</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> At one time practically all the musterers in Canterbury got their boots from Pannells in Christchurch and even now I think the majority do; so much so that a Canterbury man will quite understand if you refer to boots simply as Pannells. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,43</i>)

>  O'Briens and Pannells also have a place as distinctive boots worn by country folk, and particularly those mustering sheep. <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Pannells: boots, as also are O'Briens and R and G s. Mr Pannell died some years ago but his name will stay in musterer's parlance for some generations .. He kept a huge tome containing the measurements and pencilled traces of his clients' feet. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,59</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> They'll be rolling swags in the bunkroom, fixing the pack saddles too, re-nailing their 'Harry Panell's' .. [Panells; Parnells] <i>(1997,Different Worlds,23</i>)

### pannican
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pannikin](p-per.md#pannikin))

### pannie
 
 <i>n.</i> ? a pan-sized fish

>  The fish were not big - typical "pannie" snapper of the inner Hauraki Gulf... Some may argue that nine is a lot of snapper per person. It is if they are all substantial fish, but if they are the size of the standard Hauraki Gulf pannie snapper, it is not excessive. <i>(Oct 13, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B15</i>)

### panniken
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pannikin](p-per.md#pannikin))

### pannikin
 
 <i>n.</i> with a fixed side-handle (often, later, occas. as large as half-gallon, cf DIPPER)

>  <b>H S CHAPMAN</b> Of course in these [bush] whares there are degrees of comfort and accommodation. In one tea is boiled in the kettle and drunk out of a pannikin: in another the tea is <i>made </i>in a tin teapot and drunk out of crockery. <i>(Nov 24, 1847,Letters to His Father, 1843-1851</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Nov 24 H.S. Chapman Letters to His Father [TS, ATB] in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 140]

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> in excursions of this nature [a bush journey in Auckland], the `menage' is necessarily very limited. A tin pot serves as tea-kettle and tea-pot and each man carries a `pannikin' for a cup and drinking vessel. <i>(1847,Notes from Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Johnson Notes From Jrnl in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 119]

>  There was only wood enough to make one panakin of tea. <i>(Apr 18, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,33,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1862,AJHR,E/9/26</i>) [NOTES: [used often in backblocks farm context: get quot]]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> It was then arranged what else we would take with us, viz., a hatchet, a billy for tea, no tent ..., panikins, and some tea; ... <i>(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,25</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Lauper in 1960 Pascoe (ed) Over the Whitcombe Pass 25]

>  [At the abandoned Maori camp was] a pannikin of native manufacture made of totara bark ...[sic] <i>(Feb 12, 1864,Over the Whitcombe Pass,2/87,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> We peeped in at the men's huts - a long, low wooden building, with two rows of `bunks' (berths, I should call them) in one compartment, and a table with forms around it in the other, and piles of tin plates and pannikins all about. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,34,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CHEVALIER</b> The billey (a tin pot for boiling the water) and three pannikins (tin cups) <i>(1866,Reminiscences of a Journey,10</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> The pannikins hung in the Stockade hall, <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,5</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> The pannikins hung in the Stockade hall, <i>(1869,Wit and Humour: Local Songs...,5</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... he was considered sufficiently rewarded in having the `honour' to drink his <i>`pannikin' </i>of tea at the boss's deal table, where he was not half so comfortable, or half so much at his ease, as the men in their tent or hut. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,6</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/i/9,1889 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> ... a tin pannikin apiece, and a `billy' for boiling the water. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,7</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> With their pannikins in hand,/ Yellow grains to measure off,/ That are gathered in the trough,/ <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,185</i>) [NOTES: (`The Waiohine Rush')]

>  <b>BURTON</b> A visitor to William Rolleston's station complains of a diet of `baked mutton, potatoes boiled in a bucket, station made bread with dripping instead of butter, tea without milk, all served on tin dishes and plates, with pannikens for water or tea.' <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,22</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A large round `wheel' of bread ... a butcher's knife and three large enamel pannikins (mugs). <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,59</i>)

>  <i>pannikin</i>: A tin drinking cup. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/26</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  Milking the Goats cleaning the pannikins making the Tea <i>(Apr 23, 1841,Journal of Edward Ward,293,1966 ed.</i>)

>  All ranged around, a motley crew/ Of pannikins and pots a few,/ Adorned a sort of scaffold; <i>(Feb 14, 1851,Lyttelton Times,8</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> He also found me a pannikin which did not look very inviting being black with dirt. <i>(Apr 24, 1851,Journal,7/193 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> A knife, fork, spoon, pannikin, tin plate, and tea-pot, form a sufficient yet not extravagant equipment [for the bush traveller]. <i>(1853,Auckland,125</i>)

>  ... to purchase timber for floors, tables, beds, forms, panicans, plates, soap [etc]. <i>(Sep 30, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/430</i>) [NOTES: (B.Y.Ashwell)]

>  <b>SMITH</b> [We must not] hang up our pannikins, for fear of some harm coming to the owner of the place, &c. <i>(Jan 15, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 15 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 363]

>  Tin mugs, or pannikins, as they are called, holding about a pint at least. <i>(1889,Wild West Coast,1</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [MS. S.J.B.] [Westland diggers carried a] Maorie Hen or a Billy of Mussells and a pannican of skilly. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,13</i>) [NOTES: c. 1890's (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 13]

>  <b>MOFFAT</b> [I] stripped some bark off a cedar or totara tree [in Nelson], I am not sure which, built a maimai big enough to crawl into, managed to get a fire, caught and roasted a woodhen, boiled some flour in my pannikin, ... [c 1860]. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,61,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pannikin, n</i>. a small tin cup for drinking. The word is not Australian. Webster refers to Marryat and Thackeray. The `Century' quotes Blackmore. This diminutive of <i>pan</i> is exceedingly common in Australia, though not confined to it. [1830] <i>(1898,Austral English,339</i>)

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> Having nibbled some cabin-bread and made himself a panikin of tea ... <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,25,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> PANNIKIN: Large drinking mug. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,187</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 187]

>  Pigeon and kaka stew, ... and ... several large pannikins each of choice billy tea. <i>(Sep 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,408</i>)

>  <i>pannikin n</i>. a small cup or mug.- panni'kin boss n. an overseer, ep. one of restricted power. [dim of pan; D. baas, master] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> ... a pannican <i>(1933,Autobiography,38</i>)

>  <i>Tin drinking mug, holding a pint as a rule. In the early days the explorers used them for boiling to save carrying billies. ([1874 Kennaway Crusts], p. 41; Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement], p. 54) They are now usually made of enamel an d called pints or mugs.</i> ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> They made tea in the big billy and ladled it out with a pannikin. <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,168</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> PANNIKAN: A tin drinking cup. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The cookshop was an old army hut, and this morning there was a pile of snow that had come in through the ridge of the roof down the middle of the table. We cooled our tea by placing the pannikins in it. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,8</i>)

>  <i>A small tin cup or `billy-can'. </i> <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),289</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As the pannikins of tea were being passed round, Morrie produced a bottle of Johnny Walker and poured a good lashin into each. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,75</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The ordinary enamel pannikins generally used by men on stations were not good enough for Jeff. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,21</i>)

>  <i>pannican ... n</i>. small tin cup used in the bush: (<i>Coll</i>.) the head; <i>off one's pannican</i>, insane, demented; <i>pannican boss</i>, subordinate manager or overseer as opposed to big or quartpot boss. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `These were 240-lb bags,' Chaffey would stress, leaning against the bar or squatting with smoke-oh pannikin of tea, gently moving it to and fro by wool-greased knee. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,14</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Early next morning ... I got up, lit the fire, and set the pannikins to boil : we then ... broke camp. <i>(Mar 24, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/55,1863 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Mar 24 in 1863 Butler First Year]



 
 <i>n.</i> large metal cup serving in the bush as an all-purpose vessel; a tin pot (used as a billy or pan.) (See [cf. also billycan](../A#also-billycan))

>  The majority are up, dressed, washed, andhave drunk a pannikin of tea and eaten asnack of cake or dry bread. <i>(Sep 28, 1878,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> ... tea boiled in a common tin pot, or pannikin as the sailors call it. <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,11</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> ... some of them new arrivals. And they often come without either money, axe, panakin, or bill-hook. <i>(Aug 24, 1863,Journal,69,1963 ed.</i>)

>  Milking the Goats cleaning the pannikins making the Tea <i>(Apr 23, 1841,Journal of Ensign Best,293,1966 ed.</i>)

>  I have known it [sc. the southern woodhen] carry off a meerschaum pipe, spoons, pannikins, boxes of matches; and on one occasion.. it actually stole a watch from a bushman's hut. <i>(Apr 3, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  You know how vulgar jugglers attempt to emulate this trick, .. sometimes by emptying a pannikin of flour, a pound or two of raisins, and all the <i>et ceteras</i>, into the same magic "beaver," and after a mumbo-jumbo whisk or two, turning out a smoking-hot, thoroughly-boiled tempting plum-pudding. <i>(Jun 21, 1845,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,62</i>)

>  .. she then threw a panican of soapy water over him... <i>(Mar 16, 1872,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  It is supposed that he fell into the water while getting water in a pannikin which is missing. <i>(Sep 25, 1908,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THE MIXER</b> He'll criticise each one told off / As a "pannikin devotee". <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,69</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> short for pannikin boss

>  <b>BREMER</b> A man called Bob Donoghue was the second in command or pannikin boss of the navvies as they were called, and besides being pannikin he was also the first aid man. <i>(1983,Port Craig,17</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  My power is such to make or break - / I'm a Pannikin, get me? <i>Ibid.</i> 13 There's not a pannikin can say that we could not do our work. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,7</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The other principal species formerly taken were the inanga and kokopu (<i>Galaxias</i> and <i>Retropinna</i>), upokororo (<i>Prototractes</i>), and panoko (<i>Cheimarrichthys</i>); while the lamprey (piharau and korokoro) and fresh water crayfish (koura) were taken in considerable numbers in some districts. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,71</i>)

>  ...why is a boss in New Zealand sometimes referred to as a pannikin? I can't pin down the answer to this one. A pannikin is a small drinking vessel used by workers. The expression "pannikin boss" was first recorded in 1911, in the <i>Maoriland Worker</i>: The 'pannikin' boss ... is the man who for a wretched shilling a day more than his fellow unionists sells his body to a mercenary employer to rush, wear and sweat others." <i>(May 22, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CAIRD</b> A shepherd or drover would think himself hardly used indeed if he had not his pannikin-full of tea three or four times a day. [check quot] <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,12</i>)

### pannikin boss
 
 <i>n.</i> a person with minor authority, a foreman. (slightly contemptuous) --> [ca1950 (freezing works.) a leading-hand (Colin Gordon, Foxton)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pannikin-boss,</i> or <i>Pannikin-overseer, n</i>. The term is applied colloquially to a man on a station, whose position is above that of the ordinary station-hand, but who has no definite position of authority, or is only a `boss' or overseer in a small way. <i>(1898,Austral English,339</i>)

>  The `pannikin' boss usually is in evidence. This is the man who for a wretched shilling a day more than his fellow Unionists sells his body to a mercenary employer to rush, wear, and sweat others. <i>(Apr 20, 1911,Maoriland Worker,8/15/3</i>)

>  <i>Pannikin Boss.</i> - Slang. <i>B.</i> who has little authority. <i>B</i>. of rouse-abouts, etc. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>R.GILBERD</b> Foreman have the same name [as in U.S.] but `pannikin boss' may be New Zealand. <i>(1950,Letter</i>)

>  <b>PUGH-WILLIAMS</b> The foremen of a stevedoring gang. Origin possibly in the days when watersiders did a full day's work. He used to make himself responsible to see the water pannikin was full and replenished often. <i>(May 1951,Letter</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... all the half-pie pannikin bosses ordering me about because there's no-one like the working man for putting the boot into the working man. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,227</i>)

>  <i>pannikin boss n.</i> minor overseer on a station. one without much status. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The manager of a sheepstation was called a <i>pannikin boss</i>, a term I have heard used, along with <i>straw boss</i>, for a foreman on building jobs in New Zealand. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,146</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> With the leak days of the Depression only a little behind men who had known relief camps and pannikin bosses had no zest for more mud and for Army sergeants; ... men still on relief had little urge to fight for the country that had given them so little. <i>(1986,Home Front,1/72</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pannikin boss</i> foreman on building and other jobs; originally manager of a sheep atation. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,82</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pannikin boss</i> foreman on building and other jobs; originally manager of a sheep atation. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,82</i>)

>  .. a man might be engaged in the street or in the pub, more likely after "shouting" a few beers for the "pannikin boss". <i>(1993,Wharfie,p12</i>)

>  The "pannikin" boss usually is in evidence. This is the man who for a wretched shilling a day more than his fellow Unionists sells his body to a mercenary employer to rush, wear and sweat others. <i>(Apr 20, 1911,Maoriland Worker,15</i>)

>  Keith Snalam of Blenheim writes: "As an apprentice boy in the 50s, I frequently heard the term 'pannikin boss'. I'm sure it was used as a comment on the state of mind of the foreman or leading hand, that person having just had the big boss from the office on his hammer ... The foreman would be seen approaching the workers in a state which these days would be described as stressed out. Hence the term, 'here comes the panickin' boss'!" Maybe. Dave Bijoux of Mt Eden suggests: "The pannikin boss drank from the pannikin, as did all the workers: a boss who metaphorically drank from their cup, was part of the community, and was somewhat despised as a prostitute for the workers. Ronald Thomas of Pamlerston North adds: "Perhaps there is a connection between a metal cup that holds a little liquid and a trumped-up boss who holds little authority, but makes a lot of noise about it." <i>(Jun 26, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  "He introduced himself as a pannikin boss," said a witness of another man in the Magistrate's court to-day. "'Pannikin boss'?" said Chief Detective Boddam, "what is that?" "The Foreman," explained the witness. <i>(Sep 22, 1915,Evening Post,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hired overseer or middle manager on a large farm or station.

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Pannikin-boss or pannikin-overseer .. applied colloquially to a man on a station, whose position is above that of the ordinary station-hand, but who .. is only a 'boss' or overseer in a small way. <i>(1898,Austral English,339</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> At smoke-oh he used to take a standing jump over a bale of wool and then Brucksaw, the 'pannikin boss' (head general hand) used to carry it out of the shed single-handed. <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,105</i>)

>  <b>GOULTER</b> Ted, at twenty, is running Blairich, and Jack, only seventeen, is 'pannikin boss' at Hawkesbury. <i>(1955,Keeper of the Sheep,126</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The manager of a sheepstation was called a pannikin boss ... <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,75</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> .. I'm just the pannikin boss, not the one who controls the storeroom. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,104</i>)

>  A person in charge of a team of farm workers: pannikin boss. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### pannikin struck
 
 <i>adj.</i> panic-stricken (a pun?)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Of particular interest ... was the fact that the boat had a liquor licence. ... Singles soon turned to `double-headers' as we headed into the ... semi-darkness of the canyons. ... [The liquor] fired [the Maoris'] imagination ... Panic and fear is contagious - it was not long before several of the gang became `pannikin struck'. <i>Ibid.</i> 146 A bugle sounded and whistles blew. Someone yelled, `The Japs are on us.' Our tent was `pannikin struck'. Some of us were not even out of bed before the enemy struck [[on a Home Guard exercise] ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,124</i>)

### pannikin tea
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I remember the times when pannikin tea and "brownie" with the shearers was the joy of my life. <i>(Dec 14, 1899,Otago Witness,57</i>)

### pannikinful
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "We made what we call 'hoosh.' That was made with pemmican emergency ration and powdered biscuits. Each man got a pannikin full. ..." <i>(Mar 26, 1909,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  For breakfast each man had a whole pannikin full of biscuit and seal liver, fried in bacon and pemmican fat, with a spoonful of jam to follow. <i>(Oct 29, 1910,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

>  " .. I am afraid that their position might have been attended with greater disasters, had not one of the runholders been in a position to supply them with flour, limited to a pannikin full each, in order that all might have an opportunity of obtaining one farinaceous meal on the road. ..." <i>(Sep 20, 1862,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> We were doubly anxious to be liberal after hearing a very good-tempered Maori man contrast the large-handed hospitality of his people to English travellers, with the `one pannikinful' of flour, - about half a pound, - doled out to him when he visited any one in Auckland. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,24</i>)

>  ... on the arrival of the first wagon with flour, nine miles from the diggings, it was surrounded by a crowd of miners from the field, when the whole of the flour was handed out ...at 2s. 6d. a pannikinful. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,171</i>)

>  Near Timaru, Canterbury, a man named William Carter, a laborer, killed himself on Christmas morning, by drinking a pannikin-full of neat brandy off at a draught. <i>(Jan 20, 1875,Evening Post,2</i>)

### panui
 
 <i>n.</i> a public notice; announcement, presentation [ORIGIN: Maori, -]

>  HE PANUI. He Panui tenei kia matau mai ... <i>(Dec 23, 1875,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  In the course you will learn korero (speaking), whakarongo (listening), tuhituhi (writing), panui (presentation), and language structure in a safe and comfortable whanau environment. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  At the Native Land Court's enquiry concerning the Titi Islands, Mr John Moffett raised an important point. He submitted that part one of the `Panui' of the Native Minister for ascertaining the persons entitled to the rights conferred under section 24 of the Land Act, 1908, to the Titi Islands off Stewart Island as described under that section, ... <i>(Feb 1910,Southland Daily News</i>) [NOTES: 1910 Feb Southland Daily News in 1979 Wilson Titi Heritage 155]

>  He Paanui [heading] Regular meetings are now being held at Te Herenga Waka Marae for the kaiawhina (helpers) in preparation for the opening of the new wharenui on 6th December. <i>(Oct 31, 1986,Victoria Univ. Weekly Staff Circular,813</i>)

>  Music, panui, and korero are delivered in a 'whanau friendly' format, keeping listeners up-to-date with what's happening in their tribal areas, in both Maori & English. <i>(2008,Mana,85/37</i>)

>  [heading] <b>PANUI</B> All Tawhana Land Owners a hui will be held at Piripari Marae, Waimana, on Feb 26... <i>(Feb 24, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,44</i>) [NOTES: *Heading of classified notice in newspaper.]

>  Panui Tanga. <i>(May 27, 1854,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>) [NOTES: Sub-heading above an advertisement published in Maori]
