### papa
 
 <i>n.</i> a vessel

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> There are 1,200 kits of <i>kumara</i>, large baskets of <i>taro, papa</i> or bark cases of birds cooked ..., including <i>tui ... </i> <i>(Jul 16, 1849,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1849 July 16 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 48]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a plant

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Geniostoma ligustrifolium </i>A. Cunn. Common name: <i>Maori privet</i> Maori names: <i>Hangehange, papa </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,63</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Geology) esp. papa rock, a bluish calcareous marl found in the North Island. [[? get dateline from dict. and complete quot.] With the exception of the beds of conglomerate and of limestone, all the members of the Upper Whatatutu are popularly termed `papa'. Thus the term `papa' includes rocks from claystone to sandstone, and on account of this wide meaning has not been used in the text of this report, except under the heading of [????]]

>  It is proposed later to put a coat ofpapa on the position beyond Hicks'house. <i>(Oct 9, 1917,Wanganui Herald,8</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> [At Arapuni dam-site] at the slightest vibration tons of <i>papa</i> came tumbling down. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,187</i>)

>  <i>papa</i> : blue clay. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) papa: flat rock, applied to soft underlying rock, blue-grey when exposed, chalky if dry and greasy if wet. After rain, a weak support for hillside soil. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed. ed.</i>)

>  PAPA A sofe mudstone occurring over large areas of New Zealand. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  The Maori word for a soft bluish clay. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),289</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `Up here the creeks and rivers just made straight courses carried away trees, roots and branches like straws. Cut the green flats on the banks back to solid rock and papa.' [From a letter written by Jock Anderson, dated 11 May 1897] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,100</i>)

>  The underlying rocks are sandstones and marls, which are all generally termed "papa", which is the native name for earth, or the earth or anything smooth and flat, such as a board. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> MANIA, thin lamina of sandstone used for cutting the `pounamu'. The natives fasten them in frames after the manner of a stonecutter's saw. The word `papa' has a similar signification. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,309</i>)

>  <b>ST JOHN</b> ... a reef composed of a peculiar and disagreeable kind of rock. Its Maori name is <i>papa</i>, its scientific appellation I know not; it is blueish in colour when dry; gets reddish under water. <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands</i>) [NOTES: 1873 St. John Pakeha Rambles in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 565]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Papa and other clay formations take longer than limestone soils to break in from fern ... [fn.] The term `papa' is applied in the South Island, not to clay or heavy soil, but to a porous, brittle limestone rock. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,231</i>)

>  Underlying the limestones everywhere [in Hawkes Bay] are blue clays locally known as `papa'. <i>Ibid.</i>25/355 ... blue marly crumbling clays, called `papa' by the natives. <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/351</i>) [NOTES: (Hill)]

>  <b>REEVES</b> [Papa] is a blue, calcareous clay often found with limestone which it somewhat resembles. The Maori word <i>`papa' </i>is applied to any broad smooth, flattish surface, as a door, or to a slab of rock <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,25</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Maori word for a bluish clay found along the east coast of the North Island. <i>(1898,Austral English,339</i>)

>  Beyond the volcanic formation - that is, from about Urenui on the north and Hawera on the south - the country is generally broken, and the formation is known as papa - a calcareous blue clay, capped in many places by shelly limestone. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,7</i>)

>  The blue and yellow clays, often locally [Parapara, Nelson] called `papa', are sometimes well banded ... <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No 3 (N.S.)/59</i>)

>  These clays which are commonly known by the Maori name `papa'. <i>(1905,TrNZI,42/575</i>) [NOTES: (Park)]

>  The Patea claystones are of the type which throughout New Zealand is popularly called `papa'. <i>(1922,TrNZI,53/60</i>) [NOTES: (Morgan)]

>  Underlying the limestones everywhere are blue clays locally known as `papa,' ... <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/351</i>)

>  The road over the Devil's Elbow south of Tutira was also badly shaken. Wherever there is papa [ed fn: a bluish sedimentary rock] there will be a mess when the heavy rains come, as whole hill sides will slip away. <i>(Mar 8, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/117,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <i>papa n</i> -s [Hawaiian, Tahitian, Marquesan, & Maori] : a bluish New Zealand clay like indurated pipe clay used for whitening fireplaces <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1632</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> papa: a clayey sandstone. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

>  <i>~ n</i>. New Zealand bluish clay [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>papa ... n</i>. N.Z. bluish clay [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>papa n</i>. soft bluish NZ clay. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,542</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 349]]

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> ... giant ulcers of erosion scabbed with weeping crusts of clay and papa ... <i>(1959,New Zealanders, The,45,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (After the Depresssion) .]

>  Then [moa] bones were dug out of the blue papa, twenty feet under the surface of the ground. <i>(Jan 7, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  The crop on land dressed with papa was noticeably of a better colour than that on the no-lime plot, but the limed plots were superior to the crop on the papa plot. <i>(1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,12/303</i>)

>  <b>NEAME</b> Rather than dig tunnels the Chinese sunk very deep races in the papa. <i>(1985,Gold, Coins, Greenstone, & Bottles,30</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> <i>Papa</i>: a hard sandstone found in thin slabs, used as a saw to cut the "pounamu". <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,311</i>)

>  A correspondent has written to a local paper on "Papa as a fertiliser." <i>(Feb 8, 1913,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The PAPA would be left until all the clay and soil had been removed, and then they would again have their section surveyed to assess the amount of papa to be shifted. <i>(1974,Open Country Muster,172</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Geology) flat, blue-grey hard rock [North Is.]; porous limestone [South Is]. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Mr. Perham states that, at the time of completing his survey, 2nd May, 1879, there had been no flood in the river for three years, but after a flood, lasting from May 3rd to 8th, the sand was scoured down to the rock (<i>papa</i>), the bar being forced out to about 10 chains from its former position, with 8 to 9 feet at low water. <i>(Sep 13, 1881,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  The papa formation still continues, and Mr Parsons intends to remove the drilling plant to the spot, and set to work at once. <i>(Jan 16, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,8</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The term 'papa' is applied in the South Island, not to clay or heavy soil, but to a porous, brittle limestone rock. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,231</i>)

>  <b>BARRER</b> This cliff now has a beautiful road cut out of its papa face, but the only track then was a spur which was naturally both rough and steep. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,108</i>)

>  <b>CHINN</b> .. before me was over a mile of steep zigzagging road the foundation of which was slippery papa ... <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,47</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> So different when compared to the tried and proved sheepstations on the papa (mudstone) ... <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,xii</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> I was bushed, sick of bouncing over papa and looking at sheep's arses. <i>(2002,High Times in the High Country,174</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Geology) [papa rock]

>  The manager reports that he has attained a depth of 75 feet with the boring rods at the spot where the papa rock was struck. <i>(Jun 7, 1875,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  The night has been very misty and disagreeable; trenches are muddy, and their subsoil of papa rock greasy and slippery. <i>(Jan 16, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,8</i>)

>  The makers, Messrs Simons and Co., guaranteed the dredge to excavate solid papa rock at the rate of 50 tons per hour. <i>(Jun 25, 1910,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> The track followed the creek, a muddy yellow-grey stream, its banks over hung with ferns and moss-covered papa rocks. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,88</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> It was a steep hill ... not improved by the fact that it was of papa rock foundation, ... <i>(1927,Follow the Call,111,1975 ed.</i>)

>  Unfortunately, in many extensive forest-areas the land is of clay or `papa rock', both of which are almost impervious to water. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/444</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

>  <b>GASCOYNE</b> [paapa] In the corner about a quarter of an acre of <i>paapa</i> rock is exposed at low water, with plenty of depth for a laden boat up to the edge of the <i>paapa</i>. <i>(1916,Soldiering in NZ,161</i>)

>  ... skin broken ... by a piece of papa rock. <i>(Oct 14, 1950,NZ Truth,17/1/8</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> In the <i>papa </i>rock on the north-east cape of the North Island there was the facsimile of a gigantic human footprint. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,119,1935 ed.</i>)

>  Resting on these Waikaremoana sandstones, the next formation, is a great thickness of calcareous clayey marls, known universally on the East Coast by the name of `Papa Rock. This papa rock occupies probably the largest surface are of any rock met with between the East Cape and Napier. <i>(1877,TrNZI,19/567</i>) [NOTES: (S. Percy Smith)]

>  The lower stratum is papa rock. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/550</i>) [NOTES: White]

>  <b>BROOKES</b> The country [in Taranaki] is principally composed of papa rock. <i>(1892,Frontier Life,150</i>)

>  The papa rock referred to both here at Raupo is an indurated calcareous marl of presumably Cretaceo-tertiary age ... <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/459</i>) [NOTES: (Mulgan)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Geology) . [esp. papa clay, papa country, papa land, papa rock, papa soil]

>  Gold will not, for instance, be found in "Papa" country, and the greater part of the up-river district is of that formation. <i>(Sep 26, 1888,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> They shovelled the stiff yellow clay from cuttings and sidings and blasted and picked the papa rock subsoil. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,78</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> That Papa Rock is beastly stuff to slip. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,62</i>)

>  As we steamed along the splendid reaches, lined on either side by almost perpendicular papa cliffs,.. we were constantly turning from side to side to admire some perfectly lovely fall. <i>(Oct 30, 1897,NZ Times,Oct. Suppl. : 5</i>)

>  It is papa country - that bluish-grey, old sea mud which is found on this eastern seaboard from Napier up to Hicks Bay - a soft, unstable, crumbling formation, which water and weather easilyshapes into irregular contours, which is slipping and changing all the time, but which, by its innate fertility, readily grows and retains the best of English grasses and quickly hides the scars made by the eternal slips. <i>(Jan 12, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  Lower down the beach on a papa shelf.. there are the remains of a bollard. <i>(Jan 5, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,32</i>)

>  Round the papa cliff there was scarcely foothold. <i>(Oct 5, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,93</i>)

>  When I see that truck I know there's a big fish in the pool by the papa cliffs. <i>(Oct 24, 1992,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  Taking the ford was risky; the Papa gravel sometimes proved to be very soft and caused the wheels to spin. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/53</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> ... on a river `papa' country <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,24</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> Weary years of farming on a <i>papa</i>, slipping farm. <i>(1903,Bill's Philosophy,23</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> ... the steep papa cliffs of the Wanganui River above and near the junction ... <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/177</i>)

>  In the papa land to the south-west of the Main Trunk line ... Fred Simmons, ... , told me of the existence of a papa cave in the vicinity of the site of the Matthews' hut. ... It's in a very narrow papa gorge of a creek that flows into the Matthews stream. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/445</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

>  The blue <i>papa</i> can grow wonderful clover, and it is mostly blue <i>papa</i> country. <i>(Aug 1954,Here & Now,17</i>)

>  The blue <i>papa</i> can grow wonderful clover, and it is mostly blue <i>papa</i> country. <i>(Aug 1954,Here & Now,17</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On the southern side, the bay is bounded by a small headland from which a papa reef runs out into the sea. It was from this papa reef that the surfing used to be done. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,138</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Where a tributary creek flows into the Ohara stream, ... , stands a small, almost impregnable papa bluff. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,131</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> In the East Coast papa soils, selecting a fenceline is a serious job. <i>(1973,Historic Sheep Stations NI,11</i>)

>  <b>McCORMICK</b> Only two patches of bush had been preserved ...: one stood ... within sight of our home; the other ..., known as the Domain, covered the banks and <i>papa</I cliffs for a mile or so on either side of the river. <i>(1959,Inland Eye,8</i>)

>  <b>SCHOLEFIELD</b> <i>Papa</i> country produces the readiest crops of grass. <i>(1909,New Zealand in Evolution,12</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> There [275] is evidence in districts where the ground consists of papa clay or mudstone that the grass sward cannot by itself hold the country together ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,274,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> ... steep marl slopes. Although on the station itself there is only a small proportion of land of this type, yet speaking broadly of the `papa' country of the east coast of the North Island, ... <i>(1926,Tutira,39</i>)

>  He regularly won the vegetable-growing compeuition - showing an early urge to get ahead by taking advantage of the growing power of the local papa loam - though he chuckles over this achievement because there was only one other competitor. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,95</i>)

>  The ground consisted of Grey papa clay that does not let water sink in. <i>(Oct 2005,NZ Memories,No 56, p61</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> At 6.30 p.m. they came to their camp site ... after crossing the ravine in which streams flow in this `papa' country, 50 feet deep, by means of native letters. <i>(1966,Richard Taylor,56</i>)

>  <b>R. GILBERD</b> Claystones and sandstones can be drilled with a `papa auger'. This must be of N.Z. origin. <i>(1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (Okaihau)]

>  ... the rich limestone and papa country already described. <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/356</i>)

>  "They are only eating seaweed on the edge of the papa reef." <i>(Jun 2002,NZ Memories,No. 36/5</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> We have good times mucking about at the Bully Pond: mud-slides on the papa rock, catching bullies, paddling the waka and duck diving. <i>(1999,Long Hot Summer,71</i>)

>  White clover and several grasses grow well even alongside the now historic papa rocks. <i>(Mar 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  The walls of the Split were of grey papa rock, soft and crumbly as sandsoap where the water hassoaked it, but hard as young rock underneath.T <i>(Dec 24, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/235 : 5</i>)

>  The South Pacific bored down 462 feet on Saturday night. The strata is [sic] now chiefly papa rock. <i>(Feb 19, 1884,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  About two metres below there's a shelf of papa rock some five metres by three. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 59/18</i>)

>  The Harbour Board has accepted the tender of Mr. T. O'Connor, Auckland, at GBP4377, for the removal of papa rock to deepen the entrance to the inner harbour. <i>(Dec 23, 1896,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Over to the right is where undercut papa cliffs crashed down to block the Waikare-Taheke River and form the lake, 2200 years ago. <i>(Oct 11, 2003,Dominion Post,E15</i>)

### Papa
 
 <i>n.</i> earth goddess in Maori creation myth, wife of Rangi the sky god. [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [Papatuanuku](p-per.md#papatuanuku))

>  <b>BACON</b> <i>papa</i> - In Maori mythology, Papa was the earth-god, Rangi the heavens. ... Today, in legend, Papa is still the earth, but in particular the term is applied to a type of blue-grey clay rock. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,182</i>)

### papa  whariki
 
 <i>n.</i> a covered area [citation deleted: it was for kokiri centre (and was already included there, q.v.) not this item]

### papa country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land with a soil base of papa that, though fertile, is prone to slipping in steep areas.

>  Good papa country: 700 acres, 5 roomed house, shearers' whare.. <i>(Jul 1, 1947,Hawera Star,1</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. this papa country was on the move, and those thousands of trees had been planted in an attempt to hold it. Papa country - the blue papa in particular - is of excellent quality ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,206</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Today the short head has a razor back ridge for the head and awkward broken papa country to pull on. <i>(1993,Taranaki Dog Trialing,6</i>)

### papa creek
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small river that runs through exposed papa soils.

>  On Sunday, the 21st, Capt. Northcroft ascertained the place where they crossed the river and went up a papa creek. <i>(Jan 31, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

### Papa'a
 
 <i>n.</i> a non-indigenous Cook Island resident [ORIGIN: Polynesian]

>  It takes a look at some New Zealanders (the locals call them Papa'a) who have settled in the Cook Islands, ... .. New Zealanders with no Cook Islands whakapapa have no reciprocal right of settlement, and successive governments have made it extremely difficult for Papa'a to settle. <i>(Jul 27, 2006,Time Out,28</i>)

### papa-namu
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>(Coprosma grandifolia)</i>

>  ... to find specimens [of aweto at Petane] it is best to look under the papa-namu <i>(Coprosma grandifolia). </i> <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/211</i>)

### papaauma
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [papauma](p-per.md#papauma))

>  The various Coprosmae, Karamu, Taupata, and Papaauma [sic], with their brilliant, laurel-like leaves, and yellow or red berries, must not be overlooked. <i>(Dec 21, 1882,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

### papai
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Papaii, <i>Col[enso]. Aciphylla squarrosa. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> <i>A.squarrosa, </i>Forst. - ... The `Papaii' of the North Island Maoris, (Colenso); the `Kuri-kuri' of those of the South Island, (Lyall). <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,50</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Papai is the small edible kind of speargrass. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,65</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 65]

### papaka
 
 <i>n.</i> a crab

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Papaka</i>, the crab: the largest is about two and a half inches across the carapace; a small crab, called the <i>rerepari,</i> abounds in the salt marshes. There is also a small crab found in the mussel, which has a very round red body and short legs. I have met with a little fresh-water crab, seventy miles inland; it is not above half an inch across the shell, delicately formed, of a dusky green color. There is no lobster in New Zealand. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,415</i>)

### papakainga
 
 <i>n.</i> original home, settlement; ?village lands; the homeground of one's kinship group. [Maori papa-kainga[a3-macron] the earth floor of a home] [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Another proposal for consideration is that brought forward last year at Ngaruawahia to buy back from the Government an area of land at Taupiri, and there establish a large Papa-kainga, also making it the headquarters of the Native Land Board. <i>(Apr 17, 1909,Wanganui Herald,5</i>)

>  No lease or alienation can be given effect to or registered unless the Land Transfer Registrar receives the documentary evidence that the Maori alienating has a papa kainga reserve - <i>i.e.,</i> proper land left to support himself and family. <i>(Nov 29, 1897,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Ruatoki is buzzing. A central Tuhoe papakainga [ed fn: The earth floor or site of a house, but, as Ngata indicates, it has become synonymous with marae] or marae of ten acres has been fixed near the school, with a tribal title like Porourangi. <i>(May 22, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/29,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>HOWE</b> Papakainga - `homeground' does not reveal the spiritual and emotional connotations this word implies. Papakainga is that area where one's original kinship group came from and lived. Whether or not the area has buildings on it, it has strong emotional overtones for any Maori. In this age of the urban Maori, the ability to refer to ancestral areas - the place where one's own history was made - is a matter of deep pride. Once known, one can never leave it - though one may be physically absent all one's life. <i>(1985,Towards Taha Maori,21</i>)

>  The marae trust has battled for two years to get permission to build a papakainga (Maori village) of 51 houses. <i>(May 16, 2002,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> At one time Whangamumu was a Maori papa kainga which is a Maori settlement. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,224</i>)

>  Island Bay's Tapu te Ranga marae's plan to build low-density papakainga housing is upsetting many previously supportive neighbours. <i>(Aug 2, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

>  On the fifth and final wananga which was on the 12th - 14th May we took the final journey to her papakainga at Matahiwi Marae... <i>(Oct 2006,Pu Kaea,4</i>)

>  Growing up in Te Waipounamu, I realised that most of the Maori I knew or grew up with had travelled and left their papakainga. <i>(1997,Mana,Autumn : 77</i>)

>  <b>Bernard Gadd</b> So that's my choice: stay at school, maybe get my exams, get me a good job. Or chuck school and save our papakainga (sure, they say there was a marae there once), our turangawaewae, our own home bit of ground back up country ... <i>(1981,Where to Go?,19</i>)

>  It discriminates between "Maori real estate" and "papakainga lands," the latter being such land as shall be considered necessary for the support of the native people, and to be absolutely inalienable. The rest, after the papakainga lands have been clearly defined, may be leased, and in certain cases sold. <i>(Dec 6, 1897,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  THE RATING OF NATIVE LANDS BILL brought down by the Premier, provides that native lands at present paying half rates, shall be liable for full and special rates. Lands belonging to indignant [sic] natives and Papakainga land set apart for the individual members of tribes will be exempt as at present. <i>(Jul 1, 1904,Wanganui Herald,5</i>)

### papaki
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Fam. <i>Squatinorajae, (Papaki.)</i> - This extraordinary-looking fish abounds on the New Zealand shores, it is very repulsive in its appearance, and has little claim to the title of angel fish; the natives, however, eat it, drying it the same as the shark. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,626</i>)

### Papalagi
 
 <i>n.</i> a European (Samoan equivalent of Pakeha) [ORIGIN: Samoan]

>  <b>Bernard Gadd</b> I know some papalagi think that us Islanders drink a lot. <i>(1981,Where to Go?,40</i>)

>  <b>WENDT</b> Beyond .. the coral reefs lay a fairyland ruled by papalagi, the builders of these aeroplanes and the messengers of Jehovah. <i>Ibid.</i> 10 The palagi trader gave him all the credit he wanted. <i>Ibid</i>. II. 13 His great-grandfather .. was head of their aiga when the papalagi missionaries arrived in Sapepe. <i>Ibid.</i> II. 161 He always talks of palagi like they are his best friends. <i>(1979,Leaves of Banyan Tree,I.i 8</i>)

>  She wrote in her diary that the only papalagi (foreign) house on the entire island of 800 to 1,000 people was the dispensary. <i>(Apr 24, 1983,New York Times,VI. 48/1</i>)

>  The dunny doors were marked in Samoan and this fact caused some distress to the glittering <i>palagi</i> guests. <i>(Mar 1984,Metro,16/2</i>)

>  We had tried but the Palagis there handled her like machines. <i>(Jun 2, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),39/1</i>)

>  <b>STAIR</b> These marvellous visitors they called <i>papalangi. </i> <i>(1897,Old Samoa,i. 24</i>)

>  <b>CHURCHILL</b> The older people are becoming very jealous that the knowledge of them [sc. old legends] shall not spread to the Papalangi foreigners. <i>(1902,Samoa 'Uma,vi. 70</i>)

>  <b>KEESING</b> Sick enough even to try <i>papalangi</i> (western) medicine. <i>(1934,Modern Samoa,viii. 393</i>)

>  <b>LEWIS</b> <i>Palagi</i> is a most useful word which I shall be employing frequently. It is an abbreviation of <i>papalagi</i>, which means literally `sky-bursters'. It is applied to white people in general. <i>(1938,They Call Them Savages,i. 23</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> .. this man .. calling himself Papalangi (an European). <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,347</i>)

>  <b>BURCHFIELD</b> papalagi /pa|pa:la ^i/. Also papalangi and in shortened form palagi (see below). [Samoan <i>papalagi.</i>] A European, a white man. Also attrib. or as adj. `The first vowel of this word is ofter omitted in colloquial speech, but the initial consonant is then released more slowly than usual.' (G.B. Milner) This shortened form is more usual in New Zealand English. In Samoan the word has no pl. inflexion. <i>(1988,Lexicographical & Linguistic Studies,195</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> <i>Papalagi</i> (foreign) mannerisms as a spring board for future activities. <i>(1928,Coming of Age in Samoa,xi. 168,1929 ed.</i>)

>  Badgered by <i>papalagi</i> (white) planter, Prime Minister Fiame Mata'afa Faumuina Mulinu`u II last week handed down the harshest decree of his six-year regime: cricket was banned (Wednesdays and Saturdays excepted). <i>(Feb 18, 1966,Time (NZ edition),26/3</i>)

>  Perhaps, I think, her family disapproves of her presence in papalagi bars. <i>(Jan 26, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

### Papamoaisation
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The Papamoaisation of much of the New Zealand coastline is simply "inevitable". <i>(Dec 31, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

### papane
 
 <i>n.</i> a river-fish

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> One day he went fishing for cocker bullies, or papane. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,81</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> One day he went fishing for cocker bullies, or papane. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,81</i>)

>  <i>forsteri, sucker; a river fish. Called</i> papanoko <i>south of Taupo. </i>CHEIMARRICHTHYS FOSTERI. <i>Panokonoko</i>, or <i>Papane.</i> During March Mr. Elsdon Best secured examples caught at Hiruharama, on the Wanganui River, North Island. Large numbers are annually secured by the Maoris of this locality. The panokonoko is much esteemed as a food fish. <i>(May 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,V/96</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps and Hodgkinson Further Notes on Edible Fishes]

### papanga
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [papango](p-per.md#papango))

### papango
 
 <i>n.</i> a small dark native diving duck; also called New Zealand scaup, black teal [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <i>116. Aytha novaeseelandiae</i> (Gmelin, 1789) <i>New Zealand Scaup (Papango) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,38,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Scaup Aythya novaeseelandiae</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>New Zealand scaup, black teal, papango. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,150</i>)

>  FULIGULA NOVAE ZELANDIAE, Gml. Papango. Black Widgeon. In the hill-country, a few years since, this was sufficiently common; a small tarn, near Lake Coleridge, yet retains the name of Widgeon Lake, ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/73</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 102. FULIGULA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gml. Black Teal. Papango. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,38</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> FULIGULA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND SCAUP.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Papango, Tetepango, Matapouri, Titiporangi, and Raipo; `Black Teal' and `Widgeon' of the Colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,259,1888 2nd edn 2/273 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The species we have identified in the Kaipara and Hokianga are - the Putangi, or `paradise-duck' (<i>Casarca variegata</i>); the grey duck, or Parera (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>); the brown duck, an allied species or variety of the last; the Papanga, or `teal,' or `widgeon' (<i>Fuligula N.Z</i>.), and some other varieties that may be imported birds, or crosses, or other native species. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/225</i>)

>  <i>Fuligula novae-zealandiae.</i> - Papango, Scaup, Black Teal. Both Islands. Flight very feeble. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> We are standing on the banks of the Horowhenua Lake ... a pair of Papango ... are floating on the placid waters; ... a solitary Pekapeka is flitting silently overhead, chasing ... the minute insect-life upon which this bat subsists; ... and the melancholy note of the Fern-Sparrow, calling to his fellows among the rushes, has grown languid and finally died away; ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/98</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Tutira lake bore on its bosom a fleet of eight or nine hundred papango, widgeon (<i>Fuligula Novae Zealandiae</i>). <i>(1926,Tutira,204</i>)

### papangoke
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Pangohengohe, papangoke,</i> a fresh water fish, from four to eight inches long, and scaleless: syn.<i> papangoko. </i> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,414</i>)

### papanoko
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  PUBLIC MUSEUM. DONATIONS FOR MAY .. Mr B.J. Edwards - Fish from the Upper Wanganui, called Papanoko. <i>(Jun 5, 1899,Wanganui Chronicle,3</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... the <i>papanoko</i> is a scaleless fresh water fish, about five inches long ... <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,499</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> The papanoko is thus described by the Captain Mair: `Small fish from 6in. to 8in. length, and very deep in proportion, weighing about the eighth of a pound. ...' <i>(1866,Handbook Fishes of NZ,143</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> PAPANOKO (<i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i> ). ... identified ... with the panoko (or papanoko) of North Island Maoris. <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/298</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i> Haast. PAPANOKO or PAPANE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,42,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i> : [European Name] Torrent-fish or shark-bully [Maori Name] Papanoko. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Papanoko <i>Cheimarrichthys fosteri</i> Haast <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,409</i>)

### papapa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> [No New Zealand flowers are] remarkable for either beauty or fragrance except Kaha Kaha, Wara Wara [Kowharawhara] and the Papapa (or the Parpuppa). The Kahakaha are fragrant and oderous [sic]; it varies in colors sometimes being dark and sometimes of a light pink and yellow. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,111,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Pomaderris kumeraho</i> A. Cunn. Common names: <i>Poverty weed, gumdigger's soap </i>Maori names: <i>Kumarahou, papapa </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,84</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Gaultheria antipoda-</i> Forst.f. Common name: <i>Snowberry </i>Maori name: <i>Papapa</i> ... This little shrub is common throughout New Zealand in scrubland. Possibly it is the plant referred to by James Cowan [in Maori Today] ... although Williams identifies `papapa' as both <i>Pomaderris elliptica</i> (Rhamnaceae) and <i>Gaultheria antipoda-</i>. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,52</i>)

### paparangi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANGAS</b> New Zealand has, besides a great many small land snails, three of considerable size; ... and two large flattened snails, the <i>Helix Busbii</i>, or `papa-rangi' * [fn * `The shell that fell from heaven.'] of the natives, which is of a curious dark olive-green colour ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,83</i>)

### Paparoa skink
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) one of the Speckled skink sub groups. Restricted to alpine grasslands high on Paparoa Range. [Speckled skink <i>Oligosoma infrapunctatum</i>]

>  The Paparoa skink is a recent discovery from the Paparoa Ranges in the Buller region. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/17</i>)

### papataniwhaniwha
 
 <i>n.</i> a native daisy. [Maori papataniwhaniwha[a3-macron]]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>L[agenophera] Forsteri,</i> D.C. ... <i>Papataniwhaniwha.</i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,272</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Lagenophora [pumila] ([The Dwarf] Lagenophora) ... Native Daisy</i>: Maori name, <i>Papataniwhaniwha.</i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,410,1940 p. 439 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> On damp cliffs grew ... Lagenphora Forsteri, the native daisy - Papa taniwhaniwha, Arundo fulvida, and other plants. <i>(1926,Tutira,104</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Lagenophora ... pumila </i>... A small perennial daisy. ... Native Daisy. PAPA-TANIWHA-NIWHA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,45</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Lagenifera pumila Papataaniwhaniwha </i>... Small rosettes or tufts, often forming patches ... (=<i>Lagenophora pumila</i>). ... <i>Lagenifera petiolata Papataaniwhaniwha</i> ... Very small rosettes or tufts ... (=<i>Lagenophora petiolata</i>). <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,200</i>)

### papati
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. <i>Plectognathi</i> there are three species: <i>Tetraodon Hamiltoni,</i> the <i>Monacanthus parika</i>, and the <i>Diadona globulas. Kopuawai, Koputaratara, Papati </i>and <i>Totara</i>, all these are descriptive names, implying that it is covered with sharp spines, and is inflated; this singular fish is frequently cast up on the shore, and sometimes of a large size. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,625</i>)

### Papatuanuku
 
 <i>n.</i> earth goddess in Maori creation myth, wife of Rangi(nui) the sky god. [ORIGIN: Maori, short form Papa q.v.]

>  In the beginning was Te Kore, an all enveloping darkness. Papatuanuku, the earth mother, and Ranginui, the sky father, were locked in a tight and suffocating embrace, until their children could bear the darkness no longer. <i>(Dec 14, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> Tane went to Kurawaka and he did as Papatuanuku had told him to. The woman he created was named Hineahuone, Woman made of Clay; and Papatuanuku was complicit in this act. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,47</i>)

>  I like muso's [sic] who are trying to help to heal the sickness that seperates {sic] people whatever part of papatuanuku they come from. <i>(Aug 14, 2000,Salient,No. 17 : 35</i>)

### papatupu
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori customary land [mainly used between c. 1890 and 1920. Jim Cameron writes : See eg. <i>NZ Historical Atlas</i> (1997), p. 83; Annual Reports Native Land Court c. 1920 (AJHR G-9)]

>  At this advanced stage of the history of New Zealand there should not be any such thing as papatupu land. <i>(1908,AJHR,G-1M</i>) [NOTES: <i>Reports of Royal Commission on Native Lands and Native Land Tenure 1908-1909</i> (Stout-Ngata Commission).]

### papauma
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [broadleaf](bri-byo.md#broadleaf))

>  ... the curious red fruit of the Titoki, or Titongi, (<i>Alectryon excelsum</i>); .. of several species of <i>Coprosma</i>, - particlularly the Karamu (<i>C. lucida</i>), and <i>C. robusta</i>), of the Papaauma (<i>C. grandifolia</i>), and of the two littoral species, Taupata (<i>C. retusa</i>), and Tataraheke (<i>C. acerosa</i>); of the Koropuku (<i>Gaultheria depressa</i>) ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Papauma (broadleaf) .. Griselinia littoralis .. Timber heavy, dense, and of great stength, grain prettily marked, but timber liable to warp; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)

>  Papauma (broadleaf) .. Griselinia littoralis .. Timber heavy, dense, and of great stength, grain prettily marked, but timber liable to warp; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>G[riselinia] Littoralis, Raoul. Kapuka; Papaumu; Broad-leaf. </i>Timber strong, close-grained and durable; ... <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,239</i>)

>  <i>pa[a]pa[a]uma, </i>a tree (Griselinia littoralis), broad-leaf. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  BROADLEAF, PAPAUMA (<i>Griselinia littoralis</i>). <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/252</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> The Maori <i>papauma</i> is rarely used for this tree <i>[Griselinia littoralis</i> in Westland] ... <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,157</i>)

>  <i>papauma n. NZ =</i> BROADLEAF. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,542</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Broadleaf (griselinia littoralis) [sic] called in different localities by the Maoris Puka, Papauma Kapukas the latter being the name used in the South. <i>(Dec 11, 1894,North Otago Times,1</i>)

>  Griselinia littoralis is called "papauma," but its berries are known as "huariki." <i>(May 29, 1909,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Among other native plants likely to resist sea-winds are pohutukawa, ngaio, tutu, pingao, harakeke, puriri, koropuku, papauma (Griselinea) ... <i>(Aug 29, 1912,Evening Post,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  88 Papauma (broadleaf) slab. <i>Ibid.</i> 21 305 Papauma (broadleaf) slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,17</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  I saw, without leaving the footpath, the following trees, shrubs, etc.: Koromiko (several species), kohekohe, mahoe, papauma (not Griselinea) ... <i>(Aug 29, 1912,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Papaauma, <i>Col[enso]. Coprosma grandiflora. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Coprosma australis</i> (A. Rich.) Rob. Maori names: <i>Manono, papauma Coprosma robusta </i>Raoul maori name; <i>Karamu</i> (name applied to any large-leaved <i>Coprosma) Coprosma acerosa</i> A. Cunn. Maori names: <i>Tarakupenga, tataraheke, tatarahake </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,86</i>)

### papaumu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Papaumu. Coprosma grandifolia, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/43</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

### papaungungu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> SPATULA {RHYNCAPSIS} VARIEGATA. (NEW-ZEALAND SHOVELLER.) ... <i>Native name.</i> Tete, Pateke, Putaitai, Kuriwhengi, Kuruwhengu, Papaungungu, Kahoho, and Wetawetangu; `Spoon-bill Duck' of the colonists. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/269</i>)

### paper
 
 <i>n.</i> cigarette papers, usu. 'the papers'

>  All three being smokers, it was said that unless they were together, no-one could have a smoke, since one had the tobacco, one had the papers and one had the matches. <i>(Dec 2001,NZ Memories,No. 27/40</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> <i>paper</i> a cigarette paper <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,98</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Feeling an enormous sense of loss, Bob reached into the deep pocket of his bushman's jacket and took out his packet of tobacco and papers. <i>(1984,Razorback,17</i>)

### paper bus
 
 <i>n.</i> rural delivery van or service car. [cf cream lorry, mail bus, mail car, paper car]

>  <b>Bernard Gadd</b> It took a while to get to the taxi stand. And then the long wait for the paper bus. <i>(1981,Where to Go?,21</i>)

### paper car
 
 <i>n.</i> a newspaper delivery vehicle

>  At a convenient interval Mr W. Kennedy on behalf of the residents presented Mr and Mrs Wilson with an exceptionally fine case of cutlery while Mrs Wilson was also the recipient of two presentations from The Invercargill Tourist Co. for her kindness in providing breakfast to the drivers of the early morning paper car. <i>(Aug 20, 1918,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  While in Invercargill last week Mr W. J. Wesney, President of the Otautau Progressive League, interviewed Mr Cooper, the Chief Postmaster, with reference to the delivery of the mail in Otautau every morning by the Invercargill paper car. <i>(May 5, 1925,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  I mean he stopped Ivan in the paper car, told him about the rolling. <i>(Nov 26, 1958,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

### paper collar
 
 <i>n.</i> a white collar worker [also used attributively]

>  We are sure he had nothing to learn from the paper collar gentlemen who study the art of boating and criticise good men from the River Bank. <i>(Dec 19, 1881,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

>  The zeal and activity which ushered into existence the "Paper-Collar Rush," at Wynyardton Beach, have considerably abated. <i>(Jan 28, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... I will go on to Christchurch, where I enjoyed a real spell of what diggers call a `paper-collar life'; . <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,86</i>)

>  .. we have now sundry drapers' shops with large plate glass windows, full of all imaginable articles of dress to tempt the unwary paper collar gentry and frizzled-hair ladies, into needless extravagance. <i>(Jul 18, 1867,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  Counter-jumping trade may jeer us, / We're no paper-collar band... <i>(Sep 13, 1879,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  " .. Criffel, I think, will eventually become a field for the enterprise of the true digger - the man of patience, perseverance, and energy - and though there are some good men on Criffel, paper-collared effigies are made of too flimsy a stuff these mountains to o'erturn or their gorges explore." <i>(Aug 29, 1885,Otago Witness,29</i>)

>  We working men are proud of our Member and don't want any paper collar toff to tell us whats [sic] what. <i>(Apr 13, 1893,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  Life is so short that man is but a paper-collar on the neck of time. <i>(May 10, 1894,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  One would almost think that he belongs to the "portmanteau and paper-collar" class, or "heads I win, tails you lose." <i>(Jan 25, 1871,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

### paper leaf
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i>, rangiora [[1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers, supplied by W. Best, Otaki (And)]

>  paper leaf <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i> Ver. (W.B.) <i>(1925,TrNZI,57/945</i>)

### paper lolly
 
 <i>n.</i> a wrapped sweet

>  PAPER LOLLIES, assorted 1s 9d. <i>(Nov 4, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> The paper lollies would be showered into the middle of the day room and it would be first come first served... . <i>(1961,Faces in the Water,98</i>)

### paper mulberry
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Broussonetia papyrifera</i>, aute,

>  There are some strips of the raw material, the inner bark of the paper mulberry, the "gampi" of the Japanese, as well as specimens showing the numerous uses to which paper is put in Japan, where certain sorts of it serve as handkerchiefs, clothing, hangings, and for other purposes. <i>(Aug 23, 1880,Star, The,4</i>)

>  Very little clothing sufficed on some islands, and that was mostly of a cloth made from the bark of the paper mulberry; on other islands no clothing was worn at all. <i>(Oct 9, 1894,Nelson Evening Mail,4</i>)

>  <b>1/III/33</b> [One only fibre-yielding plant] was generally cultivated, and that ... was not indigenous; ... the Aute, or Paper-Mulberry tree (<i>Broussonetia papyrifera</i>); ... It has long been nearly, if not quite, extinct. <i>(1869,TrNZI,(Colenso Botany North Isd)</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>aute</i>, broussonetia papyrifera, paper mulberry. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,14</i>)

>  <i>Aute,</i> n.<i> 1. Broussonetia papyrifera, paper-mulberry</i>, formerly cultivated by the Natives in New Zealand. [so 1957/23, <i>natives</i> uncapitalised] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,28</i>)

>  Dr. P.H. Buck.. believes that early Maori navigators brought paper mulberry plants to New Zealand from their homes in the tropics. <i>(Jun 17, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  He was a big-framed man, with nothing but a piece of cloth round his loins, a garment hammered out of the bark of the paper mulberry tree. <i>(Jul 5, 1902,Evening Post,5</i>) [NOTES: Written by Bishop of New Guinea with reference to New Guinea.]

>  The Ihungara was formed of a lock of human hair twisted with a rope of "aute" (paper mulberry bark), and was kept at Mokoia in a house made of wood, also brought from Hawaiki, and thatched with Mangemange <i>Lygodium articulatum</i>, a climbing fern. <i>(May 1, 1883,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  These are sometimes closed with blinds of course mat, and inside the house partitions are formed by hanging cloth made of the paper mulberry. <i>(Jul 29, 1884,North Otago Times,4</i>) [NOTES: Reference to houses in Samoa.]

### paper nautilus
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ...at water's edge. holding a captured female kakapo in one hand and a recently found intact paper nautilus in the other. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/31</i>)

>  When he reached [White Island] the paper nautilus was coming ashore in hundreds. <i>(Dec 16, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### paper of lollies
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Some few nights back an inebriate delivered up a bunch of feathers, a paper of lollies, a song book, a pocket Testament, a photograph, and a peg-top. <i>(Nov 13, 1871,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The Minister got up like a father about to divide a paper of lollies among his family, and let them out for a run, as good boys who had worked well. <i>(Sep 20, 1906,Northern Advocate,2</i>)

>  The man was kneeling, with a paper of lollies in his left hand. <i>(Dec 8, 1864,Wellington Independent,2</i>)

>  Seeing the girl with the paper of lollies he accused the woman of the shop with selling on a holiday. <i>(Jan 28, 1897,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

### paper road
 
 <i>n.</i> with <i>paper</i> in the sense existing only on paper, on a map, nominal, paper-road, -town, -township are fairly common terms in New Zealand, though the usage is old in English (cf OED. 1d and 10d from 17c. : also W3 3)

>  In accordance with the provisions of the 94th clause of the Public Works Act, 1882, it is hereby notified that the Road, commonly known as a paper road, running between sections 43 and 57 and 44 and 58, Grey District, is closed as a Public Road. <i>(Jul 27, 1887,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  There was no formed road but a surveyed 'paper' road through the paddocks to the reserve. <i>(Aug 2004,NZ Memories,No. 49/53</i>)

>  Access is via a paper road. <i>(1996,NZ Whitewater,pp 174</i>)

>  The Magistrate said Atkinson had to realise that people could walk across his farm on these `paper' roads, even though stock might be affected. <i>(Jul 10, 1957,Press, The</i>)

>  Our last zizag breaks the spider threads spun from tree to tree and tenacious kihikihi provide the processional as we stride out along the paper road which takes us back to Fairfield. <i>(Jul 24, 2004,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

### paper town
 
 <i>n.</i> with <i>paper</i> in the sense existing only on paper, on a map, nominal, paper-road, -town, -township are fairly common terms in New Zealand, though the usage is old in English (cf OED. 1d and 10d from 17c. : also W3 3)

>  The site fixed upon, in Mr. Brame's paper township, is impracticable, and probably will ever remain so. <i>(Dec 22, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Mr Macandrew told his hearers at Port Chalmers a few days ago, when addressing them on the political situation, that the advancement of Liberal ideas in one particular part of the country depended on whether a railway was to enter at one end of a paper township. <i>(Sep 19, 1879,Clutha Leader,7</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> `Weld Town,' for example he noted `another paper town situated on the beach in Bruce Bay bight. ... Gillespies ... never even a paper township but entirely a digging one. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,15</i>) [NOTES: 1957 C. Douglas in Pascoe Explorer Douglas 15]

>  On Friday Mr. James Mackenzie, Commissioner of Crown Lands, and Mr. Heslop, member of the Taranaki Land Board, who had business at Takaora, went down as far as Ohawe township, a paper township of a class of which, by the way, Mr. Mackenzie thinks Taranaki has an inconveniently large supply. <i>(May 30, 1903,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  The "Boy Jones" assures us in the most peremptory manner that WELLINGTON IS NOT TO BE THE FUTURE CAPITAL! [sic] but that that privilege has been most craftily recommended for the <i>paper township</i> of Canterbury! <i>(Oct 18, 1850,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

### paper wipe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Some bleater of a doctor has now discovered that the pocket-handkerchief is a terrible danger as a disseminator of hostile germs and advises the use of paper wipes a la Japanese. If Wellington folk took to paper nose-rags they'd require to carry a pack on their back, especially when the weather was like that of Sunday ...last. <i>(Jul 21, 1906,NZ Truth,57/1/5</i>)

### paper-collar bushman
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf paper-collar swell](../C#cf-paper-collar-swell))

>  <b>MONEY</b> He was not one of these paper-collar bushmen, who expect their men to do every thing while they look on, but always took his share of work or of tucker as one of themselves, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,29</i>)

### paper-collared swell
 
 <i>n.</i> a white-collar worker; clerk cp broken-down swell.

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [The population of a goldfield] includes a section of the community known as the `paper-collared swells' who are the government officials, medical men etc. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,86</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> `The paper-collared swells' did not number ten. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,183</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Paper-collared swell.</i> A `white-collar worker' (esp. a clerk): New Zealand: ca. 1860-1900. A. Bathgate, <i>Colonial Experiences</i>, 1874-cited by B., 1941. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1129</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>paper collared swell</i> white collar worker, usually a clerk, probably <i>collar proud:</i> in Alexander Bathgate's <i>Colonial Expressions.</i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,82</i>)

### paper-run
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A smart boy is wanted for a paper run. <i>(Jun 30, 1908,Bush Advocate,5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to work a newspaper delivery round [also paper run n. - see DNZE cits.]

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> there's a fortune to be made paper-running at the diggings ... <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,133</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

### paperalia
 
 <i>n.</i> [A term probably coined by Christchurch Stamp Dealer, Laurie Franks [19**-19**], to cover non-philatelic paper-based oddments in his regular auctions (see quot). Since used for collectable paper ephemera including pamphlets (but seldom books), indentures, wills, engraved invoices, even sometimes bus, tram and train tickets. Term possibly derived by analogy with Marginalia.]

>  PAPERALIA. Books, Photos, Historical Documents, Old Prints, etc. <i>(Dec 17, 1985,Laurie Franks Ltd World General catalogue,[5]</i>)
