 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(Jan 1958,NZ Timber Journal,46/1</i>)

### para
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Marattia fraxinea</i>

>  Tkhe king fern or para (Marattia salicina) is indigenous to New Zealand and some Pacific Islands... Para is also a favoured food for wild pigs, which has contributed to its demise in the wild. <i>(Nov 6, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  The para (<i>Marattia fraxinea</i>) is not uncommon in gullies in the Maungataniwha Ranges. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/205</i>)

### para/paro
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk (short for paralytic)

>  <i>paralytic</i>: drunk <i>para</i>: drunk <i>paro</i>: drunk <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: Goldie Brown Collection]

### parachute hackle
 
 <i>n.</i> a feather wound around the wing of a fishing fly instead of around the hook.

>  Ted tied an Adams dry fly with a parachute hackle to imitate these insects pinned to the water by the wind. A parachute hackle is simply a feather wound around the wing of the fly instead of around the hook. <i>(Oct 29, 2005,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

### parachute kid
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  But Ho found that more than a third of Hong Kong Chinese aged under 20 who had been living in New Zealand less than five years were so-called "parachute kids", with one of both of their parents working back in Hong Kong. <i>(Sep 22, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

### paradise drake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  As I write I hear the quacking, or rather cawing, of a paradise drake ...[sic] The drake caws exactly like a 6d. woolly dog. <i>(Mar 28, 1867,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/237,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

### paradise duck
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Tadorna variegata</i> [=painted duck, =paradise goose, =parrie, =putangitangi.]

>  <b>DAVIS</b> He had been out deer-stalking when a paradise duck had foolishly committed suicide by flying in front of his foresight while he was squeezing the trigger on a young stag. <i>(1964,Watersiders,122</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> We found the large bay ... covered with paradise ducks which induced me naming it Duck Bay ... <i>(1909,Murihiku,196</i>) [NOTES: 1813 Sep Robert Williams's report in 1909 McNab Murihiku 196]

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> This day returned to Albion cove saw ... a Parot of brownish colour and a large white bird as large as a turky and a small sort of brown teal duck, a large black and white Duck called a Paradise Duck a black bird called the organ bird shags a Black Robin with a white breast ... <i>(Mar 9, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 9 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 359]

>  I found [round Wellington] Teal, common Wild Duck, the paradise Duck, a variety of the Grebe, a kind of Water Hen the proper name of which I do not know. <i>(Jun 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,230,1966 ed.</i>)

>  The Paradise and common duck, teal, widgeon, water-hen, and diver; are all found in great numbers on the rivers.; ... <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,47</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 59. Casarca variegata. ... Pooa dugghie dugghie of the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>Forst</i>. Putangi tangi of the natives of Cook's Strait; Paradise Duck of the settlers. <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/198</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 59.]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Putangitangi - Paradise duck <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/381</i>) [NOTES: (Dictionary NZ Language)]

>  <b>DILLON</b> The birds are caw-caw, a kind of parrot very good to eat, pigeons, and large ducks about the size of a small goose - the people here call them paradise ducks - <i>(Jan 16, 1843,Letters,18,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> In the afternoon, we ... were fortunate enough to shoot some `putangitangi' for supper. This bird has obtained the name of paradise duck, from the beauty of its plumage: ... <i>(Jan 15, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,217</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Walked along shingly beach ... to a large pool by the side of the sea, in which were swimming a number of Putangitangi, or Paradise Ducks, ... this being the season of their moulting. <i>(Jan 16, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 16 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part III (Church in the Colonies VIII) 12]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> These (wild ducks of different sorts) are principally the black, the grey, the blue-winged, and the paradise duck, or `pa tangi tangi', as it is called by the natives. The last is nearly as large as a goose, and of beautiful plumage <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/57</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> ... the paradise ducks come from here [to Okarito] from all quarters during the moulting season. <i>(1847,Great Journey,60,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> .. the <i>paradise duck</i>, or <i>Pu-tangi-tangi, (Caesarea variegata</i>,) is remarkable for its size, for the beauty of its plumage, and the delicacy of its flesh. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,164</i>)

>  Wild ducks, called `Paradise Ducks', nearly as large as a Goose, splendid eating. <i>(1848,Otago Journal,3/47</i>) [NOTES: (R. Donaldson)]

>  <b>McLEAN</b> We caught some young <i>putangitangi</i> or Paradise ducks. <i>(Dec 6, 1850,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Dec 6 Mclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 59]

>  (Torlesse.) ... the Paradise Duck, or New Zealand variegated goose. <i>(Jul 5, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> PUTAKITAKI .us (carsarca variegata),;a duck with beautiful plumage, rather larger than the common grey variety. It is very common in the south of New Zealand, and is generally known as the paradise duck. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,312</i>) [NOTES: (Vocab.)]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> There are also paradise ducks, hawks, terns, red-bills, and sand-pipers, seagulls, and occasionally, though very rarely, a quail. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,ix/133</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> I don't think a minute passed without a brace of wild ducks flying past, - grey, blue, and Paradise. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,181,1870 ed.</i>)

>  Grey ducks, Paradise ducks, (which are rather a species of geese) teal, and widgeon frequent the streams. ... Blue mountain-duck is found in the more elevated regions. <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,31</i>)

>  (Black Stewart Island) The following is a list of the birds shot ... <i>Platycercus auriceps</i>, yellow-top paroquet; <i>Nestor meridionalis</i>, brown parrot; <i>Carpophaga Novae Zealandiae</i>, wood pigeon; <i>Haematopus unicolor</i>, black oyster catcher, or red bill; <i>Haematopus longirostris</i>, pied oyster catcher; <i>Ocydromus australis</i>, Weka, or wood hen; <i>Casarca variegata</i>, Paradise duck; <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>)

>  <i>Casarca variegata</i>. - Putangitangi, Paradise Duck. Both Islands. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> CASARCA VARIEGATA. (NEW ZEALAND SHELDRAKE.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Putangitangi; Putakitaki in the South Island; `Paradise Duck' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,241,[1888 2nd edn 2/264] ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARKER</b> There are eleven species of Native Ducks belonging to nine genera, all found elsewhere, except two--the little Flightless Duck of the Auckland Islands (genus <i>Nesonetta</i>) and the Blue Mountain Duck (<i>Hymenolaemus</i>). Among the most interesting of the non-endemic forms, are the Paradise Duck or Sheldrake (<i>Casarca variegata</i>), the Brown Duck (<i>Anas chlorotis</i>), the Shoveller or Spoonbill duck (<i>Rhynchaspis variegata</i>), and the Scaup or Black Teal (<i>Fuligula Novae-Zealandiae</i>). <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,117</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Prof. Parker `Catalogue of New Zealand Exhibition' 117 in 1898 Morris Austral-English 128]

>  <b>WILSON</b> her precious Paradise ducks [fn Maori <i>Putangi tangi</i>] had discovered that their wings ... were now strong enough for flight ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,176</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Mar 9 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 176 ...]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Paradise-Duck<I/> ... bird-name applied to the New Zealand duck, <i>Casarca variegata</i>, Gmel. See Duck quotation, 1889, Parker. 1845. E. J. Wakefield, `Adventures in New Zealand,' c. 1. p. 57: <i>(1898,Austral English,340</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> (Birds S.W.) The Paradise [sic] Duck [F.N. <i>Casarca variegata</i>] in a flax bush. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,235</i>) [NOTES: c1899 (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 235]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Wood-pigeons (<i>kukupa</i>) and kaka parrots are found in the forests ..., and the handsome paradise-duck (<i>putangitangi</i>) and blue mountain duck (<i>whio</i>) are numerous in the streams and lakes [of Western Southern Alps]. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 64</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> PARADISE DUCK (<i>Casarca variegata</i>), Putangitangi of the Maoris, is a handsome bird variously mottled, freckled and lined with brown and white on a brownish to black ground. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  paradise duck A native New Zealand highly-coloured duck. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),290,8th ed.</i>)

>  <i> paradise duck or paradise sheldrake n : a highly colored New Zealand duck (<i> Tadorna variegata </i>) related to the sheldrake <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1636</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> PARADISE DUCK <i> Tadorna variegata </i> Other names: Putangitangi, Rangitata Goose. Description: 25' Of intermediate size between the ducks and the larger species (Canada Goose and swans). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,87</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Paradise duck .. .. putangitangi .. .. <i> Casarca variegata </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-12</i>)

>  <i> paradise-duck </i> ... n. large N.Z. duck (Maori, pui tangi tangi). <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i> paradise duck n.</i> a New Zealand duck with bright plumage. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,818</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 543; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 349]]



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [painted duck](p-per.md#painted-duck),[putangitangi](pop-pyo.md#putangitangi))

>  Both sanctuaries in the Botanical andWoodhaugh Gardens are live withwaterfowl, the grey duck (parera) beingespecially numerous though there are alsothe Paradise Duck (Putangitangi). <i>(Aug 16, 1933,NZ Tablet,27</i>)

>  There are many of the duck tribe, and among them the paradise duck (putangitangi) is particularly abundant. <i>(Aug 13, 1842,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,92</i>)

>  The killing of the paradise duck has been prohibited for one year from April 25th next. <i>(Dec 22, 1906,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  The hunters caught poaka (wild pigs), parera (grey duck), putakitaki (paradise duck), kereru or kuku (pigeon), and hurepo (bittern). <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,249</i>) [NOTES: Written of 1940]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The migrating landscape was a huge mob of moulting Paradise ducks - hundreds ofthem. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,107</i>)

>  Through the Sedgemere Gate and on to Molesworth, where mountains were parched, with shale slip flanks running down to the river and the narrow green flats where nothing grazes but hares, geese and paradise ducks. <i>(Nov 15, 1999,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  Paradise ducks. They mate, you know, for life. <i>(May 24, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> ...a pair of paradise duck, each distinct in the plumage of its gender, flew twice up and down the gully, giving their discordant cries. <i>(2007,Drybread,11</i>)

>  "Paradise ducks!" he said, delighted. "They're protected now." <i>(May 7, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  The paradise ducks have not occupied all of the North Island, and I take them to be very local. <i>(Nov 20, 1890,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  There were out on the plain very large "mobs" of paradise ducks. <i>(Nov 2, 1920,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> We enjoyed a delightful bathe ourselves, much to the distress of a family of paradise ducks. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,186</i>)

>  Mr. Hogg asked the Minister of Lands whether he had made inquiry into the alleged destruction of paradise ducks in the Mackenzie Country... <i>(Jul 29, 1908,Otago Witness,38</i>)

### paradise flapper
 
 <i>n.</i> a paradise duck

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> Seal killed a Paradise Flapper on a Lagoon - ... John killed a couple of Grebe which he got out ... <i>Podiceps</i> sp. <i>(Dec 27, 1856,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,60</i>)

### paradise shelduck
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> I can hear the resident pair of paradise shelducks honking to each other, female high and male low. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,8</i>)

>  Half a dozen paradise shelduck drifted in, honking in an adolescent way. <i>(Apr 17, 2004,Dominion Post,E11</i>)

>  There would be plenty of paradise shellducks around, as well as Canada geese in the Wairarapa area. <i>(May 4, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

>  The two pairs of paradise shelducks nesting in the wetland reared 17 chicks between them. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/22</i>)

### parae
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> We reached a little parae a small grassy plain shut in on every side by forest where we found two dry sheds ... <i>(Nov 1, 1852,Journal,8/49</i>)

### paraerae
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori sandals of plaited flax. [Maori paraerae[a1-macron]] (felt as pl)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> I had often looked forward with dread to the time when my shoes would be worn out, ... but now I can trudge along ... with a pair of native sandals, called y the natives <i>pairairai</i>, made of the leaves of flax, or, what is more durable, the leaves of the ti or flax tree. <i>(Oct 21, 1847,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Oct 21 Brunner Expedition Middle Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 288]

>  ... they [natives] wear shoes made of flax, which they call<i> paralas</i>; they are very humorous creatures ... <i>(Jul 20, 1848,Letters from Otago,16,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> PARAERAE, a sandal formed from the plaited leaves of the <i>phormium tenax</i>,;or the `ti' (<i>Cordyline Australis</i>).;Three different descriptions of sandal are made by the natives. That called `paraerae hou' or `kuara' is made of a single layer of plaited leaves of flax; that called `takitahi' is similarly made from the leaves of the `ti;' the third, called `torua' is made of the leaves of the `ti,' but plaited in a double layer, so as to be very durable. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,311</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The next day, ... the coast becoming very rocky, we exchanged our boots for Maori sandals (pereiras), which allowed better foothold on the smooth boulders. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,48</i>)

>  <b>HAMMET</b> Did some very rough climbing round the points of the cliffs. Impossible to get along with shoes on so that you must either have pararas or walk in your stockings. <i>(Aug 14, 1863,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Aug 14 Hammet Journal in 1863 Sep 1 Examiner (Nelson) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 102]

>  Bill made `parara' or Maori sandals of flax. <i>(1896,NZ Alpine Journal,2/9/155</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... to allow Bill [a Maori] to make himself some Maori sandals, or `parara' out of flax. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,231</i>)

>  Two pairs of sandals (<i>paraerae</i>), made from the plaited leaves of the cabbage-tree (<i>Cordyline</i>).[Found in cave at Upper Taieri.] <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/175</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Sandals made from the leaves of the cabbage-palm ... were sometimes made; they were called <i>parewai</i> in the North and<i> tahitahi</i> in the South Island. ... The southern name for a sandal generally was <i>paraerae ... </i> <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,240</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The whites would copy the native way of scraping flax for various purposes [c1840], and when their boots gave out would sometimes wear Maori `pararas' or sandals. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 He would walk down to Dunedin wearing pararas (Maori sandals) and carrying his boots ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,12</i>)

>  <i>pa[a]raha</i>, a climbing plant (Calystegia sepium), a convolvulus. The far-famed Te Rapauraha took his name from this plant as a reminder that when the body of his father was eaten by his enemies they used the leaf (rau) of this plant as a relish. another convolvulus (C. soldanella) is po[o]hue. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/156</i>)

>  We followed [the sealers returned c1870s from West Coast to Stewart Island] with our eyes admiring their new hand made seal skin caps with the crest of a penguin or some other Antarctic bird sewn on front. Still wearing their flax <i>pararos</i> as it takes a long time to get used to wearing boots after a sealing trip. ... [78] It is in such places that the sealer is seen at his very best; travelling at great pace over rugged sea-piled rocks ... with their feet covered with flax or grass mats tightly laced ... <i>(1930,Topsail Schooner 'Lark', The,73, 78</i>) [NOTES: c1930 Capt. William McKillop The Topsail Schooner `Lark' (MS Tasmanian Collection, State Library of Tasmania) 73, 78]

>  <b>SMITH</b> I secured a pair of native sandals or pararas. <i>(1940,Early Adventures in Otago,42</i>)

>  ... we did not see any traces of natives, till we came to Open Bay [near Jackson Bay], where on the beach we saw a broken spear & a pair of old parraras (a kind of sandal made of flax). <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,139,1979 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 139; see also c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 38]

>  shoes - pa|hra'rra [paraerae] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,111</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 111; also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 284]

>  ... sealers `still wearing their flax <i>pararos</i> making their way to their favourite public house on their return to Riverton ... as it takes a long time to get used to wearing boots after a sealing trip'. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,38,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: ?d W. McKillop The Topsail Schooner Lark 75-77 in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 38 [fn] [reference ?c1800, ?c1900]]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> This evening, Huruhuru made me a pair of sandals, such as are in common use among these natives. They are called `paraerae', and are made either with the leaves of the flax-[210]plant, or of the `ti' (cordyline Australis). ... Mine were a pair with double soles, called `torua', ... <i>(Jan 13, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,209</i>)

>  <b>W H S ROBERTS</b> They even had to make themselves paraerae - that is sandals made by plaiting flax leaves. [lit: planting: typo for plaiting] <i>(1914,History of North Otago,21,1978 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1906 W.H.S.Roberts in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 21]

>  <b>COWAN</b> Accidentally the [Maori] fugitives ... shod with <i>paraerae</i> or sandals of flax-leaves, ... crossed the dividing range, descended the valley of the Rakaia, and emerged on the Canterbury Plains. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 85</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Quite accidentally the fugitives discovered a pass between the Alps ..., and toiling on ... shod with <i>paraerae</i>, or sandals of flax-leaves, they crossed the divide. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,253</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Maori sandals of plaited flax. [Maori paraerae[a1-macron]] (felt as pl)

>  The party made sandals or paraerae of flax-leaves for the rough passage over the trackless heights of rook [sic] and snow. <i>(Dec 14, 1906,West Coast Times,4</i>)

>  ... we did not see any traces of natives, till we came to Open Bay [near Jackson Bay], where on the beach we saw a broken spear & a pair of old parraras (a kind of sandal made of flax). <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,139,1979 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 139; see also c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 38]

>  shoes - pa|hra'rra [paraerae] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,111</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 111; also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 284]

>  ... sealers `still wearing their flax <i>pararos</.i> making their way to their favourite public house on their return to Riverton ... as it takes a long time to get used to wearing boots after a sealing trip'. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,38,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: ?d W. McKillop The Topsail Schooner Lark 75-77 in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 38 [fn] [reference ?c1800, ?c1900]]

>  ... sealers `still wearing their flax <i>pararos</i> making their way to their favourite public house on their return to Riverton ... as it takes a long time to get used to wearing boots after a sealing trip'. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,38,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: ?d W. McKillop The Topsail Schooner Lark 75-77 in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 38 [fn] [reference ?c1800, ?c1900]]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> This evening, Huruhuru made me a pair of sandals, such as are in common use among these natives. They are called `paraerae', and are made either with the leaves of the flax-[210]plant, or of the `ti' (<i>cordyline Australis</i>). ... Mine were a pair with double soles, called `torua', ... <i>(Jan 13, 1844,Southern Districts of NZ,209</i>)

>  Milking the Goats cleaning the pannikins making the Tea <i>(Apr 23, 1841,Journal of Edward Ward,293,1966 ed.</i>)

>  Herries Beattie describes a different method: "This tussock was placed in the paraerae (sandal) and the foot inserted. Then the tussock was brought up the leg and tied above the ankle. This protection was called puru and the sandals were then known as tupuru." Patiti was also regarded as "a fine thing to put in the paraerae to keep the feet warm and if one was wading it was warmer with patiti around the feet than without it." <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 11/40</i>)

### paraffin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>paraffin</i> ... (normally called <i>kerosene</i> in NZ and Australia). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,544</i>)

### paraha
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> [Island Bay, Wellington] But always ... [sic] the sand had splayed out in fawn-coloured drifts, the pale paraha bells had trembled their taut mauve silk, elastic to the touch, against the wind. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,70,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>paraha,</i> a climbing plant (Calystegia sepium), a convolvulus. The far-famed Te Rauparaha took his name from this plant as a reminder that when the body of his father was eaten by his enemies they used the leaf (rau) of this plant as a relish. Another convolvulus (C. soldanella) is pohue. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: J.C.Andersen]

>  [Te Rauparaha's] name signifies a food leaf, the paraha. <i>(Dec 22, 1898,Otago Witness,31</i>)

### paraiwhara
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUNT</b> The aborigines term themselves Moriori, their conquerors still call them <i>paraiwharas</i>, or blackfellows - not from their complexions, ... - but, in their ideas, the term is synonymous with slavery. <i>(1866,Chatham Islands,29</i>)

### parakeet
 
 <i>n.</i> any of numerous usually small slender parrots with long, graduated tails. See also native parakeet [<i>Cyanorhamphus spp. (formerly Platycercus), KAKARIKI, BUSH PARAKEET (1843 Dieffenbach NZ) ee <i>C. malherbei</i> ORANGE-FRONTED P.; <i>C. auriceps</i>, YELLOW-FRONTED P.; and <i>C. novaezelandiae</i>, RED-FRONTED P.] (See [also kakariki](k-kea.md#kakariki))

>  <i>(Dec 4, 1907,Evening Post,8</i>) [NOTES: Those from the Auckland Islands closely resemble the New Zealand parakeet, but are much larger.]

>  The list covers the greater part of the indigenous birds, and practically includes the whole of the gulls and sea fowl, and all the smaller varieties of the land birds from the tui, huia, kiwi, and morepork downwards, such, for instance, as the fantail, wren, Robin, kingfisher, parrakeet, native lark, cuckoo, blue duck, etc., etc. <i>(May 16, 1906,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  Within the last three years all these birds, and the morepork as well (I said "all," that is a mistake, as I did not see or hear the parrakeet, that I have not seen for years), I have seen in the thick part of the bush on the side of Mount Cargill. <i>(Nov 25, 1908,Otago Witness,86</i>)

>  Surely a bird had swept by him in the loose bush, and that was the egregrious cackle of a parakeet far off! <i>(Jun 6, 1891,Star, The,1</i>)

>  Other toasts, both general and local, followed, the agricultural interest, the pastoral interest,and a number of other interests besides, and one gentleman convulsed the meeting by a speech on the subject of the <i>parakeets,</i> which are the pests of Long Bush at the present time, and like the rabbits of the west, are playing sad havoc with the products of both gardens and fields. <i>(Oct 24, 1873,Southland Times,3</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> In the morning ... I went ... to the Indian Cove, & shot there two new Kingfishers [[ed. fn] Now the new Zealand Kingfisher ...], some new yellow headed Flycatchers [[ed. fn] Now the Yellowhead <i>Mohoua ochrocephala</i> (Gmelin, 1789).], a fine large green and brown Pigeon [[ed. fn] Now the New Zealand pigeon ...] ..., a small Perrokeet, green with a red forehead ... [ed. fn] Now the Red-crowned Parakeet ... <i>(Apr 2, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/245,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1840,NZ Journal</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Let but a grass-green <i>parrakeet</i> alight/ To pluck from some wild <i>coffee</i>-bush in sight,/ And nibble .../ The scarlet berries; let some<i> kingfisher</i>/ Slip darting from the post .../ <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/154</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... to say nothing of Korimako and Pitoitoi ..., we never saw or heard the notes of either the Piopio, the Tieke, or the Hihi, all of which birds were at one time more numerous even than the Huia. The <i>Zosterops</i> was everywhere abundant, also the Grey Warbler and Rifleman, and ... we found the Tomtit comparatively plentiful; the Parakeet chased its mate through the tree-tops with sharp cries of `twenty-eight'; ... and among the tangle of the underwood the ever-present Flycatcher displayed its pretty fan-like tail. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/14</i>)

>  Platycercus pacificus. Kakariki. Parrakeet. Common. ... Platycercus auriceps. Parrakeet. Common. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  PLATYCERCUS NOVAE-ZEALANDIAE, <i>Sparrm</i>. - Parrakeet. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/125</i>)

>  Of these birds there are two species, the larger of the two being the red-fronted paroquet and the smaller the yellow-fronted species. Of the two, the red-headed [*check] species is the better linguist. <i>(Sep 9, 1890,Otago Witness,39</i>)

>  One of the bones came from a parakeet - Campbell's lost parrot at last. <i>(May 2005,NZ Geographic,No 73 / 9</i>)

>  <b>PARKER</b> There are three species of parakeet, the red-fronted (<i>Platycercus Novae-Zelandiae</i>), the yellow-fronted (<i>P. auriceps</i>), and the orange-fronted (<i>P. alpinus</i>). The genus Platycercus is found in New Zealand, New Guinea and Polynesia. <i>(1889,Catalogue of Vienna Exhibition,118</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Parrakeet, n.</i> (various spellings) The New Zealand Green Parrakeet (called also <i>Kakariki</i>, q.v.) has the following species - Antipodes Island P.- <i>tycercus unicolor,</i>. Orange-fronted P. <i>alpinus</i>ller Red-fronted P.- P. <i>novae-zelandiae,</i> Sparrm. Rowley's Parrakeet <i>Platycercus rowleyi,</i> Buller. Yellow-fronted P.- <i>P. auriceps</i>, Kuhl. <i>(1898,Austral English,341</i>)

>  We have also no less than four species of parrakeets ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,196</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kakariki. 1. </i>n. <i>Cyanorhamphus novae-zealandiae, small green parrot,</i> parakeet. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,108</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> Parrakeets were very common, and developed a keen appetite for fat. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,11</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> The liquid notes of tuis and moko-mokos rang out in unison, and kaw-kaws and [90] parakeets made splashes of colour as the sun caught their iridescent wings. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,89</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> Bush robins, parakeets, tomtits, grey warblers, riflemen, pigeons and the bush hawk are still to be seen. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,129</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The birds I noticed were the bell-bird, the blight-bird, the yellow-headed tit, the ground lark, the little parakeet, the banded dotterel, and the native snipe. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,275,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> In one [of Buller's aviary cages], there were parrakeets, yellow heads, New Zealand canary, fantails, robins and tom tits, and a pio pio (New Zealand thrush) one of our best songsters - ... <i>(1935,Annals of a NZ Family,129</i>)

>  <i>(Jun 1, 1966,Weekly News,17/1</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]

>  <b>HUNT</b> I heard a parakeet yesterday. <i>(1985,I'm Ninety-five</i>) [NOTES: (Wattie, Stewart I.)]

>  It (sc. <i>Copromea</i> scrub) was in fruit, and on the fruit were feeding two kinds of parrakeet, unknown in New Zealand. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,11</i>)

>  <b>PATRICK</b> Parakeets were then very numerous in Karori. <i>(1990,From Bush to Jubilee,16</i>) [NOTES: Quote from HOLLARD ?1890]

>  'Twas in the year of fifty-six / When some odd million paroquets / Came practising their evil tricks / About the bush Green Island. <i>(1889,Lay of the Old Identities, The,29</i>)

>  THE CHATHAM ISLANDS [From the <i>Lyttleton Times</i>.] .. The land birds now found are a large kite, the pigeon, the tui or Parson Bird, .. the parakeet (<i> Platycerus Sp.</i>[sic]) .. all identical with the birds from the same genera found in New Zealand. <i>(May 5, 1866,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  The parakeets were caught by dropping a hat on them . . . <i>(Dec 4, 1907,Evening Post,8</i>)

### paraki
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Retropinna retropinna</i>, smelt (freshwater)

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> Whitebait in pristine condition are beautiful... There are local names for them, too - elephant ears and Don Doolin 'bait (which isn't true whitebait at all), and cucumbers (which are actually smelt, Retropinna retropinna, or paraki). <i>(1989,Homeplaces,104</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> December is a glorious month of dietary amongst the natives on the coast ... The rivers, large or small, abound in eels, <i>hawera, upukuroro, haparu, patiki</i> and <i>parauki;</i> ... [fn] ... parauki is <i>Paraki</i> smelt (<i>Retropinna retropinna</i>). <i>(1848,Journal of an Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Brunner Expedition Middle Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 293]

>  Paraki - this is the small fish now called the `silvery'. It was described to me as smaller than the minnow and of a lighter colour. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 63]

>  19 RETROPINNA RETROPINNA (Richardson). <i>Smelt.</i> Called `paraki' by the Maoris of Arahura, on the west coast of the South Island. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/1</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <i>Vernacular Name</i>.- Powell [TrNZI 2/84] was the first to point out that two fish were referred to as `smelt' in north Canterbury. These were <i>Galaxias attenuatus</i> and <i>Retropinna retropinna.</i> At present Canterbury residents refer to <i>R. retropinna</i> as `silvery,' on account of the silver line running along the side of the half-grown fish. Maoris refer to this `silvery' as `tikihemi,' and to the adult as `paraki.' <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/291</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <i>Retropinna retropinna:</i> [European Name] Smelt [Maori Name] Paraki. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <i>Retropinna retropinna</i> (Richardson). Smelt. <i>Paraki. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/40</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> In some other localities these young [smelt, <i>Retropinna</i>] are called tuarenga. The half-grown fish is termed tikihemi and the adult paraki. Certain races of fish have a broad silvery band running along the side; hence the term `silvery.' It is to be regretted that in Canterbury the term smelt is still used to apply to the minnow, <i>Galaxias attenuatus. </i> <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,54</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> SMELT (PARAKI) <i>Retropinna retropinna</i> (Richardson) ... They are often referred to as Silvery, while in other parts they are called Cucumber Fish. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,113</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> <i>Paraki</i>, a small fish like white bait, caught at the mouth of Waitaki. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,312</i>)

### parako
 
 <i>n.</i> the fern <i>Asplenium obtusatum</i>

>  Mouki (<i>Asplenium bulbiferum</i>) and parako (<i>Asplenium obtusatum</i>) were used for skin complaints. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/77</i>)

### parakoka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> In Otago the general name of the [flax] plant, as a whole, is `Korari,' - or as metamorphosed by the settler, pronounced and spelt `Koura<i>d</i>i,' [a-macron] or `Coradi' [a-macron]. The same term is applied in the North Island equally to the whole plant, and to the dried flower-stem when cut for rafts or other purposes; or exclusively to the flower-stalk. In the North [83] Island, the Flax-plant is also known as `Harakeke,' or `Herekiakia,' - its usual name there. ... The lower part of the flower-stem is `Kaikaha;' the outer part of the leaf `Parakoka;' [a1, o-macron] the refuse of the leaf `Hunga-hunga;' the flax, dressed or not, or scraped fibre, manufactured from the leaf, `Whitau,' or `Muka.' <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,82</i>)

>  The principal sorts are large brown Parrots [ed. note [Kaka, <i>Nestor septentrionalis</i>.]] with white or greyish heads, green Parroquets, [ed. note [Red-fronted Parrakeet, <i>Cyanorhamphus novae-zelandiae.</i>]] with red foreheads, large wood Pigeons, ... <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/806,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/806 (Queen Charlotte Sound) [also entered at parroquet]]

### Paramatta
 
 <i>n.</i> a fabric

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>`A fabric like merino, of worsted and cotton. So named from Paramatta, a town near Sydney, New South Wales.' (Skeat, `Etymological Dictionary,' </i> s.v.) According to some, the place named Parramattameans, in the local Aboriginal dialect, `eels abound,' or `plenty of eels.' <i>(1898,Austral English,341</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Paramattas, fine cloths originally made from the Paramatta wool, with silk warps, though now woollen.' <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,19</i>)

### parani
 
 <i>n.</i> a native daisy, <i>Lagenifera petiolata</i>

>  <b>STARK</b> PARANI (New Zealand Daisy) <i>Lagenifera pumila </i>The Parani is a small native Daisy which is available in both the main islands of New Zealand. <i>(1979,Maori Herbal Remedies,75</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Lagenifera petiolata </i>Hook.f. Maori name: <i>Parani </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,42</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Lagenifera strangulata Parani</i> ... Very small rosettes or tufts in open patches ... Daisies white, but tiny ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,200</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> brandy [ORIGIN: Maori, transliteration]

>  "Rum" (Rama) and "Brandy" (Parani) are merely "tots" of humanity, the names of children, "reincarnations" of bottles perhaps held in high esteem by the parents. <i>(Jun 9, 1906,Evening Post,6</i>)

### paraoa
 
 <i>n.</i> the sperm whale

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the leviathans, who sport in shoals around these shores, the para paraua [a's-acute], or <i>sperm-whale</i> (Physeter macrocephalus); the tohora, or <i>right-whale </i>(Balaena bysticoetus); the mungu nui, or <i>black physeter</i>; the sun-fish (Diodonmola); <i>fin-back</i> (Balaena physalis); the <i>musculus</i>, or large-lipped whale; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/323</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> The paraoa or sperm whale, the pakake or humpback whale, and the black fish (a relative of the dolphin and the common porpoise) were used as food. <i>(1966,Maori Life and Custom,31</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Par|awa, s. A sperm whale; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,190</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Maori bread

>  bread - pahbah [paraoa] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 112]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> I saw the Maori Jesus/ ... His breath smelt of mussels and paraoa. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,347,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> paraoa <i>bread </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  Even food is glorified by the transfer of its name to fresh morsels of humankind. Braving the rain there are Hukas (Sugars), there are sweet girls dubbed Winika (Vinegar), there is Ti (Tea), Miraki (Milk), Paraoa (Flour), Arani (Orange). <i>(Jun 9, 1906,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>Patricia Grace</b> 'Mauria mai he riwai,' if they wanted spuds; 'Mauria mai he paraoa,' if they wanted bread. <i>(1980,It Used to be Green Once,46,Penguin 1986 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an object made from whalebone, often a patu or weapon.

>  He then seized another gun, which he threw away, and took a whalebone (paraoa). <i>(Jun 5, 1863,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  In keeping with the native traditional custom the following presents were made:- three korowai mats and one piu-piu by Mrs Mahuta, one korawai and two piu-pius by Mrs Ngakuru, one taiha, one patu paraoa (kotiatu). <i>(Aug 17, 1908,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  .. Te Moro went after him in the water, and in closing with him Matene said "Pai Marie, Pai Marie," when Moro dispatched him with a paraoa (whalebone weapon). <i>(Jun 4, 1864,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  These were brought down to Aramoho on Wednesday, together with a quantity of arms, guns, patu, paraoa, and grand mats, which were given up by the conquered tribes. <i>(Mar 18, 1865,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  .. but that he had sent his parawa to the Governor in token of submission, and that in future there was to be peace on the Wanganui river [sic]. <i>(Apr 12, 1865,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

>  [Reprinted from the Wellington <i>Spectator</i>, Sept.24.) .. The same ceremony was gone through by all the other chiefs present at the delivery, and was likwise performed on a patu paraoa, an instrument of war, also a very old heir-loom of Taratoa's tribe. <i>(Oct 8, 1853,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

### paraoa rewana
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> Local farmers had donated ... money to.purchase flour for the paraoa rewana ... <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,282</i>)

### parapara
 
 <i>n.</i> the bird-catching tree, a small tree of northern North Island, with long leaves and sticky fruit that sometimes entrap small birds. [Maori]

>  Commonly known plants that have insect-trapping defence mechanisms (usually sticky hairs) include tomato, potato, petunia, rhododendron, and our own notorious bird-catching tree parapara with its sticky seed pods. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/14</i>)

>  Parapara, also known as the birdcatcher tree, often snares fantails on its sticky pods. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/84</i>)

>  In our little bush, we have the parapara, or bird-eater <i>(Dec 16, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pisonia Sinclairii, <i>Hook. fil. ... Nat. name, `Parapara',</i> Col. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 209</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Para-para, <i>Col[enso]. Pisonia Sinclairii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <i>The Parapara. Pisonia umbellifera</i>, Seeman. ... This plant is found in several localities north of Whangarei ... <i>(1884,TrNZI,16/367</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[isonia] Brunoniana, Endl</i>. ... A glabrous shrub or small tree ... [576] <i>Parapara.</i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,575</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Pisonia Brunoniana (The Parapara)</i> ... Maori name <i>Para-para;</i> sometimes called by the settlers the <i>Bird-catching plant</i>. North Island: Auckland province chiefly. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,159,1940 p. 165 ed.</i>)

>  <i>parapara</i>, a tree (Pisonia Bruniniana), bird-catching tree: its sticky leaves are liable to entrap small birds; the tree does not eat them as the sundew does entrapped insects. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> 4. Parapara (<i>Heimerliodenron brunoniana</i>) leaves opposite, 4-15 in. long; ... fruits long thin, very viscous. [north N. Island]. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,163</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>PISONIA BRUNONIANA.</i> Parapara is a small tree of quick, rather soft growth carrying leaves up to 40cm long, of dark glossy green ... Nodular ridges down the length of the `fruit' ... secrete a glutinous substance ... Most spectacular is the ability of this tarry substance to hold small birds. ... anyone who has seen a waxeye struggling to free itself from amongst a cluster of these fruits can easily understand why parapara has earned the name `bird-catching tree'. ... Where it grows with pukanui (<i>Meryta sinclairii</i>) two of our largest-leaved trees come together. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,60</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> On the mainland as well as on islands, and extending to East Cape and beyond, are the small trees parapara or the `bird catching tree' (<i>Pisonia brunoniana</i>) and tawapou (<i>Planchonella costata</i>). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,110</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The Poor Knights are home to.. theparapara plant which traps small birdson its sticky seed pods. <i>(Apr 26, 1993,Evening Post,21</i>)

### paraparaua
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [paraoa](p-per.md#paraoa))

### parapox
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a viral disease affecting deer.

>  New Zealand is the first country in the world to report Parapox virus. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 31</i>)

>  .. further tests showed that the animals suffered from Parapox virus. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 32</i>)

>  A stag affected by Parapox virus with lesions on the antlers (caption.) <i>(1987,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 33</i>)

### parara
 
 <i>n.</i> the broad-billed prion, a sea-bird.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> We also got four kinds of Petrel in their burrows,- the Mutton Bird, Parara, Titi Wainui, and the Kuaka. <i>Ibid.</i> 201<i> Prion Vittatus</i> - Broad-billed Petrel - Parara or Parera. The latter word is elsewhere applied to the Grey Duck - Anas Superciliosa. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,16</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> These are the names used by the southern Maori; in the North Id. Kuaka, Parara and Titi are applied to other birds. Kuaka: <i>Pelicanoides Urinatrix</i>, or Diving Petrel. Black Bill. Wing 4.7 inches. Titi Wainui: <i>Prion Turtur</i> or Dove Petrel. Parara: <i>Prion vittatus</i>, or Broad-billed Petrel. Bill very wide. Wing, 9.5 inches. Titi: <i>Puffinus Griseus</i> or Mutton-Bird. Sooty brown. Bill horn colour. Wing, 12 inches. <i>(1940,Rakiura,205</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> ... I ... came upon Arthur , who'd just dug out two adult parara, another species of petrel, also called the broad-billed prion. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,53</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BROAD-BILLED PRION <i>Pachyptila vittata Other names</i>: Parara, Blue Billy (Chathams). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,38</i>)

>  <i>47. Pachyptila vittata vittata</i> (Forster, 1777) <i>Broad-billed Prion(Parara) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,24,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Broad-billed Prion Pachyptila vittata vittata</i> (FORSTER, 1777) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Whalebird, scooper, blue-billy, parara. ... Body blue-grey with conspicuous black open-pattern across upper wings.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,85</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> flax sandal (See [paraerae](p-per.md#paraerae))

>  <b>ROBERTS</b> rode down to Dunedin with a pair of pararas lined with red blanket instead of boots. <i>(1890,History of Oamaru & North Otago,7</i>) [NOTES: quote from 1853]

>  The word for flax sandals worn by some Maori at the turn of the century is pararas, pronounced on the West Coast and in Stewart Island as "palala". <i>(Jun 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [also para](p-per.md#para))

>  <i> parareka n</i> -s [Maori] : POTATO FERN <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1639</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### parataniwha
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Elatostema rugosum</i> begonia (fern)

>  [The king fern] often grows in association with supplejack (Ripogonum scandens) and parataniwha (Elatostema rugosum). <i>(Nov 6, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  Green parataniwha leaves, for example, produced anthocyanin in response to drought. <i>(Nov 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 60/62</i>)

>  In wetter parts of the forest you'll see the pyramidal buttresses of pukatea or New Zealand laurel, and glades of parataniwha, a native begonia with a distinctive pink blush on the long toothy leaves. <i>(Jan 2, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  Parataniwha (Elatostema rugosum) also known as the native Begonia . It's a little known foliage plant that loves damp shady conditions. Its leaves are a mix of khaki colours with serrated edges.. <i>(Aug 27, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D10</i>)

>  Elatostema rugosum, or parataniwha of the Maoris, is the name of the very distinct plant with bronzy-green foliage. <i>(Apr 27, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,56</i>)

>  There are even a few ramarama, small shrubs with trademark "bubbles" on their olive and red upper leaf surface, and a carpet of parataniwha. <i>(Dec 26, 2005,Dominion Post,B2</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Word list] parataniwa a slimy succulent plant growing in the dense forest <i>(Aug 1843,Journal,51</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Elatostemma <i>rugosum</i>, A.Cunn. ... HAB. Northern Island, ... Nat. name, `Parataniwha', <i>R.Cunn</i>. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 227</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Parataniwha, <i>Col[enso]. Elatostemma rugosum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names)</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Parataniwha, <i>Col[enso]. Elatostemma rugosum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  ... the roots of the Tikoraha (<i>Cordyline stricta</i>), were tied separately for baking ... for their Kao, or prepared sweet potatoes, they used the leaves of the Parataniwha (<i>tostemma rugosum</i>.. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/36</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>Elastostema] rugosum, A.Cunn.</i> Stems stout ... erect above, sparingly branched, 1-5 ft. high. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,636</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Elastostema rugosum (The Wrinkled Elatostema) </i>Maori name, <i>Parataniwha</i>. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,143</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Another herb with attractive foliage is the PARATANIWHA or so-called NATIVE BEGONIA (<i>Elastostema rugosum</i>), though it is in no way related to the begonia, ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,246,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>ELATOSTEMA RUGOSUM, parataniwha</i>. The botanically improbable name `begonia fern' is heard in the Far North. Like a begonia, the plant has thick rhizomes and juicy stems; ... Like a fern, it grows in great drooping cascades on damp banks ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,82</i>)

### parathruster
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HUGHES</b> We were part of the army's Ready Reaction Force and many of our men were rufty tufty parathrusters to boot. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,92</i>)

### parau
 
 <i>n.</i> a slave

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Near Matukutuki near the Mokau] The natives say that until lately these remote forests were infested with `Parau' native bushrangers or slaves who had run away from their masters and were guilty of every enormity, and woe to the unfortunate traveller who went alone through these forests. <i>(Feb 5, 1850,Journal,7/62</i>)

### parawai
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also paraoa](p-per.md#paraoa))

>  Te Teira then placed a parawai (bordered mat), at the Governor's feet, which his Excellency accepted. <i>(Mar 12, 1859,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  They thoroughly clean the flax, and it is the same description of flax that is used for the parawai, the korirangi, the hana, and all the other fine garments of the Maori. <i>(Dec 31, 1870,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The other garments of skins are the <i>huru huru</i>, and the <i>parawai</i>; the former is frequently worn, especially during the cold season, by the people of Cook's Straits and the districts south of Taupo [and is made of dog's skins] ... The other dress, called parawai, is exceedingly scarce; it comes from the Southern Island, and is made of strips of dog's fur, arranged indiscriminately all over a very large mat of the finest flax. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/323</i>)

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> The Governor accepted the offer, ... and Teira laid at His Excellency's feet a <i>Parawai</i> (a Taranaki Mat) as a symbol that the offer was accepted. <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,29</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He was seldom seen in any dress but the picturesque native garments of flax; and a commanding figure he was ... in a finely woven and beautifully patterned <i>parawai</i> or <i>kaitaka</i> cloak. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/134</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> He showed me several beautiful mats, kaitaka and parawai cloaks made of flax, with plaited borders; also a valuable mat made of kiwi feathers, and a patu-punamu, or greenstone axe. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,69,1933 ed.</i>)

>  The garment was a parawai or kaitaka cloak with a taniko border. <i>(Jul 30, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/103</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

### parcel
 
 <i>n.</i> a quantity or weight of gold

>  The Bank of New Zealand shipped their second parcel of Thames gold to England yesterday, per s.s. 'Pheobe,' from the Manukau. The parcel comprised 1,745oz., and a small box of silver containing 144oz. <i>(Feb 19, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> The first public sale of gold, the product of this province, was held at our auction mart, on Saturday [11 December 1852]. The parcel offered for sale comprised about six ounces of gold dust, and about ten ounces of auriferous quartz, dug by Mr. Coolahan's party, at the Wynyard diggings, Coromandel Harbour. <i>(1853,Auckland,106</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Dec 13 Cornell and Ridings Gold Circular in 1853 Swainson Auckland 106]

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> A few days after the sale [11 December 1852], the following Gold Circular was published, describing the quantity, character, and other particulars of the parcels of gold which had recently arrived from the [Thames] goldfield. <i>(1853,Auckland,105</i>)

>  <b>CAPT ANDERSON</b> They have succeeded in forming a small race by which they are enabled to form a sluice. Mr W. Parker produced a parcel of gold (five ounces) the proceeds of one day's work of his party of five. <i>(Jul 26, 1863,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1863 July 26 CAPT ANDERSON in 1948 Cowan Down the Years in the Maniototo 38]

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Amongst the things I abandoned was a couple of specimens which we got in the Little River, and a small parcel of gold, which we found in prospecting, ... <i>(May 14, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 May 14 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 413]

>  <b>2/174</b> One parcel of calcined stuff from the `John O'Groat,' weighing only 11 tons, yielded 11oz. 4dwt. retorted gold. <i>(1874,TrNZI</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> On one occasion I had bought a parcel of gold from the representative of a party of diggers ... <i>(1925,Random Recollections,28</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> On one occasion I met Matt in the town and he told me in a most secretive and confidential manner that he had come down with a nice parcel of gold. <i>(1964,Doctor in the Mountains,84,1974 ed.</i>)

>  A very rich parcel of specimens from the Prince Imperial mine was crushed on Saturday, 16 cwt. of stone producing 1967oz. of gold. <i>(Sep 22, 1884,Hawera & Normanby Star,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> floating tealeaves `strangers' ?particles

>  <b>FRAME</b> She handed him his special teacup on its matching saucer ... He took it, and blew the parcels from the top. She stayed a while, talking, while he drank his tea. <i>(1963,Reservoir, The,82</i>) [NOTES: [The Teacup]]



 
 <i>n.</i> ? a wine harvest

>  In Gisborne, the gewurtztraminer was "exceptionally good", and there were good parcels of chardonnay and merlot. <i>(Apr 29, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) area of land.

>  <b>TAIT</b> In 1896 A Rintoul bought out his brother's share, and added by purchase other parcels of land over the years until the holding consisted of 502 acres. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,422</i>)

>  <b>ECCLESTON</b> I got a job as head shepherd on a big Otago station. It was a mighty parcel of land. <i>(1977,Irish Kiwi,82</i>)

>  <b>ECCLESTON</b> So big were the so-called 'parcels of land' that it was considered easier to assess their areas, not in acres, but in square miles. <i>(1977,Irish Kiwi,88</i>)

>  "Keith Hall" is a highly productive parcel of land that Mr Ian Parks and his family have farmed for over 30 years as a fattening unit. <i>(May 4, 1993,NZ Farmer,29</i>)

>  They bought two parcels of land that they believed could really do justice to the label. <i>(Mar 24, 2001,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  Amazing views from a prestigious north facing parcel which has never been priced better. <i>(Dec 7, 2001,Hawke's Bay Property Guide,36</i>)

>  4.1 ha: Build the home of your dreams on this parcel with preferred views. <i>(Jan 31, 2002,Fencepost.com,Propertybrokers</i>)

>  By June 1999, Lundy had told Mr Porter he wanted to buy three parcels of land ... <i>(Feb 14, 2002,Dominion,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small pack of wool.

>  He objected to have a pound of wool taken off every parcel ... <i>(Sep 19, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,4</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> A preliminary lever was used to squeeze the wool to a fairly small 'parcel' ... <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,238</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (woolbuyers')

>  The [wool buyer] takes into account the assessed `yield' which he can expect from each type. (Yield is the amount of wool fibre left after the wool has been washed, and the foreign matter, grease, `suint' (sweat), dust, and vegetable matter have been removed.) The yield is expressed as a percentage of the whole `parcel' of wool. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,71</i>)

### parcel out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to divide land for selection.

>  .. - a block of the fattest land in the colony - was parcelled out amongst a syndicate of twelve gentlemen notoriously known as the Twelve Apostles. <i>(Oct 8, 1887,Weekly News,8</i>)

>  The Waimarino Block of nearly half a million acres is being surveyed at such a snail-like pace that at the present rate it will take ten years to get it finished and parcelled out to settlers. <i>(Oct 1890,NZ Farmer,409</i>)

### pare
 
 <i>n.</i> carved slab over the doorway of a whare

>  There was much debate over the propriety of his entering the building under a pare, which made prominent the figure of a woman with her organs exposed in exaggerated fashion. <i>(Mar 28, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/214</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <i>pare</i>, carved slab over the doorway of a whare. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]



 
 <i>n.</i> tupare, a shrub

>  <b>WEBSTER</b> The Maori call this shrub tupare [sic], and for Tuhoe its significance is that it [193] grows on the sacred mountain. The leaves were much prized as pare or head wreaths ... the main route winds among boulders ... to disappear into thickets of taupare.[sic] <i>(1979,Rua,192</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a woman's bodice

>  Writer Patricia Grace has one such treasure that is dear to her heart: a colourful woven pare (woman's bodice) which she constructed about 1955. <i>(Mar 29, 2003,Dominion Post,F2</i>)

### Pare
 
 <i>n.</i> Paremoremo Prison (See [also Parry](p-per.md#parry))

>  It's more than folklore, I'm told, that at least one child has been conceived in the Pare visitor's room. <i>(Dec 5, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Because we were all maximum security prisoners, our debates had to take place at Pare.<i>Ibid.</i> 71 The Pare audience was never reluctant to voice an opinion. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,70</i>)

>  Sometimes the heavies arrive ... crims edgy from Pare release, quick off the draw. <i>(1987,Metro Fiction,8</i>) [NOTES: (Keogh, Chiaoscuro)]

>  My mate and I are stuck in the maxi block at the moment waiting to be shipped up to Pari. <i>(Jul 29, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  [To a prisoner]. 'You know you're problem, don't you? You have the Pare syndrome.' I could never work out what the Pare syndrome was... But I assume the symptoms were an ability to think for oneself. <i>(Nov 4, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### parea
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) Chatham Island pigeon, Hemiphaga chathamensis . [ORIGIN: Maori, aka Chatham Island wood pigeon.]

>  The parea is the local version of the New Zealand wood pigeon or kereru. While it is relatively rare and confined to the south-west, numbers have grown in the past few years .. Parea are often seen on the ground feeding and sometimes nest close to the ground making them easy targets for feral cats. <i>(Dec 19, 2006,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.chathams.co.nz/content/view/47/96/]

>  The parea or Chatham Islands pigeon (<i> Hemiphaga chathamensis </i> ) is closely related to the kereru .. The parea population dropped to less than 50 in the 1980s, due to forest clearance and introduced predators ... <i>(Sep 2007,Internet - see Notes,2</i>) [NOTES: www.teara.govt.nz/TheBush/NativeBirdsandBats/LargeForestBirds/4/en]

>  One of New Zealand's most endangered birds, the Chatham Islands pigeon, or parea, may be making a quiet recovery. <i>(Oct 27, 1992,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  Mr Dix also fenced a piece of bush around his home to protect it from stock to "see more birdlife on the Chathams" [sic], especially wood pigeon (parea) around my home". <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 13</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Parekuraism
 
 <i>n.</i> a jargon-laden and unclear statement; a convoluted and not particularly enlightening answer or explanation (after Maori MP Parekura Horomia)

>  The remarkable feature of this row has been Horomia's style. His parliamentary answers were impenetrable. Having tried to deconstruct dozens of Parekura-isms, I have developed a theory: he tries to say several sentences at once. He can't bear to pick which sentence to say first, in case someone interrupts him, before he's managed to say the others. So he blurts it all out as a compound. <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Parekuraism of the week: "Ultimately the disconnect between the monitoring regime and a rationale for the monitoring regime was reflected in the absence of anyone addressing the possible contribution of information from the monitoring system to the accuracy of the PQs (parliamentary questions)." Deputy State Services Commissioner Tony Hartevelt wonder whether a lack of clarity might have contributed to Te Puna Kokiri's failings. <i>(Oct 20, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Parekuraism of the week. "The Health workforce advisory committee recently noted that, in 1991, reforms had rejected a developmental approach to the health workforce as being an aspect of provider capture and an unnecessary interference by the state in the marketplace." <i>(Apr 14, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### parera
 
 <i>n.</i> also called grey duck a brownish native duck with grey wing-feather tips.

>  The hunters caught poaka (wild pigs), parera (grey duck), putakitaki (paradise duck), kereru or kuku (pigeon), and hurepo (bittern). <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,249</i>) [NOTES: Written of 1940]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Anas ... superciliosa</i> ... Grey Duck. PARERA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,90</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>parera</i>: grey duck. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  <i>109. Anas superciliosa superciliosa</i> Gmelin, 1789 <i>Grey Duck (Parera) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,37,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Grey Duck Anas superciliosa</i> GMELIN, 1789 ... OTHER NAME: <i>Parera</i>. ... Until about 1960 grey ducks made up about 60 per cent of New Zealand's dabbling duck population. Now, with the introduced mallard overwhelmingly dominant, they comprise less than 20 per cent. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,144</i>)

>  <i>parera n</i>. grey NZ duck. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,545</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  [Names used for Air NZ fleet aircraft include:] ZK-NAR `PARERA' Grey Duck <i>(Jun 1988,Pacific Way,98</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The species we have identified in the Kaipara and Hokianga are - the Putangi, or `paradise-duck' (<i>Casarca variegata</i>); the grey duck, or Parera (<i>Anas superciliosa</i>); the brown duck, an allied species or variety of the last; the Papanga, or `teal,' or `widgeon' (<i>Fuligula N.Z.</i>), and some other varieties that may be imported birds, or crosses, or other native species. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/225</i>)

>  He has had a rough time of it surveying a block on the ranges up the Wai-o-hine in the Wairarapa ...[sic] Once ...[sic] they had nothing to eat but a tui and a more-pork. ... They were stewing the two birds when a kuku-pa (bush pigeon) flew up and perched. Bob shot it ... This is not exactly what the Britishers call `sport' - but there is more of nature about it than 30 gents in pink and tops ... waiting for a wretched little fox who, when he is killed, is not equal for culinary purposes to a more-pork. Once coming up the Waikato in 1851 your Uncle James got a pot-shot at a lot of parera (grey duck) ... <i>(Mar 22, 1894,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/597,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A duck Panada <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/339</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Par|era; A duck, goose, &c. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,190</i>)

>  The native grey duck or "parera".. is one of the Dominion's finest sporting birds. <i>(Aug 14, 1935,NZ Free Lance Annual,31</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>pareira</i>, or ducks, are principally found up deserted creeks, or unfrequented rivers; ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/299</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,407</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Plantago</i> sp. Common name: <i>Plantain</i> Maori names: <i>Kopakopa, parerarera </i>... [In Williams 5th ed.] the term `kopakopa' was identified as<i> Plantago</i> spp. but in the 7th ed. it is identified as <i>Myosotidium hortense</i>, ... Taylor and Andersen identified `kopakopa' as plantain ... <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,79</i>)

### Parihaka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Parihaka prisoners faired better onthe whole than their counterparts. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  It has even been said that Gandhi heard the Parihaka story and was suitably inspired... "Why do we celebrate Guy Fawkes Day? Why not have Parihaka Day?" <i>(May 1, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  [Hdg] Could Guy Fawkes Day become Parihaka Day? <i>(Dec 14, 2003,NZ Catholic,6</i>)

>  The district has been invaded by a partyof Parihaka Maoris, some 80 strong, inpursuit of that commercially mysteriousproduct known as fungus, and once styledin derision "Taranaki wool." <i>(Mar 23, 1899,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The story of the Taranaki village of Parihaka and its two visionary leaders, Te Whiti o Rongomai and Toku Kaka, whose campaign of passive resistance culminated in 1881 in the invasion and destruction of Parihaka by 1500 militia, has been a lightning rod for cultural and political energy in this country... The exhibition brings together for the first time the wealth of artwork inspired by Parihaka. <i>(Aug 26, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  As the author rightly says in his preface, Parihaka has achieved an "almost mythic status in our national consciousness"... Even his defiantly impartial hero, reading about Parihaka, is reminded of "what he had seen and experienced as a child and a youth in Ireland; the dawn horror of eviction; red skies and the smell of burning hovels; lamentations and anger; riots and his father dead on the waterlogged road." <i>(Aug 26, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG (ed.)</b> Again we gather in this place, here at Parihaka, on the marae of peace, to hear the word of God - we listen, we pray, we sing. <i>Ibid.</i> 37 The rape of Parihaka - a peaceful, and almost unarmed, defenceless village - was to be carried out not by British Imperial Forces but by true New Zealanders, and the invasion plans were hurried up because the Governor was out of the country. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,33</i>)

>  No one put up a fight - indeed, it was against the rules to have a weapon of war in Parihaka - but the passive gestures had their own power and poetry, as simple and effective as a Vietnam war protestor sliding a flower's stem down the barrel of a gun. <i>(May 1, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  This stone was deeply significant, astone by which to remember the pain ofthe land wars in Taranaki that culminatedin the destruction of Parihaka in 1881. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Paarihaka is new Zealand's legendary village of peace. ... It's no secret Parihaka is the crown jewel as far as tourism is concerned here in Taranaki, and probably the whole country. <i>(Nov 20, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  Those acquainted with the story will know how both Te Whiti and Tohu - the prophets of Parihaka - were imprisoned by the colonial authorities and how their lands were subsequently taken and confiscated. <i>(Dec 14, 2003,NZ Catholic,6</i>)

>  Scott stumbled onto Parihaka when he was in bed with chickenpox and happened to be reading a British high court law report. "A place called Parihaka was in it, and I couldn't believe it, it was all about libel action." <i>(Aug 10, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  Parihaka under Te Whiti is, in fact, a splendid example of a successful communistic settlement - held together, as "New Australia" could not be held, by "the religion of the thing." <i>(Apr 4, 1899,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  [Walker] believes that Parihaka was an immensely crucial event, a great symbolic moment not only in our history but in world history. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  There you have the whole process of how the Maori preserved his albatross, ready to be shipped to Parihaka, a donation from the Chathams to feed the yearly March 18 gathering of the tribes to hearTe Whiti's latest message to his race. <i>(Sep 14, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,18</i>)

>  One of the things I'd like to see come out of this [exhibition] is the awareness of people generally throughout Aotearoa, their understanding of what Parihaka means, and why it's so significant to the people of Taranaki. Many people really don't know much about Parihaka and its early history, or the implications of Parihaka. <i>(Nov 16, 2000,City Voice,Parihaka Supplement : [4]</i>)

>  At this time.. bands of native fanatics..were massing themselves together atParihaka, and singing paeans to thepseudo prophet Te Whiti. <i>(Jun 2, 1883,Auckland Weekly News</i>)

>  The passive resistance carried out by Te Whiti and the other brave people in this small Taranaki settlement of Parihaka against the might of the British and settler armies was a major event in colonial history... Hinchcliff's <i>Parihaka</i> brings to life the personalities and events of the 1880s. <i>(Jul 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C7</i>)

### parikau
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> There are two other small fresh-water fish, the parikou and the karengorengo, but little information is at hand concerning their form or habits. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,143</i>)

### parish
 
 <i>n.</i> in parts of North Auckland, Auckland and Gisborne a subdivision of a county. [R. Mason notes: on county maps of Auckland district some areas are `parishes', see `hamlet'] (See [sc. a survey land division](../S#sc.-a-survey-land-division))

>  Parish of Aotea, Queen's County - Allotments, [sic] Nos. 30, 42, 86, 144A. Parish of Arapohue, County of Marsden - Allotments Nos. 1A, 44, 74, 99, 101 ... <i>(Jun 9, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,7</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [`Terms of Purchase of Land in the Settlement of Otago' agreed on by the `Otago Association' and the Company] 11. Reservations to be made ... of the Sites of Villages and Towns ... in the Several Parishes and Hundreds ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,317</i>)

>  <b>STALLWORTHY</b> ... in the year 1894, a letter was received from the Lands and Survey Department, notifying that section 42, Kopuru Parish, had been gazetted an Aratapu Domain ... <i>(1916,Early Northern Wairoa,142</i>)

>  PARISH Under the old land-subdivision system in the Auckland Provincial District, a subdivision of a county. Still in use in parts of the North Auckland. Aucland and Gishorne Land Districts. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  There is to be a sale of lands in the Parish of Waitemata, at Auckland, on February 1, 1842. <i>(Nov 27, 1841,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  Notice is also given of the sale of general country lands on the 26th June, in the following districts:- Parish of Pupuke, county of Mongonui; parish of Hunua, county of Eden ... <i>(May 28, 1866,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### park
 
 <i>n.</i> constr. "the park", refers to various `parks' in various districts and among various groups: usually in cities to the premier rugby union venue); but also to racing or trotting (Hutt Park), or to various National Parks (e.g. among Auckland trampers and skiers, <i>Tongariro National Park. </i>

>  <b>MASON</b> <i>The Park</i>: Among Auckland trampers and skiers, means `Tongariro National Park'. <i>(Jun 7, 1952,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (Wellington)]

>  <i>The Park</i>. To all Auckland and Wellington mountain club members this is, of course, Tongariro National Park. This must be fairly old usage, but it does not appear in <i>The Tramper</i> until 1941. I remember it in Auckland about 1932. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,30</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail]



 
 <i>n.</i> a parking space; a place to park (a car) in. ca1960 at least [ca1982 Professor P.Trudgill had never heard it before arrival in NZ: eg to find a park for a car)]

>  Notices mean nothing to a driver in search of a handy park ... There were four cars parked right under the clearly marked notice: Private property .... <i>(Apr 4, 1963,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>park</i>: The place where one parks a car is called a park. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,74</i>)

### park one's ears to
 
 <i>phr.</i> to hear

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `Come off it. You wouldn't like your sister-in-law to park her ears to all I know about you. You and your bow-tie.' <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,125,1976 ed.</i>)

### park the pineapple
 
 <i>phr.</i> to urinate?

>  I'll say one thing for Queenslanders ... at least they yell `stand clear' before they park the pineapple on your boots. <i>(1979,Loosehead Len's Gluepot Greats in $1,54</i>)

### park up
 
 <i>v.</i> settle, retire to

>  ...he's thinking he, too, will "park up" on the mainland. <i>(Jun 26, 2004,Dominion Post,E2</i>)

### parka
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DICK</b> `Put your parka on, Bruce,' I called as a flash of lightning crackled along the telephone wires, ... <i>(1964,High Country Family,12</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... special clothing, including a short oilskin or <i>parka</i> ... and especially a <i>pack. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,161</i>)

>  A japara parker, bush shirt and waterproof peaked cap would complete the then fashionable [trampers'] outfit of the era. <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,68</i>) [NOTES: [25 years ago]]

>  <b>GILDERDALE</b> <i>parka </i>Windcheater, anorak. F.E. Dickie's <i>Snow in Summer</i> (1967) page 26. <i>(1982,Sea Change,241</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> The girl took boots and parka from the porch, and Tinsley put on his coat. <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,36</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [pakihi](p-per.md#pakihi))

### Parker's boy
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [Parkhurst boy](p-per.md#parkhurst-boy))

### Parkhurst boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a reformed juvenile offender sent from Parkhurst prison in the Isle of Wight to Auckland in the 1840s [ORIGIN: British dialect, also erroneously Parker's boy]

>  But while we wish never to see the face of another Convict, or Parkhurst Boy, arrive in this colony, we are still persuaded that it is quite practicable to convert these pests of society into benefactors of their race. <i>(Nov 25, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Then there were the Parkhurst boys, who had committed petty crimes, were reared, and then the people at Home sent out here a shipload of those boys. <i>(May 19, 1888,Te Aroha News,6</i>)

>  Curious enough we find, in a parallel column with the notice of the arrival of the Parkhurst boys, which we are glad to say was the only importation of the same class of characters, the following paragraph in reference to the 'Jane Gifford' and 'Duchess of Argylle,' the immigrants by which ships so recently celebrated the twentieth anniversary of their arrival in the colony. <i>(Oct 31, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,9</i>)

>  THE PARKHURST BOYS.Among the circumstances which have contributed to the popularity of New Zealand as an emigration-field with intending colonists at home, its total freedom from the baneful effects of "Convictism" is, probably the most powerful. <i>(Dec 10, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  <b>PHILIPS</b> A great deal of mischief was done ...by the importation of `Parkhurst Boys, as they were termed, who were sent out from home a reformatory. <i>(1886,Memoirs of the Post,11</i>) [NOTES: [1850s] 1897 [? pub 1886] Philips Memoirs of the Post 11]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> A number of boys, reformed juvenile offenders, from the Parkhurst prison in the Isle of Wight, [286] have from time to time been sent out to Auckland, for the purpose of being apprenticed to such of the settlers as would take them; ... <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/285</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> [In 1842] when the secretary of state appointed a judge and as attorney general for New Zealand, he sent out one hundred Parkhurst boys as emigrants; and these reformed prisoners, in one year after their arrival, doubled the felony cases in the colony, ... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,2/65</i>)

>  <b>BEASLEY</b> Runaway sailors, escaped convicts from Sydney, (locally known as `Parker's Boys' on account of the association of some of them with Parker's Gaol in Sydney) and others of a similar type provided the principal labour supply [in Auckland] <i>(1936,Pioneering Days,18</i>) [NOTES: [c1840] 1936-7 Beasley Pioneering Days 18]

>  Another party of natives had set out along the coast, and towards evening returned, having succeeded in overtaking and capturing two more of the gang, "Wilson," [sic] <i>alias</i> Hicks, and a <i>Parkhurst</i> boy. The three former were committed to take their trial, and the Parkhurst boy remanded for further examination. <i>(Dec 14, 1844,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The prisoner, George Smith, who was sentenced to thirty days' imprisoment [sic] for the assault, is a Parkhurst boy, and came from Auckland. <i>(Jul 18, 1846,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

### parkihi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pakihi](p-per.md#pakihi))

### parking
 
 <i>n.</i> petting on a park bench

>  The boys who take me out take it for granted that we should go parking afterwards. <i>(1951,Sports Post,7</i>)

### Parkinson's rata
 
 <i>n.</i> [<i>Metrosideros parkensonii</i>]

>  Another excitement is the flowering of the shrubby rata, Metrosideros parkinsonii, which has springtime, vermilion red bottle-brush flowers from September to November. This rare tree produces its flowers on its old bare stalks, something no other rata or pohutukawa does... In nature, this shrubby rata is found only in Great Barrier Island and in northwest Nelson. <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Radio NZ / National Radio,E13</i>)

>  There are ten native species of rata in New Zealand all in the genus Metrosideros. Four of these form trees (northern rata, southern rata, Bartlett's rata and Parkinson's rata) and the remaining six species are all lianes. <i>(Jan 21, 2010,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzpcn.org.nz/page.asp?flora_vascular_flowering_plants_dicots_rata]

### Parks and Reserves
 
 <i>n.</i> the local body department which looked after council parks and reserves

>  <b>McCAULEY</b> Then there was a smarmy-looking young guy who worked for Parks and Reserves. <i>Ibid.</i> 109 Parks and Reserves volunteered to trail them and no one tried to dissuade him. <i>(1990,Bad Music,107</i>)

### Parliament
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  In constitutional terms, the word "Parliament" refers to all of the people needed to pass legislation - that is, both the 120 members of the House or Representatives (known as Members or Parliament, or MPs) and the Sovereign (the British monarch, respresented in New Zealand by the Governor-General). <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/50</i>)

>  "Parliament" also refers to a physical place: most specifically the debating chamber where MPs meet, also the building that contains the chamber, and more generally still this building and the several others in which MPs have their offices. This complex consist of four buildings: the Parliamentary Library, Parliament House, the Beehive and a 22-storey high-rise called Bowen House. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/50</i>)

>  <i>parliament ... (Parliament)</i> council forming the supreme legislature of NZ, consisting of the Governor-General and the House of Representatives. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,546</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 822]]

### Parliament Building(s)
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> Once Government House occupied part of the large and sprawling territory now covered by the Parliamentary Buildings. <i>(1934,Journalese,30</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> ... they met in the lounge of what proved to be a superior hotel near Parliament Building. `Very swept up,' he apologised. `But a lot of the bars are a bit embarrassing for women.' <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,150</i>)

>  Justice Minister Geoffery Palmer ... has prevailed in his popular campaign to save two chippies from extinction with the advent of the new High Court building opposite Parliament Buildings. ... Mr Palmer says he hopes the spirit of the former fish and chip shop will nevertheless live on ... <i>(Aug 10, 1988,Dominion,1</i>)

### Parliamentary Commissioner
 
 <i>n.</i> an officer of parliament, appointed by the Governor-General on the recommendation of the House of Representatives. [Jim Cameron writes : Britain has a PC for Administration (the ombudsman), a collocation recorded in NSOD. In NZ the ombudsmen's formal alternative title (almost never used) is PC for Investigations. There is also a <i>Parliamentary Commissioner for the Environment</i>.]

### Parliamentary Service Commission
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Most of the perks are administered by the Parliamentary Service Commission, a group of senior MPs who gather together to bilk the taxpayer. <i>(Oct 26, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C10</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

The word comes from the north of Scotland and has never been in very general use here. (Cf. <i>poorly?</i>) (C.) <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]
