 <i>n.</i> [ca1950 (and earlier) Northland (Irene) Cheap cotton (usually working) trousers ca1935 Havelock.]

### Parnell
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  `I think it is important,' he said, `that people get a perspective of me as not someone that comes from a background of pink gins, quiche and Parnell but someone who had to come up the river in a cabbage boat. <i>(Sep 26, 1987,Evening Post,2</i>)

### Parnell grit
 
 <i>n.</i> a rock classification

>  Mr M.R.Gregory - a study of Waitemata rocks in the western area of Auckland City, including an examination of the relationship of the Manakau Breccia and Parnell Grit; . . . . <i>(Dec 1961,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,11/3</i>)

>  This bed, well-known as the "Parnellgrit," consists of sea-born ashes andmud, and occasionally angular fragmentsof solid hornblende and augite-andesitelavas, often several inches in diameter. <i>(1898,AJHR,C-9/39</i>)

### Parnell shout
 
 <i>n.</i> a Dutch treat [->ca1930 Auckland (P.S.Ardern) -->ca1945 Auckland (R.Mason); ca1940 Auckland (Raydia Farquhar) (from the once decaying genteel but proud suburb of Auckland - from the 1930's on, poor but independent; now (1984) a thriving trendy area.)] (See [also Dutch shout](dou-dyn.md#dutch-shout),[Yankee shout](y-yc.md#yankee-shout))

>  Finally, they resolved themselves into a committee of ways and means, and escaped from the dilemma by what is vulgarly known as a 'Parnell shout.' <i>(Oct 1, 1892,Observer (Auckland),4</i>)

>  This week's password is "Jamie Woodser"... A "Jamie Woodser" is the equivalent of a "Parnell shout" which is the only kind of "shout" now permitted. <i>(Sep 1, 1916,Press, The,6</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Parnell shout</i> paying for oneself, going Dutch on an outing. Mostly pre-WWII Auckland. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,78</i>)

### Parnellese
 
 <i>n.</i> words, style, and manners relating to the suburb of Parnell in Auckland.

>  She filmed the video to the Philistines frame by frame, entirely out of photographs of her portraying both a missionary and a Maori in colonial New Zealand, reflecting her self-stylised position as Parnellese, as her father's family hails from Parnell. <i>(Oct 15, 2008,Scoop,1</i>)

>  Her extraordinary facial features hint at a heritage that includes Guyanese, English and, as she puts it, Parnellese. "My father was from Parnell and my grandmother still lives there. It always takes people by surprise when I tell them I'm part-Parnellese," she jokes, with an impressively filthy laugh. <i>(Mar 22, 2009,Scoop,14</i>)

### paro
 
 <i>n.</i> a basket

>  The women attended to ... the coarse weaving of small baskets (<i>paro</i>) of green [9] flax, as dishes for their food ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/8-9</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Maori races)]

### paroa
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of biennial rye-grass.

>  <b>LEVY</b> Dominance of any one species, excepting perhaps the very palatable Paroa Italian ryegrass and Manawa ryegrass (short rotation) influences palatability. 183 For primary forest burn Paroa Italian ryegrass. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,135</i>)

>  Ryegrass cultivars differ markedly in susceptibility. Ruanui and Ariki are often more severely infected [with blind seed disease] than Manawa and Paroa, probably because the latter two cultivars flower later. <i>(Oct 19, 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>)

>  Our work has demonstrated that Nui ryegrass is severely affected when sown under the biennial ryegrass Paroa, less so under Tama, and most successful with Rahu ryecorn and Amuri oats. <i>(Mar 1979,NZ Journal of Agriculture,58</i>)

### parore
 
 <i>n.</i> a northern New Zealand sea-fish. [Maori] , also called blackfish, black rock-fish, mangrove fish, shit-fish [<i>Girella tricuspidata</i>]

>  I love that creek. It is full of mangroves, and yellow-eyed mullets, and eels, and ducks, and crabs, and kingfishers, and five herons, two good shags, and so far I have observed one particularly fat parore poking its fishy snoot beside a bank at high tide. <i>(Mar 11, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family GIRELLIDAE. Girella tricuspidata ... , the blackfi sh or parore, is another seaweed eater; dark brown above, lighter beneath, striped in life, it inhabits warm northern waters. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> PARORE One of our most common and most neglected fish. The Parore is eagerly sought in Australia where some anglers fish for nothing else. He is called a Nigger or Luderick there and catching him is a specialised art. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,174</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>PARORE</i> (Blackfish, luderick) <i>Girella tricuspidata </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,229,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> The sea also brought them snapper, mullet, parore and flounder; whitebait in season; and oysters, pipi and karahu (periwinkles). <i>(1983,Whina,38</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Parore Girella tricuspidata</i> Known variously as blackfish, black snapper, black bream, and mangrove fish. Also present around southern Australia and known as luderick. ... Present around shallow reefs and mangrove estuaries of northern New Zealand ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,1986</i>)

>  [species name] parore .. [scientific name] <i>Girella tricuspidata</i> .. [other name(s)] black bream, blackfish, black snapper, old maids, mangrove fish, pacific bream <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <i>parore n. NZ</i> dark brown fish found in estuaries and coastal regions. Also called <i>blackfish</i>. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  I remember as a kid dropping stuff down the hole [of the Rawene wharf toilet - a hole over the water] into a seething mass of parores. <i>(May 24, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Spectrum Documentary Rawene (Northland)]

>  Sometimes men, using bread for bait, caught parore [off Point Chevalier], which we called black snapper or `shit-fish', because they were reputed to eat at sewer outlets. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] parore 1498-1523 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Par|ore, <i>s</i>. a fish so called; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,190</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The whole of the long range of the New Zealand Coast is abundantly supplied with beautiful fish. Of this variety of 78 species I will name a part, viz. ... Parore, a white fish 6 feet long and 4 inches wide, small tail; ... <i>(1843,NZ Journal,40,1956 ed.</i>)

>  English Name Native Name Rock Cod Whapuka Snapper Tamuri Black rock-fish Parore ... ... <i>(1849,NZ Journal,(Vol. IX) 125</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5)]

>  Parore, or Mangrove Fish. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/245</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> PARORE OR MANGROVE FISH ... A fish 18in long, Dr. Hector says, with black bands on a dark ground; height equal to half the length; peritoneum black. Does not take bait, but frequents rocks among the mangroves at high water. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,70</i>)

>  GIRELLA TRICUSPIDATA (Quoy and Gaimard). <i>Mangrove-fish; Parore</i>. Very common in the vicinity of kelp and mangrove, North Auckland Peninsula. Distribution: Common from Bay of Plenty northward. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/116</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Girella tricuspidata</i> (Quoy and Gaimard). BLACKFISH; PARORE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,36,1971 ed.</i>)

>  ... and its flavour resembles that of its commoner relative, the parore <i>(Girella multilineata). </i> <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,9/290</i>) [NOTES: (Sladden and Falla)]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE BLACKFISH <i>Girella tricuspidata (Quoy & Gaimard)</i> ... Maori name: PARORE. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,103</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Parore, mangrove fish or blackfish .. .. parore .. .. <i>Girella tricuspidata</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> PARORE ... <i>Other names: Girella tricuspidata;</i> blackfish, black perch; black bream (Tasmania). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,242</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> PARORE [<i>Girella tricuspidata</i>] ... In Australia blackfish, black perch, black bream are names in wide use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,42</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] parore, black 1501 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### parra
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of parera q.v.

>  It was a parra duck, a pretty little grey, timid-looking thing. <i>(Nov 1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,117</i>)

### Parrie
 
 <i>n.</i> Paremoremo Prison [ORIGIN: abbreviation] (See [Parry](p-per.md#parry))

### parrie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a paradise duck [ORIGIN: abbreviation, also pari]

>  <b>MILLER</b> ... the melancholy honk of a flock of `Parries' flying overhead ... accentuated the eeriness of the night ... <i>(1946,There Was Gold,136</i>)

>  [Reminiscences] We were unable to eat even a morsel of our parrie stew [reminiscences of c. 1932] <i>(Aug 1950,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,4/19/77</i>)

>  It's a great thrill to see the colourful paradise duck now so widespread and so numerous throughout the country. ... For many years, the pari has been considered just another duck. Bag limits and season length restriction for grey duck, mallard, shoceler and the pari were always lumped together, and it was never considered remarkable. <i>(Jul 5, 1981,NZ Times,13</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> `A paradise drake,' he said, ... somebody must have killed [the duck] because the old parries [short for paradise][sic] mate for life. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,97</i>)

>  <i>Paradise Shelduck Tadorna variegata </i>(GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Paradise duck, pari, putangitangi. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,142</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Much of what we saw was left to the pukeko in particular, but also the weka, blue and white heron, a pair of paris (paradise ducks) ... and the mushrooms. <i>(1985,G'day Country,149</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Then again, you can become profoundly irritated with the habits of paradise ducks that will insist on honking drearily overhead. In some valleys a discordant orchestra of parries follows you for hours. <i>(1988,Hills, The,32</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paradise duck, Casarca variegata.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> parrie: a paradise duck (casarca variegata). Actually a species of goose, it frequents the back-country riverbeds. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,59</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  49. <i>Platycercus alpinus</i>, Buller. Orange-fronted Parroquet. - Amongst the large flocks of parroquets which spread over the country during the last summer, this small bird was fairly represented, but was not at any time so abundant as either of the two preceding species. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Sceptic</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  49. <i>Platycercus alpinus</i>, Buller. Orange-fronted Parroquet. - Amongst the large flocks of parroquets which spread over the country during the last summer, this small bird was fairly represented, but was not at any time so abundant as either of the two preceding species. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,480</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  47. <i>Platycercus novae-zealandiae</i>, Sparrm. Red-fronted Parroquet, Kakariki. - Years ago it was very common in the numerous Fagus woods in the Malvern district ... <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,480</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  50. <i>Platycercus Rowleyi</i>, Buller. Small Red-headed Parroquet. - It may be of interest to give the measurements of a specimen obtained here on the 16th January last; ... <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  48. <i>Platycercus auriceps</i>, Kuhl. Yellow-fronted Parroquet, Kakariki. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,480</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

### parroquet / paroquet
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [parakeet](p-per.md#parakeet))

>  A person sued another for GBP2 for the detention of a paroquet. The defendant's counsel asked that the plaintiff be non-suited, on the ground that the paroquet came under the head of <i>ferae naturae</i>, and that any such bird on escaping from its custodian became <i>res nullius</i>, and could not be the subject of larceny or a civil action. <i>(Nov 17, 1888,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The following is a list of the birds shot ... Platycercus auriceps, yellow-top paroquet; Nestor meridionalis, brown parrot; Carpophaga Novae Zealandiae, wood pigeon; Haematopus unicolor, black oyster catcher, or red bill; Haematopus longirostris, pied oyster catcher; Ocydromus australis, Weka, or wood hen; Casarca variegata, Paradise duck; Notes (Black Stewart Island) <i>(1872,Appendix Canterbury Provincial Council,39</i>)

>  <b>Hutton</b> 49. PLATYCERCUS AVRICEPS. Kuhl. Yellow-top Parroquet. Kaka[r]iki. P. alpinus. BULLER <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,19</i>)

>  Following is the prize-list for the Dominion Canary Specialist Club Show, held in St. Thomas's Hall, Newtown, on Saturday :- .. Paroquet. - Miss Petterson 1. <i>(Feb 1, 1915,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Mrs Light's Special 5s, for best Parrot or Paroquet in show. <i>(Jul 27, 1905,Bush Advocate,5</i>)

>  <b>DOUGALL</b> Penguins and paroquets abound [at Antipodes Island]. <i>(1888,Far South,17</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> There are great numbers of large grey Parrots that have very beautiful plumage and small Parroquets flying in great plenty so that I frequently killed two or three at one shot. <i>(Apr 12, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 April 12 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/206]

>  <b>BAYLY</b> During our ramble [in Queen Charlotte's Sound] I saw Wood Pigeons, Parroquets, Grey Parrots, Poey Birds & Quails & vast variety of singing Birds but no animal great or small or any fruit Trees of any kind whatever. <i>(Nov 15, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Nov 15 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/213]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The principal sorts are large brown Parrots [ed. note [Kaka, <i>Nestor septentrionalis</i>.]] with white or greyish heads, green Parroquets, [ed. note [Red-fronted Parrakeet, <i>Cyanorhamphus novae-zelandiae</i>.]] with red foreheads, large wood Pigeons, ... <i>(Feb 1777,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/806 (Queen Charlotte Sound)]

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> We saw two or three beautiful species of the parrot and the paroquet, a small bird resembling the sparrow; .. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/255</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> Among them are many sorts of wild ducks, large wood-pigeons, sea gulls, rails, parrots, and parroquets. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,187</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> See here are met .../ The robin's chirp and lintie's trill,/ And mocking bird, with fantail heard,/ And parroquet's green clamouring shrill. <i>(1912,Castle Gay,60</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ...New Zealand Crow - Kokako of the natives. Parrot - ... or kaka of the natives. Paroquets - two kinds, ... called Kakariki by natives. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,65</i>)

>  There are [in West Southland] green parroquets, large hawks, and several kinds of wild ducks, besides Emus, green birds & woodhens, which are birds of an excellent flavour. <i>(1828,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1828 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 222]

>  The birds are not remarkably musical - the linnets are rather so; The Bell-Bird, is of a purple colour, with a white spot on the breast, shaped like a bell. The Saddle back, is a brown bird with a bright red spot on its back like a saddle, these two last species, are about the size of a thrush. There are green parroquets, large hawks, and several kinds of wild ducks, besides Emus, greenbirds and woodhens, which are birds of excellent flavour. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,104,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Powaitere</i> - A Parrot, or Paroquet. - Of these birds there are several kinds; all of them small ... nearly the same in plumage - a bright green; yellow or red under the throat and tail; and red or yellow about the head. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),54</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Parroquets</i>, with crimson-feathered heads and breasts, with silver gray, purple, and green bodies; <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/297</i>)

>  Indeed the number of parroquets seen flying about gave it [Middle] rather the appearance of a tropical island. The cassiowary has also been seen in different parts of the island, ... <i>(1841,GBPP,(No.311) 106</i>) [NOTES: 1840 June 28 Thomas Bunbury's Report in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 106]

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> The parroquets are usually green, with yellow and red about the neck and tail. <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,28</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... perroquet <i>(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/30</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> We frequently observed among the branches a small green parroquet (Trichoglossus aurifrons), which was so tame as scarcely to move at our approach. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/76</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>Kakariki</i>.- There are two species of this little green parroquet, (<i>Platycercus Novae Zelandiae</i> and <i>Trichoglossus Aurifrons</i>) neither of them much larger than a canary bird. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,163</i>)

>  There are a great many pigeons and a small green bird called the paroquet, also quails ... <i>(Oct 27, 1849,Letters from Otago,41,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Saw several New Zealand Parroquets, a King-fisher, a Mocking bird but none near enough to observe. <i>(1853,Journal,1/149</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> There was sufficient wood, however, to harbour abundance of parroquets - brilliant little glossy green fellows, ... <i>(Mar 24, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/58</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> In the woods of New Zealand several small species of green parroquets occur. <i>(1866,Polynesia,63</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... the hoarse croak of the ka-ka ... The air was gay with bright green parroquets flitting about ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,93,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Several kinds of birds are indigenous to the woods and waters; among them the oyster-catcher, bittern, kingfisher, cormorant, quail, wild-duck, mocking or [16] parson bird, parrots, parroquets, woodhen, pigeon, etc. (the [sic] gigantic Moa bird is believed to be now quite extinct). <i>(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,15</i>)

>  The Parroquet may be frequently observed in confinement, and the Tui ... has to exchange for the dewy nectar of bush flowers, a monotonous diet of soaked bread ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/64 PLATYCERCUS NOVAE ZELANDIAE, Sparrm. Kakariki. Parroquet. ... PLATYCERCUS AURICEPS, Kuhl. Kakariki. Parroquet. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/44</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 48. PLATYCERCUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Sparrm. Crimsom-top Parroquet. Kaka[r]iki. <i>P. Forsteri</i>. FINSCH. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,19</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Poowhaitere</i>, n. <i>paroquet. </i> <i>(1871,Dictionary of the NZ Language,122</i>)

>  The first year after it had been completed the roof was entirely torn off by an invading army of kakas and native parrots, which came there in thousands just as the paraquets have done in more recent times. <i>(1894,Oamaru Mail</i>) [NOTES: 1894 Oamaru Mail in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 32]

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... and if one is hard pushed for food, the smaller birds, such as the crow, tui, paraquet, and saddle-backs, are all acceptable. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,41</i>)

>  Besides the tui, the woods were enlivened by the kakariki (<i>Platycercus, N.Z.</i>), a little green parroquet, who, swaying under his green canopy, utters a peculiar shrill cry. <i>(Nov 21, 1863,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

### parroquet's beak
 
 <i>n.</i> kaka beak, parrot's beak

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Clianthus puniceus (The Scarlet Clianthus)</i>... This tree [shrub] is known as the <i>Red Kowhai, Parrot's Bill</i>, or <i>Kaka's Beak</i> Native name <i>Kowhai-ngutu-kaka</i>, signifying the <i>Parrot-beaked kowhai. </i>It is also called in the Urewera Country <i>Ngutu-Kakariki,</i> the <i>Parroquet's Beak. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,210,1940 p. 221 ed.</i>)

### parrot
 
 <i>n.</i> used for various New Zealand birds of the parrot family, or parrot-like birds, often as a second element of a loose compound

>  <b>COLENSO</b> <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ,1894 ed.</i>) [NOTES: see KAKA]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Several kinds of birds are indigenous to the woods and waters; among them the oyster-catcher, bittern, kingfisher, cormorant, quail, wild-duck, mocking or [16] parson bird, parrots, parroquets, woodhen, pigeon, etc. (the [sic] gigantic Moa bird is believed to be now quite extinct). <i>(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,15</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> The woods abound with divers kinds of birds, such as parrots, wood-pigeons, water hens; ... <i>(Jan 15, 1770,Journal,115</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> At our return we found that about 9 Shags, ... 1 Woodcock, 1 Sandpiper, ... several Pohebirds, two large Parrots [[ed. fn South island Kaka.], a Parrokeet, & several other small birds had been killed. <i>(Apr 17, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/256,1982 ed.</i>)

>  The principal sorts are large brown Parrots [ed. note [Kaka, <i>Nestor septentrionalis</i>.]] with white or greyish heads, green Parroquets, [ed. note [Red-fronted Parrakeet, <i>Cyanorhamphus novae-zelandiae</i>.]] with red foreheads, large wood Pigeons, ... <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/806,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/806 (Queen Charlotte Sound)]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A very fine species of crayfish (<i>cancer homarus</i> Lin.) longer than the lobster, some shell-fish, and ... a cormorant, duck, pigeon, or parrot gave us an agreeable variety at our table <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/144</i>)

>  - and in the space of two hours shot eleven Brace of Parrots, Wood Hens, & Curlews; <i>Ibid.</i> 50 The Best kinds were Ducks, Curlews, a bird very much resembling a Wood Cock & Sea Pies; - Shags, Wood Hens, & Parrots were in great abundance ... the Wood Hen very much resembles the Common Barn door Fowl in England <i>(Nov 1791,Journal of Voyage in HMS 'Chatham',48</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> We saw two or three beautiful species of the parrot and the paroquet, a small bird resembling the sparrow; ... <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/255</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> BIRDS MET WITH IN PELORUS ...New Zealand Crow - Kokako of the natives. Parrot - ... or kaka of the natives. Paroquets - two kinds, ... called Kakariki by natives. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,65</i>)

>  <b>CRAIK</b> Among them are many sorts of wild ducks, large wood-pigeons, sea gulls, rails, parrots, and parroquets. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,187</i>)

>  <i>Par&#769;.rot</i> (p&abreve.r&#769;.r&ubreve.t), <i>n. [Add.] Mountain parrot. (Zoo.l.) </i>See KEA. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> This day returned to Albion cove saw ... a Parot of brownish colour and a large white bird as large as a turky and a small sort of brown teal duck, a large black and white Duck called a Paradise Duck a black bird called the organ bird shags a Black Robin with a white breast ... <i>(Mar 9, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 9 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 359]

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> I bought for two blue beads a cockatoo - or rather parrot (nestor), the most common bird in New Zealand, and good to eat - ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,95,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> Those of most sevice for the table are the various kinds of water fowl, and the parrot and wood pigeon. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,47</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> In the lower regions the forest was much enlivened by birds. I shot some parrots [fn Nestor meridionalis. Kaka of the natives.], pigeons [fn Columba spadicea, Lath. Kukupa.], and wattle-birds [fn Glaucopis cinerea, Gmel. Kokako.]. Among the branches of the trees hopped the neat fantail flycatcher [fn Muscicapa flabellifera, Gml. Piwakawaka.], and a social bird of a yellow colour, which has been depicted by Forster [fn Orthornyx heteroclytus, Lef. Popokatea.], and which had much the habits of the finch. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/29</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i>

>  THE KAKAPO. - Two specimens of this somewhat rare species of parrot, the kakapo, were brought into town last week by a native named Tamiti Wiremu. <i>(Mar 3, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The third taiaha from the left is ornamented with red feathers of the kaka or parrot, and with the white hair from the dog. <i>(Dec 23, 1903,Otago Witness,48</i>) [NOTES: [caption]]

>  <i>(May 11, 1773,Cook's Journals</i>) [NOTES: [see quot. HAWK]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The parrots were of two sorts; one small and green, and the other very large, greyish-green, with a reddish breast. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/158</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Parrots making a great deal of noise in the trees close to us, but we slept well till daylight. <i>(Mar 30, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,302,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> <i>Powaitere </i>- A Parrot, or Paroquet. - Of these birds there are several kinds; all of them small ... nearly the same in plumage - a bright green; yellow or red under the throat and tail; and red or yellow about the head. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),54</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Pigeons, nestors, parrots, rail, kiwi, swamp-fowl, water-fowl, owls, parson-birds, all these and more were eaten; while the native dogs and rats were held to be great dainties. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  On other slanting poles, or on the hut's roof, you see a dusky ashy-red parrot (kaka) or two, climbing and clutching with their hooked beaks. <i>(Oct 1, 1842,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,120</i>)

### parrot's beak
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Clianthus puniceus</i> [-] (See [kaka beak](k-kea.md#kaka-beak),[parrot's bill](p-per.md#parrots-bill),[parroquet's beak](p-per.md#parroquets-beak),[red kowhai](ra-red.md#red-kowhai))

>  We stock a fine variety of healthy stock grown in the open. Titoki or N.Z.Oak, each 1s6d; .. parrot's Beak, red 1s, white,[sic] 2s ... <i>(Aug 9, 1906,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Another novel exhibit is white parrot's beak. <i>(Sep 13, 1906,Taranaki Herald,5</i>)

>  ... the Maori name [kowhai ngutukaka] ... adopted in a translated and shortened from.: check however that it is not an English generic name - <i>(1926,TrNZI,670</i>) [NOTES: see P-BILL below 1853 cit.; (Andersen Pop. Names.)]

>  <b>WHITE</b> <i>Clianthus puniceus</i> (Parrot's Beak) is the most gaudy-looking plant possible, here in its native land. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,92</i>)

>  <b>NAIRN & SON</b> <i>(1912,Catalogue (Nairn & Son)</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Clianthus (Red Kowhai, Parrot's Beak </i>or<i> Kaka Beak) </i> <i>(1943,NZ Gardening Dictionary, The,43</i>)

>  <i>parrot's-beak</i> or<i> parrot's-bill</i> or<i> parrotbeak, n,</i> pl <i>parrot's-beaks</i> or<i> parrot's-bills</i> or<i> parrotbeaks</i> [so called fr. its curved standard] : CLIANTHUS 2; <i>esp</i> : KAKA BEAK <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1644</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Parrot's beak, kaka-beak; kowhai, red .. .. kowhai ngutu-kaka .. .. <i>Clianthus puniceus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>Clianthus perniceus</i> (the Glory Pea and Parrot-beak flower of the settlers, and <i>Kowhai ngutukaka</i> of the natives). - This is .. a native of only the Northern Island ... <i>(May 29, 1880,Otago Witness,7</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Clianthus puniceus</i> (See [parrot's beak](p-per.md#parrots-beak),[kaka beak](k-kea.md#kaka-beak),[parrakeet's beak](../P#parrakeet's-beak),[red kowhai](ra-red.md#red-kowhai))

>  <b>DOMETT</b> <i>Clianthus puniceus</i> parrot-bill <i>(1872,Ranolf and Amohia</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

 <i>n.</i> kaka beak

>  <i>(1886,TrNZI,18</i>)

>  This drawing ... was taken from [293] a fresh specimen of the plant `raised in England from seed gathered by the missionaries in New Zealand, where it is said to be called "Kowhaingutu-kaka," or Parrot's-bill, and to grow to the size of a large tree' <i>(sic)</i> ... <i>(1835,Transactions Horticultural Society London,ser.2, 1/519</i>) [NOTES: 1835 Transactions of the Horticultural Society of London 2nd series, vol. 1/519 in 1886 TrNZI 18 292-293]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The banks of the Keri-keri are also the only known habitat of the elegant Clianthus puniceus, a leguminous plant, which the natives appropriately call parrot's-bill - ko-waingutu kaka. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/250</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... the perfumed manouka[sic], the scarlet myrtle and giant fuchsia, the red and yellow parrot's bill, the splendid clematis or virgin's bower; ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/365</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Clianthus <i>puniceus,</i> Banks et Sol. ... Nat. name, `Kowhaingutu kaka', a parrot's bill, <i>Cunningham</i>. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 49</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### parrotfish
 
 <i>n.</i> in New Zealand usually a generic common name for various wrasses, such as banded parrotfish, green parrotfish, orange parrotfish, Sandager's parrotfish, scarlet parrotfish; a generic name for various fishes usu. of family Labridae, brightly-coloured with beak-like jaws [AND Labriformes, bright with beak-like jaws 1803] [see also rock cod]

>  The fishing party in the whaleboat obtained some very fair sport, making a good haul of blue cod, trumpeter, and parrot fish. <i>(Mar 7, 1874,Otago Witness,11</i>)

>  The officer in charge of the boat produced lines, and we were soon hauling up cod and gurnets and parrot-fish in abundance. <i>(Mar 24, 1883,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  <i>parrot-fish NZ & Austral.</i> brightly coloured fish with teeth forming structure like parrot's beak. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,546</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 823]]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] parrotfish 1692, 1715, 1746-8 PARROTFISHES 1663-1748 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  ... the mullet, latris, and parrot-fish are [54] are always after them <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/53</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Wrasses</i> A family (Labridae) of tropical to warm reef-dwelling fishes. Most of New Zealand's sixteen species are shared only with Australi. In most parts of the world these fish are called wrasses; the name parrotfish is incorrect, being properly applied to a separate mainly <i>Pseudolabrus </i>spp. (?formerly <i>Labricthys</i> spp. (esp. <i>L. psittacula</i>) [Morris] =scarlet parrot fish, soldier fish, spottie <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,107</i>)

>  The officer in charge of the boat produced lines, and we were soon hauling up cod and gurnets and parrot-fish in abundance. <i>(Mar 24, 1883,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  There are about half a dozen different kinds of the parrot fish, but all related to the wrasse family. <i>(Apr 23, 1902,Otago Witness,56</i>)

>  Further along are parrot fish with their beak-like mouths and their round bodies. <i>(Jun 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  ... the natives now and then brought Hake.. parrot fish [ed. fn <i>Pseudolabris pittensis</i>.] and leather jackets, [ed. fn <i>Cantherines scaber.</i>] <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/808,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/808 (Queen Charlotte Sound)]

>  <b>38</b> We also got ... hake, paracutas, parrot-fish <i>(1785,Cook's Third Voyage</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> ... the canoes came alongside, bringing us an abundant supply, consisting of various descriptions of fish; among which were snappers, bream, parrot-fish, benecootoos, and some excellent crayfish. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/14</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Many other fish are equally numerous, answering to our <i>hakes,.. parrot-fish, leather-jackets, cole-fish, John Dorys, sword-fish, cod</i>; various kinds of <i>skate</i> and <i>cat-fish, sting-ray</i> and <i>dog-fish. </i> <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/322</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, .. parrot-fish, flounders, skate, cat and dog-fish, sting-rays, eels. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202fn</i>)

>  <b>BARNICOAT</b> A singular fish which was new to all on board except one man who called it a parrot fish. <i>(1845,Journal,170</i>) [NOTES: (Otago)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 69. LABRICHTHYS PSITTACULA. Rich. Parrot Fish. <i>(1872,Fishes,43</i>)

>  Mr Travers mentioned the recent observance of a rare parrot-fish in the market (<i>Odax vittatus</i>), ... <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/439</i>) [NOTES: (Proceedings Wgtn Philosophical Society)-]

>  The Wrasse and Parrotfish are mostly caught outside among the kelp, and with the Spotty are indiscriminately named Kelp-fish by the fishermen, though the term Butterfish is also given to the smaller sorts. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/384</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> PARROT FISH (<i>Labrichthys psittacula</i>). ... Very little is generally known about the species called in Dunedin the parrot-fish. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,70</i>)

>  2. <i>Mokohinou Islands</i>. - Hapuku (or groper), hiwihiwi, barracouta, maomao, spotty, parrot-fish, leather jacket, korokoro-pounamou. ... 7. <i>Portland Island.</i> - Tarakihi, moki, frost-fish, barracouta, king-fish, john-dory, warehou, maomao, parrot-fish, flying-fish, smooth-hound, soldier fish. ... 16. <i>Moeraki</i>. - Rock-cod, gurnard, parrot-fish, butter-fish, sprats, conger-eel <i>Ibid.</i> 24/211 Parrot fish (<i>Labrichthys psittacula</i>). NUMBER OF FISH RECORDED - 12. LOCALITIES - Mokohinou, Portland Island, Moeraki, Cape Saunders. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  When we see the mackerel splashing along we know they are after squid; the mullet, latris, and parrot-fish are always after them, and even the moki and butter-fish join the hunt. <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/53</i>) [NOTES: (Henry)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Parrot-fish, n</i>. name given in Australia to <i>Pseudoscarus pseudolabrus;</i> called in the Australian tropics <i>Parrot-perch</i>. In Victoria and Tasmania, there are also several species of<i> Labricthys</i>. In New Zealand, it is <i>L. psittacula</i>, Rich. <i>(1898,Austral English,342</i>)

>  At the Portobello Marine Fish - hatchery the swimming form has been taken from the stomachs of the following fishes: Smooth-hound (<i>Mustelus antarcticus</i>), .. parrot-fish (<i>Pseudolabrus miles</i>). ling (<i>Genypterus blacodes</i>), and Leather jacket (<i>Pseudomonacanthus scaber</i>). <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/111</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson and Thomson)]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family LABRIDAE (wrasses). Large-scaled fishes with rather silly faces, wrasses (Fig. 69.7) are known by various local names: spotties, soldiers, kelp fish, parrot fish, etc. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,224</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Parrot fish, scarlet, or soldier .. .. pau, puwai-whakarua .. ..<i> Pseudolabrus coccineus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>parrot fish ... n</i>. brightly-coloured fish with parrot-like snout. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>parrotfish n.</i> a brightly-coloured fish with parrot-like snout. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <b>FISHER</b> A number of Parrotfish (they do not live in schools) were following me around and picking up the broken off crayfish legs. The Banded Parrotfish is the most common in the shallows - it blends in with the seaweed - but deeper down the Sandaggers Parrotfish and the Scarlet Parrotfish, often called the `deep sea spotty', take over. <i>(1977,Angels Wear Black,37</i>)

>  <b>WILS</b> PARROTFISH Three varieties of Parrotfish occur in our waters - Banded, Scarlet and Sandager's (or King) Parrotfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,176</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> Most members of this family [Labridae: wrasses] have been known in New Zealand as parrotfish although in all other countries they are collectively called wrasses and the name parrotfish is applied to a completely different family. A true parrotfish (family Scaridae) has recently been found in New Zealand ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,251,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] parrotfish .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] other than Pseudolabrus sp. ... [species name] wrasse .. [scientific name] <i>Pseudolabrus </i>sp. .. [other name(s)] parrotfish. Use this code for fishing returns <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### Parry
 
 <i>n.</i> Paremoremo prison [ORIGIN: abbreviation, also Pare] (See [Paremoremo](p-per.md#paremoremo))

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> However I'd been sloughed up for over three years by this stage ... and thought I might as well give it a bash. `Anything's a go', they used to say in Pare. <i>Ibid.</i> 252 PARRY, PARE (n) Paremoremo Prison. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,128</i>)

### parsley leafed pine
 
 <i>n.</i> an indiginous pine characterised by lacy leaf appearance. [<i>Phyllocladus trichomanoides</i> aka parsley-leafed pine, parsley-leaved pine, parsley pine, celery pine, tanekaha; found in upper tip of South Island and in the North Island north of a line from Mokau to Mahia Peninsula - latitude 39 degrees.]

>  Phyllocladus trichomanoides (parsley-leaved pine). <i>(Aug 12, 1912,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The river is bordered here and there by grassy flats and hanging woods of timber trees.. the silvery parsley pine, with its purplish points ... <i>(Feb 28, 1906,Otago Witness,51</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> ... I must especially mention [from the Middle island] a new species of the parsley-leaved pine (Phyllocladus trichomanoides), which the natives call E Hutu, to distinguish it from the tanekaha, another species of the same genus. <i>Ibid</i> 1/427 ... Tanekaha ... Phyllocladus trichomanoides ... one [pine] is called hutu by the natives, and is a Phyllocladus; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/307 The forest contains many tanekaha or parsley-leaved pines (Phyllocladus trichomanoides). <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/188</i>)

### parsnip palm
 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) an introduced species also known as Stewart Island palm

>  <b>WILSON</b> PARSNIP PALM (<i>Melanoselinum decipiens</i>) has been grown in Halfmoon Bay gardens for so long that some people call it `Stewart Island Palm'. In fact it is a curious member of the umbellifer family, not a palm, and is native to the Azores ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,80</i>)

### parson
 
 <i>n.</i> an oystercatcher

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We saw great flocks of Curlews or <i>Oyster-catchers</i> [at Queen Charlotte Sound], whom the people on board the Adventure called <i>Parsons</i>. We found several black & white ones amongst them, perfectly like those we have in England. <i>(May 24, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/285</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The great feature of the southern seas is the multitude of birds ... Huge albatrosses, molimorks (a smaller albatross), Cape hens, Cape pigeons, parsons, boobies, whale birds, mutton birds and many more, wheel continually about the ship's stern, ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,1/12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MACKIE</b> In a cage I saw a bird called the parson.It takes its name from two curious white feathers on its breast. <i>(1973,Traveller under Concern,77</i>) [NOTES: Quote of 5/2/1853]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Toi or Parson <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),[16]</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> The whalers call [tuis] parsons from the two white feathers hanging out. The native name is paaroo [acutes second a, first o]. <i>(1840,Plain Truths,65</i>)

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> ... the most harmonious is the toi or tue, to which we gave the nickname of parson, from its being a beautiful glossy black, and having a small patch of snow-white feathers on the neck ... Some call this the mocking-bird, as it imitates the voice of almost every other bird, ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,28</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [Morris] Cook named this beautiful and lively bird the parson and mocking-bird. It acquired the first name from its having two remarkable white feathers on the neck like a pair of clergyman's bands. [Mr. Taylor is not correct. Cook called it the Poe-bird (qv.) The name `Parson-bird' is later.] <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,401</i>)

### Parson Williams
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On again, calling at different stations on the road till I come to Te Aute station, commonly known as Parson Williams. This is where the famous Maori College is, and it is supposed to be one of the wealthiest stations in the Hawke's Bay province <i>(Jan 7, 1911,NZ Truth,289/4/5</i>)

### parson-bird
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae</i>, tui (See [tui](tot-tyr.md#tui))

>  [from a letter to the <i>Spectator</i>] The coming of the long-tailed cuckoo to New Zealand every springtime, from the warmer Polynesian islands is a great source of trouble to the parson bird. <i>(Jun 30, 1909,Otago Witness,76</i>)

>  Writing of the tui, or parson bird, Mr Buller, in his book on New Zealand birds, says:- "The Maoris fully appreciate the mocking powers of this bird, and often devote much time and patience to its instruction ..." <i>(Feb 28, 1873,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The tuis or parson-birds will not take the trouble to insert their brush-tongues to get at the honey, but in their hurry, tear open the flowers with their beaks. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,225</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Prosthemadera ... novae zealandiae</i>. ... [75] Parson Bird. TUI. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,74</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Tui</i>, n. <i>Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae, parson bird. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,527</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Tuuii,</i> n. parson bird; ... <i>(1871,Dictionary of the Maori Language,180</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PROSTHEMADRA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (TUI OR PARSON-BIRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Tui and Koko; the young bird distinguished as Pi-tui or Pikari. <i>Ibid.</i> 88 The early colonists named it the `Parson-bird',in allusion to the peculiar tufts of white feathers that adorn its throat, and their fancied resemblance to the clerical bands. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,87,1888 2nd edn 1/94, 1/95 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> No sign of human life that morning; the shrill cry of the curlew on the beach and the full rich carol of the tui or parson-bird from the brushwood skirting the shore fell faintly upon the ear. <i>(1881,Poenamo,2/5/99</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Pigeons, nestors, parrots, rail, kiwi, swamp-fowl, water-fowl, owls, parson-birds, all these and more were eaten; while the native dogs and rats were held to be great dainties. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  11. <i>Prosthemadera Novae Zealandiae</i>, Gml. Parson-bird, Poe-bird, Tui, Koko. - Often builds under the shelter of Rubus twines, which many of us have learnt to fear or respect as bush-lawyer ... More than once I have found it near the top of a matipo (<i>Myrsine</i>) ... <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,276</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TUI <i>Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae Other name</i>: Parson Bird. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,221</i>)

>  <i> parson bird n</i>. the grey-crowned babbler; in New Zealand the tiri [Sic] named from two white feathers on the neck like a parson's bands. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>parson bird ... n</i>. grey-crowned babbler; in N.Z. the tui [Named from two white feathers on the neck like a parson's bands]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Tui</i>, [macrons u, i] n. ..., <i>parson bird. </i> <i>(1975,Dictionary of the Maori Language,449</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Parson bird's benediction. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,182</i>)

>  <i>parson bird n</i>. another name for <i>tui. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,823</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 546; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 351]]

>  <i>Tui Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae</i> (GMELIN, 1788) ... <i>Chatham Island Tui P.n. chathamensis</i> HARTET, 1928. ... OTHER NAME: <i>Parson bird. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,291</i>)

>  (Reprinted from the <i>Hawke's Bay Herald</i> November 8.) .. for I am like the nestlings of the Tui (or "parson-bird,") [sic] the dam goes to seek food, which having found she returns with it, and then the young gape their beaks wide open to receive their food. <i>(Dec 25, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  The'tui' as he is called by the Maori, or 'parson-bird', as named by the Briton, is the delightful little comforter and joy of every tennis party or five o'clock tea that is held by the bird world of the New Zealand bush. <i>(Jul 30, 1898,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  In a certain school the master in one of the upper standards had gone to great pains to tell the children about the colour, characteristics, etc., of the Tui, or Parson Bird. <i>(Oct 25, 1915,Grey River Argus,8</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> The tuis were going mad with delight; cheerful birds dressed in sober black, with two white feathers at the throat, earning them the name of the 'parson-bird'. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,00</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... and also the Toi [<i>tui</i>] bird or Parson. ... With two bands under the Chin of white Feathers, ... called Parson birds. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),34,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> [The Tui] has been called by some `the Mocking Bird' ... by others ... `the Parson Bird'. <i>Ibid.</i> 53<i> Tui</i> - This remarkable bird, from the versatility of its talents for imitation, has, by some, been called `the Mocking Bird'; and, from its pecu-[53]liar plumage, has by others been denominated the Parson Bird'. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),52</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The graceful <i>tui</i>, or parson-bird (<i>prosthemadera Novae Selandicae</i>) sported amongst the yellow blossoms of the <i>kowai </i> <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/29</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [The Tui] from the versatility of its talent for imitation, has, by some, been called the `Mocking Bird', ... by others, from its peculiar plumage, the `Parson Bird'. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,164</i>) [NOTES: [check]]

>  <b>HALL-JONES</b> ... but our parson bird being beyond reach held on his discourse with much nonchalance. Altogether this bird is a most remarkable one: clothed in feathers of deep black from head to foot, he wears a most grave and sacerdotal aspect. This is not all: he bears out closer the clerical resemblance by the possession of two pure white feathers under his chin, and the parody is complete when he commences to utter his guttural yet energetic notes. Sitting on the branch of a tree as a pro tempoire [????] pulpit, he wags his paw [????] and shakes his head, bending to one side, and then to another; as if he remarked to this one and to that one ... etc <i>(1971,Mr Surveyor Thomson,55</i>) [NOTES: 1850s in 1971 Hall-Jones Mr Surveyor Thomson 55-56 (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>MACKIE</b> In Wm. Swainson's garden saw numerous Parson birds among the trees uttering their loud clear whistle ... they a good deal resemble our blackbirds. <i>Ibid.</i> 96 The only native bird we find commonly kept in cages is the `tue', Parson bird. <i>(Feb 15, 1853,Traveller under Concern,79,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EARP</b> [Quoting a recent letter of Sept. 17] We had a pudding for dinner today ... composed of a green parrot and some parson-birds which one of our fellow-pasengers shot; they were delicious, particularly the parrot. <i>(1853,New Zealand (various authors/titles),105</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The parson bird is as big as a starling with a glossy starling looking appearance save that from his throat projects a cravat-like tuft of white feathers - whence his name. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,44,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Then we crept softly up ... to have a good look at the Tui, or Parson-bird, most respectable and clerical-looking in its glossy black suit, ... and white wattles of very slender feathers ... curled coquettishly at each side of his throat, exactly like bands. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,93,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Several kinds of birds are indigenous to the woods and waters; among them the oyster-catcher, bittern, kingfisher, cormorant, quail, wild-duck, mocking or [16] parson bird, parrots, parroquets, woodhen, pigeon, etc. (the [sic] gigantic Moa bird is believed to be now quite extinct). <i>(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,15</i>)

>  PROSTHEMADERA NOVAE ZELANDIAE, Gml. Tui. Parson-bird. ... in the eyes of the omnivorous settler, it possesses the higher merit of furnishing an article of food, and no weak sentimental feeling saves it from the camp-oven. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/55</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 11. PROSTHEMADERA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gray. Parson Bird. Tui. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,5</i>)

>  A popular name for the New Zealand tui; the two white tufts on the throat suggested the white bands often worn by clergymen. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),291</i>)

>  <i>parson bird n</i> : TUI <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1645</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> `Tui,' Parson-bird or Poe-bird of Capt. Cook Melliphagidae; Prosthemadera Novae Zealandiae. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/305</i>)

>  It is very pleasing to hear the deep rich notes of the parson-bird .... <i>(1890,TrNZI,23/482</i>) [NOTES: (W.Colenso)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Parson-bird, n</i>. the New Zealand bird <i>Prosthemadera novae-zelandiae</i>, Gmel.; Maori name, <i>Tui</i> (q.v.). See also <i>Poe</i>. <i>(1898,Austral English,342</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,401</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The Tui, or `Parson Bird,' as he is irreverently called, because he has a tuft of white feathers on his throat which are supposed to resemble a clergyman's bands, is shining black and metallic blue. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,70</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> The most striking objects were the kohai [sic] trees ...generally full of large black parson birds which suck the flowers. <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,120</i>)

>  The tui is called the parson bird because it has a small tuft of white feathers growing under its beak. <i>(Sep 10, 1910,NZ Truth,272/1/4</i>)

>  <b>ALEXANDER & CURRIE</b> <i>Parson bird</i>: (or <i>tui</i>): ... <i>(1926,Treasury of NZ Verse,291</i>)

 <i>v.</i> to pay up [ca1984 Wellington Still in use.]

>  Blackwell parted up with the "fiver" and received the book for safe keeping. <i>(Dec 2, 1926,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> part up: hand money over. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>)

### part-house
 
 <i>n.</i>

### part-Maori
 
 <i>adj.</i> of mixed Maori and Pakeha descent (see also noun)

>  I was only part Maori - or that was how I saw myself. I didn't know for sure, quarter or eighth. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,41</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> Of late years several part-Maori families have left the Island ... <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,39</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> Here Tane was lucky; the policeman was part-Maori, ... <i>(1969,Awatea,111 Appendix II,1978 ed.</i>)

>  He knew he was part-Maori ... <i>(1988,Te Kaihau,217</i>)

>  <b>MACRAE</b> He had a thing against people he called `jiggaboos'. Chris had not heard this expression before. When she first heard him unsmilingly talk about jiggaboos this and jiggaboos that, she put it down to yer [47] Kiwi jokers over-reaction to the cultural cringe... `How can she say that when you know Gaz is part-Maori?' ... `What? Hey ... You never told me yer had jiggaboo in yuh!' <i>(1988,Awful Childhoods,47</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> You're part-Maori, aren't you? <i>(1989,Frigate Bird,121</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori, especially one with largely non-Maori ancestry (also as adjective)

### partial impact
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Partial Impact explains light, heat, electricity, gravitation, Freethought, Socialism, epidemic diseases, - explainds in short everything in the heavens above, the earth beneath, and the waters under the earth, except the unwillingness of the world to receive thte theory itself. <i>(Jul 31, 1880,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  At the meeting of the members of the Philosophical Institute last evening, Professor Bickerton read a paper "On temporary and variable stars," in which he treated of the partial impact of two large bodies as a possible frequent occurence, and as giving a perfectly satisfactory explanation of temporary and variable stars, of double stars, of all kinds of nebulae, of comets, and indeed of all the varied phenomena of the universe. <i>(Jul 5, 1878,Star, The,2</i>)

>  This appears to be the position of Professor Bickerton's theory of Partial Impact, and we should say that he is justified in asserting that, "The fact that astronomers are working without it has already caused irreparable loss to science ..." <i>(Feb 12, 1894,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

>  Putting on full steam ahead, Hoani steered straight for the electric freight car, and disregarding Professor Bickerton's partial impact theory, he crashed full into Trow's wagon. <i>(Dec 31, 1921,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  In 1878 Bickerton revealed his partial impact theory of the creation of new stars which formed the basis of his theory of cosmic evolution. The notion that the oblique collision of two stars produces a new, hot, rapidly expanding body without destroying the original two in the process explained virtually all astronomical mysteries for Bickerton, but provoked only indifference or scepticism from scientists. <i>(Sep 13, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Partial impact appears competent to explain the occurrence of temporary, double, and variable stars, nebulae of various kinds (the kind depending on the nature of the impact), comets, and finally stars or suns accompanied by bodies of smaller size. <i>(1879,TrNZI,XI/121</i>)

>  The hypothesis I am about to describe suggests that this is the case, but especially says further, that if they do knock against each other that the blow may be a mere graze of the two outside, or sometimes a large piece of each may be struck off, and in extremely rare cases they may meet fair; but as this latter isso extremely rare, the other cases are those chiefly considered, and so the theory is called Partial Impact. <i>(1880,TrNZI,XII/191</i>)

>  <b>JENKINSON</b> It was on 4 July 1878.. that Bickerton read before the Philosophical Institute of Canterbury his paper "On Temporary and Variable Stars" in which the main facts of the theory of partial impact were first announced. <i>(1940,New Zealanders & Science,00</i>) [NOTES: Pages needed]

>  <b>von HAAST</b> This was the first occasion upon which the Professor was to enunciate publicly his theory of "Partial Impact" which was to become an obsession with him. <i>(1948,Life & Times of Sir Julius von Haast,235</i>)

>  <i>Partial Impact: a possible Explanation of the Origin of the Solar System, Comets, and other Phenomena of the Universe</i>. By Professor A.W. Bickerton, F.C.S. ... <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/125</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The hypothesis that I am about to describe suggests that this [collisions] is the case, but especially says further that if they do knock against each other, the blow may be a mere graze of the two outsides, or sometimes a large piece of each other may be struck off: and in extremely rare cases they may meet fair; but as this latter is so extremely rare the other cases are those chiefly considered, and so the theory is called <i>Partial Impact.</i> The effect of the collision of our two stars will be to strike a piece off each other where they touch, and each star will travel on with a slice cut off the side of each. The parts of each which met will be left behind by both the retreating bodies, and will probably remain where the collision occurred. But, owing to the way it has been struck off, the two sides being impelled in opposite directions, it cannot help revolving, and it is not unlikely that nearly all the thousands of rotating bodies and systems in the universe may have thus been set spinning by partial impact. At partial impact, rotation must ensue. The matter will tend to spread out more or less in a flare, with frequently gas at the poles and the middle body produced by the collision will generally be very hot, proportional to the mass. <i>(1948,Life & Times of Sir Julius von Haast,236</i>) [NOTES: 188? A. W. Bickerton to the Canterbury Philosophical Institute quoted in 1948 Von Haast Life and Times of Sir Julius Von Haast 236]

>  <b>LEE</b> As a youngster I remember the New Zealand press talking of Bickerton and his theory of partial impact. <i>(1965,Rhetoric at the Red Dawn,110</i>)

>  I want to know.. why your theory of partial impact is not being recognized and accepted in the scientific world. <i>(Jul 11, 1910,Triad, The,XVIII(4) : 49</i>)

>  He will never get weary / Of answering any query / On the partial impact theory; / Will you, Bick? <i>(1923,College Rhymes,14</i>)

>  It is most singular that this first, this fundamental, this absolutely basic idea of a third body, has for a quarter of a century been missed by astronomers. They have been bombarded with tens of thousands of papers and pamphlets. Diagrams showing the third body have been broadcasted before them by thousands and by hundreds. A new term - "Partial Impact" - was specially coined to express the idea for their benefit and guidance. But there they sit in their specialized turrets, regardless; and this prime idea, that the parts struck from grazing suns are welded into a third body, has failed to impress them. <i>(Apr 1, 1908,Progress,188</i>)

>  The Cabinet has decided to aid Professor Bickerton, of Christchurch, to proceed to England and Europe to lay before scientists there his "partial impact theory," subsidising up to GBP300 any amount that may be raised by subscription. <i>(Mar 19, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,8</i>)

### party at Kelly Browne's
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  One advertisement that I cannot walk away from is the "Party at Kelly Browne's" advertising campaign presented on behalf of an insurance company... It is about a young woman - Kelly Browne - who decides to have a party while her parents are away on holiday. <i>(Sep 1, 1999,Norwester,8</i>)

>  "It's like that classic TV ad, party at Kelly Brown's. Kids will invite 20 and 200 will turn up."But what do you do when "Kelly Brown" has a party every weekend? <i>(Nov 27, 1999,Dominion,17</i>)

>  There's a party at Kelly Browne's place, or at least that's what more than 400 young people who converged on a front lawn near Palmerston North on Saturday night thought. <i>(Mar 15, 2000,Dominion,9</i>)

>  [Hdg] Kelly parties again. Police were dealing with up to 150 teenagers who had been turned away from a birthday party in Tawa last night. Porirua senior Sergeant Steve Whitehead described it as "Kelly Brown syndrome". <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Dominion,3</i>)

>  [Hdg] 'Kelly Browne' party draws teens... Hundreds of teenagers turned up at a Tawa party last night after it was broadcast on a radio station, creating a 'Kelly Browne situation" police say. <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  And finally, was the property ever used for a party thrown by Kelly Browne or anyone who ever had anything remotely to do with Kelly Browne. <i>(Mar 26, 2000,Evening Post,A3</i>)

>  That popular advertising slogan from a few years back - "There's a party at Kelly Browne's place" -was all about happy, shining people rushing off to enjoy mirth and merriment, but the likelihood is that Kelly Browne's place was about to be trashed and that soemone was going to get evil on someone's ass. <i>(May 8, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  [Hdg] There's a party at Kelly Browne's. The cultural phenomenon of how to engineer the next big thing. <i>(Feb 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  This [sc. Phil Goff's barbecue] became such a famous gathering that posters were made, borrowing from the famous TV ad banner, "Party at Kelly Browne's" - "Party at Phil's". <i>(Jun 30, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

### party-hopper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Her once-proud National government is propped up by a weird and mysterious bunch of party-hoppers and turncoats who collectively fear an early election. <i>(Nov 1999,North & South,14</i>)

Then, consider Peter McCardle, National's sincere but pathologically downtrodden backbencher, who hopped to NZ First, then back-hopped away from NZ First to stay with the National government, which had made him a minister. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  As for the Jackson dilemma the choice facing Anderton is stark; allow him some leeway or get on with the anti-party-hopping legislation now languishing at the bottom of the order paper. <i>(Jun 10, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Just think, anti-party-hopping legislation - and groan. <i>(Mar 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Now the party is pinning its hopes on the widely discredited "party-hopping" legislation, which ACT vehemently opposed when it was introduced, to get rid of her. <i>(Nov 11, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Party hopping law on way out. ### paruwhatitiri
 
 <i>n.</i>

### pash
 
 <i>n.</i> a petting session [OED says 'pash" goes back to US 1914 1953 15 M A29 Thames DHS 27 PASH [M14-16 2] -->1984 14 F E122 Wgton Girls C. 27 PASH -->1953 14 M A4 Thames DHS 27 PASH DE LA -->1953 16 M A31 Thames DHS 27 PASH DE LA! -->1953 16 M A32 Thames DHS 27 PASH DE LA ! -->1953 15 M A26 Thames DHS 27 PASH DER LA -->1984 16 F E37 Pakuranga Coll. 17 TO PASH -->1984 14 F E120 Wgton Girls C. 27 PASHING -->1953 16 M A31 Thames DHS 27 PASH,THE]

>  At least she hasn't cried as often as Roha has for Hone though! Roha and Hone, they went round together and once I saw them having a pash. Eee! I grin at my big sister. Never mind, Roha. Plenty other boys down in Wellington and you can pash up large with them ... <i>(1977,NZ Short Stories,149</i>) [NOTES: 1977 Ihimaera (Yellow Brick Road) in NZ Short Stories 149]



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal, New Zealand & Australia) a passionate kiss

>  The Beige Brigade, which calls itself a group dedicated to keeping cricket fun, says it will run a "Pash for Cash" competition during upcoming West Indies tour. <i>(Jan 12, 2006,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  Attempts at rages. Boys desperately trying to cop a pash from anyone they could find <i>(May 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (informal, New Zealand & Australia) to kiss passionately

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fenced pasture sown with appropriate amounts of paspalum seed.

In seasons when feed is superabundant and there is really no need for any special supplementary feed, the paspalum-paddock is likely to get away. <i>(Feb 20, 1918,NZ Journal of Agriculture,90</i>)

### pass
 
 <i>n.</i> a narrow or small aperture, down which the quartz is passed from the upper to the lower tunnels

>  Clad in trousers and singlet, they are bust shovelling the quartz down a "pass" to the level below. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(8) : 20</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> As the mine workings reached greater depths the ground generally became more solid and stable and more and more shrinkage stopes were worked, until this method became the principal one in use for stopes up to about forty feet in width. The method consisted of putting in the level timbers in the usual way, with short chutes or `passes' installed every other set to allow all the ore in the stope to be lowered evenly as it was worked. <i>Ibid.</i> 209 ... including ... chutes for ore-handling, or `passes' as they were generally known. <i>Ibid.</i> 315 <i>Chutes or passes</i>: Chutes for running the broken ore out of the stopes into the hand-trucks were, for shrinkage stopes, placed between alternate sets (about 10 ft centres). They were about three feet wide, mounted three inches above the truck, and fitted with top and bottom doors. The bottom door was removed and the flow of ore into the truck controlled by jambing a pass-bar across the chute. (This was a short bar about three feet long with a D-shaped handle). The bottom of the chute was inclined at about 50 degrees from horizontal. For filled stopes, the chute was installed at the bottoms of the cribbed ore passes, leading up through the filling to the face of the stope; the ore passes were usually spaced from 18 to 25 ft apart along the stope, this being a convenient distance for men to shovel the ore. <i>Ibid.</i> 329 <i>Passes:</i> See chutes. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi,193</i>)

### passing lane
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes: British dictionaries (including OED) have <i>crawler lane</i>, which I have seen in NZ (there was one so called on the Bombay Hill highway) and <i>fast lane</i>, current literally and metaphorically here, but not <i>passing lane</i>. (British road atlases use the term <i>passing place</i>. For a time recently there were <i>passing bays</i> on the Rimutaka Hill road). The corresponding Australian term, at least in Victoria, is <i>overtaking lane</i> (Personal observation).]

### pastoral
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> [of land, used for or suitable for stock farming as opposed to arable or cropping]

>  EXCEPTIONAL OPPORTUNITY FOR THOSE IN WANT OF AGRICULTURAL OR PASTORAL FARMS. <i>(Feb 23, 1899,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  <b>LOVELL-SMITH</b> [quoting James Coutts Crawford who made the trip in April 1864 : probably from Recollections of Travel in NZ 1880 [check]] `When daylight broke I found we were on the cent from the mountain country to the plains. We were passing through a fine pastoral country, with grassy valleys and hills decreasing in height.' <i>(1931,Old Coaching Days,109</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The <i>Anise</i> plant is very abundant in all the pastoral districts near Nelson; and ... it much improves the ... meat fed on land where it grows. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,246</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Similar stations to those formed in the Wairarapa have lately been established by squatters from Wellington, at Kaikoura, a fine district of pastoral country ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,127</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> ... the pastoral stations north of Otago extend ... until they meet the stock-owners' out-stations South of Canterbury. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),181</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> The south-eastern portion of Marlborough consists mainly of the Wairau plains, one of the richest and first occupied pastoral districts in New Zealand, ... <i>(1866,Polynesia,120</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The experience he gained in work on the newly broken-in settlements was useful ... when he came to take up a pastoral block in Hawke's Bay ... <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,3</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Pastoral ranges will be allotted to purchasers of freehold land in the Settlement [of Canterbury] ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,15</i>)

>  From the appearance of an opening to the S.E., it is presumed that communication with the district of the Looker's-on on the east coast, which is well known to be a pastoral country, will be discovered. <i>(Nov 17, 1871,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> [pertaining to or engaged in stock raising, usu sheep farming, as distinct from 'agricultural' farming, ie arable farming or cropping]

>  To those whose proclivities tend towards a pastoral life, occupation as stockriders, shepherds,[ampersand]c., on one of the large cattle- and sheep-runs common in the colony may be obtained. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,157</i>)

>  <b>REEVES</b> [In Canterbury c. 1850] The Australian coo-ee, the Australian buck-jumping horse, the Australian stockwhip and wideawake hat came into New Zealand pastoral life. <i>(1898,Long White Cloud, The,242</i>)

>  ... there are open grassy plains, of no great fertility, but yet suited to pastoral pursuits. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,15</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> The fat stock of Westland is another adjunct to our pastoral wealth, and the district now turns out some of the primest beef. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,89</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> He arrived there in 1852 after a lifetime in the Navy and entered into partnership with (amongst others) Mallock and Lance, giants in the North Canterbury pastoral world. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,29</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS</b> The Rees family barely had time to get settled again at their home station before the quiet pastoral barony ... was invaded by a noisy and often drunken avalanche of gold-seekers. <i>(1971,King Wakatip,73</i>)

>  Fencing wire has been part of New Zealand's pastoral tradition for many years and has been used by the handyman to repair almost anything. <i>(Sep 1985,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,16</i>) [NOTES: 1985 Sep Standards (SANZ) 16]

>  <b>STACK</b> ... this [hut] proved to be the home of a cultured English gentleman, who ... had found in the pastoral occupations of a squatter in the wilds of New Zealand congenial employment for all his energies. <i>(1972,Through Canterbury,25</i>) [NOTES: [c1860] a1906 Stack Through Canterbury (1972) 25]

>  <b>FITTON</b> The pastoral occupations of New Zealand ... are described in another chapter; ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),91</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> The Pastoral Occupations of New Zealand. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),216</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> The first two stages of the Colony's growth, pastoral and agricultural, had clashed. The time when the pastoral industry had been of supreme importance was passing by; but pastoralism still lay ... across the path of progress in agriculture. <i>(1938,High Country,150</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i> [sense 3b, in the sense 'pertaining to large runholders as a political power group': pastoral interest, pastoral lobby]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When I first read this inscription [in the Memorial Church, Cave], the word `sheep-men' made me involuntarily flinch. Years later, when I had forgotten it, I suddenly met it again, and again I recoiled. So aghast was I that I spent weeks collecting every old term for `runholders' I could, and I found thirteen, viz., `flock owners,' `flock masters,' `pastoralists,' `run holders,' `run owners,' `sheep owners,' `sheep holders,' `sheep masters,' `squatters,' `station holders,' `station owners,' `stock holders,[sic] and `stock owners.' The word `sheepmen' is a modern Americanism, and using this anachronism mars a splendid memorial. ... [12] Of all the thirteen terms found in old documents, the word `runholder' was the most used, and it is the word that I think should be substituted. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,11</i>)
