>  <b>C W RICHMOND</b> We have just advised the Governor to assent to the new land regulations of the province under which pasturage leases will be granted for 14 years at a yearly rate of 1d. per head on the sheep depastured. <i>(Jan 29, 1857,Letter,257</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>Kerr et al.</b> The earliest tenure available for 'pastoral land' was the pasturage licence issued by the respective Provincial governments. By 1865 most of the 'pastoral' land of New Zealand was occupied under a system of pasturage licences. [pasturage lease] <i>(1979,Review of Rentals for Pastoral Leases, A,5</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  Every holder of a pasturage licence granted before the 1st April, 1857, who shall, at any time within six months (if a resident in the province - if an absentee within twelve months) after the passing thereof, make an application to the Commissioner for the purchase of the pasturage land comprised therein. <i>(May 23, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  H. Furguson and Co. applied, by their agent, Mr. Learmonth, for a pasturage licence for 1000 acres of land up the Poerua river, Okarito district. <i>(Nov 8, 1871,West Coast Times,2</i>)

### pasturage run
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The terms in which pasturage runs may be obtained in the Canterbury district, ought to occupy the most serious attention of the settlers. <i>(May 10, 1851,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

### pasture
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) grazing or grass cover on land, that can not be quantified.

>  It is unprofitable to graze-off drystock when pasture on the home farm is being poorly utilised. <i>(Sep 1997,NZ Dairy Exporter,29</i>)

>  Small set of cattle yards plus loading bank. Cover: a high standard of well maintained English pastures. <i>(2001,Hawke's Bay Today,32</i>)

>  Pasture is the cheapest livestock feed - when it's grazed. Pasture is a living community - intertwining plants of grass, clovers, herbs, weeds and bare ground. <i>(2001,Lifestyleblock.co.nz,Dalton Pasture management</i>)

### pasture louse
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep.

>  <b>PETER</b> Pasture lice is the common name for sheep around here. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,3</i>)

>  Pasture lice: sheep. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  .. and 130 for sheep including pasture, paddock or ground lice ... <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### pasture-pulling
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) up-rooting of pasture, usually in damp areas.

>  Pasture-pulling persists. Soil aerating machines have not reduced pasture-pulling in trials at Ruakura. Pasture-pulling ocurs on many dairy farms. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Dairy Exporter,38</i>)

### pasty and sack
 
 <i>phr.</i> bed and beer? food and bed?

>  <b>MONEY</b> Swiftly did I return unto `mine host', who staightway produced `pasty and sack' for my refreshment, <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,83</i>)

### pat
 
 <i>n.</i> a cow-pat q.v.

### Pat
 
 <i>n.</i> a Chinese (usually pl.)

>  Only a few weeks ago `Truth' was called upon to protest against the preference given to the Chows in the markets. It has become the rule rather than the exception for auctioneers to keep the best and choicest products for their pets, the Pats, because they are the largest buyers. <i>(Nov 26, 1910,NZ Truth,283/4/6</i>)

### Pat Maloney
 
 <i>n.</i> only (one) ? [cf. Pat Malone, on one's]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> This strictly to my Pat Maloney ... <i>(1950,Otago Interval,111</i>)

### pata
 
 <i>n.</i> a wood

>  <b>POLACK</b> There are many other woods of much service to Europeans, differing in quality, among others: the 31 <i>Pata</i>.. 45 <i>Parekireki</i>, &c. Of all these woods there are abundant quantities. <i>(1838,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/400</i>)

### patai
 
 <i>n.</i> a garment

>  <b>POLACK</b> The smallest garment woven is called the<i> patai</i> [a1-acute], principally worn by females for an inner garment; the<i> korowai</i> differs but little from the former, except in having a deep fringe of knotted flax strings. The <i>tatata</i> [as-acute] is similar in size to the former, having pending from every part, an infinity of small cords six inches long, of no use or ornament; its origin must be ascribed to the arbitrary taste of fashion, <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/177</i>)

### pataka
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori storehouse raised upon posts. (See [also whata](../A#also-whata))

>  Amongst the things Mr Seddon is taking to London is a carved model of a pataka (Native food storehouse), which is to be used as a casket to hold an address that Mr Seddon is to present to King Edward from the Cabinet ministers in New Zealand. <i>(Apr 23, 1902,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  Messrs. Cashel and Co.'s store, consisting of a large double marquee erected near the centre of the paddock in front of Belmont House, was now flooded, and the stores and other merchandise forming the stock-in-trade were conveyed to the latter place and to the pataka close by, raised some five or six feet above the ground, and also occupied as a store by Mr. Anson. <i>(Feb 15, 1869,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  Fox has also undoubtedly the finest carved "pataka," or food store raised on piles, in the colony. <i>(Sep 9, 1880,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

>  Sufficient for two days remain in their patuka, or store. <i>(Dec 23, 1890,Otago Daily Times,38</i>)

>  <b>ANNABELL</b> The groceries were brought by dray from Waitotara to the "pataka" a small building on high legs on the other side of the Saddle, beyond the lakes. <i>(1984,Ngamatapouri,20</i>)

>  One by one they found and burned / nga whare tapu, urupa, pataka / the peach groves, the kumara grounds, / the corn and pumpkin fields. <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>pataka</i> : store-house. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Pat|aka, s. A stone so called; a store-house. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,190</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> A third sort of structure are the provision-houses (pataka), which are built on poles to prevent rats from entering them. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,270</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> We landed at the pataka, or stage. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/283</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> ... the native clearings, with here and there a cooking hut or a <i>patuka</i> [sic] (an elevated storehouse for grain) erected amongst the trees, diversifying the scene. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/240</i>) [NOTES: [?error or typo frequent here]]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Near to the cook-houses there stood a carved <i>patuka</i>, which was the receptacle for the sacred food of the chief; and nothing could exceed the richness of the elaborate carving that adorned this storehouse. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/126</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> Not far from [Te Heuheu's] house is a splendid <i>pataka</i>, or store house; and it is elevated on four red posts, in fact the whole concern is painted red. <i>(Jan 18, 1858,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Jan 18 Smith Notes of a Journey in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 365]

>  <b>GRACE</b> Some time before we came to Taupo a handsome `Pataka' (viz. a Maori store for food) had been burned. Sir George Grey when on his visit to Taupo saw the said Pataka and told Te Heuheu (no doubt in a jocose way) that it was a sign of his `Rangatiratanga.' <i>(Mar 24, 1858,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Mar 24 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 76]

>  <b>ANGAS</b> In each court [yard of a pa] stands the house and cook-house, as well as the elevated `pakuta,' or store for food, of one of the families. <i>(1866,Polynesia,153</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Pataka, n.</i> Maori word for storehouse, supported on a post to keep off rats. See Whata. <i>(1898,Austral English,343</i>)

>  I soon had plenty of it [sc. puha] cooked and dried and stored away in a rat-proof pataka. <i>(Sep 8, 1917,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Hence, when ... a large tangi or other party reduced his pataka or rua kumera contents, he ... made shift with fern-root, berries, or mamaku (tree fern) until the next year's crop came in. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,281</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The pataka is a storehouse resting on posts, which are generally richly carved, so raised as to protect the stores from rats and other harmful things. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,190,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> same as futtah q.v. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  *<i>Pataka</i>.-A correspondent tells me that this is the general name for a <i>futtah</i> (store on legs) in the North Island. Austral English derives our word from <i>whata</i> (Maori). <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> ... the [Maori] woman brought filled baskets of kumeras and maize and onions up the cultivated slopes to fill the patakas against the time of war. <i>(1938,Promenade,227</i>)

>  <i>pa[a]taka,</i> a food-storehouse raised on posts: the one on a single post is a whata. Many pa[a]taka were as elaborately ornamented as a house. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/15</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>pataka:</i> raised storehouse. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Those are <i>pataka</i> for storing choice food like <i>kumara,</i> preserved birds, and <i>hinau</i> bread. <i>(1948,Taina,19</i>)

>  <i>pataka</i> A Maori storehouse supported on a post. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),292</i>)

>  <i>pataka n</i>. storehouse [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> `What are those little things like bird houses [at Whakarewarewa Pa?'] I asked. `Patakas, ...Food storehouses.' <i>(1964,Stag Party</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> PATAKA Store house, usually patterned after dwelling house, but much smaller and elaborately carved. Always set on poles as protection against rats. <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>)

>  <i>pataka ... n.</i> storehouse [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> ... the wind that shakes the tree above the pataka,/ <i>(1979,Collected Poems,579</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Food was stored in the <i>pataka</i> , a small house standing on a high platform out of the way of dogs and rats. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,3</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> pataka <i>storehouse for food </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  <i>pataka n.</i> Maori store-house raised on posts. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,550</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Sometimes the storehouse was set up on legs 3 ft. or 4 ft. high and called a <i>pataka</i> ... <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/22</i>)

>  On the brow of a slight acclivity, and overhanging it, they have built a stage or terrace of small boughs, on which they eat and sit during the day. It is called by them a pataka. <i>(May 30, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,4</i>)

>  [From the <i>Maori Messenger</i>] They also explained that the matangohi is sacred, his flesh is never eaten, neither is his head exposed, but the body is hung up in a tree and cut up something like that of a pig, after which his remains are placed on a pataka or stage in a tree and there left. <i>(Nov 20, 1850,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,4</i>) [NOTES: Part of serialised journal 'by His Excellency the Governer-in-Chief of New Zealand'.]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a food storehouse.

>  <b>WESTMACOTT</b> He was only an occasional employee of Mac's, at ten shillings a day, and this time was there to construct a pataka to store the food where the rats could not reach it. <i>(1977,After-Breakfast Cigar, The,14</i>)

>  <b>ADDENBROOKE</b> We had a large pataka which stood on long tin-covered legs to keep rats and mice from entering. <i>(1992,Home from the Hill,27</i>)

### patapatow
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [patu](p-per.md#patu))

### patatai
 
 <i>n.</i> a rail

>  <b>HAY</b> The Patatai (<i>Rallus Philippensis</i>) is a small land-rail, plumaged much like the partridge. <i>Ibid.</i> 225 The Putoto (<i>Ortygometra Tabuensis</i>) is a crake, often confounded by settlers with the patatai. It is a smaller bird altogether, having partridge tints on the back, and a grey breast. It chiefly inhabits raupo swamps. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/221</i>)

### patate
 
 <i>n.</i> a tree [aka patete]

>  I myself will build my house, the posts of which shall be maere (Mira salicifolia) and patate (Aretia schefflera). <i>(May 2, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,7</i>)

>  I visited Day's Bay for the first time last week; and, within half a mile of the sea, and below 150 feet above sea-level, I saw, without leaving the footpath, the following trees, shrubs, etc. ..rangiora, *makomako, akeraho, akepiro, rohotu, ramarama, kawakawa, patate, houhou ... <i>(Aug 29, 1912,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Aralia <i>Schaefflera</i>, Spr. ... Nat. names, `Pate', <i>R.Cunn</i>.; `Patate', Middle Island, <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 95</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Patate, Middle Island, <i>Lyall. Schefflera digitata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Patate (<i>Schefflera digitata</i>) is another small tree remarkable on the same account. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/196</i>)

### patch
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I was wearing my colours - the insignia of the Epitaph Riders which is sometimes referred to as a patch. ... He had head injuries and went to the headquarters in Lincoln Road where he left a note that his patch had been taken. ... If a member of the gang lost his patch he had to try and get it back himself. He had still not got his patch back, he said. <i>(Aug 17, 1975,Dominion,3/4</i>)

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> `So, Winston's joined a gang has he?' ... `Hasn't got his patches, or his jacket yet, I know that.' <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,63</i>)

>  The police said that the gang grew to more than 30 patches ... <i>(Jun 4, 1989,Sunday Star (Auckland),A6</i>)

>  Would it be true there were about 43 members of Black Power ... Mr marsh: Yes, that would be true but not patch ones. <i>(Jun 27, 1989,Dominion,3</i>)

>  He was in the gang but he wasn't a patch. <i>(Nov 20, 2002,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a distinct small area holding considerable alluvial gold, esp. strike a patch

>  "As to the claim, I think it has been proved already that the gold is there, but it seems as if it is going to be patchy, or rather we are only going to get the gold in patches... If we could get a continuous week's work on a patch such as we have had it would yield over 1000oz for the week. .." <i>(Nov 6, 1901,Otago Witness,25</i>)

>  I don't expect there is much in the washing dirt, at least what we have thrown out yet, but as the gold lies very patchy we may come across a small patch yet before we work our claim out. <i>(Oct 1, 1861,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>McKAY</b> Within [the block] lie the rich alluvialdiggings of Lyell Creek, famous for its"patches" and the coarse character of itsgold. <i>(1895,Report on Geol. of SW Nelson,80,1897 ed.</i>)

>  This field surpassed all others in a series of rich patches; ... these patches were magnified into leads - they did not prove so. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,18</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> `Say, stranger! you ain't struck a patch anywhere about up there, have you?' <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/i/9,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WOOD</b> Mrs. Arkwright had been hoping that ... he might strike a patch this week. But ... Dick had declared the claim to be worked out long ago. <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,13</i>)

>  It appears the Try Again party .. became more fortunate, striking on to a nice little patch, and .. secured 21oz before they discovered it was probable they were over their boundary... the rush at Storey's Gully has not yeilded much, as the ground seems very patchy. <i>(Dec 5, 1889,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  It would seem as if two prospectors had the good fortune of "stiking" a patch of the precious metal... Tucker Hill was always patchy and always poor, and it is not likely that the late [sic] find will lead to the discovery of any extensive auriferous deposit... In my opinion the Tucker Hill find is but a patch... <i>(Jul 26, 1894,Otago Witness,23</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an area rich in kauri gum (See [(cf. pocket](../(#(-pocket),[sink](sho-spa.md#sink),[digger)](dig-dou.md#digger)))

>  <b>THOMAS</b> The posssibility of finding a `pocket' or better still a good `patch' added interest to the [gumdigger's] day's work. <i>(1970,Way Up North,31</i>)

### patch burn
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to burn small patches of tussock or scrub to maintain stock carrying-capacity.

>  I previously had to patch burn against getting burned out in summer or autumn. <i>(May 3, 1945,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

### patch member/patched member
 
 <i>n.</i> a full member of a gang, entitled to wear a patch (See [patch](p-per.md#patch))

>  <b>David Geary</b> So it was an easy sidestep from bootboy to gang prospect. Though I should say he never graduated to full patch member. <i>(2008,Six Pack 3, The,106</i>)

>  Nikora had been associated with the Nomads gang, but was not a `patched' member, Mr Justice Barker said. <i>(Nov 11, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)

### patch of bush
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>S PERCY SMITH</b> Captain McDonnell was leading with the best of the Contingent through a patch of bush where it was as dark as Erebus ... <i>(Oct 19, 1866,Taranaki Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Oct 19 S. Percy Smith Taranaki Journal in 1922 Cowan NZ Wars 2/531]

>  No change took place until we reached the Parerau camping-ground, where there is a patch of bush, and a pretty little stream ... <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/341</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Here we drive in and ask for a paddock[sic no .] Past the shearing shed - past the homestead to a beautiful place with a little patch of bush - tuis - magpies -cattle - <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,51,1978 Gordon ed.</i>)

>  Mr. Henry also states that the skeleton ... was found at the edge of a patch of bush, about 200 acres in extent (locally known as the 'Wilderness') ... <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/79</i>)

### patch plant
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Raoulia australis</i> and other <i>Raoulia</i> (cf also scab)

>  On the following morning a start for the summit was made, and after winding their way from one gorge to another, the party at last got on the slope of the mountain, which for a long way up is covered with a dwarf moss, and patch plants of every hue, which can only be compared to a rich Brussels carpet. <i>(Feb 28, 1872,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  At Ophir and along the sunny side of Raggedy Ridge, wherever the tussock has been destroyed by persistent burning, the ground is becoming covered with the patch plants Raoulia australis and Raoulia parkii, which, however beautiful they may be, so effectually cover the ground, as with a carpet, that the growth of grasses or other fodder plants is impossible where they exist. <i>(Mar 25, 1908,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 1867,Linnean Society Journal,10/</i>) [NOTES: Buchanan Notes on the Botany of Mount Egmont (And)]

>  ... all the flowering plants ... that form wood are nearly stemless, and in most cases are aggregated in the form of hard hasssock-like patches ... forming what are known as <i>patch plants.</i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: [?1875 2nd edn](Hector)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> HECTORELLA COESPITOSA (<i>Hook.,F.</i>) ... many of these class [sic] of plants, which are known as `Patch Plants', having only one terminal flower [at the end of] each branch. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,45</i>)

>  <i>(1900,TrNZI,32/130</i>) [NOTES: (Cockayne)]

>  This subalpine `patch plant' [<i>Raoulia australis</i>] ... <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/330</i>) [NOTES: (Cockayne)]

>  ... the patch plants usually found in these alpine and subalpine regions are abundant. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,5(NS)/23</i>) [NOTES: (Park, Cromwell)]

### patch poison
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to drop or lay poisonous bait for possums and other noxious animals in specific areas.

>  Mr Urquhart says the council wants to patch poison the blocks using 1080 carrot ... <i>(Aug 15, 1996,NZ Farmer,10</i>)

### patchy
 
 <i>adj.</i> [gold mining]

>  Many of these ventures have paid well, but the progress will naturally be slow, particularly as the ground is so very patchy. As a `fossicking' locality this is a favourite place ... <i>Ibid.</i> [?p] The hills are also auriferous and patchy. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,18</i>)

>  Many of these ventures have paid well, but the progress will naturally be slow, particularly as the ground is so very patchy. As a `fossicking' locality this is a favourite place ... <i>Ibid.</i> [?p] The hills are also auriferous and patchy. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,18</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> Though the ground had proved in general very `patchy' yet a large amount of gold had been taken out. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,69</i>)

>  <b>GALVIN</b> The lodes are what the [quartz] miner terms `very patchy' ... <i>(1906,NZ Mining Handbook,17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a clown

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> Archie Drummond, our patchy, was standing at the opening of the tent at the time ... [fn] Clown. <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,3</i>)

### pate
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) <i>Schefflera digitata</i>, a native tree usually with seven fingers or leaves. [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [patete](p-per.md#patete))

>  A few hours later, John Morrison found a pate tree with some wood rose, <i>Dactylanthus taylorii</i>, growing from its root system... To date Hauturu is the only offshore island where <i>Dactylanthus</i> has been found. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/28</i>)

>  The sap of our native pate has mild fungicidal properties known to help ringworm and other skin ailments .. If that doesn't inspire you to feel warmer, you could try lighting your fire by vigorously rubbing some dried pate sticks with some kaikomako. <i>(Jul 7, 2007,Dominion Post Indulgence,19</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|ate, <i>s</i>. A tree, the branches of which are used by the natives in producing fire by friction. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,190</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>Pate</i> [e-acute] (<i>Aralia polygama</i>,) trunk slender, and pithy, grows to twenty feet, leaves virent digitated and epinated at the edges; the wood is made use of for procuring fire by friction. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/400</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Pate tree is by no means handsome as a tree or shrub, having nothing remarkable, only that its wood when decayed produces fire by rubbing or friction, if rubbed by a harder piece of wood, ... [Glossary 119 pate, patete <i>Schefflera digitata</i>]. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,107,1956 ed.</i>)

>  Aralia <i>Schaefflera</i>, Spr. ... Nat. names, `Pate', <i>R.Cunn</i>.; `Patate', Middle Island, <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 95</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pate, <i>Col[enso]. Schefflera digitata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea</i> ...; .. the Pate, <i>Schefflera digitata</i>; the Horoeka, <i>Panax crassifolia</i>: and, on the sea-coast, ... the Karo, <i>Pittosporum crassifolium</i>; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i>Myoporum laetum ... </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Pate[e-acute] (<i>Schefflera digitata</i>). A small tree, common everywhere in Otago. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/34</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>pate</i>: much-branched small tree 15 ft. to 20 ft. high. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[Schefflera digitata, Forst.] Pate; Patete. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,233</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> A wide variety of fruits are probably taken [by the saddleback]: on Hen Island pate (<i>Schefflera digitata</i>) and whauwhaupaku (<i>Neopanax arboreum</i>) are an important berry source. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,234</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Most prominent among the broadleaf species here [in Westland] are toro (<i>Myrsine salicina</i>), Westland quintinia (<i>Q. acutifolia</i>) ... stinkwood (<i>C. foetidissima</i>) ... and pate (<i>Schefflera digitata</i>) with hutu (<i>Ascarina [158] lucida</i>) ... <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,157</i>)

>  PATE/ <i>Schefflera digitata</i>/ <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,23</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Other common shrubs are the thin-leaved hangehange (<i>Genistoma rupestre</i>), kanono (<i>Coprosma grandifolia</i>) and pate (<i>Schefflera digitata</i>) with its large palmately compound leaves. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,103</i>)

### pateke
 
 <i>n.</i> brown teal

>  The same cannot be said for nine pateke or brown teal released in August, but on the day we visited, Conservation Department staff found the first nest, with five eggs, and were hoping it was a good omen for the reclusive, threatened native duck. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Dominion,9</i>)

>  They live in the shadow of glamour birds like the kiwi and kakapo, but on Friday the diminutive brown teal was a star in its own right. <i>(Nov 6, 2000,Evening Post,13</i>)

The birds will be settled at a wildlife sanctuary. <i>(Jul 1979,Dominion,5</i>)

### patent attorney
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes : The term corresponds to the English <i>patent agent</i> which is not in NSOD or Collins, but is in ODL.]

### Patent Slip
 
 <i>n.</i> a slip for ship repair at Evans Bay

>  There was a fierce beauty about its scale, the precision of its movements, the way everything meshed to deliver the raw power to haul ships out of the water at the old Patent Slip in Evans Bay... Wellington Provincial Council let a contract for a slip in Evans Bay in 1868 but wrangling between the council and contractor stalled progress and the job was completed by the Wellington Patent Slip Company in 1873. For the next 96 years the slip hauled 6000 ships up the rails on its concrete incline - a total of more than two million tonnes deadweight. <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Evening Post,29</i>)

### patere
 
 <i>n.</i> an abusive song

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> SOME MAORI WORDS ...<i> patere</i> distinctive song <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

### paternity order
 
 <i>n.</i> a court order declaring a man to be the father of a particular child. [Jim Cameron writes : See Family Proceedings Act 1980 s 51. This term does not appear to be in legal use in England and is not recorded in NSOD, Collins or ODL. Before 1968, called an <i>affiliation order</i>. The current English equivalent appears to be a <i>declaration of patentage</i>.]

>  <i>curse, Paterson's n.</i> nickname for a noxious weed called also <i>Mrs. Freer Weed</i> [Derived from part taken by a Federal minister, named <i>Paterson</i>, in banning Mrs Freer, an overseas visitor]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),7</i>)

### patete
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> also called seven-finger a small tree whose soft wood was used by the Maori in making fire.

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Schefflera digitata (The Patete)</i> ... Maori name <i>Patete, Ohau</i>, (the latter name chiefly on Banks Peninsula). The wood is soft, and was used by the Maoris in obtaining fire. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,331</i>)

### patiki
 
 <i>n.</i> [?pakihi]

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [flounder](fin-fly.md#flounder))

>  <i>From the Auckland Times</i> .. The natives yesterday, in ten or twelve large canoes, brought in an abundant supply of fine fish; mullet, snappers, cawhy, patiki (the flounder of Europe) and other delicious species; which they disposed of to the inhabitants of the town at less than 1d. per lb. <i>(Mar 21, 1843,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,3</i>)

>  The tukutuku is bound by the patiki or flounder resembling Wairarapa and an abundance of support... <i>(Oct 26, 2006,Wairarapa News,20</i>)

>  Not only has Boreas been strong, but he has chopped round with marvellous suddenness, and the patiki and staunch built yacht alike have had to lay at their moorings during the festive season. <i>(Jan 1, 1910,NZ Truth,236/7/3</i>)

>  Sand Flounder New Zealand Flounder, Patiki, Three corner, Tinplate <i>Rhombosolea plebeia.</i> ... Black Flounder - Patiki - <i>Rhombosolea retiaria. </i> <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/197</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  RHOMBOSOLEA PLEBEIA (Richardson). <i>Sand-flounder; Patiki</i>. One of the commonest flounders, frequenting deeper water than the yellow flounder, and never found in estuaries of rivers. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/122</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>THOMAS</b> But the memory will be far more enjoyable to me if I call the flounder a patiki, and in that case the home-made harpoon type of spear we always used must be called a purau. <i>(1970,Way Up North,57</i>)

>  <i>patiki ... n</i>. N.Z. sole or flounder [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Both flounder (<i>patiki</i>) and sole (<i>patiki rori</i>) have a fine, delicate flesh with little bone, ... <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,73</i>)

>  The old Maori used a long slender spear to catch sand flounder, or patiki, on the mudflats at night.... <i>(Dec 8, 1982,Evening Post,37</i>)

>  [Flatfish submitted for opinion included] (1.) The flounder, or patiki, wrongly named `plaice,' about one-tenth adult size. (2) The turbot sole, wrongly [559] named `brill,' about one-fortieth its adult size: ... (3.) The lemon sole, wrongly named `flounder,' ... (4.) The New Zealand sole ... <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/558</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> FISH [MET WITH IN PELORUS]. Whiting (or resembling the European Whiting). Kahawi. Flat fish - Patiki, plentiful ... <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,68</i>)

>  <i>Pa&#769;.ti-ki</i> (pa.&#769;.te.-ke.; <i>colloq.</i> p&abreve.t&#769;.&ibreve.-k&ibreve.),<i> n. (Zoo.l.) </i>[Maori.] The New Zealand flounder <i>(Rhombosolea monopus),</i> of the family <i>Pleuronectidae. [Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>PATIKI</i> (Rhombosolea monopus). <i>Ibid.</i> 304 <i>Rhombosolea monopus</i> Gunther Patiki, or Flounder <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,21</i>)

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are confined to the south. ... The following is a list of the fishes which are chiefly met with in the market: hapuku, kahawai, red-snapper, snapper, tarakihi, moki, trumpeter, frostfish ... gurnard, mullet, sea-mullet, ... flounder or patiki, ... grayling, smelt, kokopu, sardine, sprat, eel (tuna), black-eel, conger-eel [168], and silver-eel. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Patiki, n</i>. the Maori name for the <i>Flounder</i> (q.v.). The accent is on the first syllable of the word. <i>(1898,Austral English,343</i>)

>  `Patiki,<i>s</i>. a fish so called.' <i>(1820,Grammar and Vocabulary of Language of NZ,190</i>)

>  <b>TORLESSE</b> I went with J. Deans in a canoe down the Avon spearing Patiki. <i>(1958,Torlesse Papers,81</i>) [NOTES: 1849 citation]

>  <b>POWELL</b> SAND FLOUNDER (<i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i>), Patiki of the Maoris, is one of the three closely allied species of flat-fish [sc.with the Yellow-belly and Greenback] marketed in New Zealand. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,66</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND SAND-FLOUNDER (PATIKI) <i>Rhombosolea plebeia</i> (Richardson) ... The common Sand-flounder is known from one end of New Zealand to the other, sometimes by other names in different localities. In Auckland it is known as the Dab.../ Some refer to it as Tinplate or Three Corner; the latter name is derived from its fancied trangular shape but why Tinplate I am at a loss to know. To other than fishermen and those intimately connected with the fishing industry it is just a Flounder. Some say it is Tinplate because when cooked it fills a plate. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,191</i>)

>  The patiki, an excellent fish, which is something like a plaice, comes in about the middle of October. <i>(Jun 1, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,51</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> In the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. In the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> `A fine place for spearing soles or<i> patike</i> (the best of fish).' <i>(May 31, 1844,Diary</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `Patiki, common name for the sole and flat-fish; the latter is found in rivers, but decreases in size as it retires from the sea.' <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,412</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> <i>Patiki</i>, a fish shaped exactly as the English flounder ... are here <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,132</i>)

>  Flounder or Patiki (<i>Rhombosolea monopus</i>). Probably the yellow-belly (<i>R. leporina</i>)is included among the returns. <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/213</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Snapper, <i>warehou</i>, butterfish, trumpeter, <i>moki</i>, flat-fish (<i>patiki</i>), and other fine sea fish were plentiful. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,107</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Patiki (pah-tee-kee)</i>: The flounder. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,186</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SAND FLOUNDER ... <i>Other names: Rhombosolea plebeia</i>; diamond, tinplate, dab, square; patiki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,216</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> I cook the patiki with two eggs each,also mashed potatoes. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,86</i>)

>  flatfish - battik [patiki] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,110,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 110; also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 282]

>  Fish is very fine here. Partikies [<i>patiki</i>] or Soles, are speared with a Bayonet or a stick at night the flood tide coming in; ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),46,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... they were fishermen from the northward, and had come to this place to spear flounders (patiki,) by torchlight. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/322 The <i>patiki</i> [a-acute], between the large flounder and the sole, is equally excellent with the European fish, as are also the <i>mackarel</i>, of which there are several varieties. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/238</i>)

### patiki-mohoao
 
 <i>n.</i> black flounder [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  RHOMBOSOLEA RETIARIA Hutton. <i>Black Flounder; Patiki-mohoao</i>. This species, known generally as `river-flounder,' is occasionally taken in the sea. Common in the estuaries of most large rivers, and often taken many miles inland. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/122</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> Hutton. RIVER-FLOUNDER, BLACK FLOUNDER; PATIKI-MOHOAO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,29,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND BLACK FLOUNDER or RIVER FLOUNDER (PATIKI-MOHOAO) <i>Rhombosolea (Adamsoma) retiaria adams</i> Whitley and Phillipps/ It is known to fishermen and those in the fishing industry as the River Flounder as it is caught mostly inside or near the mouths of rivers and enters lakes where it lives chiefly among weeds. To some it is known as the Maori Flounder. I have helped to catch these fish in various rivers in New Zealand, Both in the North and South Islands, from the Bay of Islands, Bay of Plenty, Canterbury and Otago. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,204</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLACK FLOUNDER ... <i>Other names: Rhombosolea retiaria;</i> mud flounder, river flounder, estuary flounder; patiki-mahoao (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,215</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLACK FLOUNDER</i> (Mud flounder, patiki-mahoao) <i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i> ... Coloration is distinctive; the back is usually dark grey-green with darker mottling and a variable number of brick red spots. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,310,1984 ed.</i>)

### patiki-rori
 
 <i>n.</i> common sole [.] (See [sole](sho-spa.md#sole))

>  PELTORHAMPHUS NOVAE-ZEELANDIAE Gunther[u-dieresis]. <i>Common Sole; Patikirori</i>. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (not common, North Auckland Peninsula). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/122</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Peltorhamphus novae-zeelandiae</i> [sic] Guenther. SOLE; PATIKI RORI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,29,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> NEW ZEALAND COMMON SOLE (PATIKI RORI) <i>Peltorhamphus novae-zeelandiae</i> Gunther <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,206</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> COMMON SOLE <i>Other names: Peltorhamphus novaeseelandiae</i>; English sole, sole, New Zealand sole; patiki-rori (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,218</i>)

### patiki-totara
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  RHOMBOSOLEA MILLARI Waite. <i>Yellow Flounder; Patiki-totara</i>. Found in abundance, Bay of Islands, Hauraki Gulf, and Bay of Plenty. It is not so commonly seen in Wellington as the sand-flounder. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (not so abundant south of Napier). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/122</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Rhombosola leporina</i> Guenther. YELLOW FLOUNDER; PATIKI-TOTARA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,28,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> YELLOWBELLY FLOUNDER ... <i>Other names: Rhombosolea leporina</i>; patiki-totara (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,217</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>YELLOW-BELLY FLOUNDER</i> (Patiki-totara) <i>Rhombosolea leporina </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,308,1984 ed.</i>)

### patiti
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) tall tussock; a kind of grass. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Where yellow patete* is spread to the sun / And yellow taukinae* where clear waters run . . . * Patete, tussock. *Tuakinae, a native shrub. <i>(Aug 26, 1908,Otago Witness,76</i>)

>  The camp shelters were made of patiti (tussock) and taru (grass) woven on sticks and were of a temporary nature merely designed to keep off the rain and wind. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>) [NOTES: From Beattie "Traditional Lifeways of the Southern Maori"]

>  Maori names for tussock varied from region to region. In one southern dialect, <i>wi</i> was used for silver tussock (further north the same word was applied to rushes) and <i>patiti</i> seemed to be a general term for tall tussocks, with <i>maania</i> an alternative or the word for red tussock in particular. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/40</i>)

>  We made the trek in through the patiti to a spot with a view of the sea and, facing the other way, inland to the Paparoa Ranges. <i>(Jan 12, 2008,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The hills [at Otakou] are clothed with wood, or a species of grass (<i>tritium Scabrum</i>), known by the name of `patiti'. <i>Ibid.</i> 19 Species of grass (tritium scabrum), known by the name of `patiti'. <i>Ibid.</i> 195 We now advanced along the plain [towards Moeraki], which was covered with `patiti' and ... stunted fern and `tutu'. These plains are called `pakihi,' a word used in the North Island to signify dried up. So that `tupakihi,' the name by which the `tutu' is there known, appears to be a contraction of `tutupakihi,' or `tutu' of the [196] plains, which are well called `pakihi,' being for the most part arid. <i>Ibid.</i> 312 PATITI, a coarse grass growing in tufts (<i>tritium scabrum, Raoul</i>).; <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,10</i>)

>  The soil [on Campbell Island] is very wet ... the surface between the tussocks (Patiti) carpeted with beautiful mosses ... The M'Quarrie cabbage, cotton plant (I have an idea that good serviceable paper might be made from the latter), and wild carrot grow abundantly ...The inevitable Piri-piri appears everywhere. ... [178] Greatest elevation [Antipodes Islands] 700 feet, the hills dotted with high tussock (pa-ti-ti), and patches of M'Quarrie cabbage and cotton plant. ... The albatross here is the `lowland', and lays two eggs. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/177</i>) [NOTES: (Hector Outlying Islands)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a hatchet

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Single combats with the meri or the patiti (stone-club, or tomahawk), to decide a dispute, were formerly frequent, but are now discontinued. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/125</i>)

>  .. Mrs Lister not having been long in the country, and seeing a young savage running up with a patiti in his hand, concluded that it was with the intention of murdering her husband, and by attacking the boy she probably saved her husband's life. <i>(Oct 7, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  He was met and informed that his unfair practices would not be tolerated any longer; and was requested to retire with his band, to which he objected and began to brandish his patiti, and threatened to chop off a few heads ... <i>(Jan 13, 1880,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  The exhibit comprises 5 floor mats (whariki); .. 2 patu patiti (hatchets); .. and one hinaki tuna (eel pot). <i>(Aug 20, 1885,Evening Post,4</i>)

### patoo
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [patu](p-per.md#patu))

### patotara
 
 <i>n.</i> a bronze-coloured, pungent-leafed shrub, Leucopogon fraseri (See [also mingimingi](mar-moa.md#mingimingi))

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Papapa [Patotara] is a little flower growing on a short stem, with sharp prickly leafs, has a fragrant smell but is not handsome; it bears a small red berry. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,111,1956 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [?1956 ed. note]]

>  Patotara. Leucopogon Fraseri, A. Cunn. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/46</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Leucopogon Fra[s]eri (Fra[s]er's Leucopogon)</i> ... Maori name <i>Pa-totara. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,328,1940 p. 348 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> A small densely rooting heath, patotara (Leucopogan Frazeri), during this third period began to colonise suitable localities. <i>(1926,Tutira,169</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1940,Coming of the Maori, The,2/86, 88</i>)

>  <b>HUTCHINSON</b> ... the vernacular [name] varies in different districts. ... Leucopogon fraseri ... heath, patotara, goblin orange ... Carmichaelia odorata ... broom, wild liquorice ... Muehlenbeckia australis ... grape weed ... Muehlenbeckia complexa ... wirebush <i>(1947,At Omatua's Fireside,127</i>) [NOTES: [check]]

>  <b>LEVY</b> Poa tussock, danthonia tussock, stunted bracken, stunted manuka, monoao, mingimingi, tauhinu, patotara, and <i>Pimeleas </i>were the more dominant plants of the poorer areas, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,10,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The PATOTARA (<i>L[eucopogon] fraseri</i>) is a dwarf, bronze-coloured, pungent-leafed, golden-fruited epacrid ... common in heaths ... throughout New Zealand. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,277,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <i>patotara</i> [s] <i>Cyathodes fraseri</i> (A.Cunn.) Allan <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,56</i>)

>  <i>patotara</i> [s] <i>Cyathodes fraseri</i> (A.Cunn.) Allan <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,56</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Leucopogon fraseri</i> PAATOOTARA ... A dwarf hea th ...(=<i>Cyathodes fraseri</i>). 2. <i>Botrychium australe </i> <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,116</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Cyathodes <i>oxycedrus</i>, Br. ... Nat. name, `Pa totara', Middle Island, <i>Lyall. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 164</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a fern, Botrychium australe

>  The bracken of the British Islands is represented by a closely similar plant, having as wide a range of distribution [as no other British and NZ fern has]; and the same remark applies to the representative forms, with the exception ... of the patotara (<i>Botrychium ternatum</i>). <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/252</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk Brit. and NZ Floras)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pa-totara, Middle Island, <i>Lyall. Cyathodes Oxycedrus. </i>Patotara, <i>Col[enso]. Botrichium Virginicum. </i>Patotara, <i>Col[enso]. Leucopogon Fraseri. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>BOTRYCHIUM ... australe </i>... Southern Moonwort. PA TOTARA. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,17</i>)

### Patricia Bartlett hair-do
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> ... a severe-looking woman with one of those Patricia Bartlett hair-dos - <i>(1984,Ratana,53</i>)

### patrol
 
 <i>n.</i> a surf patrol

>  ... I learned that the Piha patrol had that day recovered the body of a man lost from the rocks - out of patrol hours - a week before. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/37</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  ... finding no security and no prospect of food or rest in their own country, which was daily and nightly traversed in all directions by our flying columns (our `patrols' in colonial phrase) ... they were reduced to suing for peace ... <i>(Mar 23, 1861,Otago Witness,9</i>)

### patrol wagon
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>patrol wagon n.</i> the usual U.S. and New Zealand term for <i>Black Maria. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,829</i>)

### patsy
 
 <i>n.</i> a Parliamentary question designed to publicise government achievements

>  Opposition questions are generally designed to trip up or embarrass a minister, or at the very least to expose weaknesses in the Government's programme, while Government questions amount to little more than invitations to a minister to deliver a pre-written paragraph of Government spin. Such questions are known as "patsies" or "Dorothy Dix" questions, after an American journalist who wrote a famously platitudinous syndicated column giving advise to the lovelorn. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/57</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a half-g, a rigger

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>patsy</i> half-g; from Patsy Riggir, queen of the local country music scene, whose surname sounds like the rigger or squarerigger, the old half-g. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,78</i>)

### patter
 
 <i>v.</i> to eat [This comes from Morris (and P5)]

### pattie
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [patu](p-per.md#patu))

### pattoo-pattoo
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [patu](p-per.md#patu))

### patu
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> [About Cape Runaway] They kept paddling about us, calling out to us <i>Kaka kee, no Tootwais, harre yoota patta pattoo</i>; that is to say, if we would go on shore they would beat us with their patta pattos; ... <i>(Oct 31, 1769,Journal,100</i>)

>  kill - ba'ttoo [patu] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,112,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 112]



 
 <i>adj.</i> broken

>  Patu - Stuffed <i>(Sep 23, 1998,Capital Times,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a stone club used as a weapon (See [cf. mere](mar-moa.md#mere))

>  In some of the dialects .. [t]here are two forms of the preposition "for," the <i>ma</i> and the <i>mo</i>. <i>He patu mau</i> would be a thing for you to beat with; <i>he patu mou</i>, a thing to beat you with. <i>(Apr 12, 1851,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  Hook-nose on this occasion displayed an old cocked hat, and held in his hand his Patupounamu, (this green stone Mere is significantly named "the fire of the gods,")... <i>(Dec 22, 1852,New Zealander, The,4</i>)

>  Tua Nihohiho, on behalf of the Ngati-porou tribe, presented a greenstone patu to his Excellency. This relic has been handed down for twenty generations. <i>(May 30, 1883,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  .. and I have seen a splendid patu patu, or whalebone war club, with a human figure and arabesque work carved on its handle, which was found at Tomahawk. <i>(Aug 3, 1893,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  The patoo-patoo, he explained, was a stone weapon of recent maunfacture, 'powerful for striking the top of the head.' <i>(Nov 9, 1872,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  The greenstone was demanded, and Turoa, flourishing his Patoo Poonam, threatened to cleave Prophet's skull, and to cut down the house, if it was not immediately given to them. <i>(Mar 15, 1845,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> pattoo-pattoo <i>(Aug 22, 1819,Marsden's Lieutenants,151</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>CRUISE</b> Their arms consisted of spears, bayonets fastened upon sticks like pikes, pattoo-pattoos, mearees [e2 acute], and twelve muskets, which gave considerable importance to the strength of the tribe. <i>(Aug 7, 1820,Journal,136,1957 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1820 Aug 7 in 1823 Cruise Journal (1957) 136]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|atu, <i>s</i>. A war instrument; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,191</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> [M. interpreting a ?taiaha as a striking weapon?] The Chief standing up cheering them, with a kind of Spear headed Stick, a patoo [<i>patu</i>]. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),39,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Patu, n</i>. Maori generic term for all hand-striking weapons. The <i>mere</i>(q.v.) is one kind. <i>(1898,Austral English,343</i>)

>  <i>Patu n.</i> any weapon used in the hand. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>ELDER</b> [?18?] [The chiefs] were armed with their patooes (<i>patus</i>). (dagger) The root idea of the word <i>patu</i> is `to strike.' The word is applied by the Maoris to all weapons, and also, with a qualifying adjective, to pounders and beaters of flax, fern-root, etc. The early explorers, e.g. Cook, restricted the term to weapons of the <i>mere</i> type, and, as a generic term for this group of weapons is useful - in fact necessary - the usage, though not strictly a Maori one, has been followed by modern ethnographers. <i>(1932,Letters & Journals of Samuel Marsden,86</i>) [NOTES: 1932 Elder note to Letters and Journals of Samuel Marsden 86]

>  <i>patu</i>: striking weapon. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> <i>patu</i> short club of stone, whalebone, etc. <i>(1969,History of New Zealand,319</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> PATU Club-like weapon, made from stone, bone or greenstone (jade) and occasionally elaborately-carved hardwood. In spite of club-like appearance, it was a short-sword, and with its sharpened edge, could lift the top from a man's skull, or pierce between his ribs with comparative ease. <i>(1967,Evil Day,380</i>)

>  <i>patu n. N.Z.</i> a short Maori club, now ceremonial only. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,830</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 551; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 354]]

>  <b>BULLER</b> They used a variety of manual weapons, such as the mere, the hani, the hoeroa, the patu, the taiaha, the toki, etc. ... <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,249</i>)

>  <i>patu</i>, stone club; patu! imper. strike! (Club him!): patoo-patoo <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/155</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>CRAIK</b> These [spears], or rather the shorter sort, are also sometimes called by English writers Patoos, or Patoo-patoos. Lastly, they often carry an instrument somewhat like a sergeant's halberd ... This they call a Hennee. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,266</i>)

>  Close by is a greywacke patu, dug up by Wellington Harbour Board workers at the turn of the century at the Worser Bay pilot station, earlier the site of the Kakariki pa. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 47/94</i>)

>  One of our party.. found a stone patu, or pestle, about 10in in length. <i>(Mar 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  ... a short hand weapon which was fastned by a string round the wrist, was about 18 inches long, had a rounded handle and thence formed into a flat elliptic shape: this weapon, we afterwards learnt, was called <i>Pattoo. </i> ... but they soon convinced me that it was a green stone pattoo[circumflex a] they only wanted, which one of them tore from his wrist and retreated, ... <i>(Oct 6, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/567,1955 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1769 Oct 6 W.B. Monkhouse Journal in Cook Journal (1955) 1/567]

>  ... one of [the Maoris] sold his <i>patoo patoo</i> as he called it, a short weapon of green talk ... <i>(Oct 11, 1769,Banks Journal,1/406,1962 ed.</i>)

>  ... they have short Truncheons about a foot long, which they call Pattoo Pattoos 5, some made of wood some of bone and others of stone. [fn5] <i>Patu</i> was a generic name for these weapons; Elsdon Best classifies the ancient ones into thirteen different forms, each with its own name (<i>The Maori</i>, II pp. 254 flir?) <i>(Nov 12, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/200</i>)

>  But they must sometimes get whales because many of their patoo patoos are made from the bones of some such like fish ... <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/277,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Patoo Pattos</i> as they calld them, a kind of small hand bludgeon of stone, bone or hard wood most admirably calculated for the cracking of sculls. [fn] [Beaglehole] ... The most beautiful of <i>patu</i> was of course the greenstone <i>mere</i>. <i>(Mar 1770,Banks Journal,2/27,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAYLY</b> As soon as they came within about 20 yards distance from the ship the old man or chief stood up & waved a green leaf as did the man that sat before him & likewise flurrished his Papepatoo over his hed [sic] & began a speech that lasted near 15'. <i>(Jun 4, 1773,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1773 June 4 Bayly's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/208]

>  <b>LEDYARD</b> And when the men were busy ... the warriors rushed out upon them and killed them with their Patapatows, and then divided their bodies among them. <i>(Feb 1777,Journal,18,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EDGAR</b> Their Weapons consists of Spears darts and battle Axes & Patoo Patoos - the Patoo Patoo's is an instrument made of bone of large Fish or Stone -it is in shape of a battledore [cf WADDY 1]& about 16 or 18 Inches long, <i>(Feb 12, 1777,Log</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb 12 Edgar's Log in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/225]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... he immediately drew out of his girdle a pattoo-pattoo, or short flat club made of a great fish's bone ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/141</i>)

>  <b>COLLINS</b> Pat-too pat-too, <i>To beat, also the name of </i>a principal weapon. <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,560</i>)

>  The battle then became general. A number of women were in the heat of the action, among whom was Tippahee's old wife, not much less than seventy years of age, and Duaterra's wife bearing in her hand a patoo about seven feet long, made out of the jawbone of a whale. [fn] This weapon, the <i>hoeroa</i>, was made almost invariably of whale bone. All specimens extant are made from the lower jaw of the sperm whale. Both ends are rounded, one end being elaborately carved; there is often, also, some carving about the middle. About five feet in length, it seems to have been used, for the most part, as a missile weapon. - <i>Vide</i> Elsdon Best, <i>The Maori As He Was</i>, p.276 <i>et seq. </i> <i>(1814,Letters & Journals,92 and fn [=hoeroa],1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> Some of his officers stood up and regulated all their movements both by word of command and signal made by their large pattoes ornamented with feathers which they held in their hands and kept in constant motion. <i>(1814,Letters & Journals,91,1932 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [=taiaha or tewhatewha]]

>  <b>ELDER</b> <i>(1814,Marsden's Lieutenants,86</i>) [NOTES: [(for Patus) PATU; with useful note] (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> The chiefs were all seated on the ground, according to their custom, and their warriors standing up with their spears fixed upright which were from 15 to 20 feet in length and upwards. They were armed also with their pattooes (<i>patus</i>). [fn] The root idea of the word <i>patu</i> is `to strike.' The word is applied by the Maoris to all weapons, and also, with a qualifying adjective, to pounders and beaters of flax, fern-root, etc. The early explorers, e.g. Cook, restricted the term to weapons of the <i>mere </i>type, and, as a generic term for this group of weapons is useful - in fact necessary - the usage, though not strictly a Maori one, has been followed by modern ethnographers. <i>(1814,Letters & Journals,86 and fn</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> patoo patoo. [J.C.A. notes `the form has long been discarded. Moreover it is not duplicated' cf Nicholas's <i>kiki</i> `food' (kai).] <i>Ibid.</i> 1/89 The men in the canoes ... exhibited ... mats, spears, hooks, fishing-lines, thread, <i>patoo pattoos</i>, (war implements,) for all which they received many useful articles in return. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/337 A war club Pattie <i>(1817,Voyage Round the World,1/69</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [sense 1b, a pestle or pounder, aka patu aruhe]

>  Fernroot pounders, Patu aruhe, manufactured from Maire, Santalum Cunninghamii. <i>(Sep 22, 1874,Star, The,2</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> P|atu p|atu; A club for the purpose of extracting the roots of trees from the ground. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,191</i>)

 [protester group during 1981 Springbok tour]

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> ... each carrying ... a great bunch of tawharas or, in autumn, patungatungas (flower and fruit of the kiekie vine) ... <i>Ibid.</i> 150 There was in spring the delicious tawhara, the fleshy edible flower of the kiekie vine, or later the still more delectable patungatunga, the ripened seed-pod of the tawhara. <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,55,1956 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1956 (1955) Boswell Dim Horizons 55]

### patupaiarehe
 
 <i>n.</i> a fairy people of Maori legend [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Is it not probable that these Patupaiarehe derive their existence from atmospheric illusion, some of those spectral or illusory appearances which take place from the power of refraction in the atmosphere, or some other atmospheric phenomena resembling the mirage of the desert? <i>(May 28, 1851,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  Tawhiao also mentioned as a singular coincidence that one of his ancestors, before European settlement, while walking in the bush near where are now situate the Taupiri coal-mines, met a fairy, Patupaiarehe, who in a song told him that the name of this land was Tirani - the Niu Tireni or New Zealand of today. <i>(Feb 14, 1882,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  For on Ngongotaha lived the Patupaiarehe, the Fairy People of long ago. <i>(Jun 25, 1910,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  Kohu is a tragic love story of the Patupaiarehe, the fearsome white skinned dwellers of the misty bush described in Maori legend and histories. <i>(Jan 22, 2004,Wellington Contact,3</i>)

>  She was.. as pretty as one of the patu-paiarehe, the "little" people that in Waikato belief haunted the fairy mountain Pirongia. <i>(Dec 1912,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual : 35</i>)

>  The eyes of the patu-paiarehe still inhabit the high places. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/31</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> It led to a tiny waterfall, as if this were the goal of the patupaiarehe, and then lost itself under crackling puririleaves. <i>(1974,Open Country Muster,213</i>)

>  <b>SHEFFIELD in MALCOLM</b> Back in the centuries before recorded history in New Zealand, the Waitakere Ranges were.. peopled by a fairy race, the patupaiarehe. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,146</i>)

>  The semi-human people known as patupaiarehe, Turehu, Nanakia, &c., are said to have been the original inhabitants of these isles, and were here found in possession by the earliest Polynesian migrants, who probably arrived here some 900 years ago. <i>(Feb 10, 1898,Otago Witness,46</i>)

>  Patupaiarehe (fairies) were also said to hide in the clumps of kakaha. <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  It is known for certain that those seen are the atua , or spirit-like people, the Patupaearehe, &c., and not Tangata Maori or people of the Maori race <i>(1909,Journal of the Polynesian Society,III/34</i>)

>  <b>RAWLINSON</b> When the night was rolled away, and the mists began to clear, / The Patupaiarehe folk looked up like startled deer. <i>(1974,Poems to Read to You,00</i>) [NOTES: Unpaged]

>  He enlists that old favourite the Tamil bell, which he insists was discovered by a "Bishop Colenso" near Ruapuke Beach (it was found in Northland by Chiurch Missionary printer William Colenso);... the Korotangi bird valued by Maori as a Tainui taonga but identified by Menzies as evidence of a Chinese presence; and the legend about Patupaiarehe or fairy people, which Menzies alleges is a local Ruapuke story when in fact it is common to tribes throughout the North Island. <i>(Jan 25, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),31</i>)

>  He disappeared back into the bush. My imagination told me he was some sort of patupaiarehe or bush creature. <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,74</i>)

>  The green garment of the hills was a thing to be loved where dwelt.. the mystic fairy race, the patu-paiarehe, who loved the dimness of the woods and the misty tops of mountains. <i>(Mar 2, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,60</i>)

>  Had.. the Mist Maid still power to hide the coming of the bush fairies, the Patu-paiarehe, as they crept forth to entice away a mortal maid? <i>(Oct 12, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,94</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> I have very long pakaka hair, urukehufrom my patupaiarehe strain. <i>(1992,Mihipeka,105</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>patupaiarehe</i>: fairies. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> To be as best he might, moreover,/ That <i>Patu-paere's</i> pet and lover!/ <i>Ibid.</i> 1/301 The <i>Patu-paere</i> were supernatural beings in some particulars resembling European Fairies; though some apparently were Giants. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/18</i>)

>  In those good old times the interior was occupied, they say, by Turehu or Patupaiarehe, a race short in stature and of fair skin. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/32</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... directly [the Maori] enters the domain of witchcraft, `tipau' (ghouls), `patupaiarahe' (fairy demons), dreams, or tohunga imbecilities, he becomes a fear-haunted, helpless perverter of truth! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,63</i>)

>  ... she knew what it was to become the wife of a Patu-paiarehe. <i>(Apr 11, 1921,Quick March,47</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> Even to-day the Tuhoe folk speak of what they called the Tangata Whenua (the race of men that dwelt in this little region), a smaller, much more gentle, and fair-skinned people called the `Patu-pai-arehe,' or the fairy people ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,58</i>)

>  <i>patu-paiarehe:</i> fairy. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <i>(Nov 29, 1957,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>) [NOTES: 1957 Nov 29 Listener (Glover) in 1961 Reid 210]

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> We are immortal you and I, just like the patupaiarehe, the dream people who live in the hills. <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,15</i>)

>  Mount Pirongia, in the Waikato, is well known to Maoris as the home of Patupaiarehe (fairy people), said to have haunted locals and kidnapped women centuries ago. <i>(Nov 8, 1987,Sunday News,3</i>)

>  There were two Maori stories about its origins. One had it that it was built by fairies - patupaiarehe. <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>) [NOTES: Sinclair `At the Point']

>  To [the locals], the most striking thing about these foreigners was their white skin. This feature was a characteristic of creatures known to them as Patupaiarehe, the fairy-like inhabitants of the forest. These white-skinned beings of the imagination were also known as pakepakeha and pakehakeha. These words are the direct antecedents of the word Pakeha. There is nothing pejorative about this derivation of the meaning and use of the word Pakeha to denote the white strangers who came to these shores in the 18th century. <i>(May 14, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),100</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, (attrib. uses)]

 <i>n.</i>

>  This fish is caught with a hook of wood, covered with pieces of the pawa, or mutton fish shell, to give it the appearance of a fish. <i>(May 16, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  The mutton fish, or pawa, although resembling India rubber in toughness and colour, is very excellent and substantial food for explorers, both European and native; and when it can be obtained, which is only at low water, Spring tide, is much prized by these gentlemen. <i>(Sep 12, 1846,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,111</i>)

>  Mention paua and most New Zealanders will think of the disc-shaped grey-green batter-covered fritters that were once a standard in fish and chip shops... In the past couple of years farmed baby paua has come on to the market, he says - a kind of shellfish answer to veal. <i>(Sep 7, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

>  He has turned the idea around by using a modern medium - stainless steel - to create wall reliefs with slots cut to reveal a glimpse of laminated sheets of paua behind... He and his partner.. wanted paua on their menu because it is so typically New Zealand, but, at those prices, the only solution was to make it go a long way. Only a teaspoon of minced paua goes into each ravioli.. which is served with a beurre blanc and shoe-string frites of kumara. <i>(Sep 7, 2002,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  Pa Brown said he thought Ngawha needed a prison " like a paua needs an ashtray". <i>(May 19, 2002,NZ Catholic,8</i>)

>  At low water the great delicacies called pawa and pipis are to be obtained in any quantity. <i>(Jan 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,17</i>)

>  From Nelson has been sent a card of very pretty ornaments.. which are constructed.. of the Pawa shell. <i>(Oct 11, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 1</i>)

>  <i>Pa&#769;.u-a</i> (pa.&#769;.u-&adot.;<i> colloq</i>. pa.&#769;.wa. or pou&#769;.&adot.), n. [Maori.] (<i>Zoo.l.</i>) The ear-shell (<i>Haliotis iris</i>); also, a fishhook made by the Maoris from this shell. <i>[Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

>  "We'd sit in the sun, fish, get pauas, cook them, potter, sit back, enjoy the baches." <i>(Jan 19, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  My father and I were out gathering pawas on Saturday. <i>(Nov 11, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>paua</i>: shellfish whose shell grows up to 6 in. in length and has a wonderful internal lustre of opalescent greens and blues. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [yellow-foot]

### paunch dump

### pav
 
 <i>v.</i> use in a pavlova

### pavlova
 
 <i>n.</i> a meringue dessert cake, for which the first recipe was published in 1929 in New Zealand. [Note on conversation with Deidre Macken (60 yrs) about her mother's story (86 yrs). She was in the Wellington Ballet Company in 1926 and on news of Pavlova's tour wrote to her manager enquiring how they could help make her tour and reception more pleasant, her favourite colours, foods, etc. She expressed a great liking for meringue (cakes) so they consulted a chef who produced 3 attempts (e.g. a square cake was rejected in favour of a round one); finally the `soft-centre' variety was chosen. In her honour they named it Pavlova (?cake) and they presented this to Pavlova (? at a gathering) and she liked it a great deal. D. Macken is to research this - ? Ballet company records, etc. Deidre Macken (04) 384-6073 - H.D. Macken, Nairn St.]

>  The Editor,The DominionPavlova - Name and NatureSir, -- In spite of silly claims regularly surfacing especially in Australia (Dominion 2/2/99), the facts of the development of the confections and the date the name `pavlova' was applied to them have been reasonably well known for some years now. They may be found in the Dictionary of NZ English (1997), the Australian National Dictionary (1988), and the writings of Professor Helen Leach of Otago University.The name `pavlova' post-dated the ballerina Pavlova's Australasian tour of 1926, and was first applied in New Zealand by the Davis Gelatine Company in 1927 to a jelly dessert whose moulded multicoloured shape may have suggested a tutu. It was next (in 1929) applied independently (and only in NZ) as `pavlova cake' or `sponge' or `meringue' to the previously well known so-called `meringue cake' or `sponge'. The recipe change to the legendary pavlova icon is usually reputed in NZ to be the addition of vinegar to give the soft centre, and in Australia, the trademark of passionfruit in the topping. (The name `pavlova' does not occur in either the Futter book (1926) or the Terrace Tested Recipes (1927) mentioned in your report.)The elliptical simple form `pavlova' for the meringue is recorded earliest in Australia from 1940 (so AND, two of whose quotations acknowledge NZ as the originator) and in NZ from 1953 (DNZE).There is no evidence that the name `pavlova' was applied in Australia before 1940. Both countries share the earliest recorded date 1966 (and the same quotation) for the abbreviation `pav'.The New Zealand Dictionary Centre at Victoria University (Box 600, Wellington) would be glad of any pre-datings of any of the above, with author, title, date, and page references.Harry Orsman <i>(Feb 2, 1999,Dominion,00</i>) [NOTES: Check date of publication. "Terrace Tested Recipes collected by the Ladies of Terrace Congregational Church" was published by Whitcombe & Tombs in 1927 (191 pp). There were three editions in 1927; the first is held by Turnbull, the 3rd was held by Te Papa (Hector Library) but recent report (HO) says they no longer have it.]

>  I'll ask for a toll call anywhere in NewZealand and they'll say, "Mrs Holst, canyou tell me why my pavlova sinks?" <i>(May 9, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  And for dessert there's something different again: pink peppercorn pavlova... <i>(Oct 19, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),16</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> She baked a banana cake and then a Pavlova and covered it with tinned feijoa slices and cream and sprinkled hundreds and thousands over that. <i>(1991,Always the Islands of Memory,135</i>)

>  Contrary to what they'll tell you in Perth, Western Australia, Bert Sache, a chef at the Esplanade Hotel did not invent the pavlova. All he did, in 1935, was to make a meringue and cream cake that he said was "light as Pavlova", the Russian ballet dancer who six years earlier had stayed at the hotel. but the name stuck and thus was born the myth about its origins. The truth is that the large meringue with the marshmallow centre - the heart of the pavlova - was common in New Zealand in the early 1930s, even earlier. In 1927, for instance, there was a recipe for a meringue cake submitted by a Mrs McRae in the second edition of <i>Terrace Tested Recipes</i>, collected by the ladies of the Terrace Congregational Church in Wellington. <i>(May 18, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,5</i>)

>  I felt there should have been cheerios and tomato sauce, meat pies, tinned spaghetti pizzas, pavlovas, lamingtons, sausage rolls, fat club sandwiches, beer and thick cups of steaming tea. <i>(Mar 1991,More Magazine,66</i>)

>  The breakthrough [to substantiate New Zealand's claim for first use of the word] came in 1929, when the <i> NZ Dairy Exporter Annual </i> published the first known recipe for "Pavlova Cake" as we now know it. Granted, the recipe was for a two-layered pavlova, but this, after all, is a common variant ... <i>(Jun 14, 2008,Dominion Post Indulgence,9</i>)

>  A delicious party sweet - a New Zealand specialty is called Pavlova. <i>(Dec 31, 1953,Listener (New Zealand Listener),143</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> multi-coloured and layered jelly mould, having the appearance of a tasteless tutu [[Could this have been both in the jelly and meringue varieties originally an Otago-conceived dish? cf SOUTHLAND SURPRISE mentioned in ?MORE magazine]]

>  <i>Pavlova</i> [heading below a coloured picture of a fluted (`pleated' or `tutued') turned-out moulded jelly mound, coloured in layers from top to bottom green, white, orange-yellow, and red, and standing on a plain dish, with no background] <i>Ingredients </i>1 1/2 cups hot water/ 1 cup orange juice/ 1/2 cup milk/ 3 dessertspoons Davis gelatine/ 6 dessertspoons sugar/ Flavouring/ cochineal - sap green colouring <i>Directions </i>Dissolve all but a teaspoonful of Gelatine ... [arrange orange or other fruits around base: serve with cream or custard] <i>(1927,Davis Dainty Dishes,11,6th ed.</i>)

>  <i>Pavlova 8-10 servings.</i>[The coloured picture is now larger, the background, and directions, more complex] 8-10 Servings <i>(1937,Davis Dainty Dishes,39,13th ed.</i>)

>  <i>Pavlova 6 Servings</i> [Picture essential;ly same as 1937: added directions for use of refrigerator.] <i>(1943,Davis Dainty Dishes,57,reprint ed.</i>)

>  <i>PAVLOVA </i>... hot water ... orange juice ... milk ... Davis Gelatine ... sugar ... Flavouring ... Cochineal - sap green ... serve with cream or custard ... <i>(1928,Davis Dainty Dishes,11</i>)

>  Pavlova. 6 Servings. Ingredients. Davis Gelatine, Hot water, Sugar, 1/4 cup milk, Slices of oranges, 3/4 cup orange juice, Lemon essence, Vanilla essence, Cochineal, Green colouring, Citric acid. Method. First layer: Dissolve 1-1/2 teaspoons gelatine and 1 dessertspoonful sugar in 1/2 cup hot water; add a pinch of citric acid and lemon essence to flavour. Divide the mixture, colour one half red and the other green. Pour the green into a mould. Second layer: Dissolve 1 teaspoon gelatine in 2 tablespoons hot water. Add 1 Dessertspoon sugar to 1/4 cup milk; stir until dissolved. Flavour with vanilla essence. Add gelatine. Carefully pour on to the green layer, which must be firm. Third layer: Dissolve 1 dessertspoon gelatine in 1/4 cup hot water, add 3/4 cup orange juice, 3 dessertspoons sugar or more. When the milk layer is firm, arrange orange circles round the mould and add the orange jelly, which should be thickening a little at a time. Fourth layer: Re-melt the red jelly, if it has set, by standing in hot water. When cold pour over the orange layer, which should be firm. <i>If setting in an ice-chest or refrigerator, use less gelatine - First mixture: 1 teaspoon. Second mixture: 3/4 teaspoon. Third mixture: 3/4 dessertspoon. </i> <i>(1937,Davis Dainty Dishes,57</i>) [NOTES: 1937 Davis Gelatine Organisation Davis Dainty Dishes [editions 1-8 1922 to 1936] 57]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a meringue cake with various toppings [[Not in the NZ [Country] Women's Institutes' [The] Cookery Book 1936 (4th edn) to 1955 reprint]]

>  <b>BURTON</b> Pavlova is more than a national dish, it is an institution. ... Sweet, airy and laden with calories, the cake was named after Anna Pavlova who visited New Zealand in 1926. ... It is worth mentioning here that the Australians claim to have invented the pavlova, and insist that it was created in honour of Pavlova by a Melbourne woman in 1926. New Zealanders, however, might answer that in the same year a pavlova recipe appeared in a book entitled <i>Home Cookery for New Zealand </i>(although it went under the name of `Meringue with Fruit Filling') and that another recipe actually called `Pavlova' was published the following year. Certainly the classic kiwkfruit (Chinese gooseberry) topping which the Australians have also adopted must belong to New Zealand. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,131</i>)

>  I would to point out that both the Irish version and the Australian recipe are for a -->meringue cake (sugar and egg whites) and not for the traditional New Zealand pavlova which has a -->soft centre, because of the inclusion of vinegar and cornflour in the N.Z. recipe. I don't think we'll ever discover who had the original idea which transformed a hard oversweet dessert into the delicious dessert of today. I was at University in Dunedin from 1928 to 1932, and well remember my mother making pavlova during the summer holidays - the latest popular recipe from a fellow-member of the local Croquet Club - probably 1928-1929. I remember how carefully she watched over the oven and then turned it onto a plate - almost holding her breath. <i>(Jan 1, 1987,Otago Daily Times</i>) [NOTES: 1987 Jan 1 N. M. THOMSON in Otago Daily Times p.c. `Prester John']
