### pavlova cake
 
 <i>n.</i> a small cake rather than the PAVLOVA as we know it

>  <b>McKAY</b> <i>PAVLOVA CAKES</i> whites of two eggs beaten ... sugar ... cornflour ... coffee essence ... chopped walnuts. Cook like meringues. They are delightful ... a novelty. <i>(1929,Practical Home Cookery,155</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> a cake consisting of a meringue shell filled with fruit and whipped cream. [after Anna Pavlova, 1885-1931, a ballet dancer] meringue cake, meringue (sandwich) sponge (Anna Pavlova visited N.Z. in 1926) [now usu. pavlova q.v.]

>  I have for many years cherished the heretical theory that the reason for the lack of any of the distinctive flavour in much of the food served in New Zealand can be traced to the national devotion to Pavlova Cake. This sugary confection has, for most adult palates, all the charm of heavily-scented shampoo folded into well-beaten shaving cream. <i>(Mar 2, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  <b>FINLAY</b> No. 452.- MERINGUE SPONGE SANDWICH OR PAVLOVA CAKE. ...Whites of 3 eggs/ 7 or 8 ozs. Sugar/ Pinch of Salt/ 1 Teaspoon vanilla essence/ 1 Teaspoon vinegar/ Suitable tins, 8in. diam. Sandwich tins or special Pavlova cake tin. Method.- Add salt to egg whites. Whisk them stiffly; add sugar gradually and continue beating for a few minutes. Carefully mix in vinegar and essence. Place in greased tins and cook in a slow oven for 1 to 1 1/2 hours. [When cooked, turn out carefully. Fasten cakes together with a filling of whipped cream. Some tart fruit such as raspberries or loganberries may be added to the cream. Cream nay also be piled on top, finishing off with fruit used in centre.] <i>(1933,Cookery,125</i>) [NOTES: c1933 Finlay Cookery 125]

>  <i>POVLOVA CAKE</i> [sic] ...egg whites ... Maizena ... castor sugar ... vinegar ... Cathedral brand vanilla ... Beat ... egg whites ... add sugar, vinegar ... cornflour and essence last ... bake in a very cool oven ... cover with whipped cream and any fruit ... <i>(1936,Home of Compassion Book of Recipes,101</i>)

>  [no instructions for decoration - just meringue recipe] <i>PAVLOVA CAKE</i> ... sugar ... vinegar ... egg whites ... Beat egg whites very stiff, add sugar ... add any colouring matter required ... bake slowly. <i>(1936,Recipe Book [St Mary's],63</i>)

>  <b>BASHAM</b> <i>PAVLOVA CAKE</i> ... castor sugar ... whites of 3 eggs, beaten very stiff ... vanilla ... vinegar ... Grease a piece of grease-proof paper, hold it under the cold tap for a second, shake the water off, then pile the cake upon it either in a round or square shape and bake ... <i>(1939,Aunt Daisy's Cookery Book,153</i>)

>  PAVLOVA CAKE ... Beat ... whites of ... eggs ... sugar ... vinegar ... vanilla essence ... Bake ... When cooked cover with whipped cream and decorate with strawberries or raspberries or any tinned fruit. <i>(1940,RSA Christchurch Women's Section Cookery Book,38</i>) [NOTES: c1940s-1950s R.S.A Christchurch Women's Section Cookery Book 38]

>  <b>TURNER</b> Cooking terms noticed by newcomers (other than Australians) are ... <i>pikelets</i> ... and <i>pavlova cake</i>, a meringue sweet, sometimes shortened to<i> pav. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,173</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> However, those people holding the view that the [67] meringue-like delicacy ...the Pavlova Cake, is her ultimate achievement, are guilty in their assessment of women ... <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,66</i>)

>  Pavlova cake has for so many years been a staple of any party ... [`Urumoa' writing from Oamaru searched old recipe books and found] 1. Meringue cake, copied from the Otago Daily Times of June 26, 1934. This is the same as the modern pavlova without the one teaspoon of cornflour. 2. `Cookery' by Miss I. Finlay [see preceding entry], published by the Dunedin City gas department, third edition, about 1933. ... recipe No. 452 for meringue sponge sandwich or pavlova cake, for which I was surprised to see she suggested sandwich tins or a special pavlova tin. When I bought one of the latter recently I thought they were something new! 3. Two privately-published but undated recipe books from Invercargill which must be pre-1933, as we left the city in that year. Both contain recipes for pavlova. [But do they specify the name `pavlova' ? HWO] It would be interesting ... to find out when the teaspoon of vinegar and teaspoon of cornflour were added to the recipe as they give pavlovas their marshmallow-type centre. The recipes I have mentioned list only egg whites, sugar, vanilla and a pinch of salt. [Pavlova visited New Zealand in 1926: Auckland May 1926, Wanganui, Hastings, Napier, Palmerston North, Wellington, Christchurch, Timaru, Dunedin: see Maurice Hurst <i>Music and the Stage in New Zealand.</i>Pavlova cake has for so many years been a staple of any party ... <i>(May 2, 1974,Otago Daily Times,10</i>)

>  The relationship between tests, yields, and pay-outs has been exercising some dairy companies at their annual meetings. <i>(Aug 29, 1925,NZ Dairy Produce Exporter,53</i>)

>  The average pay-out will be 13.66d per lb. of butterfat. <i>(Jul 23, 1937,Morrinsville Star,4</i>)

### pay rock
 
 <i>n.</i> the quartz-miners' equivalent of pay-dirt [aka pay-rock]

>  WAIHI COMPANY'S return of GBP6,200 from 2000 tons of rock for the month and the opening up of a large block of pay rock at the lowest level has caused a considerable enquiry for shares at an advanced figure ... <i>(Jun 16, 1894,Observer (Auckland),18</i>)

>  You must ascertain the actual value per ton, and the width of pay rock at the points already intersected; also the extent of payable rock along the course of the lode (S.E.), and its width at various levels, by crosscuts made at regular intervals. <i>(Jan 5, 1872,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  The `pay-rock' forms bands or streaks. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,13</i>)

### pay-dirt
 
 <i>n.</i> 1. mining: goldbearing ground or material worth the trouble of working. 2. (informal) any profitable situation.

>  <b>MAY</b> WASH-DIRT: the auriferous gravel, sand, clay or cement in which the greater proportion of gold is found. Synonyms were `wash', `pay-dirt', or just `dirt'. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,528,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> <i>Paydirt</i>: worthwhile portion of an alluvial deposit or of the ground being excavated by a miner. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,113</i>)

### pay-shoot
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Upon this a shaft was sunk to a depth of 20ft. showing the lode-matter to be well defined throughout, the shoot of pay-ore which was carried down for 16ft. appearing to pitch towards the north. This ore-shoot appears to have characterized by the occurrence in considerable quantities of richmondite, which appeared in stringers 3in. to 7in. in thickness. High values in silver, ranging from 2 oz. to 1,700 oz. to the ton, were carried in this pay-shoot. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.3 (N.S.)/97</i>) [NOTES: (Bell-Nelson)]

### pay-streak
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  After severe north-westerly gales it is not at all an unusual thing ... to find freshly deposited beach leads, which give handsome returns for a few days until the next severe storm shifts the pay streak out to sea. <i>(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.1 (N.S.)/4</i>) [NOTES: (Bell-Hokitika)]

### payable
 
 <i>adj.</i> [goldmining] (See [cf. pay-dirt](p-per.md#pay-dirt),[pay-rock](p-per.md#pay-rock))

>  There are several tunnels at present in course of construction and the work of driving into the terrace is being vigorously proceeded with, but owing to the great distance which they have to drive, it will take a long time before they will be able to complete these, and to commence making anything payable out of the ground. <i>(Mar 11, 1870,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  On the Terrace, Bourke and party continue to obtain payable washdirt at a depth of about 28 feet. The holders of the adjoining claim, Kinsella and party, have also bottomed at a depth of 24 feet, and obtained a payable prospect. <i>(Aug 3, 1867,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  Allen and party, on the opposite side of the creek, bottomed last week at a depth of fifty feet, and struck payable dirt. <i>(Aug 3, 1867,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  On the bottom they did not succeed in obtaining a payable prospect. <i>(Aug 3, 1867,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  Near German creek, Allen and party .. expect to reach payable washdirt about the beginning of next week. <i>(Aug 3, 1867,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  Payable wash has not been struck yet by any of the holders of claims on the terrace. <i>(Aug 3, 1867,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  The richest shaft yet bottomed has nine feet of wash dirt, all payable, four feet of it going six grains to the dish all the way. <i>(Jan 16, 1891,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  That gold exists there is well known; it has long been believed payable; but like other fields it has not had a fair chance. <i>(Sep 27, 1913,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  ... thus the gold which, when spread over a large area is not `payable', is found in `rich' quantities when circumstances have brought it to one spot. <i>(Oct 4, 1861,NZ Goldfields,34,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Oct 4 Dunedin in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 34]

>  The river and creeks that run through them are auriferous - are payable. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and Resources,2</i>)

>  ... I am of the opinion that it is a payable gold-field. ... in the gullies on the west side of the Dunstan Ranges there was almost invariably gold obtained; ... the prophets of ill ... have from time to time expressed an opinion that the Otago goldfields would soon be exhausted; that the area of payable country was comparatively small, and that in a short time it would be all worked out. ... he [a prospector with Californian and Australian experience] expressed his opinion that `Quartz Creek would turn out to be a payable gold-field;' ... <i>Ibid.</i> About twelve or fifteen miles below the junction ... we first obtained payable prospects. We tried a number of small bars that we thought would pay about an ounce a-day by working with a cradle. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Have been out prospecting to the south east and bottomed tonight a hole which the men ... say is a payable prospect. <i>(Jun 21, 1863,Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger</i>) [NOTES: 1863 June 21 Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger in 1931 Aug 27 Star (Christchurch) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 96]

>  <b>PYKE</b> Three miners ... discovered payable auriferous ground on the western watershed ... <i>(1863,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1863 V. Pyke's Report in 1887 Handbook NZ Mines 8]

>  I consider the prospects of [Tuapeka] goldfield are very much above the average of what, in Victoria, would be called `payable ground' <i>(1863,AJHR,D/6/14</i>) [NOTES: (Report of Drummond).]

>  ... payable ground. <i>(1865,Evening Post,No. 52/2</i>)

>  I am of opinion that this will be a good payable goldfield. <i>(May 20, 1869,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1869 May 20 letter in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 16]

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... the hope of being the first to discover either a prospect of payable gold, or some open country fit for farming purposes, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 81 ... we had no discovery of anything payable in the way of gold, yet we yet enjoyed the trip very much. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,47</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> They were bound for a creek ... where some payable finds ... had just been made by some prospecting party. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,101</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> He struck gold in payable wash ... The wash was rather thin. <i>Ibid.</i> 344 <i>Payable, a.</i> In Australia, able to be worked at a profit: that which is likely to pay; not only, as in England, due for payment. <i>(1898,Austral English,14</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> ... a petition was presented to the Council asking that a substantial reward should be offered for the discovery of a payable gold-field, and so prevent a further loss of population. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,198</i>)

>  <b>WARDON</b> He struck gold in payable wash ... The wash was rather thin. <i>(1892,MacPherson's Gully,14</i>)

>  <i>~ a.</i> of a mine, claim, etc., able to give a higher return than the working cost. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  ... the gravel beds are quite payable, but a difficulty is that bands of clay overlie the wash. <i>(1939,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,(NS) 39/110</i>) [NOTES: (Williamson, Naseby)]

>  <i>Pay&#769;.a-ble (p&amacr.&#769;.&adot.-b'l),</i> a. [Add.] 3. <i>Likely to yield a profit; profitable; - as, payable</i> quartz;<i> payable </i>washdirt; a <i>payable</i> commercial <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> (of fleeces)

>  <b>PERRY</b> Wethers will thrive ... and clip big payable fleeces, besides taking little attention. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,96</i>)

### payable gold
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Walterson and party and the holders of the adjoining claim are the last who have bottomed on payable gold. <i>(Aug 3, 1867,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  There is a small gold rush on the Buller road, opposite Junker's. One party is on payable gold. <i>(Dec 10, 1888,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  Almost daily claims are bottoming on the payable gold at Back Creek. <i>(Jan 16, 1891,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  A number of native land owners are proceeding to Wellington to sell the Waihou block, on the Upper Hokianga river, where it is reported payable gold has been found. <i>(Dec 19, 1904,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Could you expect to get payable gold with a prospecting box? <i>(Nov 8, 1928,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> During the first fortnight there have been several rushes up to the ... Southern Alps, where the diggers are finding good, payable gold. <i>(1866,Polynesia,133</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We ... did not locate payable gold. As a matter of fact we passed the gold zone as it is now recognised, as the alluvial only extends a certain distance back from the coast line. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,42</i>)

### payable goldfield
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is a probablility that something like a payable gold field may be discovered in that direction, as the country is likely-looking, and there is no doubt that the ground will be thoroughly prospected. <i>(Mar 11, 1870,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  I would observe, however, that the alluvial deposit on the banks of the creek at Invercargill is sufficiently indicative of the probability of a payable gold-field; if not in the very township, somewhere in the locality. <i>(Jun 27, 1857,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  That the Slate River contains gold in abundance is proved beyond a doubt, and may be said to be the only really payable gold field yet found; but when could it be said until now that Nelson only contained one Slate river? <i>(Dec 23, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  You may take it from me that I and those associated with me in the venture we have launched are absolutely convinced that a payble goldfield exists at Terawhiti. <i>(Sep 27, 1913,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  The first payable gold-field was at Collingwood, in the Nelson district. <i>(Jan 1, 1907,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  GBP1,000 REWARD FOR A PAYABLE GOLDFIELD. <i>(Mar 13, 1884,Bay of Plenty Times,3</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> `We the undersigned Diggers in the Lindis Valley hereby certify that Samuel M'Intyre was the first discoverer of this Goldfield, and that he is entitled to the reward offered by the Government, the field being a payable Goldfield. <i>(Apr 15, 1861,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,23,1962 ed.</i>)

### payable ground
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The guarded manner in which Mr. M'Gregor always gives the results of his explorations leads us to believe that the gullies which he designates as "payable ground" will prove at least to bear out that description ... <i>(May 23, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  We believe that a properly-adapted water supply for the auriferous districts in Collingwood is all that is requisite to open up considerable tracts of payable ground both of quartz and alluvial. <i>(Aug 19, 1871,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,4</i>)

>  The Seven Mile journey was soon over, and the old men disembarked and were soon busily engaged in pointing out the various once payable ground, to those of the number who had not included the Point in their itinerary. <i>(Jan 14, 1914,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  An instance of these intermittent successes is given in the case of of Hartley & Riley, which, though in the ultimate a prosperous venture, yet in the initial stages gave every indication of being a failure, and persistent efforts were necessary before payable ground was reached. <i>(Dec 12, 1925,NZ Truth,8</i>)

>  That there is a large area of payable ground in the district admits of little doubt, at least to those who are acquainted with the district and its capabilities and the present writer had intimate relations therewith for some years. <i>(Oct 14, 1902,West Coast Times,3</i>)

>  The Waitahuna Dredging Company having now got over the strip of ground they were working on, are again upon good payable ground. <i>(Sep 11, 1897,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  [He] did not succeed in discovering payable ground anywhere. <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,60,1962 ed.</i>)

### payable prospect
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Many new arrivals from Victoria, and the mines of Waitahuna and Gabriel's, have taken up claims on the river bank, and have obtained payable prospects, and ere long all the available Government ground adjoining the private property, is likely to be in the occupation of the greatest wealth creators this country has yet seen - Victoria's stalwart miners. <i>(Dec 28, 1861,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  Evidence was tendered from the mining point of view to the effect that highy payable prospects had been obtained in the valley, the greater part of which had been pegged out. <i>(May 31, 1900,Otago Witness,42</i>)

>  No. 7 has been driven to 24ft., in payable prospects. <i>(Jul 8, 1914,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  H. Murphy and party have obtained payable prospects in their shaft. <i>(Apr 27, 1877,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Highly payable prospects continue to be obtained in the Great Wonder mine. <i>(Nov 10, 1883,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  The stone all through gave payable prospects. <i>(Dec 19, 1898,Colonist (Nelson),3</i>)

### payable washdirt
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  An interesting discovery has been made at Tuapeka, in the claim of the Nelson Company, namely, no less than 45 feet of payable wash dirt. <i>(Feb 19, 1863,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Payable wash dirt was met with on Saturday. <i>(Feb 10, 1879,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  About two months ago a party of men took up a claim at Mokihinui, and soon came upon payable wash dirt. <i>(Jan 14, 1882,Te Aroha News,1</i>)

### paying
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  ... we are now satisfied that we have a good paying claim; my mates think that now we have not less than 48 ounces which we have got in these six days. <i>(Aug 5, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  YOUTHS GIVEN P.D. SENTENCE Two youths involved in a series of crimes,...were each sentenced to six months periodic detention.... <i>(Jan 27, 1983,Press, The,5</i>)

### payout
 
 <i>n.</i> the cheque for milk products

>  The good payout years came along at the right time and so did the chance to buy the farm. <i>(May 9, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  The payout dropped from a high of $5.28kg the year before to $3.98 in 2002/03. <i>(Jun 24, 2004,Dominion Post,C12</i>)

>  But increasing numbers of conversion during a period of rising milk payouts have shown bigger cash surpluses for farm owners who employed a manager of contract milker rather than a 450-50 sharemilker. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,32</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the payment per kg. of milk solids paid to a dairy farmer member of a co-operative dairy company (formerly per lb of butterfat).

>  The directors of dairy factories recognise that at the end of each season their suppliers compare the payout received with that of neighbouring factories ... <i>(Jul 3, 1920,Hawera Star,7</i>)

>  The slump in payout, and a harder spring with cows short of feed, compounded a nightmare start to the season. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,25</i>)

>  <b>YEREX</b> But dairy farmers were striving at that time to offset poor returns and low payouts by milking more cows ... <i>(1989,Empire of the Dairy Farmers,117</i>)

>  Kiwi Co-operative Dairies Ltd will announce its 1998-99 payout to suppliers tomorrow. <i>(Jul 1, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,15</i>)

>  A uniform national payout for milksolids will impact heavily on land prices in Waikato and Taranaki. In Canterbury and Southland the payouts have been low, and farm operating costs are much higher than in Waikato/Taranaki. <i>(Dec 16, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  Anyone getting out of dairying this season will go out with a real bang - a super climatic season, a record payout, and incredible selling prices for the herd. <i>(Dec 21, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  The Dairy Board will announce this season's final payout in the last week of this month. <i>(May 18, 2001,Dominion,13</i>)

>  ... we want to capitalise on today's high payouts. <i>(Nov 2001,Dairying Today,34</i>)

 <i>abbr.</i> Porirua Business Development Society

### PD
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Periodic Detention

>  [Hdg] New site found for PD and probation services in U Hutt <i>(Dec 5, 2000,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  There is more joy in Heaven over one person on PD who repenteth than over ninety-and-nine just persons who have never done PD. <i>(Mar 24, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  I don't miss the weekend PD around the home or the open space. <i>(Jan 30, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  By now I was sick of losing plants and replacing lights, so I plead not guilty and refuse to pay a fine or do PD. <i>(Aug 2003,New Zealand (various authors/titles),7(2):30</i>)

>  The judge notes the male defendant's familiarity with the courts, wearily ticking off the various traffic violations, a suspended sentence for burglary, six breaches of PD ... "Your request for PD.," he tells a lawyer, "falls on somewhat stony ground." <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Then he switched to a job as a PD warden at Taupo. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 37</i>)

>  Other non-custodial sentences include periodic detention (PD), which is in NZPOD but not DNZE, and community care (recently renamed community programmes) which is in neither. Another new sanction is the non-association order (1989). <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)

>  Most of use know them as PD (periodic detention) workers. Their title, however, is now "Community Workers" and they have been an essential tool in restoring the inlet to its former beauty and natural habitat... <i>(Oct 27, 2004,Northern Courier,1</i>)

>  At 62, I'm a little too old for PD or a term in prison. <i>(Jun 17, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  <i>My country </i>rastamen on PD/ have slashed the gorse off/ the dry clay slope/ up the back of the pub <i>(Nov 30, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>) [NOTES: (Beyer)]

>  It asked the witness in the trial not to testify against Brown - `You will only get PD (periodic detention) - I could get 10 years'. <i>(Oct 18, 1987,Sunday Star (Auckland),A2</i>)

>  I certainly didn't expect to get PD,' Mrs Hunt said. `I do not even have transport to get to it, let alone the strength for scrub cutting. <i>(Jul 24, 1987,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  `You get guys driving to PD (periodic detention) while disqualified,' he said. <i>(Jun 5, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A9</i>)

>  It is the 25th birthday ... of ...periodic detention. Known [sic] universally among its followers as PD. ... Periodic detention was spawned in 1962 by ... John Robson and Justice Minister Ralph Hannan. Instead of giving soft criminals a prison term they could instead lose their freedom at the weekends and do good work for the community. <i>(Oct 23, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,27</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>abbr.</i> Preventive Detention

>  <b>McKENZIE</b> <i>P.D. </i>Preventive Detention <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,98</i>)

### PDK
 
 <i>abbr.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... chalking the curved E on the empty wagons, the railwayman's shorthand like <i>P.D.K.</i> for paddock, the wide Waltham Yards outside, and <i>PUTTY SHOP</i> for the Addington Workshops. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,22</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... PDK in Christchurch means send to the `paddock', the wide railway yards at Waltham. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,157</i>)

### pe'a
 
 <i>n.</i> the traditional Samoan, male, waist to knee tattoo [ORIGIN: Polynesian]

>  As a Samoan woman she says it is important more Pacific Island artists promote work, and this includes the malu, and pe'a, its male counterpart. <i>(Aug 26, 2003,Independent Herald (Wellington),11</i>)

>  The waist to knee tattoo, or pe'a, is an ancient and precious part of Samoan culture... Urale says when asking people why they had their pe'a done, the common reply was that it gave them a sense of Samoan identity. <i>(Jul 30, 2002,Independent Herald (Wellington),20</i>)

>  Involved in Auckland's Samoan community, Fomison in 1979 had the Samoan pe'a (the tattooing of the male legs and buttock) applied to his body. <i>(Sep 10, 2003,Dominion Post,A12</i>)

>  "In Samoa, woman give birth, men get the pe'a," goes the popular island saying. The pain suffered while getting the pe'a tatau, or traditional Samoan waist to knee tattoo, has been likened to "going through torture" and "being on the verge of death,'... The pe'a is nop just another tattoo. <i>(Dec 28, 2002,Dominion Post,E3</i>)

>  Steve has the full (and extremely painful) Samoan Pe'a, or full thigh to back tattoo. <i>(Jul 2004,AA Directions,Spring/27</i>)

>  "It is not about just getting pe'a tattooed." ... Mr Mulitalo said a Samoan man having his pe'a tattooed by a West Auckland tufuga almost had his leg amputated last year after being admitted to Auckland City Hospital. <i>(Jul 31, 2006,NZ Herald,A2</i>)

### pea
 
 <i>n.</i> part of a scales

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> ... a butcher, who used a steelyards for weighing purposes, so constructed that what was called the `pea' or ball of iron that regulated the weight, could be slipped off and on ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a piece of kauri gum

>  In order of decreasing size, the pieces of gum were known as nuggets, nubs, peas, chips, and dust. <i>(1991,NZ Geographic,41</i>)

### pea rifle
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka pea-rifle]

>  The various agricultural associationshave recently had the pea-rifle nuisancebrought before them prominently. <i>(May 1, 1912,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  A consignment of superior fowling-pieces, rifles, revolvers, &c., from the Ancion Co., consisting of - 2 double-barrel breech-loaders, 6 double-barrel muzzle-loaders, 1 single-barrel muzzle-loader, 1 rifle and 1 pea rifle ... <i>(Jun 29, 1888,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Frank Bone, aged thirteen, while out with a companion, John Hutcheson, aged seventeen, who carried a pea rifle, was accidentally shot at Otautau last night. <i>(Dec 30, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  ALL the HOUSEHOLD FURNITURE, &c., viz. .. Pea Rifle - Brace of Pistols ALSO, A very fine COW, just calved <i>Sale to commence at 2 o'clock precisely.</i> <i>(Feb 19, 1859,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  For Sale .. Pea rifles, Eley's caps and wads. <i>(Dec 26, 1862,Southland Times,1</i>)

>  <b>LORETZ</b> The family has been sitting eating their evening meal at the kitchen table when a pea rifle bullet shot between them and lodged in the wall behind them.<i>Ibid.</i> 96 Mark shoots wallabies with a pearifle. <i>(1976,Moments of Life,6</i>)

>  <b>THE SARGE</b> It would be great.. being a real soldier - not just a blundering Home Guard .. with a pea rifle or a broomstick for a weapon. <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,9</i>)

>  Two young ladies.. after dressing, werejust about to rise, when.. a bullet froma pea rifle whizzed through the air justabove their heads. <i>(Jan 12, 1917,Levin Daily Chronicle</i>)

>  We were promised legislation to put down the pea rifle nuisance. <i>(Apr 23, 1904,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  A Wairarapa settler, who is getting tired of having cows killed by pea-rifle boy fiends has erected a notice: "January to December is a close season for cows." <i>(Jul 11, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>) [NOTES: Check whether "close" or "closed"]

>  The average mother is quite deceived by the term "pea" rifle - believing, no doubt, that the weapons are used for throwing small vegetables about. <i>(May 24, 1913,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  [Hdg] Carter's Quietus Per Medium of a Pea-rifle. Ibid. Carter was lying on his back and beside him was a pea-rifle. <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,10</i>)

>  We had a row and I shot him with a pea-rifle. <i>(Apr 20, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,13</i>)

>  The "didn't know it was loaded" series ofaccidents, extensive as it is, is a metrenothing beside the series of consequencesthat have resulted since the introductionof the deadly pea-rifle. <i>(Jan 28, 1905,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  We depended on Providence, my 230 pea rifle, and two dogs ... <i>(Mar 6, 1880,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  The proprietor .. playfully pointed an American pea rifle at him, and the hammer of the weapon being defective, it went off, shooting the boy in the left breast .. Several attempts were made to extract the bullet, which was only the size of a pea, but without success. <i>(Jul 6, 1881,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  Bertie's heart .. was rejoiced with the present of a handsome pea rifle, which, however, he was only for the present to be allowed to carry when out with his father. <i>(Jun 21, 1894,Otago Witness,45</i>)

>  Armed with a pea rifle.. the borough engineer.. commenced the tricky job of shooting away the fine line without damaging the swinging wires. <i>(Nov 17, 1937,King Country Chronicle,5</i>)

### pea souper
 
 <i>n.</i> a teetotaller

>  <b>JACOBSON</b> The Chaplain said [to the Publican] `I'll take a glass of water'. `We don't sell that here; there's plenty in the creek, and if you ever come in again don't expect any bunting, because we don't fly it for peasoupers. <i>(1893,Tales of Akaroa,246</i>)

### pea, pie and pud
 
 <i>phr.</i> the usual colloquial abbrev. form of the main fare at pie-carts: =pea, pie and spud (mashed potatoes) [ca1945 at least. Wellington.] (See [pie-cart](per-pop.md#pie-cart))

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Eager crowds no longer gathered round the piecart ordering great helpings of "pea, pie and spud" or "steaka da bif". <i>(1971,Our Open Country,244</i>)

>  In fact, it appears that Mr. Yew expects Midland Hotel service (he remarks about cruets being nickled over) for his "one and six a time," and my advice is to dine there in future, or (as an alternative to avoid the restaurants) to try a "pee, [sic] pie, pud" at one of our clean and sanitary pie carts for a "zac." <i>(Jul 4, 1925,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  <i>(Oct 8, 1977,Dominion,2</i>) [NOTES: [see quot. PIE CART] (1904 May 11) 1977 Oct 8 Dominion 2]

>  <b>sla</b> <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A</i>) [NOTES: [see quot PIE-CART]]

>  <b>WALLIS</b> You see this? This is called pea pie pud. It is written on the menu over there on the wall and you may order it simply by saying pea pie pud without any inflection. ... I asked the gentleman behind the counter what it was and he said it was peas, pie and potatoes. <i>(1963,Point of Origin,201</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> `What'll it be, boys? Pea, pie and pud, steak and eggs, or just coffee?' <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,142</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> As a resident, John was virtually a regular, treated to a fare markedly superior to the `pea, pie, and pud' he had at the pie cart on the way back from university on week nights. <i>(1978,It Was So Late,XII</i>) [NOTES: 1978 ed. note in 1949 Cole It Was So Late (1978) xii]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i> pea, pie, and pud:</i> (rhymes with mud): A full meal, a MEAT PIE with a side order of PEAS and MASHED POTATOES. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,75</i>)

>  <b>LYNN</b> ... Norman and Wally went in to the Pie Cart for the usual pie, peas, pud and beetroot [c. 1920s]. <i>(1982,Under the Lynnwood Tree,93</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> ... so there was old Jacko, I mean, on his ear in front of the microphone in a plate full of pea, pie and pud. Fair go. <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,151</i>)

>  From the old "pea, pie, pud" to the gourmet pies now available in supermarkets, Kiwis have been munching the meat-filled pastries for years. <i>(Aug 7, 2002,Capital Times,20</i>)

>  Not all of us are fortunate enough to have extensive larders or the ability to flash downtown to the local specialist shop and I would enjoy learning how to prepare meals that are quick but a little different from the pea, pie and pud. <i>(Jul 2001,Cuisine,18</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I got slender payment." to buy pea pie an' pud." <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,201</i>)

>  [Hdg] Peas pie pud... His customers want what they've always wanted: peas pie pud... "It's the older generation who wants pea pie pud, but unfortunately there's not many around," he says. <i>(Aug 30, 2000,Capital Times,3</i>)

### peak
 
 <i>v.</i> ? to become exhausted; to fail. (? related to <i>peaky</i> `sickly':

 <i>v.</i>

 <i>v.</i> to put the peaks in a lemon-squeezer hat

 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [cows] to produce the greatest amount of milk.

<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [animals] to be exhausted or refusing to work.

### peak note
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tax that high milk producers pay for the processing of milk.

>  Supplying shareholders may take capital notes in exchange for surrendered co-operative shares, supply redemption rights, peak notes or redeemable preference shares. Global Dairy must be able to change the price of peak notes and the peak note standard ... <i>(Oct 5, 2001,Fencepost.com,Select Committee Submission, Dairy Amal.</i>)

>  What are they going to do with the developing Peak Notes fiasco? Peak Notes were designed as a capital funding mechanism for the cost of stainless steel required to process farmers' milk. If you supply more milk at the height [peak] of the production season you force the company to install more and bigger plant and you therefore help pay for that extra requirement by buying more Peak Notes .. farmers are struggling with all of the rules that impact on Peak Notes and there is a fundamental inequity in the system. <i>(Oct 10, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)

### peak rights
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a penalising policy that limits farmer shareholders' milk production.

>  But they and most of the other farmers they know do not want a bar of the company's peak rights proposal that would see new suppliers or existing shareholders pay $30 a litre for any extra milk they produce .. The company maintains that peak rights would see new suppliers coming into the company paying a fuller share of the capital cost to set up new factories. <i>(Nov 16, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  Dairy Group directors are unveiling changes to its new scheme, called peak rights, at a series of supplier meetings this week. Peak rights is designed to make extra milk pay for the extra capacity needed to process it. <i>(Nov 23, 2000,Press, The,19</i>)

>  Most Kiwi shareholders, and most industry commentators, are unimpressed by the peak rights concept. <i>(Mar 15, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,23</i>)

>  He believes winter milking may become a practice of the future because, instead of having to pay premium prices for peak rights, farmers will draw the season out to a year-round operation. <i>(Jul 5, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### peelie
 
 <i>n.</i> a marble

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,Yesterdays in Maoriland,288</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### peely-wally
 
 <i>adj.</i> pale and sickly [in ODE, labelled Scottish]

>  They [the English] hand their children to the care of nurses and cuddle beastly Pekinese pups, ... they allow their peely wally kids to occupy seats in a train while men and women ... have to stand. <i>(Apr 8, 1919,Great Adventure,253,1988 ed.</i>)

### peep-boo's
 
 <i>n.</i> later called eccles cakes

>  <b>McCREDIE</b> <i>PEEP-BOO'S/i> ... butter ... sugar ... eggs ... ground rice ... flour ...baking powder. Divide into balls; make a hole in each ... insert ... jam; ... dip in beaten white of egg, in sugar ... bake .. They should crack and show the jam. <i>(1915,Bungalow Recipe Book, The,9</i>)

### peewee
 
 <i>n.</i> a marble (cf. piss-wee)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Reverend Mother talks of when she used to give you marbles for being a good little boy - pee-wees and agates, and stonies and glassies and a big tor. And once she tried to play with you, and you said she funked because her knuckle was inside the ring.' <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,153</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> `Treasure' I played ringie and holie. I played funs and keeps. I had pee-wees and bully taws and changers that weren't made of glass mind you. And I won nearly every game because the kids were so dazzled by the look of my stinkers. ... They came for miles to play marbles with me ... <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,116,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,Yesterdays in Maoriland,288</i>)

### peg
 
 <i>n.</i> a boundary marker

>  RURAL SECTIONS CHOSEN ... E. Wright. 50 acres in the Christchurch district, commencing on the sixth mile peg, ... <i>(Apr 26, 1851,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> This boundary was always debatable ground - it was always imperatively necessary that claims should be `pegged out', and this rule was of necessity `more honoured in the breach than in the observance.' Pegs were not to be had, unless imported, and when introduced stood a great chance of helping to boil the neighbour's `billy'.' <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,80,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <i>peg n.</i>-peg out' v.t. to mark out a claim as required by law. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> In the matter of a claim he told me that there was a piece of terrace above his top pegs that hadn't been worked out and might be worth a trial. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,88,1963 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a shilling

>  He dreamt he'd got his 14 `peg' / And buttoned it `Down South'. <i>(May 16, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,137</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Peg.</i> 5. A shilling: Scottish c.: 1839, Brandon; Jennings, 1926. Also among New Zealand soldiers in G.W. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,615</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> policeman?; a police spy; a pimp

>  Fine. Diggers passing all day [to rush at Molyneux]. Keddell & two pegs came and stayed all night. <i>(Aug 25, 1862,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> notice, watch, survey

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> A few guys came in and pegged me, but they didn't do anything so I guessed they'd dropped their guts. <i>Ibid.</i> 176 The screws used to peg heavily for heaters during the months of winter.<i>Ibid.</i> 252 PEG (n) Notice, watch, survey. USA, from 1920s, or earlier. Origin too involved for explanation here. See Partridge. <i>Dictionary of the Underworld,</i> for an interesting derivation. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,117</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to mark out

>  <i>Peg</i> (p&ebreve.g),<i> v. t. 3. [Add.]</i> (Usually with<i> out</i>.) To mark out (a miner's claim or an agricultural selection) by pegs at the four corners, each bearing the claimant's name in conformity with Act of Parliament. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

### PEG
 
 <i>n.</i> performance enhancement grant [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  The Performance Enhancement Grants (PEGs) are taxpayer-funded, handed out by High Performance Sport New Zealand - a branch of Sport New Zealand, the agency tasked with generating international sporting success. <i>(Aug 9, 2012,Dominion Post,1</i>)

>  The Performance Enhancement Grants (PEG) scheme being announced today is a new initiative backed by the government to provide direct financial assistance to New Zealand's elite athletes. <i>(May 31, 2004,Scoop</i>) [NOTES: Press Release: New Zealand Government 31-05-2004 16:25]

>  "The PEGs programme is put in place to help athletes with living costs to ensure that they can train at the highest level. It is not a reward/bonus scheme but is direct athlete assistance to ensure that they can devote their attention to quality training and competition." <i>(Aug 9, 2012,Dominion Post,1</i>)

### peg a claim
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Could I peg out a claim of my own and in a dish swill dirt to find if there were any specks of gold in the pan? <i>(1968,Seddons, The,44</i>)

### peg and peg with
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The stone was of a dark blue colour, and thickly studded with the precious metal. The claim from which this stone has been taken is peg and peg with the Lucky Hit Claim. <i>(Dec 19, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  The Colonial Brigade, peg and peg with the last-named, consists also of a mile of the bed and banks of Bartlet's. <i>(Sep 19, 1900,Otago Witness,20</i>)

### peg away
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> As I write, four mates ... come in for a feed, and hospitably invite the various friends who shake hands with them to `peg away'. <i>(Mar 12, 1868,Town by Night,30,1978 ed.</i>)

### peg off
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>COOPER</b> Then the word is given to the intruders by their leader, to peg off the claim, ... and the claim is thus unjustly, though ... legally seized. <i>(Nov 8, 1867,Digger's Diary,10,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> On our arrival we procured a tent, tools, and tucker, pegged off a claim, and were soon hard at work. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,97</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> Although work was available in the quartz mines, many preferred the independent life of the alluvial miner, and claims were soon pegged off at the Doughboy ... <i>(1976,Difficult Country,63</i>)

### peg out
 
 <i>v.</i> . [gold mining]

>  .. we may expect soon to see many more MILES OF AURIFEROUS RIVER BEDS "pegged out" in the same manner as that of the Kawarau river claims. <i>(Mar 21, 1925,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  The consequence was that there was a <i>rush</i> - all the ground for a considerable distance above and below them, to the extent perhaps of two or three miles, was taken; a good many of the claims were "pegged" out on Monday morning following, at three o'clock. <i>(Aug 1, 1857,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Any experienced miners who arrived to peg out claims hurriedly departed after one glance at the creek and the surrounding country. <i>(Jul 17, 1930,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  Two prospecting claims were pegged out. <i>(Dec 10, 1900</i>)

>  The prospector, Goldwater, with James Mackay, native agent, and two policemen, went to peg out a prospecting claim on their discovered reef, when the miners .. declared that unless the prospector, Goldwater, showed gold, they would not allow him to peg out. <i>(Feb 11, 1873,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Borrowing a pan (<i>Anglice </i>: tin-dish) from some neighbourly miners, he progressed up the Gorge trying `prospects' in many places, and at length he was satisfied to `peg out'. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/iv/17,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> My `gaffer' pegged out a claim for himself. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,74</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> This boundary was always debatable ground - it was always imperatively necessary that claims should be `pegged out', and this rule was of necessity `more honoured in the breach than in the observance.' Pegs were not to be had, unless imported, and when introduced stood a great chance of helping to boil the neighbour's `billy'.' <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,80,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MOFFAT</b> When we arrived at `One Spec Gully' we found it pegged out from the Falls nearly to the Forks. <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,51,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Peg out, v</i>. to mark out a gold-claim under the Mining Act, or a <i>Free-Selection</i> (q.v.) under the Land Act, by placing pegs at the corners of the land selected. Used also metaphorically. [1858] <i>(1898,Austral English,345</i>)

>  <i>peg out... v. </i>mark out a gold-claim by placing pegs at each corner. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),15</i>)

>  <i>peg-out v.</i> mark out a gold-claim by placing pegs at each corner. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),622</i>)

>  <i>peg out</i> mark out boundaries of (esp. <i>NZ & Austral</i>. a mining claim). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,555</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 834]]

 <i>v.</i>

### peg-tooth
 
 <i>n.</i> of a saw

>  <b>BAINES</b> Cross-cut saws have what is called a peg-tooth and cut both ways. It is a great improvement to sharpen every fifth tooth, counting from the centre to the ends, as a drag tooth, that is, a tooth filed square across and with a considerable hook, this arrangement tends to keep the cut clear of sawdust. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,173</i>)

### peg-top
 
 <i>v.</i>

### pegging
 
 <i>n.</i>

Defendant refused to allow the pegging to go on, threatened informant with a crowbar, and with it knocked the pegs from his hand. <i>(Jul 27, 1899,Otago Witness,24</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> ... claims had been staked from the coast upward. (This pegging was duly carried out ... [sic] afterwards.) <i>(1940,Rakiura,296</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  PEGGING OUT A CLAIM ...Mr Neave, in opening, stated that the informant, at the request of Donald Simson, who held a miner's right, and was an applicant for a mining privilege, proceeded to defendant's perpetual leasehold at Waikaka, for the purpose of pegging off a mining claim. >  PEGGING OUT A CLAIM ...Mr Neave, in opening, stated that the informant, at the request of Donald Simson, who held a miner's right, and was an applicant for a mining privilege, proceeded to defendant's perpetual leasehold at Waikaka, for the purpose of pegging off a mining claim. Defendant refused to allow the pegging to go on, threatened informant with a crowbar, and with it knocked the pegs from his hand. <i>(Jul 27, 1899,Otago Witness,24</i>)

### peggy bag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A tram conductor was charged in the Magistrate's Court to-day with having stolen a peggy bag containing GBP14 10s from a lady passenger. <i>(Jan 23, 1913,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  In pretty well every "peggy bag" are carried a case of "fags," and its legitimate accompaniment, a match box. <i>(Jan 10, 1925,NZ Truth,1</i>)

Gladys Wood, Bealey Avenue, said that between 9 and 10 p.m. on February 18 a trinket, [sic] box, watch, purse, enamelled bracelet, gold neck chain, two tiki curios and a peggy bag were stolen from her bedroom. <i>(Jul 9, 1910,NZ Truth,263/7/4</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> [Mother and daughter c. 1890s] made pretty necklaces from the dried seed-pods of the field flax, which we called bluebells, tied with gay ribbons; ... and, most treasured of all, little peggy-bags of velvet, lined with bright silk or satin and threaded with silk cord for handles. <i>(1955,Dim Horizons,146,1956 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a knitted square of about 5 inches size to be sewn to others to make rugs etc., for poor people. [ca1934 Aunt Molly's Children's Session on 2YA Wellington ca1951 21st May Corresp. School on radio 2YA. Peggy squares for rugs for displaced children.]

### pekapeka
 
 <i>n.</i> a wooden spreader used for catching certain fish

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>pekapeka</i>: wooden spreader used for catching certain fish. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> At times an octopus would cling to the <i>pekapeka</i> (a wire arrangement to spread the hooks) ... <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,45</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> In the end I had many small things, pendants and <i>pekapekas</i> and <i>tikis</i>, and all of them were genuine. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,151</i>) [NOTES: The only meaning which seems to fit the text]



 
 <i>n.</i> the native bat

>  Unfortunately, cages also prevent the native pollinators such as pekapeka, the short-tailed bat, and as yet other unknown reptile or bird propagators from reaching the flowers. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,BNo. 55/28</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> New Zealand possesses only two distinct proven genera (of mammals]; the bat (<i>Pekapeka</i>, two species) and a small indigenous rat (<i>Kiore</i>). <i>(1867,New Zealand (Hochstetter),160</i>)

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> `The jade, the pounamu ... It's a beautiful stone ... Look,' he said, holding up a long hooked ear-drop of pearly translucence, `that's tau tau and the stone is called inanga. And this, the pekapeka - they say the shape comes from the bat. This is for the ear, too - the mako, like a shark's tooth ... And these,' - he ran his fingers along a row of ... nephrite adzes - `toki. See this one' - he held the heavy red-mottled adze in his palm - `that's the red kind, totoweka, woodhen's blood. The deep green, that's kawakawa.' <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,113</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> We are standing on the banks of the Horowhenua Lake ... a pair of Papango ... are floating on the placid waters; ... a solitary Pekapeka is flitting silently overhead, chasing ... the minute insect-life upon which this bat subsists; ... and the melancholy note of the Fern-Sparrow, calling to his fellows among the rushes, has grown languid and finally died away; ... <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/98</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Vespertilio tuberculatus. G. Forster. ... [182 quotes Polack 1/304] <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/181</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/181-182 1.]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Peka peka - a bat, a gamr so called <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/378</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The only original New Zealand mammal is a little bat, called pekapeka by the Maoris. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,77,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> LONG-TAILED BAT (<i>Chalinolobus morio</i>), Pekapeka of the Maoris, shelters in caves and hollow trees. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,92</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/102</i>)

>  South Canterbury is one of the few places where the long-tailed bat or pekapeka still survives in a rural landscape. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,11</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The <i>pekapeka </i>[a's-acute], or bat, and various small batlets, are very common in the land, but none of the vampire species. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/304</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a plant

 <i>n.</i> a swell shark

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SWELL SHARK ... <i>Other names: Cephaloscyllium isabellum</i>; carpet shark; draughtsboard shark (Australia); pekapeka (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,188</i>)

### Pelorus Jack
 
 <i>n.</i> the name of a famous dolphin that escorted ships through French Pass near Pelorus Sound (1882-1912)

>  "Pelorus Jack," the well-known denizen ofthe French Pass.. has been missing forthe last couple of months. <i>(Jul 3, 1912,Otago Witness,33</i>)

>  <b>BARR</b> Passing through the home of "PelorousJack" [sic] several members of the teamremained in deck to catch a sight of thecurious white pilot. <i>(1908,With the British Rugby team,80</i>)

>  Various suggestions are ventured as to the species of the finny mystery, and it is suggested that it is a cousin to "Pelorus Jack," the mysterious white fish of the French Pass. <i>(Feb 28, 1901,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  One or two five- or six-feet members of the finny [t]ribe were caught by caterers for the camp at School Bay, and it is reported that " Pelorus Jack" has forsaken his old haunt outside the Heads, where for many months he has patiently awaited a victim, to forage in Queen Charlotte Sound; at any rate passengers from Nelson to Picton no longer see the curious creature disporting itself at the heads, and, so the story goes, a huge black fin like a cross cut saw leaves a long wake in divers places in the Sound. <i>(Jan 18, 1897,Marlborough Express,2</i>)

>  While many believed he was guiding ships safely through the treacherous waters of French Pass, Mr Webber said Pelorus Jack didn't enter the pass at all... For Maori, the dolphin's history goes even further back than the 1880s. It is believed Pelorus Jack, known as Kaikaiawaro (food of the deep) to Maori, escorted descendants of Ngati Kuia into Pelorus Sound hundreds of years later. <i>(Dec 14, 2000,Evening Post,27</i>)

>  He took [his camera] to Nelson with him.. waiting for "Pelorus Jack" to do his fancy diving turn. <i>(May 25, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  The porpoise is a much smaller animal than was the original grey dolphin, Pelorus Jack. <i>(Oct 30, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/175 : 7</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> "Pelorus Jack" used to sport around the boats. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,154</i>)

>  And Champagne was a place before it was a drink, with the original Champagne Charlie personally selling his delightful bubbles here when Pelorus Jack was playing pilot and Marlborough didn't sound like wineland at all. <i>(Jun 29, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  A Maoriland oddity is Pelorus Jack of the French Pass. Jack is an albino porpoise. <i>(Oct 28, 1899,NZ Herald,765</i>)

>  Quicker 'un Pelorus Jack after a chunk ofrump steak.. I wus. <i>(Jul 25, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/23 : 244</i>)

>  New Zealand has the comforting news to-day that Pelorus Jack can say "not me" to the body of the marine mammal washed ashore at D'Urville Island. <i>(Mar 22, 1911,Evening Post,8</i>)

### pelt punching
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was [in 1956] the solo butchers who were given the tougher jobs like legging and pelt punching. <i>(Mar 12, 1982,Dominion,9/3</i>)

### pelter
 
 <i>n.</i> (occupation)

>  <b>PERRIAM</b> Ron Summers, a pelter, fell down the skin hole one day. <i>(1989,Where it all Began,164</i>)

### Peluroside
 
 <i>n.</i> a bioactive compound

>  A marine sponge with anti-tumour properties may well become a lucrative aquaculture crop. The bioactive compound <i>Peluroside</i> works in the same way as an anti-cancer drug which is currently worth NZ$9 billion a year. <i>(Jun 2005,National Centre for Aquatic Biodiversity,No 14/1</i>) [NOTES: National Centre for Aquatic Biodiversity and Biosecurity Update]

### pen
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> He taught me more about handling cargo ... than anyone else I met. Every morning I used to look for him among the crowd at the pen. <i>Ibid./</i> 228 The morning found me down at the pen as usual. The ... pen was full of seagulls looking for work. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,216</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> These vessels are fitted with tryworks or fire-places above which is a battery containing two, and sometimes three iron pots, sufficiently capacious to contain from one hundred and fifty to two hundred gallons each; the fire-place is built of bricks, so laid below as to form channels for water to preserve the floor or main deck from injury. The water is kept confined by a square, formed with a two-inch plank, around the sides, negligence in leaving this pen (as it is termed) dry, has caused many accidents; ... These fire-works are taken down when the cargo is completed, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/417</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] to drive sheep in to a hurdled pen.

>  Before penning, two sheep have to be separated ... <i>(Oct 27, 1938,NZ Farmer,9</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> pen: one dog-trial event end when the sheep have been eventually confined in a little yard of four hurdles. The dog is then said to have penned. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,60</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> various uses and applications

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Choose a sheep with its head held in a natural position in the pen. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,19</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> From the press the bales were stacked ir a loft above the sheep pens. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,117</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The brow on the larboard side of the Ship, which a few days ago was impenetrable forest, is now clear & airy, & contains an Observatory, a forge, a green hut for the woodcutters & a pen for our Sheep; and more than an acre of ground is cleared of the woods; ... <i>(Apr 24, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/266,1982 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to place stock in pens for drafting, shearing and other work.

>  In addition to three shearers, there are two baling, two at the wool table, a fleecepicker, one sweeping and a man penning. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,502</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The Maori buck did the shearing, with machines, boys penned, and did pick-up work. Wahines ... did the sorting, and Maori girls did the sweeping on the shearing board. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,110</i>)

>  They are brought to the sheep yards, where any dirty animals are `dagged' (any stained wool and pieces of dung hanging around the tail are clipped off). They are then `penned' overnight in the woolshed to protect them from rain or heavy dew. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,66</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a position of shearer in an open shed.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> In those days when men were glad of jobs, shearers used to write asking for a 'pen', just as the musterers did to ask for a 'beat' on the station of their choice. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,84</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to place stock in pens for sale.

>  At the Newmarket yards, on Tuesday, dairy and store stock were penned in usual numbers. <i>(Aug 18, 1900,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  The yarding of 8553 stores and primes that were penned at Temuka on April 9 saw the condition of the stores clearly illustrating the effects of the drought ... <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,42</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Best wethers 24s to 27s 6d (one pen: up to 29s). <i>(Aug 11, 1905,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Clayton was an `open shed', that is, the shearers were not engaged beforehand, but turned up and took their chance of a pen on the advertised starting day. <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,166</i>)

>  Phillips over with Wedders, joined the lot in the pens & counted mob (see Sheep book). <i>(Mar 7, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [?woolshed ?yards]]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Each man has his pen, which is cleared out and refilled as often as necessary... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,27</i>)

>  <i>Pen</i>.- (1) A small yard; a division in the sheep-holding part of a woolshed. (2) Shearers catch out of a p., and when they apply for work they ask for a p. `Will you keep me a <i>p</i>. for next year, boss?' is often their farewell. Two shearers usually catch out of one pen and are called <i>p.-mates</i>. <i>(Nov 1933,Press, The,15</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> esp. as get a pen, be given a pen

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I'd been out among the stations,/ Vainly seeking for a pen;/ ... <i>(1947,Pioneer Recollections,541,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> ... before the following shearing season a letter ... with the customary [pound]1 deposit was in the Station mail bag, ... asking for a pen next shearing, ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,51</i>)

>  <b>DICK</b> `... Lend me some suitable clothes and I'll take a pen alongside you. Must be nigh on forty years since I held a pair of shears.' <i>(1964,High Country Family,70</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> There isn't a pen for the type of men/ That try to hang the boss! <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,41</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> When Charlie told me that the award for shearing in Australia was two pounds one shilling per 100, I told him I would like to get a pen over there. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,108</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Pen</i>. A small fenced-in area. The sheep-holding part of a wool shed is divided into pens connected to the board by a swing door. Shearers catch their sheep out of these pens, which are usually shared, the shearers being pen-mates. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)

### Pen and Ink
 
 <i>n.</i> the Gallipoli Peninsula [slang]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Pen and ink</i>. 4. <i>Pen and Ink,</i> Gallipoli <i>Penin</i>sula: New Zealand soldiers': in 1915; occ. after. Cf. <i>Pinch an Inch. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,616</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [were] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ...<i>Pen and Ink</i> and <i>Inch and Pinch</i>, the Peninsula of Gallipoli; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

### pen and ink
 
 <i>phr.</i> loudly?

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Presently I heard a [pig-]dog sing out `pen and ink'. ... Next morning he came crawling up to the outstation dragging one of his hind legs, which appeared to be useless. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,65,1935 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> drink [rhyming slang]

>  We wander over to the bar for a pen and ink, where the talk gets round to tomato sauces. pen and ink....drink <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### pen lot
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stock sales] a group of animals in a pen for sale as one unit.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> What with mustering, drafting and mouthing on the farm; then the 11-mile drive to the saleyards, then more drafting and sorting into age-groups and pen lots on the day of the sale ... <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,84</i>)

### pen the best
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [ stock sales or shows] to have the pen of highest value or quality stock of its type.

>  He also penned the best of seven shorn ewe hoggets. <i>(Dec 20, 1951,NZ Farmer,30</i>)

### pen up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to fill pens and yards with stock, especially sheep.

>  Should she not take to the lamb she must be penned up until she alters her mind. <i>(Oct 1885,NZ Farmer,292</i>)

>  There should be someone penning up for the crutchers and also picking over the wool. <i>(Jul 1911,NZ Farmer,705</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> This [labelling pens with capacity] avoided any risks of smothering and was also of great help when penning up in semi-darkness. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,123</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> For some hours before the sale we'd be at the yards helping the local agents draft and pen up incoming stock. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,78</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> In the shed I picked up, skirted and rolled fleeces while the boss penned up and mustered sheep ... <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,112</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> pen-up, to: to fill up a - usually catching - pen. Besides penning them, a trial dog can be said to pen up his sheep. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,60</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> It was always my job to turn the grindstone for them (shearers); top shed up, pick up, pen up, press up and be there pronto with the tar pot ... <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,93</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>LILICO</b> A bitch pup I sold ... went direct to freezing works to pen up and developed into a first class forcer and backer ... The finest backing dogs I have ever seen are in the saleyards at Addington ... <i>(1934,Sheep Dog Memoirs,26</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The bell rang, the whole procedure stopped, shearers trooped out, half shorn sheep were pushed back into the catching pens. Jimmy Greave the classer hurriedly whispered to William Steuart to desist, but loppies, rousies, and even young Jim, who was assisting to pen up, followed out from the side door to where the shoots from the stands gave the nearest exit, to the level space of the outside turf, that made eventually the [boxing] ring. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,50</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> There was nothing new to me in picking up fleeces. ... I had often given a hand in the smaller woolsheds at shearing time, either penning up with the shepherds or picking up and helping the presser. <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,167</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> He had to be warned ... not to abuse the sheep while penning up.... <i>(1965,Skippers Road,94</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> Jack Frost ... was `picking up'. and I was the rouseabout - penning up the sheep, sweeping the shearing board and bailing [sic] the wool. <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,72</i>)

### pen-man
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Some men are good pen-men. Those who havewatches are working hard to get the sheepthey have on the board off, and catchanother, before the manager calls out:"Clear the board." <i>(Sep 28, 1878,Otago Witness,3</i>)

### pen-stain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a faeces stain on wool [made when sheep are kept in close quarters prior to shearing.]

>  Much of the offering was of doubtful yield, of mixed combing length, and affected by light discoloration, dust and/or 'penstain.' <i>(Jan 8, 1964,Straight Furrow,5</i>)

>  <b>BARTON</b> Ewe, wether and hogget bodywool - free from all discolouration (remove all yellow and penstain.) <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,108</i>)

>  There could be confusion in the guidelines .. the good style [of wool] 'remove definite yellow and penstain.' <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,109</i>)

>  .. large confined mobs, particularly if moved, can build up pen stain in the wool. <i>(1988,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Feb/March 16</i>)

>  Shearing: Don't pack them [sheep] in too tightly or they will dung on each other's wool causing 'pen stain.' [penstain; pen stain] <i>(Oct 2001,Lifestyleblock.co.nz,Orr</i>)

### pen-stained
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fleecewool] stained with faeces prior to shearing.

>  <b>BARTON</b> Locks, second pieces and pizzle or pen-stained wool must be kept separate. <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,110</i>)

>  <b>M H-B P</b> Pen-stained wools may be discounted in price. [penstained.] <i>(1982,Fine Wool,55</i>)

### pen-staining
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the fouling of wool by faeces when sheep are located in pens prior to shearing.

>  <b>M H-B P</b> These requirements are: prevention of pen-staining of sheep ... <i>(1982,Fine Wool,55</i>)

### pen-upper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STRONACH</b> At the back of the shed worked the `penners-up' whose job was to keep the small catching pens full for the shearers. We always alluded to them as the `pen-uppers'. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31</i>)

### penal rate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Aussie employers face added burdens. What we call penal rates - time and a half or double time for working Saturdays, Sundays or unsociable hours - are still the norm in their hospitality and service sectors. <i>(Sep 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  Until recently, in New Zealand people were seen as entitled to extra pay for working their weekends, for working while others slept, for sacrificing their home life for their job. We called these payments penal rates, shift allowances, overtime... <i>(May 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

### penalty rate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>penalty rates </i>pl. <i>n. Austral</i>. rates of pay for employees working outside normal hours. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,836</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

### pencil point
 
 <i>v.</i> to fell trees by cutting right round trunk instead of scarfing

>  <b>KENWAY</b> I remember an old hand's scornful remark on a bit of bush I had myself felled: `They've got some bally new chum at work here; pencil pointing the trees like that. <i>(1928,Pioneering in Poverty Bay,40</i>)

### pencil staple
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a slim, thinly structured staple of fleece-wool.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> It is not uncommon to find that fleeces which have a high expression of staple character also have lean or pencil type staples, particularly on the fore-arm and the britch. Wool buyers and their manufacturers have no objection to these pencil staples, but with breeders they find little favour, and a sheep is expected to produce meaty staples throughout the fleece. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,113</i>)

>  <b>SQUIRES</b> Pencilly staple - a wool staple of thin, narrow structure being pencil-like in appearance. [pencilly staple.] <i>(1991,A to Z Glossary of Wool Terms,29</i>)

>  Wool with a thin staple. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,57</i>)

### pencil vein
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... where a few `pencil veins' of dark ore, assaying from [pound]1.10s to [pounds]3 per ton in bullion, were met with. <i>(1913,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.16 (N.S.) 97</i>) [NOTES: Henderson and Bartrum (Aroha, Hauraki)]

### penciller
 
 <i>n.</i> a bookmaker

>  Paddy Hilton, the local penciller ... <i>(Aug 26, 1905,NZ Truth,10/2/24</i>)

### pendulum saw
 
 <i>n.</i> goose-saw

>  <i>(Jan 1958,NZ Timber Journal,46/1</i>)

### pene
 
 <i>n.</i> a pen or pencil [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  To enter simply grab a pene and write to us but don't forget to include your name, address and birth date. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,issue 18 summer 2005 / 2006, p16</i>)

### penguin
 
 <i>n.</i> a flightless seabird with webbed feet and flipper-like wings, living in the Southern Hemisphere. (See [hoiho](hea-hom.md#hoiho),[korora](kiw-kwi.md#korora))

>  Of the fossil remains of birds, the present collection contains above 500 specimens, referable to the various species of Dinornis, and allied genera; and to species of albatross, penguin, water-hen, nestor, apteryx, etc. <i>(Oct 12, 1850,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,2</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Many birds about the Ship of every kind: & especially we saw two <i>Penguins</i> & two bunches of Seaweeds called at the Cape <i>Sea-Bamboos.</i> (Fucus buccinalis linn.). [ed. fn] Probably Chinstrap Penguins <i>Pygoscelis anarctica</i> (Forster, 1781). <i>(Dec 9, 1772,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/191,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We saw several Shoals of <i>Pinguins</i> [[ed. fn] probably Chinstrap ...] passing the Ship - some were skipping about like Skip-Jacks; ... <i>(Dec 14, 1772,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/197,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We described ... fish & birds & a Seal, which was but small [[ed. fn] <i>Phoca ursina</i> of <i>Descr. Anim</i>., pp. 64-5] ... & a small Pinguin, of about the size of a small Teal; and different of all those I have seen before. [ed. fn] Now Southern Blue Penguin -us;Eudyptula minor minor (<i>Forster, 1781</i>). <i>(Apr 1, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/244,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We now [in s. Indian Ocean] likewise saw penguins for the first time ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/92</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>common English bird-name. ...</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,345</i>)

>  <b>PARKER</b> `The Penguins are characteristic Southern Hemisphere sea-birds, being represented in the Northern by the Puffins. They are flightless, but their wings are modified into powerful fins or flappers. Among the most interesting forms are the following - the King Penguin, <i>Aptenodytes longirostris</i>; Rock Hopper P., <i>Pygoscelis taeniatus</i>; Yellow-Crowned P., <i>Eudyptes antipodum</i>; Crested P., E. pachyrhynchus; <i>Little Blue P., E.</i> minor <i>and</i> undina.' <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,119</i>)

>  "In the forest, one of these men can follow not only a man's trail - that I have often seen them do - but a bird's trail, and will capture a wood-hen, or, amongst the rocks, a penguin, from indications that a Northern native or a white man would not have recognised. .." <i>(Nov 3, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  As this was <i>all</i> - all except the extraordinary penguin, whose habits seemed to be inpenetrable. <i>(Jun 22, 1844,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,64</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Eudyptes ... Sclateri</i> ... Big Crested Penguin. TAWAKI. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,92</i>)

>  <i>Erect-crested Penguin Eudyptes sclateri </i>BULLER, 1888 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Tawaki, big-crested penguin. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,52</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (See [also yellow-crowned penguin](ydu-yen.md#yellow-crowned-penguin))

>  4. <i>Eudyptes pachyrhynchus.</i> Yellow-crested penguin. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/193</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> GENTOO PENGUIN <i>Pygoscelis papua </i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,22</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> hoiho

>  The following is a list of the birds shot ... <i>Eudyptula undini,</i> the little penguin; the <i>Eudyptes antipodes,</i> the great penguin. <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>) [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 157. EUDYPTES ANTIPODES. Homb. et Jacq. Great Penguin. Hoiho. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,53</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?NZ (little) blue penguin

>  <i>Blue Penguin Eudyptula minor</i> (FORSTER, 1781) ENDEMIC <i>Northern Blue Penguin E.m. iredalei</i> MATHEWS, 1911. <i>Cook Strait Blue Penguin E.m. variabilis</i> KINSKY & FALLA, 1976. <i>White-flippered Penguin E.m. albosignata</i> FINSCH, 1874. <i>Southern Blue Penguin E.m. minor</i> (FORSTER, 1781). <i>Chatham Island Blue Penguin E.m. chathamensis</i> KINSKY & FALLA, 1976. OTHER NAMES: <i>Little blue penguin, little penguin, fairy penguin, korara.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,46</i>)

>  Canterbury has its own distinctive penguin, the white-flippered penguin, which is a local variant of the Little [sic] blue or fairy penguin found only in New Zealand and Australia. It is slightly heavier than the northern Little blue penguin, and has a distinctive white band along both edges of the flippers. They breed only on Banks Peninsula, Motunau Island and the neighbouring coasts. <i>(Aug 24, 1988,Press, The,21</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the black and white penguin, <i>Eudyptes robusta</i>

>  There are two small rocky, peaty islands, some islets and rocks in the group, which is a protected reserve where landings are not allowed, although visiting tourists can cruise close to the coastline in inflatable rubber boats, seeing Snares penguins by the hundred. <i>(Apr 2000,NZWords,8</i>)

>  The Snares Penguin is thus nameless, and to remedy this I propose the following: <i>Eudyptes robustus</i> sp. nov. ... Head jet black, paler on the crown, remainder of upper surface bluish black, each feather having a narrow central streak of pale blue. A rather narrow pale golden yellow band begins near each nostril, passes over the eye and ends in a backwardly and downwardly projecting crest. <i>(1955,Emu, The,53(2) : 187</i>)

>  <i>Snares Crested Penguin Eudyptes robustus</i> OLIVER, 1953 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Snares penguin, Snares Island penguin</i>. ... Snares crested penguins breed only on The Snares. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,51</i>)
