### penguin oil
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They are the penguin-oil hunters onMacquarie Island, stationed there boilingdown the penguins for a south New Zealandfirm. <i>(Jun 11, 1910,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  The Commissioner of Customs has decided that penguin oil (crude) may be admitted duty free. <i>(Jun 3, 1890,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  The boiler, digester, building material, fuel and stores which formed her outward cargo were safely landed, and before the schooner left on Christmas Day a building to contain the boiling down plant for the production of penguin oil had been erected, and Mr Thomas Smith, engineer, who went down with the vessel, was about to proceed with the fitting up of the apparatus. <i>(Jan 3, 1890,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  Many of our readers probably know that, for a number of years, an intermittent trade has been done between New Zealand and Macquarie Island in penguin oil. <i>(Jul 5, 1905,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Wool, tallow, penguin oil, and sealskins are the chief articles of export. <i>(May 8, 1879,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  By writing the life of his great-grandfather, Redmer Yska has also illuminated an obscure corner of New Zealand history: the penguin oil industry that flourished, mercifully briefly, in the subantarctic islands towards the end of the 19th century. <i>(Jun 30, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),59</i>)

>  The owner of the schooner Gratitude, from Invercargill, has been fined GBP25, and the captain GBP5, for landing a cargo of penguin oil, wrongfully described as whale oil. <i>(Mar 24, 1897,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

### penguin pub
 
 <i>n.</i> a nesting box for penguins

>  The boat dropped us at Motuara Island bird sanctuary near Ship Cove, where robins will eat from your hand and blue penguins nest in boxes, known as penguin pubs, supplied by DOC. <i>(Jun 1, 2004,Dominion Post,A17</i>)

### penguin ranch
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CARRICK</b> From all that can be ascertained the penguin-ranches [on the Snares] are not so populous as they were. ... Their ranches are readily recognised even at a distance. The ground is closely cropped of its verdure, and to a large extent the soil has been broken up and furrowed. <i>(1892,New Zealand's Lone Lands,7</i>)

### Peninsula
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> [Gallipoli Peninsula]

>  A dispatch from Sir Ian Hamilton gives some particulars of the recent fighting on the Peninsula. <i>(Aug 20, 1915,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The peninsula looks like an inferno of shreiking shells. <i>(May 26, 1915,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  At about 10 p.m. we drew in to what is known as Anzac Cove, ... the whole Peninsula from Anzac [132] to Suvla Bay was fairly lit up ... <i>Ibid.</i> 266 I am feeling as fit as a fiddle after my seven months sojourn on the Peninsula ... <i>(Jan 1, 1916,Great Adventure,131,1988 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WESTON</b> ... I had been granted the temporary rank of Major on the Peninsula ... <i>(1918,Three Years with the NZers,62</i>)

>  The Peninsula vocabulary was notorious. ... [16] Reminiscences of panders' slang were used with the quaintest inappositeness. But pre-eminently the word `bastard' passed into common use. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,15</i>)

>  <b>TREADWELL</b> The 2nd Battalion was formed of troops who had arrived at Egypt too late for the Peninsula ... <i>(1936,Recollections of an Amateur Soldier,149</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Aw dikkon, Mr A!' said Wade. `What did you say?' `Haven't you heard that one, sir? I suppose it's New Zealand digger slang. `Dikkon'. It's the same as if you'd say `come off it'. Used to hear it on the Peninsula. `Aw dikkon, dig'' <i>(1937,Vintage Murder,238</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> `Our platoon commander was at school with him - he was on the Peninsula too. ... Almost all our officers are New Zealanders - ... Lieutenant Given and our Captain are both Anzacs.' <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,145</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Of the Ruahine section of the regiment there were only 64 men lefy of the 253 landed on the peninsula. <i>(1988,Voices of Gallipoli,38</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Of the Ruahine section of the regiment there were only 64 men lefy of the 253 landed on the peninsula. <i>(1988,Voices of Gallipoli,38</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [Otago, Banks, or Coromandel]

>  A smart thunderstorm passed over the Peninsula this afternoon. <i>(Feb 19, 1877,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Cocksfoot is a dying industry on the Peninsula [sc. Banks]. <i>(Mar 21, 1946,NZ Farmers' Weekly,28</i>)

>  To the outsider, the Otago Peninsula is the entire bump. Otago people, of course, know their peninsula confines itself to the south of the harbour. <i>(Apr 15, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  In 1849 and later in 1856, Crown agents had deeds signed for the remainder of the Peninsula [sc. Banks] as a result of threats to dispossess them by force. <i>(May 27, 1992,NZ Tablet,Suppl. [iv]</i>)

>  Like a fingerprint on the land, close-spaced sheep tracks pattern the hillsides of the Otago Peninsula. Although the peninsula lies on the threshold of a major city and has a long history of human modification, it remains wild and remote in character, a place where weather and sea hold sway. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/23</i>)

>  ... well-equipped factories were organised for this work at central points on the Peninsula. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,212,1988 ed.</i>) [NOTES: =Banks P.]

>  <b>FAIRBURN</b> As a mandarin of letters you can't very well get yourself voted a jaunt on the Peninsula, can you? <i>(1956,Letters,245</i>) [NOTES: 1981 edition]

>  <b>ADCOCK</b> [Title] A Surprise in the Peninsula. <i>(1971,High Tide in the Garden,6</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> Mick, you took the Peninsula / As a strong man might.<i>Ibid.</i> 18 The Peninsula still looks insular, / Banks Island now Peninsula created. <i>(1979,Towards Banks Peninsula,16</i>)

>  Ask the same prices demanded by Southern agents, or lose forever the grip they [sc. Northern friends] had on the Peninsula trade. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  In the early days, no less than three full-sized duels were fought out on the Peninsula [sc. Banks], all with pistols. <i>(Oct 2, 1890,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  Californian thistle increases. I was surprised to find this weed is spreading on the Otago Peninsula, and also on some of the best lands in the Taieri Plains. <i>(1899,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,VII/5</i>)

>  And / a child of eight / during those long Maori Hill summer afternoons / as the light faded and shadows / made the Peninsula terrifyingly clear, / you tried your vision / and with both hands invented / a way to see it. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  <b>MAHY</b> Then, at last, the car moved forwardshooting down the familiar hill, leavingthe peninsula behind. <i>(1992,Underrunners,128</i>)

>  Dunedin, This Day. Mr. Stout will contest the Peninsula. <i>(Dec 1, 1882,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  The Peninsula Seat. Dunedin, December 6. In the event of Mr Stout not contesting the Peninsula, Mr Bastings will come out in the "Liberal" interest. <i>(Dec 7, 1882,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  A Bird's-eye View of Otago Harbour from the High Road on the Peninsula, looking towards the Heads. <i>(Apr 26, 1902,Otago Witness,36</i>)

### penmate
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka pen mate; includes social extension of shearing term]

>  The explanation is not far to seek: he, with a number of pen mates, was having a "free and easy" at a wayside pub., and he woke up one fine morning to find that his cheque was "cut out." <i>(Sep 7, 1904,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> But I somehow fancy we'll all be pen-mates on the day when they call the Roll of the Sky. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,40,1945 ed.</i>)

>  (2) Shearers catch out of a p., and when they apply for work they ask for a <i>p.</i> `Will you keep me a <i>p</i>. for next year, boss?' is often their farewell. Two shearers usually catch out of one pen and are called <i>n]-mates</i>(C.) <i>(Nov 11, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MEEK</b> PENMATE: When two shearers share a catching-pen, they are said to be `Penmates'. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Two of the shearers in particular ... who were pen mates, carried on a friendly conversation the whole day ... <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,56</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> On the third day we were joined by the sixth shearer, who was to be my pen-mate. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,94</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearer who shares the catching-pen of another shearer. [aka pen mate, pen-mate]

>  It was followed by the usual grinding and titivating up of shears, and at it again, 'like ducks, heads down, tails up' as my penmate expressed it. <i>(Mar 1, 1946,Ex-Pupils Budget,11</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> Penmate: when two shearers share a catching-pen, they are said to be "penmates." <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,111 Glossary</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> penmate: two shearers pulling from the same pen are penmates. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,60</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> If his penmate is nearly off he will speed the penner-up by calling 'Sheep-oh for me mate!' [pen-mate] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,71</i>)

### pennantia
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pennantia corymbosa </i>

>  Besides the broadleaf (<i>Griselinia littoralis</i>), fuchsia (<i>F. excorticata</i>) hinahina (<i>Melicytus ramiflorus</i>), and other widely distributed trees, an occasional pokaka (<i>Elaeocarpus Hookerianus</i>) and pennantia (<i>Pl corymbosa</i>) were to be found. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/280</i>) [NOTES: (Bathgate, Otago)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### penner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Pen&#769;.ner </i>(p&ebreve.n&#769;.n&etilde.r),<i>n</i>. [From <i>pen</i> to confine.] A workman employed at shearing time to bring in sheep from the flock outside the woolshed and confine them in pens within the shed, so that they may be ready for the shearers. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2028</i>)

### penner-up
 
 <i>n.</i> also called sheep-o in sheep-shearing, one who keeps the shearers' pens supplied with sheep (See [compare fleece-o](../C#compare-fleece-o))

>  I spoke to the rollers and penner up and told them to come and get their cheques. <i>(Feb 20, 1895,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> And he penner-up is cursing at the back, / <i>(1897,Station Ballads,101</i>) [NOTES: (`Shearing') also 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 33)]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The `sheep-oh' (penner-up) also held an important position. In addition to filling up the catching pens, he weighed the bales and recorded them, with their descriptions in the wool book. <i>Ibid.</i> 130 The shearers were, of course, the autocrats of the shed, but the `sheep-oh' (penner-up) also held an important position. In addition to filling up the catching pens, he weighed the bales and recorded them, with their descriptions, in the wool book. <i>Ibid.</i> 208 At shearing time [at Benmore, 1892] about 55 men were employed in the shed: 28 to 30 shearers, 2 penners-up, 4 pickers-up, 2 broomies, 6 rollers, 1 classer, 1 fleece carrier, 4 piece sorters, 3 pressers, 1 bale brander, 1 butcher and brander. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,112</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In the larger sheds a man is employed just to keep the catching pens filled. The catching pens are the small pens, adjoining the board, out of which the shearers catch their sheep. <i>(1949,High Country Days,196</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> One classer, two rollers, two fleece-pickers, two pressers, one penner up, make up the shed contingent and the cook and `offsider' complete the total. <i>(1951,High Country,27</i>)

>  The dogs, the roussies and penners-up. <i>(1952,Arena,31/4</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> At the back of the shed worked the `penners-up' whose job was to keep the small catching pens full for the shearers. We always alluded to them as the `pen-uppers'. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31</i>)

>  What with the dogs the roussies [sic] and penners-up and daggers brought to give them a hand in the shed and yards ... <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,127,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton `Saving the Breed')]

>  <b>HARPER</b> Sandy became the sole penner-up for sheep for over thirty shearers in the Orari Gorge woolshed. ... A working-dog was not allowed near the shed. With a `rattle' (pieces of tin attacked to a circle of wire) Sandy kept the sheep up to all the shearers. <i>(1967,Kettle on the Fuchsia, The,87</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person responsible for filling pens with sheep during drafting, docking, drenching, foot-rotting or shearing; also used at freezing works.

>  .. in a future issue I will endeavor to deal with the penner-up, a gentleman better known as the "Professor." <i>(Jan 25, 1905,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  The Shepherds' dogs are barking in the yard, / And the penner-up is cursing at the back ... <i>(Nov 19, 1896,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The 'sheep-oh' (penner-up) .. in addition to filling up the catching pens .. weighed the bales and recorded them..in the wool book. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,130</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Eventually it was decided that the musterers could have the 'penner up'and also draw reinforcements from the doggy men across the river at "Craiglea.' <i>(1949,High Country Days,59</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> At the back of the shed worked the 'penners-up' whose job was to keep the small catching-pens full for the shearers. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Twelve shearers on the board of the main shearing shed, with another 12 people around them - rousies, penners and pressers. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,118</i>)

>  <b>GIBSON</b> I was to be a penner-up. There was a penner-up attached to the slow chain. It was impressed on me frequently that it depended on the penner-up whether they got to that figure of a hundred. [penner] <i>(1990,Among the High Hills of Canterbury,28</i>)

>  That the shepherds, musterers, rouseabouts, butcher and brander, penners-up, packets, cooks, cooks' mates, rabbit trappers, rabbit poisoners, shearers, pressers, and wool scourers would all have separate unions. <i>(May 2, 1906,Otago Witness,24</i>)

### penning
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the placing of stock, especially sheep, in pens.

>  Before penning, two sheep have to be separated ... <i>(Oct 27, 1938,NZ Farmer,9</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> If a dog trial pen is available the pup should next have some practice at penning. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,10</i>)

### penning up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the filling of pens with sheep for drafting, docking/tailing, shearing or sale.

>  Shown a plan of the shearing shed he detailed the method of yarding and penning the sheep. .. He didn't go to dinner, being too busy penning up and marking the sheep, and had to handle the sheep right and left. <i>(Feb 23, 1894,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  <b>Harper</b> Latterly he ... did all the penning up in the shed and helped in any way he could. <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,43</i>)

>  In penning up, do not pack too tightly or smothering will occur. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,25</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The shearing board should be of a good width, and be separated from the holding pens by a solid wall, so as to facilitate penning up. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

>  Her owner's job was untrucking and penning up sheep and lambs at a big freezing works. <i>(Feb 1930,NZ Farmer,1</i>)

>  One daggy sheep can stain quite a few clean sheep when penning up is in progress. <i>(Oct 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,310</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> After the main shearing and penning up for a board of twenty-eight shearers.. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,24</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> In a shearing gang he [the dagger] usually combines this duty with penning-up. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,24</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> I had learned quite a lot about shed work when we were at Kekerengu and was enjoying penning up, fleece picking, skirting and rolling when Whit asked if I would like to have a go. [penning-up] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,51</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> [of a sheep dog, working sheep into pens]

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a quiet dog used to shed and pen sheep.

### penny bun
 
 <i>n.</i> a fungus

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The PENNY BUN (<i>Boletus edulis</i>), so very abundant in pine-tree plantations, is the best known member of the fleshy mushroom-shaped polypores. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,36,1929 ed.</i>)

### penny cracker
 
 <i>n.</i> a tame affair or fizzer? [figurative use]

>  <b>HYDE</b> The talk veered to politics; and he said wearily that there ought to be a bloody revolution; ... `That 'ud be no good. Penny cracker. And if, at the next elections, this lot go out, you needn't hope for an age of miracles . . .[sic] <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,160</i>)

### penny doctor
 
 <i>n.</i> the larva of the tiger beetle [The most widely known game with insects is a game named variously by T.C. students as Butcher Boys (Gisborne, New Plymouth, Kaponga, [405] Te Pohu, Newall, Sth. Wellington), Butcher Bats (Ohau, Takaka), Penny Doctor Beetles (Tauranga, Pipiriki, Rotorua), or Fishing (Morunui). It is a game in which straws which have been suitably moistened with spittle are placed in an insects [sic] hole and are flicked out when the insect bites the end of the straw. Most of the reports of this practice come from the North Island, though it was known in the South as early as 1900 (Waikouaiti).]

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> The larva [of the tiger beetle] is also known as the penny-doctor among boys, [who catch them by putting grass straws in their burrows]. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,451</i>)

>  <i>Penny Doctor</i> beetles, also known as `Butcher bats' is a game widely reported by Wellington Training College students as being played in many parts of the North Island. In this game straws moistened by spittle are pushed down the hole of the appropriate beetle. The beetle seizes the end of the straw. When the player feels the straw moving he or she flicks out the beetle. The player who can catch the most beetles wins the game. <i>(1951,Journal of the Polynesian Society,60/93-107</i>) [NOTES: 1951 JPS 60/93-107 (Sutton-Smith) in 1972 Sutton-Smith Folkgames of Children 328]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> In summer [tiger beetles] are seen on sunny clay banks; holes in the ground contain the voracious larvae (`penny doctors'). <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,148</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The little `penny-doctor' that is winkled out of its hole with a grass stalk may therefore be a descendant of the first settlers. fn] The `penny-doctor' is the larva of the tiger beetle <i>Cicindela tuberculata. </i> <i>(1977,Treasury of Maori Exploration,15</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `See those holes in the ground?' ... `When we was little we use [sic] to stick dry grass-stalks down them to catch penny-doctors.' <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,9</i>)

>  They would go fishing for "pennydoctors". <i>(Aug 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,80</i>)

### penny section
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If they want to get at these pennysection people, why on earth don't theystop using penny tickets after 10 atnight. <i>(Sep 10, 1917,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

>  [Advt] It's only a hop, skip and a Jump! From the end of the Penny Section. To H.W. Bateman's. <i>(Feb 6, 1904,NZ Observer,23</i>)

>  [Caption] The end of the penny section. The toilers homeward plod their weary way. <i>(Aug 24, 1912,NZ Free Lance,9</i>)

>  The traffic has been very large since the adoption of the penny section system, but the expenses have also mounted up. <i>(Jan 14, 1890,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The penny section is to be abolished altogether. <i>(May 18, 1920,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  Andrew Cuningham.. wonders about "the end of the penny section". This literally means the far reaches of the main urban transport route through a city or town which used to cost one penny in the gold [sic] old days. Once you've reached the end of the penny section, you are heading for the suburbs. However, the expression is also used colloquially to mean the end of the easy stuff or the end of the golden weather. Once you're out of th penny section, you have to pay more, or, in this sense, work a bit harder. <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  I have ridden in their 'busses [sic] on several occasions and have each time noticed the dissatisfaction expressed to the guard by the patrons of their line at the charge of 3d demanded from passengers getting in at any part of Molesworth-street, quite irrespective of the distance they may wish to go, even though that distance may only be to the Government Buildings, and this in contradiction to their announcement of the 16th instant, stating that on and after the 17th instant 'busses would run "penny sections, the same as Tram Company." <i>(Feb 22, 1888,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  The disturbance continued until the `penny section' was completed, and there the [tram] conductor called a policeman and handed Mullins over to him. <i>(May 14, 1910,NZ Truth,255/7/4</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> END OF THE PENNY SECTION? Lions 34, Auckland 15. <i>(1977,Winter of Discontent,151</i>)

### pension
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> Fred has the `pensions' used for fantan, round metal discs with a square hole in the middle, and a `dew nappy', the ivory half-ball that the game was played with. Pensions were put on the four corners of the card, or points between, and two people took the bank. Even money was paid on the corners and three to one in the middle. There would be several hundred pounds sometimes waiting on the half-roll of the dew nappy. <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,93</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a forger

### penwiper
 
 <i>n.</i> the scree plant <i>Notothlaspi rosulatum</i>

>  <b>KIRK</b> Notothlaspi rosulatum pen-wiper plant <i>(1899,Students' Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  Amongst the most remarkable are the fleshy-leaved buttercup [fn <i>Ranunculus hasstii</i>.], the black daisy [fn <i>Cotula atrata</i>.], the pen-wiper plant [fn <i>Notothlaspi rosulatum</i>.], ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,190</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Notothlaspi rosulatum pen-wiper plant <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> Notothlaspi rosulatum penwiper plant <i>(1906,NZ Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Notothlaspi rosulatum (The Rosette-like Notothlaspi)</i>. An erect stemless herb. ... Shingle beds in the alpine districts of the South Island. The <i>Pen-wiper Plant</i> of the Settlers [shepherds]. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,178,1940, p. 186 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... the curious and sweet-scented penwiper plant (<i>Notothlaspi rosulatum</i>) ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,97</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Notothlaspi rosulatum pen-wiper plant <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> ... Shingle beds in the alpine districts of the South Island. The <i>Penwiper plant</i> of the shepherds. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,186</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> On the higher levels, up to 5,000 feet and over, a scattered alpine vegetation, including pen-wiper and mountain buttercups. <i>(1969,Molesworth,14</i>)

>  <b>MARK & ADAMS</b> <i>Notothlaspi rosulatum</i> Hook. f. PENWIPER PLANT <i>(1973,NZ Alpine Plants,46</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>NOTOTHLASPI ROSULATUM.</i> The penwiper plant, like <i>Ranunculus haastii,</i> grows on South Island scree slopes ... The leaves form a neat circular rosette, ... in appearance much like the now forgotten penwipers made of overlapping strips of soft cloth on which Edwardian writers wiped the ink off their steel-nibbed pens. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,40</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> ... everywhere I walked I found a dozen penwiper plants. This lovely little rosette grows in deep, cool shingle ...where its long roots ...feed the huge flower head which rises and scents the air as strongly as carnation or gardenia. <i>(1980,Tussock Fever,251</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> The best known scree species is the penwiper plant (<i>Notothlaspi rosulatum</i>) ... which belongs to the cabbage family (Cruciferae). Its rosettes of grey, fleshy leaves closely overlap in a dome-like arrangement, which reminded early settlers of the similarly arranged and sewn together diamonds of felt on which they wiped their quill pens. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,191</i>)

>  <b>No. 53/35</b> Unlikely as it may seem, many fascinating plants, including the distinctive penwiper, <i>Notothlaspi rosulatum</i>, not only survive but flourish amid the shifting screes and windswept gullies far above the treeline. Ibid. 42 One scree specialist found in eatern parts of the Southern Alps from Marlborough to Canterbury, the attractive penwiper (<i>Notothlaspi rosulatum</i>), grows in a rosette of grey leaves which blend perfectly with their surroundings. <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic</i>)

>  The slide is too recent to be weathered, but on its edge there is a small patch of penwiper plant, shy and bulging with an unflowered stem that will in a few weeks open with a honey-scented head of white flowers. <i>(Oct 31, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),28</i>)

>  The strange-looking Notothlaspi rosulatum, or penwiper, grows in scree and got its name because it reminded early botanists of the felt wipers they used to wipe their quill pens. <i>(Aug 25, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,10</i>)

>  I was desirous of securing Notothlaspi rosulatum, the pen-wiper plant, but found no trace of it. <i>(Mar 18, 1924,Otago Witness,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a plant, Notothlaspi rosulatum of the Brassicaceae family, of S.I. high country areas [resembling strips of soft cloth used for wiping pens.]

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> On the higher levels, up to 5,000 feet and over, a scattered alpine vegetation, including pen-wiper and mountain buttercups. <i>(1969,Molesworth,14</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> The best known scree species is the penwiper plant which belongs to the cabbage family (Cruciferae). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,191</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> The fragrant penwiper plants can be smelt out from a great distance by wild sheep. [pen-wiper] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,79</i>)

### People's Bank
 
 <i>n.</i> the name initially used for what became Kiwibank q.v. (See [also Jim's Bank](../A#also-jim's-bank))

>  The People's Bank has more populist promise. <i>Ibid.</i> 17 If the People's Bank could shame bank fees down and bring sorely needed banking facilities to communities brutally deserted by the foreign-controlled banks, that would be a ginormous political coup. <i>(Oct 28, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Which brings me circuitously to the People's Bank issue. Other than the minnows, TSB and Southland Building Society, all of our banks are owned offshore. <i>(Feb 12, 2001,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  It was about three years ago that Jim Anderton began to channel this small-town anguish into a demand for a People's Bank. The best that NZ Post.. could come up with appeared to be a rather limited banking clip-on to its regular Post Shop services. <i>(Feb 8, 2001,Independent Business Weekly,13</i>)

>  Those who think the People's Bank or NZ Post Bank will come a gutser will be in for a surprise. <i>(Mar 4, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  For weeks, Cabinet has been mulling over whether to give the green light to the People's Bank. <i>(Feb 24, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  On the subject of the People's Bank, [Cullen's face] has till now ignored his soothing words on the subject, and come alive with malice and barely suppressed one-liners every time the bank is mentioned... But the People's Bank is expressly aimed at giving low-income New Zealanders low-cost accessible banking. <i>(Mar 3, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### people's day
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a day on which the general public attend an Agricultural and Pastoral Show; the day following judging of exhibits.

>  .. though the next or People's day (Saturday) was everything that could be desired as to weather. <i>(Dec 1898,NZ Farmer,432</i>)

>  This is how machinery row looked at Stratford Showgrounds on the afternoon of people's day. <i>(Dec 6, 1951,NZ Farmer,50</i>)

>  For People's Day on the second day brilliant sunshine followed the overcast conditions of the first day and there was a large attendance. <i>(Dec 2, 1954,NZ Farmer,18</i>)

### People's Palace
 
 <i>n.</i>

Many older New Zealanders have mentioned staying in The People's Palace during visits to Wellington, or as a staging point between the overnight train from Auckland and the Christchurch ferry. <i>(May 14, 2003,Dominion Post,B7</i>)

### pepe
 
 <i>n.</i> moth

 <i>n.</i> [general term for NZ native frogs]

### pepino
 
 <i>n.</i> The cultivated tropical South American shrub <i>Solanum muricatum</i>, fam. Solenaceae, with sweet juicy, pale cream to orange fruit with longitudinal purple stripes; also known as Peruvian melon, melon shrub, melon pear; introduced into NZ before 1940.

>  A tropical species that is gaining in popularity in the milder districts of New Zealand is the Pepino (Solanum muricatum). <i>(Jan 1950,NZ Gardener,363</i>)

>  The word pepino is also used for the cucumber in Latin America... To distinguish between the two plants the pepino as we know it is referred to as pepino dulce, meaning "sweet". <i>(Dec 1979,Orchardist NZ,349</i>)

>  Among the lesser known and more exotic fruit bearing plants.. are.. jujube.. pepino.. and sapodilla. <i>(May 1979,Orchardist NZ,112</i>)

>  The subtropical South American pepino or 'melon shrub' has been developed.. especially to suit Canterbury conditions. <i>(Jun 1982,Orchardist NZ,143</i>)

>  Two more pepino cultivars have recently been released.. by.. D.S.I.R. <i>(Apr 1983,Orchardist NZ,88</i>)

>  This fruit is most commonly known as 'pepino' (the Spanish word for cucumber), but also as melon shrub or melon pear. <i>(1980,Aglink HPP,208/1</i>)

>  He cites the economic potential of the pepino on a par with the tamarillo. <i>(Jun 1982,Orchardist NZ,143</i>)

>  Pepino is a small shrub three feet in neght belonging to the same family as the tomato and producing egg-shaped fruits, yellow streaked with purple lines. <i>(1943,Whitcombe's Complete NZ Gardener,270</i>)

>  Pepino (Solanum muricatum)... The fruits have a delicious flavour and may be eaten like a melon, or made into jams or preserves. <i>(1949,Brett's Gardening Guide,253</i>)

>  The pepino is known botanically as Solanaceae Solanum muricatum Ait, although it is commonly referred to as pepino dulce, pepino melon, melon pear, melon shrub and pear melon. (In New Zealand, we mostly just call it pepino). <i>(Aug 12, 1999,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> The pepino, Solanum muricatum, has oval shaped fruits of delicious flavour, the taste and aroma reminiscent of musk melon. <i>(1986,NZ Garden Book,241</i>)

>  For the future, keep your eyes open for kiwanos, okra, pepino, bitter melons and asparagus beans. <i>(Dec 1990,Cuisine,98</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> ... New Zealand horticulturalists have been on expeditions to the Andes and brought back other sub-tropicals to test under New Zealand conditions. These fruits include casanas, naranjillas, cherimoyas, casimiroas, carambolas, toronchis and pepinos. The last named are now available commercially. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,138</i>)

>  /p |pi:n u/. N.Z. & Austral. [S. Amer. Sp. (Peru).] (See quot. 1968.) 1968 J.C. Th. UPHOF <i>Dict. Economic Plants</i> (ed.2) 490/2 Solanum muricatum Ait. Pepino (Solanaceae). Herbaceous plant. Subtropics. Origin probably Peru. Cultivated in Centr. and S. America. Often sold in markets. Fruits frequently seedless, round to long, greenish yellow with purplish red blotches, juicy, agreeable flavor, somewhat aromatic, crisp; eaten raw. 1980 <i>Ann. Appl. Biol.</i> XCIV. 61 Pepino mosaic virus .. was found in fields of pepino (Solanum muricatum) in the Canete valley in coastal Peru. 1981 <i>Southern Horticulture</i> (N.Z.) Spring 16/1 Group three: blueberries, feijoas, avocado, tamarillos, cherries, babaco, persimmon, Asian pears, macadamias, pepino etc. 1984 <i>Sydney Morning Herald </i>4 Sept. (Good Living) 1 Pepino or Melon Pear or Melon Shrub - a shape and size simialr to an apple cucumber ... Good for decorating dishes such as pavlova and fruit salad, because when sliced the hollow centre and surrounding flesh look like a wheel. <i>(1988,Lexicographical & Linguistic Studies,195</i>) [NOTES: Burchfield]

>  The small shrubby pepino, that is native to Central America is grown widely, but is cultivated most extensively in Chile. <i>(Sep 1974,Orchardist NZ,259</i>)

>  <b>23</b> Keen gardeners.. will have noticed the pepino, which has been described as a fruit with a flavour something like a rock melon... The pepino is a member of the Colonaceae family and, as a true subtropical, requires a fairly long, hot growing season. <i>(Aug 1982,NZ Gardener</i>)

>  <b>BOLLARD</b> The pepino grows well in New Zealand and offers some promise as a commercial fruit for both fresh use and processing. <i>(1981,Prospects for Horticulture,115</i>)

>  It is the pepino <i>Solanum muricatum. Ibid,</i> 3 The pepino is closely related to the potato, tomato, and capsicum peppers; they all belong to.. the Solenaceae. <i>(1992,Alpha (Wellington),67/2</i>)

### pepite
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Stray notes on Australia and the Diggings (Griffiths Collection) Origin of the word `Nugget'. - This word, now so common amongst our population, is a strange sounding word, and destitute of any apparent entymological meaning. It originated at the Ophir diggings in New South Wales, and is probably a corruption of `ingot', which is itself a corruption of `lingot', a little tongue. The wedge of gold which Achan purloined is called, in the original Hebrew, a `tongue' of gold, as a reference to a Bible with marginal readings will show. In California, the word `lump' is generally used, and sometimes the Spanish word `pepite.' <i>(Sep 1852,Leisure Hour, The,640</i>)

### pepper
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cress](con-cyp.md#cress),[grass](go-gra.md#grass))



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Even the semi-tropical true pepper [fn <i>Piper excelsum</i>.] disputes with the false native pepper [fn <i>Drimys colorata.</i>] the right to its name, and yet another semi-tropical plant, the nikau [fn <i>Areca sapida</i>.] - New Zealand's only palm - extends as far south as Banks Peninsula, though not found on the Port Hills. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> kawakawa

>  [M. excelsum] My Son found a new kind of <i>Pepper</i> in flower with large cordated leaves ... <i>(May 25, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/286</i>) [NOTES: Check source]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> After collecting [at Q.C.Sound] a number of new plants, among which was a species of pepper, very much resembling ginger in taste ... we returned on board late in the evening. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/207</i>)

>  In the [alluvial bush land], which is wooded chiefly with <i>pukateas, kaikateas</i>, and rata trees, and thick underbrush of<i> konine</i> (fuchsia) or <i>roe-roe, kaua kaua</i> (or pepper), &c., entwined with kuriwau or supple-jack, ... <i>(1852,Wellington Gazette</i>) [NOTES: 1852 <i>Wellington Gazette</i> in Earp Hand-Book for Intending Emigrants 230]

>  Even the semi-tropical true pepper [fn <i>Piper excelsum</i>.] disputes with the false native pepper [fn <i>Drimys colorata.</i>] the right to its name, and yet another semi-tropical plant, the nikau [fn <i>Areca sapida</i>.] - New Zealand's only palm - extends as far south as Banks Peninsula, though not found on the Port Hills. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Macropiper excelsum</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pepper, native. <i>Piper excelsum. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Even the semi-tropical true pepper [fn <i>Piper excelsum</i>.] disputes with the false native pepper [fn <i>Drimys colorata.</i>] the right to its name, and yet another semi-tropical plant, the nikau [fn <i>Areca sapida</i>.] - New Zealand's only palm - extends as far south as Banks Peninsula, though not found on the Port Hills. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,184</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> The New Zealand Pepper is a small tree, 12-20 feet high, or sometimes a rambling shrub, with a very aromatic smell; it has been used for Tea, and for the cure of toothache; it is found also in Norfolk Island, and belongs to a subgenus (Macropiper) ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 227</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Macropiper excelsum</i>

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Macropiper excelsum </i> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Winteria (=Drimys) colorata </i>

>  <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New</i>) [NOTES: see 1900 Dendy]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> knot weed

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> WATER PEPPER (<i>Polygonum hydropiper</i>), called also knot weed in some places, is a plant similar to the former, but it lives in slow-running streams, grows 3 ft. high, and has flower heads 6 inches long, ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,59,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>pepper, water</i> [s] <i>Polygonum hydropiper</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> <i>water pepper</i> [s] <i>Polygonum hydropiper</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,57</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> REDSHANK (<i>Polygonum persicaria</i>) is also called persicaria, spotted persicaria, knot weed, smart weed, willow weed, and lady's thumb. Perhaps also it is this weed that on the west coast of the South Island is called water pepper or Walker's weed. It is an annual weed of gardens, crops, and wet pastures. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,59,1967, ed.</i>)

### pepperpot
 
 <i>v.</i> to scatter

>  New Zealand officialdom of the time feared the development of 'alien ghettos' in our cities, and many of the young Dutch immigrants were 'pepperpotted' all over New Zealand to work in farms and public works projects. <i>(Aug 2000,Open Eyes,1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to dot Maori Affairs department state houses in and throughout general housing areas [ca1953 at least (Naenae)]

>  Some years ago, Housing New Zealand decided to pepper-pot a long-settled area of Otahuhu with new houses, thereby introducing people of a different culture... <i>(Jan 20, 2006,NZ Herald,A10</i>)

>  Other Maori people are `pepper potted' through the structures, diluting their voice. pepper pot policy <i>(Jun 27, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,10</i>)

>  Still, here [in Sutch's <i>The Quest for Security in New Zealand</i>] ... is a discussion of ... one of the Maori `Harlems' without the `pepper pot' policy by which the Department of Maori Affairs is trying to obviate them, ... . <i>(Sep 1968,Political Science,20/1/70</i>) [NOTES: (P.S. O'Connor)]

>  <b>Michael King</b> The National Government initially accepted Hunn's recommendation [1960] that suburban Maori houses should be 'pepper-potted' among general state houses, but later abandoned the policy... <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,410</i>)

>  Opapa St, he recalls, was "pepper-potted" in line with the Government's policy of the time. One side consisted of State houses, the other was privately owned. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Evening Post,26</i>)

### pepperpotting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The new year got off to a great start vocabulary-wise when the minister of conversation (or the "chatting minister" for those keener on informal titles), Chris Carter, introduced us to the joys of "Pepper Potting ". <i>(Jan 22, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A11</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>pepperpotting</i> Maori Affairs past housing policy of sprinkling Maori families among Pakeha ones. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,83</i>)

### percentage establishment
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the number of plants in a crop in relation to seed sown.

>  OTAGO ANGLERS' ASSOCIATION March - Cup Day. - Perch competition, for prizes presented by Messrs J. Wilkie and Co., Mr W. Bremner, and Mr J. B. M'Callum. <i>(Jan 4, 1900,Otago Witness,45</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Pacific Perch <i>Trachypoma macracanthus</i> Gunther <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,406</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 406 [<i>Trachypoma macracanthus</i> aka toadstool groper]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a measure

>  The use of perch as a square measure in New Zealand - one fortieth of a rood - may be noted. NSOD gives this as 'rare, except historical'. It became historical here only in 1968 with the advent of metrication, and was until then anything but rare. The area of every piece of land that could not be expressed simply in acres or roods was described in perches. The word occurred in thousands of land titles, and was used to define the size of any section smaller than the quarter-acre 'norm'. Perch as a linear measure on the other hand (as in NZPOD) I have never seen outside the backs of school exercise books! <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [magpie perch - Australian 'migrants']

>  <i>Cheilodactylus bizonarius</i> Saville-Kent. (Tasmanian magpie-perch) <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/6</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Goniistius bizonarius</i> (Saville-Kent). MAGPIE-PERCH. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,38,1971 ed.</i>)

### Percy
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous) Geol., see cit.

>  1980 saw the introduction of a new stratigraphic parameter, the Percy. No longer do geologists illustrate the scale of the outcrop photographs with such mundane objects as hammer, lens caps, 2 cent pieces and bananas. If in the forefront of geological technique they now take as an essential item of field equipment, Percy Strong. Such is Percy's usefulness as a unit of stratigraphic thickness, and so great is the magnetism of his image on screen, that one lecturer introduced his choicest slide with the pronouncement "that's Percy", and only then proceeded to describe the rocks. It is understood that Cathy, with callous disregard for the visual effect on her husband's figure, is knitting for Percy a cap-a-pie jumpsuit with 100 horizontal stripes so that thin-bedded sequences may be measured in centi-Percies (cPe); and further that Percy himself now steers clear of field trips in areas of steeply-dipping strata, to avoid the necessity of being photographed in a horizontal position in stream beds. <i>(Feb 1981,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,51/21</i>)

### pere
 
 <i>n.</i> a shrub

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pe[acute]re, <i>Col[enso]. Alseuosmia Banksii. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

### perei
 
 <i>n.</i> an orchid with an edible root

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Forde one of my boys brought me some roots which when cooked were superior to the potatoe, the Perei. <i>(Mar 17, 1845,Journal,3/97</i>)

>  Perei. Gastrodia Cunninghamii, Hook. f. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/49</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  The <i>perei</i> is an orchid, scientifically known as <i>Gastrodia cunninhamii.</i> It is not at all a common plant. ... At the foot of each was a mass of small tubers or roots from 1/2 in. to 3/4 in. in thickness. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/53</i>)

>  Another fleshy root ... the <i>perei (Gastrodia cunninghamii)</i> - was also eaten. <i>(1881,TrNZI,13/31</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A plant resembling a long red radish (<i>perei</i>: Gastrodia cunninghamii) was valued for its root, but it was scarce, and only found in dense forests. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,99</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> The fleshy bracts of the root-climbing <i>tawhara,</i> the <i>perei </i>orchid,the young fronds of the <i>mauku</i> ... also furnished vegetable food. <i>(1929,Primitive Economics of NZ Maori,51</i>)

>  <b>BUICK</b> When digging for the <i>perei</i>, an edible root (<i>Orthoceras Solandri</i>), the diggers were warned against mentioning the name <i>perei</i>, or the root would never be found. <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,231</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>GASTRODIA ... Cunninghamii</i> ... MAIKAIKA, PEREI. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,25</i>)

>  <b>MEAD</b> After a long walk over the mania they entered the forest at 4 p.m. ... One of his boys collected some roots of the perei [<i>Gastrodia cunninghamii</i>] which when cooked he preferred to potato. <i>(1966,Richard Taylor,70</i>) [NOTES: [1845 March 17 Wangaehu]]

>  <b>FULLER</b> Perei and maikaika, types of orchis, were sought after. The perei had a mass of tubers which were cooked in the same way as aruhe. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,7</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Types of rare orchids known as perei (<i>Gastrodia cunninghamii</i> and maikuku (<i>Thelymitra longifolia</i>) were greatly prized for their masses of thick edible tubers. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,8</i>)

>  The most peculiar case is that of <i>Gastrodia cunninghamii</i>, which is called "perei," except when a person is looking for it, and then he must call it "maukuuku", or he will never find it. <i>(May 29, 1909,Evening Post,13</i>)

### Perendale
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep bred for wool and meat. [breed of sheep named after Sir Geoffrey Sylvester Peren, 1892-1980. Palmerston North sheep breeder and one of the founders of Massey University.]

>  <b>BROOKING</b> In 1960 a society was formed in order to standardise type and promote the breed which was named the Perendale in recognition of the founder's work. <i>(1977,Massey - Its Early Years,91</i>)

>  ...today Maurice farms Perendale sheep and beef on 1500 ha at The Brothers Point, just a few headlands east of the southernmost corner of the South Island. <i>(Jul 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 64/29</i>)

>  One of the reasons Perendales are back in favour is because the sheep don't need the same level of management as other breeds, Mrs Maxwell says. <i>(May 24, 2002,Dominion,19</i>)

>  Take perendales, now. They're more alert and make the dogs more alert. <i>(May 24, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  In 1968, he continued what proved to be a lifelong trend of bucking against the perceived wisdom of the day and began a Perendale stud... He says Perendales are over-intelligent and the key to being a good farmer is to know how to handle them. <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,C5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred sheep developed in the 1940s and 1950s from Cheviot and Romney genes [after geneticist Professor Peren.]

>  <b>BROOKING</b> In 1960 a society was formed in order to standardise type and promote the breed which was named the Perendale in recognition of the founder's work. <i>(1977,Massey - Its Early Years,91</i>)

>  <b>WARMAN</b> The Coopworth like the Perendale, belongs to the new generation of New Zealand bred sheep. <i>(1991,What Sheep is that?,24</i>)

>  <b>MEADOWS</b> Professor Peren developed Perendale, the breed subsequently named after him by crossing selected Cheviot rams with Romney stud ewes, and then continuing to breed with the progeny to fix the type. <i>(1997,Sheep Breeds of New Zealand,70</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  Mr Ayers says Perendale wool spins into good wool for bulking carpets, hand-knitting yarns, and for general use within the wool industry. <i>(May 24, 2002,Dominion,19</i>)

### perennial pea
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>pea, perennial [=] everlasting pea</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,57</i>)

### perf
 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) to retire (apparently) medically unfit under the P(olice) E(mployment) R(ehabilitation) F(und). [ORIGIN: acronym] (See [Perf noun](p-per.md#perf))

>  These observers tell of deepening despond as valuable senior staff.. perf and run. <i>(Oct 1997,North & South,35</i>)

>  `Perfing out' for [dollars]80,000 [heading] The number of seemingly fit police quitting the force with average payouts of [dollars]80,000 under recently introduced medical discharge provisions is being criticised within police ranks. A total of 125 officers have been granted medical disengagement on the grounds they are no longer able to carry out police work. Several have then taken up similar investigative work in the private sector. The medical option is colloquially known within the police as `perfing out', based on the acronym of the scheme's original name - police employment rehabilitation fund. <i>(Apr 26, 1987,Dominion Sunday Times,1</i>)

>  The Kapiti-Mana police district has been rocked by the loss of three senior policemen in the past week - they all intend to "perf" out of the force. <i>(May 6, 1997,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  It was inappropriate to say the latest three had "perfed" at this stage, as officially they were just on leave. <i>(May 6, 1997,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  ACT NZ police spokesman Ken Shirley said it was an affront to common decency that McLean had even applied to perf. <i>(May 1, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  In 1998 an Auckland constable perfed after he was caught socialising with criminals who were under electronic surveillance by his colleagues. <i>(May 1, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Rugby selector perfs... an officer seeking to perf is assessed after doctors' reports. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Sunday Star Times,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  <b>McGill</b> That type would never get past uniform branch. Probably throw sickies and perf themselves into better paid private security minders. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,225</i>)

>  I.. was told.. 'Well, you can't "perf"then.' <i>Ibid.</i> 18 Another "perfed" officer.. claimed policeofficers know what to say in medicalinterviews...He suffered a breakdown several yearsbefore he "perfed." ... He claimed to know of cases where bosseshad coached officers in what to say whenthey wanted them to "perf" rather thanstay in the force and face disciplinaryaction. <i>(Mar 11, 1995,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,17</i>) [NOTES: wrong source entered, clearly]

### Perf
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Police Employment Rehabilitation Fund [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  [He] has applied to leave the police under the Police Employment Rehabilitation Fund - or perf scheme. <i>(May 1, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Police Association advocate Greg Fleming said because the Perf (Police Employment Rehabilitation Fund) closed off in 1992, all officers recruited since then were eligible only for the Police Superannuation Scheme. The new scheme doesn't give officers the same hefty government funded payout that Perf offers on disengagement. <i>(Dec 10, 1997,Napier Daily Telegraph,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  Figures show $191m - comprising both taxpayer and individual officers' contributions - was made in perf payments between 1992 and May 1997. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Sunday Star Times,00</i>) [NOTES: page needed]

### perfer
 
 <i>n.</i> a policeman taking early retirement

>  So it falls to perfers, opposition MPs, worried citizens and the Police Association to draw attention to problems. <i>(Oct 1997,North & South,35</i>)

### perfing (out)
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Perfing" - getting out of the force on fitness or medical grounds, taking your superannuation with you - can net around $10,000 for every year in the force... That police officers are perfing in extraordinary numbers, more than one a day. <i>(Apr 19, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Paraparaumu has already lost five staff, most of them senior, through perfing in the past 18 months. <i>(May 6, 1997,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  [Hdg] 'Perfing out' for $80,000... The medical option is colloquially known within the police as "perfing out". based on the acronym of the scheme's original name - police employment rehabilitation fund. <i>(Apr 26, 1987,Dominion Sunday Times,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  [Hdg] Perfing still possible for 4000 police officers <i>(Dec 10, 1997,Napier Daily Telegraph,00</i>) [NOTES: Page needed]

>  Hunter is leaving through the controversial Police Employment Rehabilitation Fund, set up for officers who suffer psychological or physical impairment affecting their performance. It is commonly known as perfing. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Sunday Star Times,00</i>) [NOTES: page needed]

>  "Perfing", as it is commonly butinaccurately known (in reference to thepolice employment and rehabilitationfund), is considered by sum officers ajoke, a scam, and is routinely abused byofficers who wish to leave the force andfind the lure of leaving on medicalgrounds... just too tempting to passup... "Perfing" happens under section 28Dof the Police Act which allows police todisengage on grounds of physical orpsychological impairment, eithercompulsorily or voluntarily, and taketheir super contributions, those of theiremployer and interest in a taxfree lumpsum... <i>(Mar 11, 1995,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  'We'll whistle you off to a shrink andget you out under "perfing", don't youworry.' <i>(Mar 11, 1995,Evening Post,18</i>)
